<meta>This page continues the introductory polemic against the "Anhalt Secret Chancery," a collection of intercepted diplomatic documents from the Protestant Union that were published by Catholic forces to expose their political maneuvers during the early Thirty Years' War.</meta>

# Introduction to the Bavarian: Anhalt Secret Chancery.

About 3 $\frac{1}{2}$ years ago, the Anhalt Secret Chancery was brought to light through public printing, through which the "honesty" of the rebels—<note>original: "scil." [scilicet], meaning "namely" or "to wit"</note>—and the entire wretched <term>Calvinism</term><note>A branch of Protestantism following the teachings of John Calvin; in this context, it is used as a derogatory label by the Catholic author.</note> was presented to the whole honorable world in a brief summary, and thus the "shame-cap" <note>An idiomatic expression meaning a small covering or a fig leaf used to hide one's true, shameful nature.</note> was stripped from the Calvinists. Indeed, the most stubborn and headstrong Calvinists were overpowered by it, and their shameless conduct was so weakened that they themselves must feel ashamed of it to this day. 

Accordingly, out of pure distress over that opposing writing, the Calvinist spirit could no longer remain silent; rather, so that the Calvinist cause would not suffer even greater disaster, it spread a poisonous writing through public print—one far worse than the first—which it recently dared to defend. For in the *Chancery* <note>Referring to the original intercepted documents where the writers spoke their true intentions.</note> he wrote what he truly felt, but in this [new] writing he writes what he does *not* feel, even though he knows otherwise, and he wishes to force such knowledge upon other people. 

Yet it is all in vain: we Catholics have already grown wise to the Calvinists, and unbearded youths or mere mouths <note>A derogatory reference to young or inexperienced speakers.</note> cannot so easily deceive gray hair; much less will 600 books be able to purge or excuse their rebellion and the godless hostilities undertaken against our beloved common Fatherland for all eternity. What then should that "pepper-head" <note>original: "Pfefferkopff," a term for a stubborn, hot-headed, or "spicy" individual.</note> von Plessen do? He would have had a better case if he had not defended and protected it. For in that [book], the kind-hearted reader finds nothing at all but false and untruthful "white lead" <note>original: "Bleiweis," a white pigment used in cosmetics; here it means a deceptive facade or "whitewashing" the truth.</note> and deceitful, lame jests, the refutation of which requires the total tearing up and destruction of said book: considering that such <term>trashy pamphlets</term><note>original: "Schartecke," a derogatory term for a poorly written book or worthless piece of literature.</note> cannot be improved, but must be entirely struck out and annihilated. Therefore, let AJAX take the sponge in hand and wash himself according to the law <note>original: "Iuri," likely a reference to "iure" or legal/theatrical rules.</note> of this play.

The favorable reader might perhaps wonder why I choose von Plessen—seemingly a modest man—for this dispute, when other more stubborn Calvinists are to be found in armor and bearing arms? I am indeed aware of this, but either all my intuition deceives me, or the aforementioned von Plessen is the foremost one who to this 

<summary>The author introduces a Catholic polemic against the published "Anhalt Secret Chancery" documents, specifically attacking Volrad von Plessen and accusing the Calvinists of deceptive political maneuvers against the "Fatherland."</summary>
<keywords>Anhalt Secret Chancery, Calvinism, Volrad von Plessen, Counter-Reformation, Thirty Years' War, polemic, political propaganda</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the polemical attack against the leaders of the Protestant Union, focusing specifically on Ludwig Camerarius and Volrad von Plessen following the publication of the intercepted Anhalt diplomatic papers.</meta>

...provides the cause for this written war. This is because **Camerarius** <note>Ludwig Camerarius (1573–1651), a key advisor to the "Winter King" Frederick V and a primary target of this Catholic propaganda.</note> does not always have the leisure for it, being otherwise occupied with other business—such as secret gatherings, crafty schemes, subtle perorations, bowings of the knee, petitions, pleas, and weeping—and is continually intent on pursuing the most extreme ruin. Thus, **von Plessen** <note>Volrad von Plessen (1560–1631), another high-ranking Palatine statesman and diplomat.</note> himself undertakes to take up the defense, primarily for the sake of his own affairs. 

For when he looks upon his own writings—which he composed against all the godliness he owes and is bound to provide to the Fatherland and to the Imperial Majesty—he finds himself in no small state of anxiety. Therefore, he spares no effort or labor to see how he might cast off the hateful name of "peace-hating rebellion." Had he applied such great diligence before committing these excesses, he might have preserved his former reputation uninjured. Now, however, even with his laborious, grand, and alleged rescue-attempts, he cannot maintain his innocence in the slightest. After all, even young boys can interpret his irrevocable words and are moved to laughter by them. 

For what he has written, we understand very well; but we do *not* understand how he now wishes to interpret and explain it. Indeed, we hear him clearly enough, but we do not believe his explanation. We are not held innocent by the excusing of our vices, my dear **Plessen**, but rather by the abstaining from them. 

However, if I happen to find myself deceived, and my prophecy fails to hit the mark—or perhaps if someone other than you is the father of this "beautiful birth" <note>A sarcastic metaphor referring to the published defense or pamphlet as an "offspring."</note>—you should not be the least bit annoyed that I attribute this monstrosity to you on behalf of your much-beloved and, due to your adversity, highly-distressed companion-in-arms. For among good friends, especially those so closely related and devoted to one another, all things are held in common—even the misery into which your far too heated and highly damaging schemes have plunged you. 

This shall serve as a notable example to our dear descendants, who, by the misfortune of others, will know how to prevent their own downfall and will advise Princes to be content with their own station and not to partake in the goods of others. Fortune does not accept ambitious princes or those greedy for other kingdoms as her heirs; rather, she generally regards those born of such company as stepchildren. For:

->For the worst children, Fortune is the worst stepmother;<-
->I would rather have a humble woman of Venusia <note>original: "Venusinam," a classical reference likely referring to the humble birthplace of the poet Horace, suggesting a preference for low-born honesty over high-born misfortune.</note> than you, Fortune, as a parent.<-

->That is:<-

->Wicked children have wicked stepmothers,<-
->And I would rather have Venusina,<-
->Than Fortune, for my mother.<-

<summary>The author mocks Volrad von Plessen's attempts to defend his reputation against charges of rebellion, characterizing his diplomatic writings as a "monstrosity" shared with his colleague Camerarius. The text concludes with a moral warning to future generations against political ambition and coveting the territories of others.</summary>
<keywords>Volrad von Plessen, Ludwig Camerarius, rebellion, Anhalt Secret Chancery, political propaganda, Fortune, Thirty Years' War, Palatinate</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the heated exchange between the Palatine diplomat Volrad von Plessen and his opponent (Fabius), following the publication of the "Anhalt Chancellery"—a collection of intercepted letters that exposed the Protestant Union's strategic maneuvers, much to their embarrassment.</meta>

P L E S S I V S.
A 3
<meta>catchword: your</meta>

## To all lovers of the Truth.

Two years have now passed since several people circulated a "Monk-Bavarian" book through public printing, the title of which is: **The Secret Princely Anhalt Chancellery**. <note>The *Geheime Anhaltische Kanzlei* was a highly effective piece of Catholic propaganda published in 1621, containing intercepted documents from the Protestant leader Christian of Anhalt which revealed plans for the "Winter King" Frederick V to take the Bohemian throne.</note> The first copies of this work were delivered into the hands of the United Princes <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant states in the Holy Roman Empire.</note> who were then assembled at Heilbronn at the end of January in the past year of 1621. Its author, or rather its compiler, is said to have been Dr. W. J., a Bavarian Privy Councilor—who was faithfully assisted and supported by Dr. Leickhard—as credible people report. Indeed, his *style* <note>original: "Stylus"</note> betrays him in this regard. However, we are content to leave that matter, as with the rest, in its place. One is informed both in writing and by word of mouth that the most Serene Duke of Bavaria himself <note>Maximilian I, Elector of Bavaria (1573–1651), the leader of the Catholic League.</note> gathered various letters and other records and put them into an order so that the work might be finished all the sooner and sent to Heilbronn during the ongoing Convention of the Union, just as indeed happened at that time. All of this was done—as anyone can easily perceive—so that the United Princes and Lords might be troubled, made fearful, and filled with doubt.

## F A B I V S.

It is a wonder, Plessen, that you dare to address your writing to the "lovers of truth," yet you yourself have little love for the truth, showing yourself to be its enemy from the very beginning of your book. For the **Secret Princely Anhalt Chancellery** was not rushed for the reason you claim—to be sent to the princely assembly at Heilbronn at the end of January 1621—because at that time, there was no intention yet to publish it. It was first put to press during the Great Lent of that year, and was not sent to Heilbronn until the month of May, when the second Convention of that year was held. After all, it could not walk abroad before it was born! Furthermore, you are also mistaken regarding the *author* <note>original: "Auctoris"</note> and you deceive the reader. For Dr. W. J. was not the *compiler* <note>original: "Compilator"</note> or "patcher-together" of this collected work, much less was Dr. Leickhard his faithful assistant. For as large a nose as you have, you still cannot smell such smoke as to recognize him—though it matters little who

<summary>In this dialogue, the diplomat von Plessen accuses the Bavarian court of rushing the publication of intercepted documents to intimidate the Protestant Union, while his opponent Fabius mocks his factual errors regarding the book's timeline and authorship.</summary>
<keywords>Protestant Union, Secret Anhalt Chancellery, Heilbronn Convention, Maximilian of Bavaria, propaganda, Thirty Years' War, Dr. Wolfgang Jöchler</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the polemical dialogue between Volrad von Plessen (Plessius), a diplomat for the Protestant Palatinate, and a Catholic defender (Fabius). They are debating the legitimacy of the "Secret Anhalt Chancellery," a publication of intercepted Protestant documents that damaged the reputation of the Protestant Union.</meta>

spread your own affairs through public printing. We should be free to write publicly what you were free to write secretly, so that we might publicly learn of your imprudence. However, I do not understand why you call such a book—which was, after all, compiled from your own writings—a *patchwork* <note>original: "Centonem"; a literary work made up of fragments from other authors, or a garment made of rags.</note>. Do you even know what such a *patchwork* is? It is a blanket stitched together from rags, scraps, and other old things. Are your affairs then such tattered blankets and Hecalean skins <note>A reference to the humble, rustic scraps associated with the myth of Hecale, used here to mock the "poor quality" of the intercepted Protestant letters.</note> that they provided the material for your *patchwork*? The author of this Secret Anhalt Chancellery, whom you consider a "patch-worker," might not take such a mocking word so poorly if you would also call your own birth the mother of this *patchwork*. Indeed, certainly, from your utterly shameless pamphlets <term>Scartecken (scurrilous or worthless political pamphlets)</term> nothing else could be scraped together but such a multi-colored *patchwork*. And if the compiler’s work was poor, it certainly matched the material he had to work with.

As for the claim that His Electoral Grace in Bavaria <note>Maximilian I, Elector of Bavaria and leader of the Catholic League.</note> supposedly helped with such a book, this spread rumor is a fiction. And because your pen is exactly like this rumor, the one should be held as no more valid than the other. It is as if His Electoral Grace, amidst such highly important war affairs—with which he was quite overwhelmed at that time—had nothing else to do but take time away from such weighty business to concern himself with books! But let it be, my Plessy, that even if a nobler hand had been set to this work, would it then be destroyed? The book is ennobled by such high and excellent assistance—which, however, was not in the least necessary at the time, because you yourself helped. Had you committed less, the scribe would have written less about you.

->P L E S S I V S.<-

The authors were also not satisfied that this Bavarian compilation was issued in Munich in the German language; they also had it printed in the Italian language under the title *Thoughts of the Protestants* <note>original: "Pensieri de protestanti," translated in the text by the Latin "Cogitationes protestantium."</note>, and even had it sent to Rome, where it is openly sold in the public bookshops.

->F A B I V S.<-

It is, Plessy, contrary to the bitter truth that such a work of the Anhalt...

<summary>Fabius mocks Plessius for calling the leaked documents a "patchwork," arguing that the low quality of the book merely reflects the "shameful" nature of the Protestant letters themselves. He also denies that Elector Maximilian I of Bavaria personally edited the collection, while Plessius complains about the work's international distribution in Italian.</summary>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the heated debate regarding the publication of intercepted diplomatic documents. The "Spanish Chancellery" mentioned at the start refers to the *Hispanica Cancellaria*, a pro-Protestant polemic that was being countered by Catholic publications like the "Bavarian Book" (the *Anhaltische Kanzlei*).</meta>

[Spanish] Chancellery <note>original: "Hispanischen Cantzeley"</note> forgot the German language in Munich and traveled from there to Italy. God knows who might have translated it into the Italian language; it was certainly not printed in that language in Munich. But as for the fact that it is now publicly for sale and being sold in open bookshops, you are the ones to blame, because you yourselves committed the acts that were written about and sold.

### P L E S S I V S.

However, the reason why the United <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states.</note> and other estates, as well as their principal councilors and other officers, have not yet answered that Bavarian book (which, with secret deliberations that ill-befit the Germans, runs counter to them) with a public, apologetic defense and vindication—to preserve their reputation and good name—is for a significant reason. Aside from what was recently brought to light briefly but skillfully in the *Prodromus* <note>original: "Prodromo"; a precursor or introductory publication.</note> and the *Spanish Chancellery*, and then somewhat more extensively in *The Mystery of Iniquity* <note>original: "Mysterio Iniquitatis"; a famous work by Philippe de Mornay attacking the Papacy.</note>, they have lacked the opportunity. This delay has been unavoidable for those who had no means to respond, particularly due to the lack of printing presses <term>Truckereyen (printing houses or offices)</term>. For as everyone knows, the people of the Palatinate have been so scattered by the violent occupation and seizure of the Upper and Lower Palatinate <note>A major turning point in the Thirty Years' War (1620-1623) where the Protestant heartland was conquered by Catholic forces.</note> that those who had to flee Heidelberg for other places have had no printing press in which they might properly defend themselves against the published accusations. Furthermore, everything that was sent from or to Heidelberg has been subject to hostile interception and capture.

### F A B I V S.

My dear Plessy, do not say that we have written about all the United, but only about the Calvinists, who with reckless fury have overshot the mark by far. Because of this, you and your comrades are summoned before the judgment seat of the entire honorable world, not to be judged for a single virtue. If you still have any hope of comforting yourselves with words or the pen, then bring it to light now—but what will you achieve with it? The eloquence of Camerarius <note>Ludwig Camerarius (1573–1651), the chief advisor to the "Winter King" Frederick V and a primary target of Catholic polemicists.</note> is not enough for this, and the cleverness of Plessius even less so. For a lost reputation is not regained through a clever, subtle head or intellect. Only innocence restores a good report and name—and I shall see such innocence when I never see a Calvinist again! The lack of printing presses has not imposed silence on you out of necessity; rather, it is the mistrust of your [cause]...

<summary>Plessius argues that the Protestant Union has been unable to publish a formal defense because the Catholic occupation of the Palatinate destroyed their printing presses. Fabius scoffs at this, claiming their silence is actually due to their obvious guilt and the radical nature of Calvinism.</summary>
<keywords>Protestant Union, Palatinate, printing presses, censorship, Calvinism, Ludwig Camerarius, Volrad von Plessen, propaganda, Thirty Years' War</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a polemical dialogue between "Plessivs" (representing Philippe de Mornay, a Huguenot leader and advisor to the Protestant cause) and "Fabivs" (representing a Catholic/Bavarian perspective). The debate concerns the "Anhalt Chancellery," a collection of secret Protestant diplomatic papers captured by Catholic forces and published to discredit the Protestant Union. The previous page ended discussing the lack of printing presses available to the displaced people of the Palatinate.</meta>

...bad things. Since already several lampoons <note>original: "Scartecken"; refers to worthless, scurrilous, or poorly printed pamphlets</note> full of many useless words have come to light everywhere, and the presses in the printing houses have been rushing ever since the *Anhalt Secret Chancellery* was released—now for seventeen whole months. You did not lack printers who could have printed what you wrote; rather, you did not know what you should write. Like a fearful wolf, you only pricked up your long ears until time came to the aid of your understanding and gave you the chance to bring something deceptive onto the stage.

# P L E S S I V S .

However, because it is highly to be feared that such continued silence might be commonly taken for a secret confession or an admission of guilt, and that consequently a particular advantage might grow for the Roman faction <note>The Catholic party supporting the Holy Roman Emperor.</note>, while an irreparable prejudice <term>præjuditz (a legal term for harm, damage, or a disadvantageous precedent)</term> and detriment might fall upon the United <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states.</note> as well as the general Evangelical <note>Protestant</note> cause: therefore, unavoidable necessity has required that this current, correct, and thorough **Defense Writing** regarding the principal chapters, passages, and points of the *Bavarian Chancellery* be published through open print. Furthermore, this is done so that one may get to more specific details <note>original: "ad specialiora"</note> and, as if with an infallible touchstone, demonstrate and prove that such a **Bavarian Book** is not in the least founded upon clear and express evidence (as the author attempts to persuade the readers), but rather that all sorts of machinations, slanders, perversions, reversals, and all kinds of mutilated allegations <note>original: "allegationes"; citations or evidence presented in court</note> and citations are to be found everywhere within it. And finally, so that we may encompass much with few words, [it is shown] that the reader has been led astray <note>original: "hinder das Licht geführet"; literally "led behind the light," a German idiom for being deceived</note> by practical-political ruses and tricks.

# F A B I V S .

Regardless of the fact that silence is often an indication of a committed vice, not too much weight is placed on it in this case. For your own writings, your hands, and your own seals <term>insigel (wax seals used to authenticate official documents)</term> bring the matter so clearly to light that there is not the slightest need for further indications or proofs. And what have you achieved, my dear Plessis, with such a "necessary" answer? Out of a hundred places, you have selected only a very few which you wished to contest; yet you do not defend yourself or your dilapidated cause, but rather you draw your sword against us in vain. We have forfeited nothing, and even if we had sinned, yet

...nevertheless, from on high it is shouted at you: Holy, Holy, etc. Yet you, along with your adherents and accomplices, have been convicted of offending His most aggrieved **Imperial Majesty** <term>Kayß. Mayest.; refers to the Holy Roman Emperor, Ferdinand II</term>, as your own sun-clear testimonies and documents sufficiently demonstrate and prove. And yet you write, despite all this, that everything you squabble about in this book is based on solid foundations and good reasons. I do not deny this, for even a club-foot has its crutch; however:

> Lame footsteps trust in a misshapen gait <note>original Latin: "Clauda sed informi fidunt vestigia gressu"</note>,
> ->That is:<-

->He who limps, snaps from one side<-
->To the other, always uncertain.<-

Our foundations and reasons, however, are so steady and infallible that you yourself must bear witness to them when you see your own letters and writings. For they are taken from those very sources, and should rightly hold weight with **Plessis** <term>Philippe de Mornay, Lord of Plessis, a prominent Huguenot leader and the persona speaking here</term>, since he wrote them himself. Likewise, **Camerarius** <term>Ludwig Camerarius, the head of the Palatine chancellery whose captured papers sparked this controversy</term> must also accept them when I present his own handwriting to him. We have transcribed everything from your own **scurrilous pamphlets** <term>Scartecken; a derogatory term for worthless or libelous booklets</term>; therefore, they must be either true, or else false and lied. If they are false, then see, **Plessis**, what kind of man you are, in that you allowed yourself to act against the truth. But if everything you wrote is true—well then, we also have solid ground. And would you therefore transfer the "flowers and lights" <note>A rhetorical term for elegant or deceptive flourishes of speech</note> of your newly begun **speech** <term>Oration; a formal public address</term>—namely all sorts of tricks, distortions of speech, and various mangled **citations** <term>Allegationes; legal or formal references to sources</term>, etc.—to your own book? For throughout that work, your pages earn such a reputation. You do not prove anywhere in your book that we have erred; yet, right at the beginning, you cleverly prejudice the reader. Had you written such things only at the end, the reader might have been able to say that you were writing falsehoods. As it stands, he cannot do so yet—but you certainly can.

->P L E S S I V S<-

Furthermore, we protest and declare that we shall strive for modesty, refrain from all insults, and intend to show everyone the respect due to their rank and status. We comfort ourselves in the total hope that everything we do in this manner—to preserve ancient **German liberty** <term>teutsche Freyheit; the traditional rights and autonomy of the individual states within the Holy Roman Empire</term> and the protection of the **Evangelical Religion** <term>Evangelischer Religion; the Protestant faith</term>, as well as to rescue so many in-

After this, however, we wish to faithfully warn the kind-hearted reader / that he should take to heart this information (in which we follow the third and corrected edition printed at Munich, with the citations and references of the Bavarian Chancery <note>The administrative office of the Electorate of Bavaria, a Catholic stronghold</note>) which we have divided into two parts / and in the first part of which the following chapters are examined and refuted.

->FABIUS<- <note>A pseudonym for the Catholic interlocutor or author</note>

I had supposed / that von Plessen <note>Volrad von Plessen (1560–1631), a leading statesman of the Electoral Palatinate and a prominent Calvinist</note> would by now have been somewhat dejected / and troubled: but he still holds out good hope for himself: he promises, indeed, modesty / and gentleness: but whether he will actually demonstrate such in his deeds / let the kind-hearted reader judge for himself. A wolf caught in a pit / even while standing amidst the prey, refrains from tearing them apart: nevertheless, he looks upon the sheep in the meantime with fire-flashing eyes / and earns little thanks for his "friendship": he is an enemy even in his restraint / which is indeed very slight in the case of von Plessen / as the reader has already seen / and will see further upon reading through this book. And why should the Calvinists praise their piety and modesty so much to me? For in the *Dissertation* <note>original: "Dissertation"</note> and the *Causes of the Uprising in the Kingdom of Bohemia* / which he wished to incorporate into his smear-piece / he storms / like the bloated Cacus <note>A fire-breathing giant from Roman mythology, defeated by Hercules</note>, with great violence against His Imperial Majesty <note>Ferdinand II, Holy Roman Emperor</note> as the

> highest head in the Roman Empire: In which he calls him a prince of a sulfurous nature <note>original: "schwäfelichten Ingenij"</note>: Likewise a cruel man / born from an impure / God-damned / marriage bed / raised in the corners of the Jesuits / instructed with all kinds of perjurous teachings / and whose cruelty could be moved to no obedience. The Bohemians had considered it according to their office / that one should no longer suffer his tyranny / which was always mixed with great servitude: and he was driven out by the Free Estates not by the Count of Thurn / nor by Hollach <note>Count Hohenlohe</note>, but by his own tyranny / perjury / and cruelty. And thus far speaks von Plessen’s faithful Achates <note>A reference to the loyal companion of Aeneas in Virgil's Aeneid; here used sarcastically for a co-conspirator</note> and comrade-in-arms / who cloaks himself with the name of Luiteonis Thomsonij Carlomontij <note>A pseudonym for a pro-Bohemian writer</note>: which two equally noble writers / are to be highly praised for their new modesty / and gentleness. For this demonstrated respect, my dear.

<meta>This page continues the polemical exchange regarding the "Anhalt Secret Chancery," a collection of intercepted documents used as propaganda during the early years of the Thirty Years' War. The text addresses "Plessi" (likely a pseudonym or reference to a specific diplomat) and transitions into a formal defense of the Protestant Union.</meta>

Dear Plessi, the highest dignity and the promised modesty with which you wish to vindicate the great innocence of so many honest people—who have undoubtedly been proscribed and declared under the Imperial Ban <note>The "Reichsacht" or Imperial Ban was a form of outlawry in the Holy Roman Empire</note>—is just as much as if you wished to rescue the guilty in their supposed innocence. Regarding the other part of your information with which you threaten the Compiler: that same part, I say, you might well hold back. It is enough that you have been seen this time, and we hope for your improvement the less you present yourself again.

->P L E S S I U S .<-

# Information and Answer to the Principal Chapters, Passages, and Points of the Anhalt Secret Chancery.

## The First Chapter.

### That the Union <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states formed in 1608</note> was intended for the defense of the United Protesting Estates themselves, but in truth was aimed not against Catholic plots, but rather at the suppression and extermination of the Roman Catholics in the Empire.

## Information and Answer.

The Compiler of this Bavarian-Anhalt Chancery particularly amuses himself (page 21) with this: namely, that the United Estates, even to this day, cannot bring any specific details to light regarding what harmful plots were undertaken by the Catholics against the Estates adhering to the Augsburg Confession <note>The primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church</note>; instead, they always get stuck in such specifics. To which he has as an answer: what kind of general process was instituted and decided upon against all Protestants in general, and how one should deal and proceed with them all together, without any distinction—that has already been known for a long time and in great abundance from the book of Paul Windeck <note>A Catholic polemicist known for his anti-Protestant writings</note>. But dear friend, what does Schopp <note>Kaspar Schoppe, a notorious Catholic convert and controversialist</note>, the Spanish-Austrian

<summary>This page introduces a formal rebuttal to accusations that the Protestant Union was an aggressive rather than defensive alliance, specifically addressing claims made in the "Anhalt Secret Chancery" propaganda.</summary>
<keywords>Protestant Union, Anhalt Secret Chancery, Imperial Ban, Augsburg Confession, Paul Windeck, Kaspar Schoppe, Thirty Years' War, religious polemic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a dialogue or polemic regarding the religious tensions in the Holy Roman Empire, likely following a discussion on legal precedents and imperial authority.</meta>

Rath <note>likely a reference to a specific counselor or author</note> in his *Sacred War-Trumpet* <note>original: "Classicobelli Sacri"</note> chapter 13, whisper into the ear of the Imperial Majesty? That he should have those Princes—who had seized the monasteries and ecclesiastical estates, and who would obey neither the Imperial Majesty nor the Holy See in Rome—hanged from the gallows, following the fine example of Emperor Otto the First. What is now said of everyone in general also affects each one *specifically* <note>original: "in Specie"</note>.

### FABIUS.

What sort of wisdom you are bringing to the floor in this book, I cannot yet perceive, since from the very beginning I sense little prudence. Do you perhaps take us for sponges <note>meaning: those who soak up any nonsense</note>, that we should give any weight to such worthless words of yours? We have not hindered the Protestants in the slightest regarding their freedom up to this point. Now I repeat, and at the same time request, that you would recount our crimes committed against the general Public Peace <note>term: Landtsfrieden - the legal state of internal peace within the Holy Roman Empire</note> in an orderly fashion, one after another. You introduce two writers of—I know not what kind of—martial spirit, who have published *certain private* writings, but who have never touched the commonwealth; they have no seat or *standing* <note>original: "Respect"</note> in the Council, and their speeches should no more be attributed to our common good than to one or another person who utters vain threats. For, my dear fellow, why should I be much afraid when two or three draw the sword against me who cannot even use it properly? I only have cause to truly fear when those who have the power to do so venture to undertake something hostile against me: namely, when the Calvinist Princes and Lords sound the alarm, hold great assemblies, establish strong alliances among themselves, and deliberate with one another—not for their *defense* <note>original: "defension"</note>, but for my *offense* <note>original: "offension"</note>. The question is not what I might deserve if cruelty and violence held sway, but what suffering the enemy intends to inflict upon me. This, this, I say, is what I must fear, and what has been put into action by you. Now, go ahead and accuse the Catholic Estates <note>term: Catholischen Ständen - the Catholic territories and rulers represented in the Imperial Diet</note> in the Empire and our high authorities of the same. If we have ever broken faith and trust, or ever taken such a thing into our minds, then triumph over me; but as it stands, I will harness you to my wagon. We do not care much for private individuals, nor do we let them disturb us; every man’s tongue and pen errs to his own harm, and the common utility <note>original: "gemeine Nutz"</note> is not affected by the chatter of one or another, otherwise no kingdom could endure. And, my dear fellow, what sort of coarse, irresponsible pranks have your Calvinist pastors...

have committed, they may atone for **privately** <note>original: *priuatim*</note>: you should therefore not ascribe the anger of a few to everyone in common. We do not wish to defend the lack of restraint of a single writer, but nonetheless, we wish to examine those cited by you a little more closely. For **Windeck** <note>J. P. Windeck, a Catholic controversialist</note>, though he is somewhat [harsh] in his writing, nevertheless spins no silk <note>an idiom meaning he does not flatter or treat gently</note> for those Catholics who neglect good morals and virtue. Furthermore, I am in no doubt that the heretics have no greater enemy than this **Windeck**. However, he has only to do with the rebel Calvinist ringleaders and ministers, as can be seen from his **Protestation** and **Epilogue** <note>original: *Epilogo*</note>, or the conclusion of his **Prognostici**, where he writes among other things: "Solely and alone against the masters of the heretics, the Doctors, and Calvinist **Preachers** <note>original: *Prædicanten*</note>, but not against Princes and other authorities, have I undertaken this struggle or dispute, whom I have not the slightest intention to **insult** <note>original: *Iniuriiren*</note>, as I here publicly and **solemnly** <note>original: *solenniter*</note> testify." Thus far **Windeck**. The other is **Scioppius** <note>Kaspar Schoppe, a scholar known for his fierce polemics</note>: but because he is still living, you might challenge him to the fight yourself. He is armed himself and does not need my support to rescue him from danger.

## P L E S S I V S.
<note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader</note>

Thus it is also **notorious**, known, and widely rumored that at the Imperial Diet held at Regensburg in the year 1594, and at other Diets following it, it was blustered out without shame by Jesuits and Franciscans (or Barefoot Monks) against the protesting Evangelical Estates and the absent Lords—as if one wished to spit in their faces: Namely, that His Imperial Majesty could no longer look upon the protesting heretics in the Empire without danger to his conscience, and that now the right time and hour were at hand to draw the sword against them. Now (they said) there were good opportunities and means available. For the Protestants were so embittered against one another and so divided because of internal discord and disunity, that some—being incautious and entirely secure—and others—being weak and unprepared—could very easily be overwhelmed and brought to obedience to the **Roman See** <note>original: *Römischen Stuls*; referring to the Papacy</note> without any effort. This was then sufficiently testified to shortly thereafter by the **execution** <note>The "Execution of Donauwörth" (1607), where the city was placed under imperial ban and forcibly re-Catholicized</note> that befell the poor city of Donauwörth: which finds itself so distressed by its own damage that even the poor citizens—formerly free citizens of the Holy Roman [Empire]—

## FABIUS.
<note>Fabius is a persona in this dialogue, likely representing a Catholic defender, named after the Roman tactician Fabius Maximus.</note>

This I will blow away again with a single breath, just as you have blown it out with a single breath. For in the year 1594 at Regensburg, not a single Capuchin monk preached, but only the Jesuits. Yes, I say, the Jesuits preached, but in a far different manner than you claim. It is also publicly evident that at the very beginning of your book, you have fabricated what you wrote—lest you should lack for anything but the truth—before you even finished the book; indeed, you might as well have left it out entirely. For at the aforementioned Imperial Diet <term>Reichstag: the deliberative assembly of the Holy Roman Empire</term> at Regensburg, many Protesting Princes were present in person, who would have either denounced such insolent and far too bold preaching, or would have vindicated and avenged themselves on account of it. The Jesuits are accustomed to weighing their words at home first, so that afterwards, in public, they might have weight and authority both for the substance of the matter and for their modesty. Now, if these men (to grant you something here for your sake) or those men brought something perhaps too bold onto the path, they meant you Calvinists by it, and wished to set a limit to your presumption, which increases more and more from day to day. For from a careless beginning, you have always gained the upper hand, venturing soon to start an uprising and even to rule over others. And at that very Imperial Diet, you made many complaints and—as you called it at the time—presented many "points of grievance" <note>original: "Beschwärnußpuncten"</note> to His Imperial Majesty, and among other things, you let it be known that you wished to proceed according to the statutes of the Holy Roman Empire, provided that His Imperial Majesty would not remedy your alleged grievances. And pray, what were your grievances at that very time? You were forbidden from divesting yourselves of Catholic Church property <note>original: "Kirchengüter"</note> and were told you should restore the same. This was your grievance—just as if you took pleasure in our ruin. Furthermore, the Emperor was quite burdensome to you when he passed judgment in spiritual matters; namely, as a judge to whom the Calvinist spirit was entirely suspicious. Special mediators were heard on both sides, who did not end the dispute but, as they ran against one another, only delayed it further, as if through eternal obstinacy, and only for the end that those who [held] other people's 
goods...

estates they had seized, they might not be robbed of their possession again. I will discuss this no further, since even the Lutherans themselves—except for a few whom you then brought to your side through cunning and deceitful ways—recognized your demands as unseemly. This was especially true of the Duke of Saxe-Weimar <note>Friedrich Wilhelm I, Duke of Saxe-Weimar (1562–1602)</note>, who was at that time the Administrator of the Electorate of Saxony; he told you that you should be content with what is eternal, following the prompting of Dr. Hunnius <note>Aegidius Hunnius (1550–1603), a staunch Lutheran theologian who opposed Calvinist "intrigues"</note>, who uncovered your practices in a published writing. However, when you failed in your efforts at that time, you let it trouble you little; instead, at the following Imperial Diet <term>Reichstag: the deliberative assembly of the Holy Roman Empire</term>, you submitted your lamentations and petitions again, even more numerous than before. By this, you hindered the Imperial counsels and deliberations—you, who were on the verge of being driven out of the Empire, and who remain in it with no legal basis, but only through the mercy of other people.

Is it not a grand display of pretension to claim that the city of Donauwörth was forced under the yoke by the preaching of the Jesuits and Capuchins? As if it were unknown to almost the whole world from whence such trouble befell the said city! For because the citizens blocked the alleys and public streets to the Catholics, and thereby sought to prevent them from their ancient, customary processions—wishing to entirely deprive them of the small remaining remnant of their religion—those in need of help called upon His Imperial Majesty. Thereupon, His Majesty reminded the said citizens of the Treaty of Passau <note>The 1552 treaty which guaranteed religious freedoms to Lutherans, but here cited regarding the rights of the remaining Catholics</note> concerning the public Peace of the Land. But when such Imperial admonition found no place among those citizens, the Duke <note>Maximilian I of Bavaria</note>, upon receiving Imperial orders, brought the rebels to obedience by force. Therefore, if they complain of such servitude, they have themselves to thank: they well deserved it by their excessive desire to dominate. They have thereby provided a useful example to the Free Imperial Cities, that they should not trust too much in their luck, nor cast out others by whom they were previously accepted and taken in.

->PLESSIS<- <note>Likely referring to Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader often cited in religious polemics</note>

Furthermore, one can still well remember what was negotiated at the same time, in the name of Emperor Rudolf <note>Rudolf II, Holy Roman Emperor (reigned 1576–1612)</note>, with the Duke of Bavaria, regarding a general execution <term>Execution: the legal enforcement of an Imperial decree, often by military force</term> against the Protestants, by the Count of Hohenzollern, President of the Imperial Chamber Court; and that the said President gave two different, contradictory reports—

...fabricated reports <note>original: "tiones erdichtet"</note>: in one of these (which was diligently circulated here and there to mock the Protestants <note>literally "to twist a nose at them"</note> and to pull the wool over their eyes), he informed Emperor Rudolf that he had been unable to obtain anything from the Duke of Bavaria, despite applying all possible diligence, because the Duke did not wish to burden or trouble himself in the slightest with such Imperial commissions and executions. In the other, somewhat more secret report, however, he declared that the Duke of Bavaria was minded—provided His Imperial Majesty would show and provide the necessary and sufficient means of execution—that he would then undertake such a general execution <note>a legal term for the military enforcement of an Imperial decree</note> against the Protestants. It is therefore beyond all doubt that, had the United <insert>Protestant Union</insert> not formed an alliance with one another at that time, and had the intended progress of the aforementioned general execution against the Protestants not been prevented by such an alliance, then the neighboring estates and cities—especially the smaller and weaker ones—would have fallen into the same danger as Donauwörth. Indeed, even the more powerful ones, such as the Duke of Württemberg, Ulm, Nuremberg, and still others, would have been deprived of their glorious safety and welfare.

->FABIUS<-

This speech of yours, Plessis, brings little credit to your nobility; for I do not know what sort of confused counsel he brings to the floor, which was never even discussed by the Princes, but was devised by the Calvinists for pure deception. For Emperor Rudolf neither assigned any such execution to the Duke of Bavaria, nor exhorted him to undertake one. Since, at that inglorious time, the army of the Calvinists had attacked Alsace, which caused considerable terror not only to the Bishop of Strasbourg, whom they persecuted, but also to other Catholics. However, so that the Duke of Bavaria might suppress such an uprising and come to the aid of the Catholics, he himself recruited troops, intending to attack the Calvinists with war in like manner if they continued further and did not cease their hostile proceedings. Therefore, His Imperial Majesty—who desired above all to promote the common welfare and peaceful prosperity—dispatched the Count of Hohenzollern to the Duke of Bavaria to settle and quiet the public affairs again, but not so that the highly esteemed Duke would incite the Protestant estates to war. Since at that time there was no cause for the innocent to be punished, but only for the restless destroyers of the general peace to be...

For after the Union <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states formed in 1608</note> heard that the Duke of Bavaria had undertaken and begun military preparations, they immediately dispatched their legates to him, who brought back not only peace but also [assurances] from there. And this is the entire "Execution" <note>A legal or military enforcement of an imperial decree</note> which you unfairly invent as having been intended for all Protestants. For those who wish to strike us, we strike in return; and we have stabbed no one except the one who stabbed us first.

But dear Plessis, how did the secret report of the Count of Hohenzollern become so well-known to you? If it was so secret, then it would not have been allowed to fly out; but if it has become public knowledge, then there was nothing secret about it. You might say it should have remained secret. Yes, that is so, and it would have remained so, had you not invented it in secret and subsequently brought it to light. The Count wrote no such thing, nor did His Imperial Majesty read any such thing. It is entirely your own [invention], so that the reader may know what it is worth.

->P L E S S I V S<-

What the compiler, on page 22, supposedly recounts regarding Otto von Pack, he concludes—according to his own opinion and pretense—quite differently. For Sleidanus <note>Johannes Sleidanus, a contemporary historian of the Reformation</note> expressly testifies that although Duke George of Saxony and others had intended and kept in mind the "Execution" on account of such a conspiracy <note>original: "Coniuration"</note>, nevertheless, a rumor and outcry arose that there was some truth to it. Furthermore, the circumstances of the time, the place, and the persons have been described in such detail that anyone experienced in the public dealings of that time can easily understand that Pack did not suck such things out of his own brain <note>A common idiom meaning to fabricate or make something up</note>. However, what he (the compiler) further brings to the floor regarding Cardinal Truchsess, the city of Nuremberg, against Margrave Albert of Brandenburg, and the Gotha Execution—since such things do not belong to this point of controversy and dispute—they shall be rightly set aside in their place.

->F A B I V S<-

As far as I can perceive, Plessis, you continue with a shameless heart and publicly display your worthless vanity without hesitation. For regarding Pack, that godless, worthless man would have placed dear Germany into certain ruin with his highly damaging lies, had his deceit not been discovered in time, and those who are now in armor

He himself, with his own pen, forged the alliance between great lords and princes, and—based on a precedent obtained through <term>subreption and obreption</term><note>Latin: sub & obreptitiè. Legal terms for obtaining something by concealing the truth or making false statements.</note>—he went in all secrecy to the Landgrave of Hesse, which was then an undeniable incitement of war against the Catholics. But it did not stop there; rather, the Duke of Saxony was also immediately exhorted and drawn into the game. Now, when both of these highly esteemed princes took up arms, everyone was dismayed by it, nor could anyone know where such <term>war preparations</term><note>original: Kriegspræparation</note> were intended to lead. Therefore, it was deemed advisable that the causes of such an uprising should be published throughout Germany in public patents, according to the custom of the ancestors, and that war should be formally declared against the enemies. When this occurred, everyone sought to reveal their innocence and cast the blame upon the instigator of such unrest. What happened? The ringleader and founder of the aforementioned mischief was reported and presented by the Landgrave of Hesse: who, like butter in the sun, was utterly terrified and, being without a conscience, could not maintain his worm-eaten, dilapidated cause with either solid words or sufficient arguments. He would have been immediately brought to due punishment had the highly esteemed Landgrave not protected him; yet he eventually became hated even by him, and after he saved himself by fleeing toward Antwerp, he was executed there by the sword. And this Calvinist, <term>Plessius</term><note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marny, a prominent French Huguenot writer.</note>, dares to defend the aforementioned raving traitor of so many princes, and to approve—against so many reigning princes—such an alliance concocted by that dishonorable <term>Pack</term><note>Otto von Pack, the lawyer who forged the documents.</note> himself. Nor does he let it rest with this loose testimony, but for good measure—albeit falsely—he cites <term>Sleidanus</term><note>Johannes Sleidanus, the famous 16th-century historian of the Reformation.</note>, so that the good-hearted reader may well perceive from all this, right at the beginning of his book, that little shame remains in this noble blood. Let Sleidanus speak, and let everyone listen to him who still held any regard for the virtue and faith of this man von Plessen, which, if he ever possessed such, he has now entirely and utterly lost.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the political tensions within the Holy Roman Empire following the Reformation, specifically focusing on the fallout of the "Pack Affair" (1528) involving Landgrave Philipp of Hesse.</meta>

# The Pack Affair and the Threat of War

Saxony, William and Louis—both Dukes in Bavaria—the Elector of Mainz, the Archbishop of Salzburg, and the Bishops of Bamberg and Würzburg had allegedly allied themselves with one another to wage war against him (the Landgrave) and the Elector of Saxony on account of Religion. And so that he might give credit to his words, he <note>Referring to Otto von Pack, a counselor to Duke George of Saxony</note> showed a copy of the aforementioned confederation and alliance to the often-highly-esteemed Landgrave, and furthermore promised to present the original document to him. Consequently, as they prepared themselves for war and recruited troops, everyone wondered where such military preparations were aimed. For, as commonly tends to happen, there were various discussions and rumors circulating at that time. But when they were finally ready and sufficiently prepared for war, they sent the aforementioned copy of the alliance that had come to them everywhere and had it published. At the same time, they wrote to those who were said to have made such an alliance with one another and sought to discover their intentions through their envoys. However, all of them together, as well as each one individually, excused themselves from the matter and maintained that the entire thing was fabricated and could not be proven in the slightest.

But above all, Duke George of Saxony—the highly-esteemed Lord Landgrave’s father-in-law—diligently insisted that he (the Lord Landgrave) should reveal the author of such an instigation; otherwise, he would hold that the Landgrave himself had given cause for the incitement of an uprising in Germany. Indeed, the first attack was aimed at the neighboring Bishops, for which reason they also recruited mercenary troops in great haste and in such numbers as they could manage.

However, since the aforementioned Pack <note>Otto von Pack, the source of the alleged conspiracy documents</note> was unable to produce the original according to his promise, the highly-esteemed Lord Landgrave paused somewhat. Upon the intercession and mediation of both Lord Electors—Richard, the Archbishop of Trier, and the Elector Palatine—he allowed the troops to be disbanded on this condition: that to pay for the costs of the war, they should pay the Lord Landgrave 100,000 Gold Guilders <term>Goldgülden: a high-value gold coin used throughout the Holy Roman Empire</term>. Specifically, the Elector of Mainz was to pay 40,000, the Bishop of Würzburg the same amount, and the Bishop of Bamberg 20,000. But when the envoys of the Princes—who had been secretly slandered and disparaged by the aforementioned Pack—later met on a certain day, this Pack, whom the Lord Landgrave had presented, was convicted of his untruthfulness. He was dismissed by the highly-esteemed Lord Landgrave and finally, after wandering about in error for several years, was executed by the sword in Antwerp <note>original: "Antorff"</note>.

<summary>This page describes the "Pack Affair," where a forged alliance document nearly triggered a religious war in Germany, ending with the Landgrave of Hesse extracting financial settlements from Catholic bishops and the eventual execution of the forger, Otto von Pack.</summary>
<keywords>Otto von Pack, Landgrave Philipp of Hesse, Duke George of Saxony, religious war, forgery, Gold Guilders, Mainz, Würzburg, Bamberg, Antwerp</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the polemical defense against the accusations of "Plessen" (Philippe de Mornay, Lord of Plessis-Marly) regarding the "Pack Affair" and the origins of the Protestant Union.</meta>

Thus far **Sleidanus** <note>Johannes Sleidanus (1506–1556), the foremost historian of the Reformation</note>, by whom this man von Plessen is likewise convicted of untruth, just as was the oft-mentioned **Pack** <note>Otto von Pack, whose forged documents in 1528 nearly started a religious war by claiming Catholic princes were plotting to exterminate Protestants</note> before his time. For, my dear Pless, where does Sleidanus explicitly write that **Duke George of Saxony**, after he was required to defend and answer for himself in a proper manner, remained under suspicion—according to the rumors spread by the common rabble—that he approved of Pack’s dealings and that there was some truth to such a conspiracy? Where in Sleidanus are the circumstances of the previously planned war to be found—if I may use your own *formal words* and specific phrasing—proving that Pack did not lie, but rather discovered the truth? What person who reads Sleidanus is so foolish that he would place more faith in the lies of Pack than in the truthful words of so many Princes? Pack’s deceit has been publicly exposed, refuted, revealed, and condemned, and indeed at the very time it was committed. What faith, then, after so many years, should the kind-hearted reader place in such a wicked <term>Vocativo</term> <note>Latin: "Vocative case," used here as a derogatory play on words for a "shifty character" or "summoned witness"</note>? Sleidanus is a great witness for you only because he is on your side, and Pack was much more similar to your faction than a "clubfoot" is to the name it is praised by. Regarding **Cardinal Truchseß** and the citizens of **Nuremberg**, since you intend to do nothing against **Margrave Albrecht of Brandenburg**, I will likewise bring nothing further to the floor on that matter, for I know for a certainty that the faults cast upon us have often originated from those very people doing the casting.

### PLESSIS.

Furthermore, the compiler of the *Anhalt Chancery* <note>A reference to the "Anhaltische Kanzlei," a collection of secret diplomatic documents published to expose the Protestant Union's plans</note> writes on page 23 and following: that the members of the **Union** aimed solely and exclusively at the Catholics with their undertaking, because as soon as the Union was established in the year of Christ 1608, they granted a contribution for 30 months, and thereafter, until the year 1617, they contributed a large sum of money annually. Likewise, that the heads of the Union in 1618 took up the uprising in the **Kingdom of Bohemia** which served their intentions, and—before a single Catholic Estate, or even the **Roman Imperial Majesty** <note>The Holy Roman Emperor</note> of most glorious memory himself, had recruited a single man for his own defense—they applied with all diligence to their allies and to a great Prince well known to them, requesting that 15,000 soldiers be sent to them. And indeed, as concerns first the contributions made by the Union over time, one should not unreasonably marvel that the shameless compiler dared to reproach the Union for such a thing, since it is hidden neither from him nor from everyone else in the entire **Holy Roman Empire** that the **Roman Catholic League** also (by virtue of the established alliance) [contributed] various notable...

For in this case, he provides arguments against himself, and such an entire charge and accusation could be retorted against the Romanist League with much better legal grounds.

## FABIUS.

These are far too long-winded digressions, in which [Volrad] von Plessen <note>A leading statesman of the Electoral Palatinate and advisor to Frederick V.</note> himself has become mortally lost. For who has reproached you, or accused you, for bringing financial aid together? It is rather to be praised that one practices thrift at home for the benefit of the common good, and is publicly liberal and generous. For this very reason, the Catholic princes have undertaken such things for the pleasant memory of worthy posterity. And in this regard, we are not in the least angry with you; rather, we complain because you have so carelessly squandered your own resources toward an evil end—not for the use and benefit of the Holy Roman Empire—and have plunged such a magnificent kingdom <note>Likely referring to the Kingdom of Bohemia.</note>, which could have remained peaceful among so many enemies, into ruin. You had it in mind to make war on others, but not to protect your own. You had the desire to take from us what is ours, to use force, and for us to resist the same. This, this, I say, is to be counted among the vices; for this you are accused and held as public enemies. It is not the transaction itself, but the cause of such a transaction that is to be condemned. And you, according to the habit of Calvinist deceit, pass over in silence the single...

<meta>The previous page concluded with a defense of the Catholic League; this page continues the rebuttal against the Protestant Union's accusations of aggression.</meta>

...alone, which should rather have served as your sole excuse. The author of the *Anhaltine Chancery* <note>A famous collection of secret documents published in 1621 exposing the plans of the Protestant Union</note> relates that you enriched your treasury to put into action that which your leaders of the Union had already long ago undertaken against the Catholics. They had already resolved—in case you have perhaps forgotten the plot you spun—to attack Alsace with the help and assistance of foreign princes and to grant the same to the Count Palatine <note>Frederick V, Elector Palatine</note>. Indeed, an even greater work: to unite the Ocean <note>The Atlantic/North Sea</note> with the Adriatic Sea, so that nothing more would remain for the Catholics upon the sea. And, because of the Swiss obstruction, you intended to open the pass into Italy by force of arms; the only thing that hindered you was that you could not bring together a larger army, as you had presumed to make the beginning with only 15,000 men. 

In the meantime, Mansfeld <note>Ernst von Mansfeld, a prominent mercenary general</note> had already prepared himself for the business of helping the Count Palatine to the Kingdom of Bohemia with his army and 400 horsemen paid for by the Duke of Savoy. Furthermore, another 2,000 men were expected through the Ansbach territory who, under Mansfeld's command, joined the rebellion of the insurgent Bohemians who had fallen away from their natural Lord and King <note>Ferdinand II, Holy Roman Emperor</note>. Beyond this, more troops were being recruited in other places, so that Germany trembled, as it were, on account of the increasing hostile power, ignorant of where such military force was primarily to be directed. 

We, however, in the establishment of our League <note>The Catholic League</note>, selected none of the United <note>Members of the Protestant Union</note> whom we wished to attack; we were mindful of no one’s misfortune and threatened no one. Our sole intention was to remain steadfast and, if we were perhaps attacked, to defend ourselves. Therefore, your godless efforts, which you employed toward our downfall, are not unjustly held against you. We would have been ruined by now, if only you had been able to ruin us. Our drawn sword has forced yours back into its scabbard.

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of diplomatic alliances in Europe, specifically focusing on the strategic importance of the Duke of Savoy and his relationship with the Protestant Union.</meta>

His ancestors, he himself, and his eldest son are related by marriage to the most powerful princes in the entire world, such as those of France, Spain, and Portugal. Because of his high <term>prerogative (legal precedence or priority)</term>, he takes precedence over all Italian princes, including the Grand Duke of Florence himself. For Pope Pius V, in a <term>bull (a formal papal decree)</term> in which he granted Cosimo de' Medici the title of Grand Duke, explicitly reserved such prerogative for the Duke of Savoy. Furthermore, he holds the perpetual title of <term>Vicar of the Empire (a deputy acting for the Holy Roman Emperor)</term>. Finally, at the time when the <term>League in France (the Catholic League during the French Wars of Religion)</term> was dissolved, this Duke established such a firm friendship and—as the common man calls it—correspondence with King Henry IV and the King of Great Britain, that even to this day it can never be dissolved. It is therefore not so very surprising that the <term>Count Palatine (the Elector of the Palatinate)</term> and other <term>United Princes (members of the Protestant Union)</term>, following the example of these two kings, have likewise allied themselves with the Duke of Savoy. This friendship was highly useful to the aforementioned Duke at the time when the Spaniards broke the peace and made war upon him—especially during the time when the Governor of Milan, Don Pietro de Toledo, made a hostile incursion into Piedmont with 4,000 men in the name of the King of Spain. For when the said Don Pietro had besieged the city of Vercelli for some time and finally captured it under certain conditions, leaving the remainder of that entire province in extreme danger, this Duke, among others, called upon the United Princes for help. They sent several thousand men from Germany to Piedmont under the command of the Count of Mansfeld. However, now that peace has been reached, a total forgetting of all past dealings established, and the restitution of the aforementioned city of Vercelli confirmed, the Duke of Savoy (who considered it advisable that peace is most securely maintained under the shield, and had explicitly and specifically reserved this for himself in the articles of peace) did not wish to leave himself exposed. Instead, he provided the Count of Mansfeld with the necessary money in Germany so that he might recruit experienced officers and colonels into his pay and keep them in good readiness in case of need.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Plessius (representing the Protestant/Evangelical perspective) and Fabius (representing the Catholic perspective) regarding the origins of the Bohemian Revolt and the Thirty Years' War.</meta>

# PLESSIUS.

At that very same time, it happened that in Prague, out of a patience that had been frequently violated and overtaxed, a tumult arose. The Bohemian Estates <note>The assembly of nobles and representatives in the Kingdom of Bohemia.</note> feared—and not without reason—that through the secret, cunning practices of their adversaries and faithless countrymen, the Directors and the Evangelicals <note>Protestants.</note> might be deprived of their lives, their kingdom, their freedom, the Letter of Majesty <note>original: "Mayestätbrieff" - a 1609 charter guaranteeing religious freedom to Bohemians.</note>, and other favorable privileges. Consequently, they recognized their obligation to one another and prepared to defend themselves to preserve their lives, freedom, and religion. They also accepted, with both hands and great gratitude, the offer of the Count of Mansfeld <note>Ernst von Mansfeld, a prominent mercenary commander.</note> (who was well able to justify this on behalf of the Duke of Savoy, whom he served for pay). As soon as the said Count joined them, they found themselves 4,000 men stronger than before. Should the Count Palatine <note>Frederick V, the Elector Palatine.</note> and other Evangelical Estates have forbidden or prevented the helpful assistance shown by the Roman Catholic Duke of Savoy for the sake of the general political welfare? Indeed, at that time, the Bohemian affair was regarded both inside and outside the Empire as a common cause for all Evangelicals. It does not follow, however, that anything evil was undertaken against His Imperial Majesty—though this is forced upon the reader as if it were an article of faith in the *Bavarian Cento* <note>original: "Bayerischen Centone" - likely referring to a polemical Catholic publication or "patchwork" of arguments from Bavaria.</note>.

### FABIUS.

If we Catholics were as dumb and senseless as you make us out to be, we might perhaps place some belief in your words; but as it is, you trouble yourself in vain. In former times we were children, but now you are the children—and with your thick, hairy beards at that! The Fury <note>A reference to the Furies of mythology, implying a spirit of madness or rage.</note> undoubtedly seized the rebels, but it did not arise from "frequently violated patience"; rather, it was caused by Calvinist impatience to instigate a new unrest. Why should I let a little worm crawl over my nose <note>A German idiom meaning to let someone provoke or insult you without reaction.</note> and get indignant about it, if I were forbidden from building churches in another territory when I already had them elsewhere and yet did not even attend them? The Calvinists did not lack for churches; rather, they lacked the fear of God, even though they nonetheless used it as a pretext. This mischief, however, originated within the Imperial Court from the countrymen themselves. Oh, why then did you drive them out of Bohemia? Those who are harmful and contrary to me, I would rather have near me, so that I—

pay attention to them, and might guard myself against them. Mansfeld <note>Ernst von Mansfeld (1580–1626), a prominent mercenary commander for the Protestant cause.</note>—so that you might freely and safely bypass the cruelly-conceived wrath of Emperor Matthias <note>Holy Roman Emperor Matthias (reigned 1612–1619), whose policies contributed to the outbreak of the Thirty Years' War.</note>—was summoned into Bohemia for the purpose of ruining that kingdom rather than losing it. But, my dear Pless <note>Likely referring to Philippe de Mornay, Lord of Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader and diplomat.</note>, was Mansfeld then at liberty to lead the soldiery he had under his command wherever they pleased? Furthermore, others had no lawful reason to follow the Count Palatine's so impudent footsteps into Bohemia, because the "injuries" of the Bohemians—though none were actually inflicted upon them—did not concern the Princes of the Empire. For what did the Count Palatine and other Princes of the Union <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states formed in 1608.</note> have to do with Bohemia? Who rightly weighed the importance and arguments of the matter? Did the Count Palatine or others do this? How did the scepter <note>A symbol of royal authority.</note> come to him in the kingdoms of another potentate? To whom did he have the right to give orders therein? It was, you say, a "common cause." What kind of common cause is that? The Calvinists raged in Bohemia; was such fury therefore a common cause? Otherwise, you would have sat idly at home and would not have had to receive such stiff blows. What kind of beautiful community is this of yours, as I might say? For just as the Bohemians, according to their great presumption and with disregard for their King's commands, behaved in Bohemia, did you therefore also wish to act thus in Germany? What? You Calvinists wanted to have everything in the Empire according to your ambitious discretion, even though nothing at all was owed to you in the same. You wanted to appropriate another's freedom, and you preferred that others elsewhere should become rebellious, so that it would not be inconvenient for you to instigate misfortune in Germany as well. Did it perhaps pain you that you had to desist from your <term>proceedings<note>original: "Procedere" - legal or political course of action</note></term> and get the short end of the stick? What business was the Bohemian Church of yours? Did you have a desire, through such a presumptuous example, to build churches on other people's ground and soil? Did you hold such good fellowship with the Bohemians when the Royal Councilors were thrown out of the windows from above? <note>A reference to the Defenestration of Prague (May 23, 1618), where Protestant lords threw Catholic regents from a window of Hradčany Castle.</note> If so, you surely knew about it and were companions in such unheard-of cruelty. Was such a dangerous rebellion common to you? Did you then take up arms against your own Lord? Yes, certainly you prepared yourselves for this, and as soon as others beat the drum, you ran toward the general war because your counsels had previously been held in common. 

But why should I trouble myself long on their account? They say: at that time, nothing hostile was undertaken against the Emperor. For what purpose, then, were the weapons intended? Does not he set himself hostilely against the Imperial Majesty who dares to plunge the Imperial Councilors into ruin and to hold them as his enemies? Whom was your soldiery intended to attack? For those whom you considered your enemies had either departed from Bohemia of their own accord or had been driven from there in a hostile manner; what then did you have to fear from such impoverished persons? This <term>tragedy<note>The author uses "Tragœdi" metaphorically to describe the disastrous political upheaval.</note></term> was aimed at your King and the Imperial Majesty himself—not so that he should rule differently among you, but rather that he should no longer rule at all. Another king was forged <note>Refers to Frederick V of the Palatinate, the "Winter King," chosen by the rebels to replace the Habsburgs.</note>, who stayed in hiding for a long time until he showed himself a short time later in the bloody tragedy; although, after his mistreatment, he had to lay aside the royal person again and thereafter greet foreign lands in flight, thereby leaving behind a sad example for <term>posterity<note>original: "Posteritet" - future generations</note></term>.

->FABIUS<-
<note>Fabius serves here as a persona representing the Catholic/Imperial viewpoint in this dialogue.</note>

This "truth" of yours, of which you boast, is an open vanity. For while you have indeed sought His Imperial Majesty with words, in deed you have reached for the Royal Throne and Crown. The name of the Officers and your cited oppression by them has been used only as a pretext for appearance. Will my words prove my innocence if I commit the vice itself? Can one be called an obedient subject when I divest myself of the obedience I owe? And of what use was your protestation? You say: "We recognize the Emperor as our King," yet we interfere in Royal affairs. His Imperial Majesty sends us Royal Officers, but we send them back to him—and indeed, out of "special reverence," out of the window <note>A sarcastic reference to the Defenestration of Prague in 1618, where Protestant lords threw Imperial regents from the Hradčany Castle windows.</note>. How many supplications <term>Supplicationes: formal written petitions or humble pleas</term> did you deliver into the hands of His Imperial Majesty? What is the cause of such great lamentation? And what kind of yoke has so heavily burdened your necks? to whom you say that His Imperial Majesty <note>original: "Kay. May." - Kaiserliche Majestät, referring to Ferdinand II</note> could have prevented all of this, if he had interposed himself as a neutral judge between the two quarreling parties. But what do you say, Plessy? You call upon the judge for help only after the court has met, after the verdict has been passed, after the condemnation and punishment of the wrongdoers—after the innocent Lords of the Council had already been hurled down from the Tarpeian Rock? <note>A reference to the Defenestration of Prague, comparing the castle windows to the cliff in Rome used for executing traitors.</note> What sort of judgment did His Imperial Majesty then have left to give? Should those who had only just painfully stood up be struck to the ground anew? Could those who had just escaped death in a contrary storm take flight again? Should one rather hand the scepter to you and appoint an avenger? 

Yet, out of so many, no one "muddied the water," <note>An idiom suggesting no one acted with malice or provoked the chaos unnecessarily.</note> and no one committed a vice for the preservation of the Fatherland; rather, out of love for the common good, you took everything in hand so that the same should not be destroyed along with the obedient; therefore, you also cast out the obedient. Who would ever punish such a thing further? It is thus: if the Emperor had gone to Prague, you would have passed such a verdict for the Emperor—as the judge—to judge and to punish the guilty: "King, surrender your office and substitute ours; you keep the scepter, but we wish to use it; you shall be called King, but we wish to be it." And indeed, among all Royal prerogatives, this alone would have been the last: **"Do everything that we shall command you."**

->P L E S S I V S.<-

But when the Bohemian Estates <term>Bohemian Estates: the representative assembly of the nobles, knights, and royal cities</term> heard that Lord Kain and others had been dispatched into the Kingdom of Bohemia merely to spy upon it and to instigate further mistrust there; and also that the Count of Bucquoy <note>Charles Bonaventure de Longueval, Count of Bucquoy, a commander of the Imperial forces.</note>—who was to lead into Bohemia troops recruited from everywhere and recalled from the Hungarian borders—had been summoned in all haste from the Netherlands; and because of this, many at the Imperial Court laughed in their sleeves that the Bohemians themselves had given the Austrians cause to subdue the Kingdom under the yoke with an armed hand, and to rob the Estates of all their privileges and the recently obtained Letter of Majesty <term>Letter of Majesty: a 1609 document guaranteeing religious freedom to Bohemian Protestants</term> by right of war (as they boasted); and furthermore, when it was noted that the aforementioned Count of Bucquoy had been summoned from the Netherlands to undertake a military campaign against the Bohemians (just as 50 years ago the Duke of Alba <note>Fernando Álvarez de Toledo, known for his harsh suppression of the Dutch Revolt.</note> had marched from Spain against the Netherlanders with similar instructions), the Bohemians were not a little outraged and transformed; nevertheless, they always protested that they [remained] always
