they wished to remain faithful subjects of Emperor Matthias: indeed, they remained so steadfast under Imperial protection that they chose to await the first blow upon Bohemian soil and ground (even though they could have anticipated all of this and led their military forces out of the Kingdom).

## FABIUS

What then have the Bohemians not done to submit themselves to their King? Who would expect more from them? Truly, no sensible man, for this is indeed far too much: the Bohemians accuse their King’s envoy, claiming he arrived in Bohemia only as a spy <note>original: "Kuntschaffter"</note>. But what was there to spy out? As if he alone did not know what was already known to the entire world? Did you go to the Town Hall <note>The Old Town Hall in Prague, site of the 1618 Defenestration</note> in secret, in the bright light of day, and there—with no one but God knowing—hurl the Royal Officers out of the windows? Did you banish others into misery, and did no one see how their property was confiscated <note>original: "conuastret," likely referring to the seizure or devastation of assets</note> or otherwise plundered? When you elected new councilors and gave them the ostensible title of "Directors" <note>The Thirty Directors were the provisional government established by the Bohemian estates</note>, did the city of Prague wander elsewhere so that it should not know what had transpired? Did no one hear when the drums were beaten with great terror and godless weapons were taken up against the natural King? We who are far removed, as well as foreigners, have well perceived your presumption. 

But perhaps such an Imperial Legate arrived to investigate the ringleaders or the heads of the rebels? This too was not unknown: for it was not in secret, but publicly, with foreheads and faces held high, that the Count of Thurn, Ruppa, Fels, Schlick, and other rebels gave a beginning to such a dishonorable business. The vice is therefore open to the light of day, of which they were the causes, and yet the aforementioned Imperial envoy is scolded by you as a spy. If he exhorted some of yours, whether with gentle or sharp words, to the obedience of your rightful Lord and King, then he acted rightly and well, and did not wish to stir up discord among you: for discord is to be praised when the good are separated from the evil, and no good man is harmed by the disobedience of another. For he who is vexed by the improvement of others is despondent, and nothing good can ever come of him.

## PLESSIUS

However, after Emperor Matthias passed away, the mistrust and the bitterness of spirits increased therefrom from day to day, more and more, and finally, at the Imperial Election Day at Frankfurt, the Bohe—

The Bohemian Estates <note>The representative assembly of the Kingdom of Bohemia</note>, who were staying nearby, were denied all audience and turned away. Consequently, they had to travel back home with their business unfinished. Yet, to avoid extremes, a middle path—which is much safer—should not have been unfairly rejected. (For if either Daniel or Wolfgang <note>Daniel Brendel von Homburg and Wolfgang von Dalberg, former Archbishops and Prince-Electors of Mainz</note>, both former Prince-Electors of Mainz, had held such high influence in Frankfurt, this would undoubtedly have been handled differently). Thus, this insult and injury—received, as it were, in the sight of the entire Holy Roman Empire and Europe (for that is how they perceived it)—pierced the hearts not only of the Directors and the most prominent lords of the people, but also of all the inhabitants of the entire Kingdom, like a sharp sting. This resulted in a universal turning away of minds and the most dangerous of changes.

->FABIUS<- <note>Likely a pseudonym for a commentator or interlocutor in this political dialogue</note>

Here, His Imperial Majesty is accused, and the Prince-Electors are dragged through the hedge <note>A metaphor for harsh criticism or "raking over the coals"</note>, though with very little caution. For those who were sent by the Bohemian Estates to Frankfurt to claim their legal rights before the Emperor—in the absence of a King—on behalf of the Kingdom of Bohemia, should have been held in higher reputation. However, they failed so completely that they were not only denied admission to the session of the Electoral College, but were not heard in the slightest, even though they repeatedly requested an audience while staying near the aforementioned Electoral College. And if either Daniel or Wolfgang, both Archbishops of Mainz, had still been alive, they would have acted more modestly and, to avoid the greatest shame, would have found some middle ground through which—even if nothing were granted to the Bohemian claim—their honor and reputation would have been respected. But as it stands, these same envoys were admitted nowhere, but were despised and rejected in the sight of the entire Holy Roman Empire and to the astonishment of all Europe. Thus, all of Bohemia, having been so highly insulted, had to proceed to the ultimate means of desperation. They take such rejection to heart with great pain, and nothing remains for the despised but pure revenge. For if they were not permitted to elect an Emperor, they certainly have the power to crown another King; let us rescue our own honor from the shame of others. Are you then, Plessis <note>Likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader</note>, also clever and witty enough? If a disgrace occurred at Frankfurt, the imprudent Calvinists caused it; and why did you dispatch your envoys to Frankfurt for the Electoral Assembly? Are you then

...also, by an edict from His Electoral Grace of Mainz, were you summoned there? What business did you have with the Electoral College, since you brought no electoral hat <note>The "electoral hat" or *Kurhut* was the symbol of rank for a Prince-Elector of the Holy Roman Empire.</note> with you? Your King has his seat in that highly esteemed place, but not you, who are far too lowly and insignificant for such a location. But so that your folly would become all the better known to the whole world, you set out for the purpose of stripping your King of his existing right to vote, even before you had demonstrated that he had forfeited said kingdom. What, I ask again, did you have to do in Frankfurt? At that time, he was still your King, and therefore also an Elector, and yet you nevertheless claimed to have kept your justice and honor undiminished. Who, then, diminished it for you? Perhaps the Electors? They did not do it, but merely did not permit it, because such honor was due to another. Now, however, it was considered a disgrace that they did not receive what they desired; indeed, it was rather a shame that such a thing was even desired. For other people's property is not honestly desired, nor is it honestly granted. Now, if pride is ashamed, grieved, and tormented because of this, it will not disadvantage others, but rather the one who makes such an unjust demand. Shameless guests receive their punishment, and indeed one that is greater the more significant their rejection was.

But one should have respected such high nobility a little better: for some of the Bohemian ambassadors were of such stately nobility that they even counted some <term>Aemilians</term> <note>A reference to the Aemilia gens, one of the most ancient and noble patrician families of ancient Rome, used here to denote ancient lineage.</note> among their lineage. This understanding is much worse than if such a thing had been done by the common rabble. Therefore, they should have abandoned their unjust proceedings so that no stain or ill-repute would be cast upon their noble stock. But they were already defiled when they usurped the Electoral dignity; they had a shameful entrance, and so their exit could not have been much better. I maintain that the Emperor is also of high nobility and very impatient regarding <term>injuries</term> <note>original: "iniuri"; legal or personal wrongs/insults.</note> suffered; why then should he have suffered the Bohemian <term>Calvinists</term> <note>A Protestant denomination often viewed with particular hostility by Catholic imperialists during this period.</note> to sit unblamed and elect an Emperor—they who tolerate no King, but are accustomed only to manipulating and mocking them?

### FABIUS.

Indeed, from all that has been recounted above, nothing else appears but your sheer deception, with which you venture to pull the wool over the reader's eyes: for it is an overt fabrication that the Bohemians remained steadfast in obedience to Emperor Matthias. They called him a King until his death; but how am I to understand the name of such Royal honor and the mere naming by such mockers? For him whom you called a King, you treated as an enemy, and even undertook to utterly destroy him—you, who afterwards fell into such snares yourselves and lost the Royal title besides. For what purpose served such a large number of recruited soldiery? Did you wish to receive the King returning to you with them, or rather to forbid him his return? You had already taken it into your minds to abolish the Austrian rule, at the instigation of the Heidelberg Councilors <note>Refers to the advisors of the Elector Palatine in Heidelberg, the center of the Protestant Union</note>, who commanded Mansfeld <note>Ernst von Mansfeld, a prominent mercenary general during the Thirty Years' War</note> to come to the aid of the Bohemians until he—with a larger army which he desired and expected from elsewhere—was equal in every way to the Austrian power. For you write knowingly and against your conscience that a greater military preparation <note>original: "Kriegspræparation"</note> was instituted by you for the use and benefit of the people. You took up arms not for defense <note>original: "defension"</note>, but for the offense <note>original: "Offension"</note> of the innocent and those who did not expect such a thing. For whom did you have to fear, since no prince of the League <note>original: "ligistischer Fürst," referring to the Catholic League</note> had recruited troops against you? Only His Imperial Majesty <note>original: "Kay. May." / Kaisertliche Majestät</note> had recruited against his rebels, which then struck fear and terror into you. You had nothing to fear; rather, your presumption and the good opportunity of the Bohemian tumult to unjustly seize other people's goods and thereby increase your own gave you such "courage." Thus, after achieving victory in Bohemia <note>original: "Böhaymb"</note>, His Imperial Majesty would have turned against you, yet you had—as you write—not the slightest thing to fear on that account. For when His Majesty had now become the Victor and had suppressed the greatest power of his adversaries, peace was concluded at Mainz through the mediation of Marquis Spinola <note>Ambrogio Spinola, a Genoese general in Spanish service</note> at the request of the more peaceful members of the Union <note>The Protestant Union</note>. But your own conjecture, born of anxiety, deceived you; for only that which never existed can fail a man. For if you wish to wage war against me, then you certainly have nothing to fear from my war. Thereafter, you can also [justify] yourselves with un...

<meta>This page continues the defensive argument regarding the origins of the conflict, specifically addressing the alliances and military movements in Alsace and Bohemia.</meta>

[You cannot] gloss over or, much less, excuse [your actions] by our example: For you were the first to enter into the alliance, and we only followed you because we believed such a thing was directed against us. You attacked the **Alsace** <note>original: "Elsaß"; a border region between German and French territories, often a flashpoint for conflict</note> in a hostile manner; we, however, in order to protect what was ours, initially prepared for a counter-defense when your heated temperament advised you toward peace, only so that you might continue the war at a better opportunity. The stung fisherman lets it be a warning to him <note>A proverbial expression meaning once bitten, twice shy</note>; why then should we not also take heed of our defenses? We have therefore watched all the more diligently and also recruited foreign aid—not, indeed, for the offense of a single person, but for the defense of our innocence. You, however, were again the first to reopen your armories for the **Bohemian War** and threatened us with dangerous warfare. Since you have now forced us into a counter-defense, well then, you have received it and felt it quite clearly.

You need not reproach me once again regarding the **Duke of Savoy** <note>original: "Hertzog von Saphoyen"; Charles Emmanuel I, who had complex dealings with both the Protestant Union and the Empire</note>; if it was fitting for you to treat with the **Roman Crown of the Empire** <note>original: "Römischen Cron deß Reichs"; the Holy Roman Imperial title</note>, then such a Duke, had he been elected, could well have accepted such a crown. Yet such a dispute does not concern us, for the whole matter lies in this: that with his military power, through your deceitful hypocrisy, you did not wish to help him, but rather yourselves, to the Roman Crown; and so that you might maintain it, you had already resolved to exterminate us all together.

The discord of the Duke of Savoy with the **House of Austria**, and the causes thereof, I do not desire to settle here; and however great it may be, it will provide little support for your vice-ridden deeds. For he has nothing to complain of regarding such a powerful Duke; rather, the Duke has cause to complain about you as faithless and flattering deceivers. You praise him, to be sure, in this place, yet you know well what you have thought and written of him elsewhere. For recently, after the capture of the city of **Heidelberg**, a book found there was released through public print, in which the aforementioned Duke is unfairly and hideously dragged through the hackle <note>A metaphor for harsh criticism or "combing through" someone's reputation, derived from the tool used to comb flax</note>. 

You **Calvinists** are accustomed to praising princes when you are hungry; but when you have become full, they must endure your scolding. To increase further hatred, you would gladly justify yourselves by the **Imperial Marriage**, as if His Imperial Majesty did not have the power to choose a bride to his liking without the advice and assistance of the Calvinists? In betrothing himself to this lady, he does no one any wrong: For where there is only one to be married, other princely houses cannot be considered or respected at the same time, but they should not be despised for that reason. In innocen—

## PLESSIUS

And we have recounted these particulars for this reason: so that the reader may grasp with his own hand the basis upon which the Compiler <note>The author of the document being critiqued</note> accuses only the "Disloyal Ones"—as well as the Directors and their Councilors—of inciting unrest and tumult throughout the whole world, while asserting that everyone else sat still and that the aggrieved parties had no cause for complaint. However, that an Italian Prince should strive for other kingdoms is nothing new. For it is certain that both the recently deceased Dukes of Ferrara and Parma strove for the Polish Crown, and both kingdoms, Bohemia and Hungary, were regarded in Italy as elective kingdoms. Regarding the seizure of several places in the Alsace, which is said to have happened during the passage of troops: even if that were the case, it would not be of such great importance that one should make it out to be so cruel. For such a Duke, as an experienced man of war, would not have wanted to depart from his own territory and domain unless he had known beforehand where he should turn back, so that in case of need, he could return safely to his own property through the captured places. In just such a manner, King Henry II of France, in the alliance made with Elector Maurice of Saxony, expressly negotiated the occupation of several cities—such as Cambrai, Metz, and Toul—so that he might withdraw safely from Germany.

## FABIUS

In the Anhalt Chancery, we have only recognized those as rebels who truly were so. And if you were not an evil counselor yourself, it would in no way vex you that evil counselors are punished. You labor here in vain, because you cannot diminish your own—and your party's—presumptuous proceedings with any amount of eloquence. It is always the case in a Council...

<meta>The previous page concluded with a warning about the loss of virtue; this page continues the critique of the advisors' failed political strategy and lack of foresight.</meta>

prudence still remains, even if fear of God and piety—the foremost virtues—are far removed. But both of these have abandoned you; for however wisely you considered these matters, you have acted just as foolishly. For what kind of prudence is it to advise making another potentate's kingdom a target for plunder, when nature and daily experience testify that ill-gotten goods also dissolve poorly? Go back and examine the histories of all times, and you will find few happy outcomes for those who wished to make themselves kings with another's crown. Many have filled their fatherland with plunder, only so that the fatherland itself would become a target for plunder; I need not introduce examples of this, for all books are full of them, and if they were not, your own miserable example would sufficiently fill all the chronicles of the years. What you have received for all these things is unknown to me, but this I know: that you have lost everything. This is the notorious <term>Calvinist prudence</term><note>A polemical jab at the Reformed political strategy of the Elector Palatine's court</note>, or rather, intolerable ambition, in which both master and servant, having strived for too much great honor, must now hear laughter instead of rejoicing, to the utter contempt of the whole world. But pride and loathsome ambition ultimately win such a wicked outcome.

It is often customary for imprudence to be excused, but not malice. Far be it from the court, but especially from the <term>Privy Council</term><note>The inner circle of high-ranking advisors to a prince</note>, to hold anything higher than conscience. For there is no more harmful pestilence to a prince than those who, setting aside all honor, strive for greater glory and wealth either for themselves or for their lord; which is exactly what you, <insert>Plessis</insert><note>Likely referring to Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot advisor</note>, and your companions-in-arms have done—God grant how you intend to justify such a thing. For by what right did you advise your prince to reach for another's royal scepter? You yourselves hold that it is wrong to take even the smallest thing from another. If that is so, is it then right to seize so many provinces occupied by the rightful lord for your own master? And I will hear no more of you saying that you received through the generosity of others what was presented to you: you accepted foreign property because you were well aware that the Bohemians <note>original: "aus anderer Leuth Haut Riemen geschnitten" - an idiom meaning to be generous at someone else's expense</note> cut straps from other people's leather. That King Ferdinand was elected, you knew well; that he was crowned, you knew well; that he had committed nothing whereby he would have forfeited the kingdom, you knew well. Yes, you also knew and recognized well the instigators at whose prompting the Bohemians [deprived] King Ferdinand of the kingdom...

-> F A B I V S <-

In the *Anhaltine Chancery* <note>original: "Anhaltinischen Canzeley," a famous polemical publication of the period exposing the secret plans of the Protestant Union</note>, we have only named those as rebels who actually were so. And if you were not an evil counselor, it would by no means vex you that evil counselors were punished. arises. For you have conspired to assist the **Bohemian Estates** <note>The assembly of nobles and representatives in the Kingdom of Bohemia</note> in order that the kingdom might become yours. The Italian princes, whom you praise so highly in this regard, may well have hoped for the kingdoms of other nations with royal courage and demanded the same, but not one of them has "led the bride home" <note>An idiom meaning to successfully complete the task or win the prize</note>. You have attacked kings in a hostile manner, brought men of noble standing to ruin, and, under the terrible pretense of peace, pursued the lands with war. Whether the Italians or other nations notice that **Bohemia** is an elective kingdom <note>original: "Wahlkönigreich"; a kingdom where the monarch is chosen by a vote rather than simple heredity</note>, as you claim, such a custom has fallen into decline after the election. The plan to seize **Alsace** <note>A strategic border region between the German lands and France</note> was only intended, if I understand you correctly, so that the **Duke of Savoy** <note>Charles Emmanuel I, who had complex alliances during the Thirty Years' War</note> could safely retreat in case of necessity; for it does not befit any sensible military commander to leave the land behind empty and without a garrison among enemies. For if Alsace or the well-situated castles therein were to be taken, one would much rather have taken the greater part of **Switzerland**, and especially that which extends to the beautiful **Lake Geneva**. Why did a more distant danger concern Alsace, and did a nearer one not affect it? Likewise, the **Count Palatine** <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate, the "Winter King"</note> was offered a part of Austria, just as he was offered Alsace. Should a garrison be installed there for the sole reason that the Duke of Savoy might more safely retreat to Italy after the completion of the war? Why then was **Constantinople** not also provided with a garrison, which might serve as a passage for the army traveling back to **Piedmont**? <note>The author is using sarcasm here to mock the logistical absurdity of the opponent's excuses</note> Alsace was therefore not attacked so that the Duke of Savoy should become richer thereby, but so that the Count Palatine might receive it as "drink money" <note>original: "Tranckgelt"; a tip or small bribe, here used derisively to mean a side-payment</note> in place of his war expenses, just as other lands were intended for his other associates. # PLESSIUS.

But regarding the fact that the Compiler (pages 27, 28, 29) still mixes in this slander—as if the heads and <term>Directors</term><note>The administrative leaders of the Protestant Union</note> with their allies had "played under the little hat" <note>A German idiom meaning to act in secret or collude behind closed doors</note> as the common German proverb says, and yet had withheld such matters from many interested parties—the answer is this: that the members of the Union granted the Electorate of the Palatinate, as Director, complete freedom to hear whatever concerned the general cause of the Union, to manage and govern it, and thereafter, at a convenient time, to report back to the United Estates either in the Convention of the Union, or through their envoys, or by letter. 

Likewise, it is beyond all doubt that the Elector of Mainz and the Duke in Bavaria, the Directors of the <term>Catholic League</term><note>The alliance of Catholic German states formed in 1609</note>, assumed equal power and directed occurring business according to their discretion in similar cases; nor were they bound to immediately communicate everything to all members of the League without distinction. It was exactly the same with the Direction of the <term>Count Palatine</term><note>Frederick V, Elector Palatine</note>, which he provided so faithfully and sincerely that not the slightest complaint is permitted from the United members, their councils, or servants. It would therefore have been much better for the Compiler to have stayed home with his unheard-of accusations and left the Palatinate unmolested by such foolish talk.

### FABIUS.

The one who published the *Anhaltine Chancery* <note>A reference to the "Anhaltische Kanzlei," a famous collection of captured secret documents that exposed the Palatinate's aggressive diplomatic maneuvers</note> has written the truth: that you United members have hidden many things in a most crafty manner. Otherwise, they would certainly not have approved of your harmful designs against the Holy Roman Empire. Ask the Imperial Cities today; ask the Duke of Württemberg and still others, whether they entered into the alliance for the purpose of making other lords' lands a prey. Rather, it was done by them so that, in case of need, they might protect themselves against opposing force; for that is how they speak, that is how they write. Who would now grant belief to you, rather than to them? 

It is, however, quite ridiculous that you so recklessly blurt out that many things were supposedly entrusted to your Count Palatine, which he alone was to perform, revealing them to whom he wished, and then finally reporting the same to everyone. But dear friend, who among the Lutherans is to be found who would have permitted and allowed such a dangerous proceeding? That which does not exceed the goal of the alliance is indeed permitted to the Palatine Directors; but what others have not—

# PLESSIUS

But as for the Compiler <note>The anonymous author of the "Anhaltine Chancery" publication</note> (*pages 27, 28, 29*) mixing in this slander—as if the heads and directors, along with their allies, had been "playing under the little hat" <note>original: "vnder dem Hütlein... gespielet"; a German proverb meaning to act in secret or collude behind someone's back</note> as the common German proverb says, and had withheld such matters from many interested parties—the answer is this: the members of the Union granted the Electorate of the Palatinate, as **Director**, complete freedom to hear matters concerning the general cause of the Union, to manage and govern them, and thereafter, at an appropriate time, to **report** <note>original: "referiren"</note> back to the United Estates, either in the Convention of the Union, through their ambassadors, or by letter. Likewise, it is beyond all doubt that the Elector of Mainz and the Duke of Bavaria, the **Directors of the Catholic League**, assumed similar powers and, in such cases, **directed** <note>original: "dirigiret"</note> occurring business according to their **discretion**; nor were they bound to **communicate** everything immediately to all members of the League without distinction. The **Direction** of the Count Palatine was of the same nature, which he provided so faithfully and sincerely that not the slightest complaint is permitted from the United members, their councilors, or servants. It would therefore have been much better for the **Compiler** to have stayed home with his unheard-of accusations and left the Palatinate **unmolested** by such foolish talk.

The one who published the Anhaltine Chancery wrote the truth: that you members of the Union concealed many things in a most crafty manner; otherwise, they would certainly not have approved of your harmful plots against the Holy Roman Empire. Ask the Imperial Cities today, ask the Duke of Württemberg and still others, whether they entered into the alliance for the purpose of making other lords' lands a prey. Rather, they did so for the end that, in case of necessity, they might protect themselves against opposing force; for thus they speak, thus they write. Who would now believe you, rather than them? My dear fellow, who among the Lutherans could be found who would have permitted and allowed such a dangerous proceeding? Whatever does not exceed the goal of the alliance may well be permitted to the Palatine Directors; but what others have not...

...wished to do, and thereby the general welfare of the Holy Roman Empire <note>original: "Heiligen Röm. Reichs"</note> is placed in danger, that is not permitted to him in the slightest. The Elector of Mainz and the Duke of Bavaria have certainly accomplished much, and therefore did not always consult others; however, they did not exceed the limits of the power entrusted to them, and at the proper time applied possible diligence so that the other allies might also know of such causes. That the members of the Union <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states formed in 1608</note> have raised no complaint regarding your Calvinist silence is no wonder. For why should I complain if all cunning and deceitful practices were kept from me? Which they would still not know of, had we not discovered them for them. No one specifically complains of your adultery and highly harmful counsels because perhaps many are not aware of them; but they are now brought to light, which is why almost the entire world makes much lamentation. Therefore, your unlawful procedure is justly held against you, and you—so that you might further destroy yourselves—put on a new mask <note>original: "Larff"</note>; but much too slowly, for we have already seen you for who you are.

### The Second Chapter.

# That the purpose of the Protesting Union was directed, and put into action, against the Imperial Majesty, the Head of the Holy Roman Empire.

### Information and Answer.

### PLESSIS. <note>Refers to Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader and diplomat</note>

The alliance was not established against the Roman Imperial Majesty, because the same was expressly exempted in the Union; as proof of this, we refer to the instruments of such alliance, which they have among other records <note>original: "Actis"</note> in Munich. However, the compiler <note>The author of the opposing Catholic pamphlet</note> believes he has given his calumny and slander a bit of color by bringing up the negotiations with the foreign prince, in which at the same time the Bohemian

# Page 41

<margin>See page 32</margin>
It is alleged—as noted on page 32 and thereafter—that the Electoral Palatinate <note>The territory of the Count Palatine, a leading Protestant prince</note> hurriedly dispatched 2,000 of Mansfield’s soldiers against Emperor Matthias in Bohemia (page 33 and following). We must know, however, that everything brought onto the stage regarding the Bohemian Crown was only to be understood in the event of the Emperor’s death; he was very frail at the time and indeed passed away soon after in the month of March, as we shall report further below.

It is, therefore, not a valid conclusion to claim that highly damaging plots were undertaken against Emperor Matthias because of the Bohemian Crown. As for why Mansfield <note>Ernst von Mansfeld, a prominent mercenary general</note> moved into Bohemia with his army, that has been sufficiently explained in the first chapter.

->FABIUS<-

That your alliance was not undertaken against the Emperor is confirmed by your highly praised writings mentioned above and your own testimony. We believe the writings—but not when they are written by the Calvinists, who always intend to do more than they put in writing. If I gird on a weapon in order to deceive, I can certainly say with truth that I do so for my protection; meanwhile, however, I have resolved in my mind to destroy you, the unwary one, at the first opportunity out of anger. But you might say: what a strange, fickle person is this? Did you not say that you had girded the sword to your side for your <term>Defense</term> <note>original: "Defension" — the legal and military right to protect one's territory</note>? I said it indeed, but I did not confess my full intent. In my affirmation, I diligently omitted it; but in a full confession, because one must specify everything that might be misleading, I hold back. This is the habit of the Calvinists: they think more than they say, and say less than they intend to do. 

If, Plessis <note>Refers to Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader often used as a representative figure for Reformed/Calvinist diplomacy</note>, your Union <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of German Protestant states</note> was only intended to ensure your freedom and religion, why then did you recruit troops so secretly when no enemy was at hand at that time? Did you perhaps fear us, who were unarmed? Why did you provide aid to the rebelling Bohemians? "Truly not," you say, "against the Emperor?" Sending troops against me is acting against me. Whoever joins my enemies deserts me; and you, Plessis, do not seek to excuse yourself for this vice either above or in this place. Words are one thing, but deeds are quite another—unless you believe that I am your good friend when I strike you with fists or otherwise beat you soundly. But you say, "Yes, the Emperor was already on his way out," and therefore...

<summary>This page features a debate between a defender of the Protestant Union and a character named Fabius, who accuses the Calvinists of using "defense" as a legal smokescreen for offensive military plots in Bohemia. Fabius argues that providing troops to rebels is an act of war, regardless of the diplomatic excuses provided in writing.</summary>

<keywords>Electoral Palatinate, Count Mansfeld, Emperor Matthias, Bohemia, Calvinists, Philippe du Plessis-Mornay, Protestant Union, military defense, Thirty Years' War origins</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the catchword "gone," leading into the discussion of the Emperor's death and the subsequent political maneuvers.</meta>

...we must apply all our effort and labor toward the Emperor's passing. Why not also toward Death itself? The end of the Imperial life was unknown to you, for you do not rule over Death; and yet, nonetheless, so that your worthless vanity might be better known, Mansfeld <note>Ernst von Mansfeld (1580–1626), a prominent mercenary general during the early years of the Thirty Years' War.</note> joined with the Bohemians while Emperor Matthias was still alive. Now, if he had lived another year or two—which could well have happened—would the idle soldiery have waited all that time for the Emperor's death, for which there was such great longing? They were not recruited to lay their hands in their laps and be idle, for pay is not spent on lazy soldiers. And even if I were to grant your desire, you would still not be innocent in this matter. For is it right that you wait for another's death so that you might seize his inheritance by force and weapons? Who appointed you as executors of the will, that you should want to assign the deceased's wealth to this person or that, out of your own wanton caprice? The Kingdom of Bohemia was promised to Ferdinand <note>Ferdinand II (1578–1637), who succeeded Matthias as Holy Roman Emperor.</note> after the Emperor's passing, and confirmed by the Estates with a sworn oath <term>Eidtspflicht: a formal legal obligation or oath of fealty</term> before Emperor Matthias had passed away, so that he should possess it once the Emperor had departed this life.

## PLESSIUS <note>Referring to Volrath von Plessen, a counselor to the Elector Palatine.</note>

For further confirmation of his allegation, the compiler cites on page 34 a letter to Volrath von Plessen, Privy Councilor of the Electoral Palatinate, written on September 11, 1618, in which these words may be read at the very beginning: "That the claims of the Bohemian Estates, the Confederated Protestants in Germany, as well as the United Netherland Provinces, all rested upon one foundation," etc. From this, he says, it follows that the Union <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states.</note> was instigated as much against their lawfully elected Roman Emperor as against other loyal Estates of the Holy Roman Empire. But to this long, entangled consequence—stretched from the verse "Is then the States," etc. on page 35, to the verse "on current," etc.—no ground or proof can be attributed, because both the premises and the antecedents are mutilated, corrupted, and false. For firstly, it is not at all admitted that at the time such a letter was written, the Bohemians had risen against their King, because it was sufficiently explained in the first chapter that the Bohemians cleared themselves of the charge of high treason <term>Perduellion: a Latin legal term for treason or hostility against the state</term> with perpetual protestations. Likewise, one cannot admit that the United [Protestants] had entered into certain pacts, a league, or correspondence with the Bohemians...

<summary>This page debates the legitimacy of the Bohemian revolt and the Protestant Union's actions prior to Emperor Matthias's death, specifically addressing accusations of treason and the pre-arranged succession of Ferdinand II.</summary>
<keywords>Emperor Matthias, Ernst von Mansfeld, Ferdinand II, Volrath von Plessen, Kingdom of Bohemia, Protestant Union, high treason, succession, Thirty Years' War</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the debate regarding the legal and religious justifications for the alliances between the Protestant Union, the Bohemian Estates, and the Dutch rebels against the House of Austria.</meta>

gone. But this is far more loathsome to hear: that the United <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states formed in 1608.</note> and the Bohemians are alleged to have had a single foundation, specifically against the House of Austria. For what business do the United have with the House of Austria, or conversely, what has that House to do with the United? Or what kind of jurisdiction or superiority could the House of Austria claim against the Union? The United recognize no sovereign other than the Emperor. Yet, from this argument—that not only good neighborliness but even a sort of vassalage <note>term: *Vasallagium* (vassalage); the legal obligation of a subordinate to a lord.</note> is owed by the United to the Austrians—such a conclusion could eventually be drawn. Since affirming such a thing is an absurdity, it will also, in my opinion, be impossible for the Austrian servants to maintain this with any basis in truth.

If the compiler had sincerely translated the words of Doctor Messenius <note>Johannes Messenius (1579–1636), a Swedish historian and scholar.</note> according to the sense in which they were written, he could have easily judged that the words—"The cause of the Bohemian Estates, the protesting United in Germany, and the Confederates in the Netherlands rests upon a single foundation, etc."—had no other meaning or significance than that these were the common and general foundations of everyone's cause. A general cause is the preservation of religion and liberty; ancient liberties and privileges are general foundations. Indeed, the Netherlanders manfully fought for, protected, and shielded these same things against the Duke of Alba <note>Fernando Álvarez de Toledo, the Spanish governor known for his harsh suppression of the Dutch Revolt.</note> and the Spaniards. The United have privileges and many old constitutions regarding both the Religious and Secular Peace. Who then would blame them if they uphold and protect these? What precious Letters of Majesty <note>term: *Mayestätsbrieff*; a 1609 document signed by Emperor Rudolf II granting religious tolerance to Bohemians.</note> (to say nothing of other old exemptions) did the Bohemians obtain from their King, Emperor Rudolf? Which of the Protestants would have begrudged the Bohemians for protecting and shielding such a dear pledge—secured with an Imperial Bull—and also themselves, under such Imperial shadow? That person, I say, would have to be a mere mouth-Christian <note>A hypocrite who professes faith only with words.</note>, an Epicurean <note>Here used as a slur for someone who seeks worldly pleasure over spiritual duty.</note>, and a godless person in his heart. What reason, then, moved the compiler to translate the words that flowed from Doctor Messenius’s good intentions in such an un-German <note>In this context, "un-German" implies something dishonest, foreign to the spirit of the language, or treacherous.</note> way?

---

->FABIUS.<-

You, Plessis <note>Likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent French Huguenot leader.</note>, have written and admit yourself that the Bohemians, the United, and the Hollanders rest upon one foundation, and that all their counsels are to be understood in that light. But, my dear fellow, toward what are the Hollanders' counsels directed, if not solely and uniquely against their King? Now, if the Bohemians intend the very same thing as these Hollanders, then well—

<summary>The text defends the Protestant Union and the Bohemians, arguing that their resistance is based on the universal right to preserve ancient liberties and religious freedom rather than a specific conspiracy against the House of Austria. It specifically cites the Letter of Majesty granted by Emperor Rudolf II as a legal safeguard for Bohemian rights.</summary>

<keywords>Protestant Union, Bohemia, House of Austria, Emperor Rudolf II, Letter of Majesty, Dutch Revolt, religious freedom, Duke of Alba, Johannes Messenius, legal jurisdiction</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the catchword "obedience," leading into the discussion of the Bohemian estates' duty to their sovereign.</meta>

# On the Rebellion and the Union

If this is the case, then they must likewise be regarded as rebels against their Lord and King. You cannot escape the blow in this instance by claiming that the Bohemians had not yet rebelled against the Emperor at the precise time you wrote those words. But what do you say to this, Plessis <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a prominent French Protestant writer and diplomat</note>: they had already cast the high Royal officers out of the windows <note>The Defenestration of Prague (May 23, 1618), which triggered the Thirty Years' War</note>; they had driven others, who were obedient and faithful to His Imperial Majesty, out of the land; they had undertaken great preparations for war; and they rested, along with the Dutch, upon a single common foundation. Now, what is this "foundation" other than an infallible decree and resolution to cast off their sworn obedience? The protestation of the Estates in Bohemia consisted of mere words, yet it was highly damaging to their Lord. 

If it is "ridiculous," as you write, to say that the United <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states</note> and the Bohemians share one cake and one foundation, then you yourself, as the author of these words, must be considered a fool. What, you might ask, does the Union have to do with the Archdukes of Austria? And what, in turn, do they have to do with the Union? Little indeed, and yet they act with enough hostility toward them. For one part of the Union—namely the Calvinists, however great they wish to be—stand against the aforementioned Archdukes in a state of perpetual battle-array, and they stake all their wealth on seizing the Austrian estates for themselves. This is what the United have to do with the Lords of Austria, to whom no Catholic subjects the Roman Empire: for that is why the Emperor is there.

But since every man is the interpreter of his own words, let us hear Plessis’s cunning. "The foundation," he says, "of the Dutch, the United, and Bohemia is nothing other than a common cause; and this common cause is the preservation of religion and liberty."

And this is your worthless defense, with which you shall do us no harm, nor shall you topple our walls with such floodgates. For tell me: if your cause is common with the Bohemians, then you were already to be considered rebels at that time. It may be that certain privileges were taken from the Bohemians, but how did that concern the Union? Are the Bohemians then your foster children, and were you appointed as their guardians? Why do you concern yourself with the affairs of another kingdom? If you perhaps had compassion for those so distressed, why then did you not petition His Imperial Majesty to relent in his anger and overlook something of the Calvinists' incapacity? To join oneself to those in tumult is not to quiet the riot, but to desire its increase. They are not to be further incited through coaxing and the promise of greater aid, but rather—as necessity demands—brought back to their sworn obedience through either gentle or harsh words.

<summary>The author accuses Philippe de Mornay (Plessis) and the Protestant Union of inciting the Bohemian Rebellion, arguing that their "common cause" of religion and liberty is merely a pretext for treason against the Habsburg Emperor.</summary>
<keywords>Thirty Years' War, Defenestration of Prague, Philippe de Mornay, Protestant Union, Bohemian Estates, Calvinism, Habsburg Dynasty, religious liberty, rebellion</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the polemical debate regarding the Bohemian Revolt and the legal standing of the Letter of Majesty, following the previous discussion of political and religious tensions in the Holy Roman Empire.</meta>

...to spend your time in obedience. You have extended weapons and arms to those who are furious, and if they were not already raving, you have made them frantic. But "freedom" has compelled you to do so: dear friends, what kind of freedom is that? Nothing of the sort was taken from the Bohemians: for who has ever had the freedom to usurp another’s rights or to overturn them? What justice do you have on another’s ground and soil that you should build there? No Emperor or King grants freedoms so that one may take what belongs to another. And did Emperor Rudolf <note>Rudolf II (1552–1612), Holy Roman Emperor who granted the Letter of Majesty in 1609.</note> grant the <term>Letter of Majesty</term> <note>A document guaranteeing religious tolerance to Protestants in Bohemia.</note> to the Bohemians for any other reason than to prevent things from going worse for him? Do you now wish to regard such a thing as a grievance? I do not begrudge them this at all—so little, in fact, that I would not wish such a thing for myself or anyone else. For what use would freedom be to a <term>sacrilegious person</term> <note>original: "Kirchenrauber" (church-robber)</note>? Freedom is cut far too short when insane presumption tries to boast with an extorted privilege. And those who strive to take the Letter of Majesty away from you again are in vain considered by you to be godless people and <term>Epicureans</term> <note>Followers of Epicurus, often used as a slur in this period for those perceived as atheistic or purely materialistic.</note>; rather, he is much more to be regarded as such who promotes various damned forms of worship—even though the Calvinists desire many things so that, in the end, they may have nothing at all.

->P L E S S I V S.<-

In conclusion, and as an appendix, the compiler now adds this limping consequence on page 36 and argues thus: Messenus <note>Likely referring to Arnoldus Messenius, a diplomat or agent involved in the intrigues of the period.</note> has in his keeping all the secret matters of the <term>Union</term> <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states.</note>, especially the dealings conducted with the Dutch, the Bohemian Estates, the Turkish vassals, the rebelling Hungarians (to whom he was sent as an envoy), the Duke of Bouillon in France, and the English Crown, and has full knowledge of the same. Therefore, the Union was undertaken against His Imperial Majesty and the House of Austria, etc. Oh, what a magnificent argument! It is almost like those that boys in school laugh at: "The stick stands in the corner; therefore, the Pope is the head of the Church." Or, if he would prefer this one: "Dr. Wilhelm Jocher was an assessor at the Imperial Chamber Court in Speyer for several years and has good knowledge of such matters and business. Therefore, he was somewhat more opposed to the Count Palatine and wanted the <term>Electoral Hat</term> <note>The symbol of the Prince-Elector's status.</note> to be transferred from Heidelberg to the art chamber in Munich."

->F A B I V S.<-

<summary>This page features a heated rhetorical exchange regarding the legitimacy of the Bohemian Letter of Majesty and mocks the logical fallacies used by political opponents to link the Protestant Union with foreign conspiracies.</summary>
<keywords>Bohemia, Letter of Majesty, Emperor Rudolf II, Calvinists, Protestant Union, House of Austria, Heidelberg, Munich, Imperial Chamber Court</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a polemical debate regarding the legality of the Protestant Union and its alleged conspiracies against the Holy Roman Emperor. The speaker, Fabius, is responding to an opponent referred to as "Plessi" (likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly).</meta>

# FABIUS.

Logic <note>original: "Dialectica"</note> is often as great a joy to you as the pipe is to those who begrudge the name of God; yet He has piped so well upon it that the poor Devil, through his own song, has plunged himself into destruction. But let us now hear what you presume to measure out to us. There were, Plessi, the secret counsels held with the Dutch and Bohemian Estates, with the Turkish vassal Gabor <note>Gabriel Bethlen, Prince of Transylvania and ally to the Ottomans</note>, the rebellious Hungarians, the Duke of Bouillon, and with the King in England. *Therefore*, you claim, the Union was not established against His Imperial Majesty. With this scheme, however, you have not depicted our argument, but your own; and so that you might say something—and I might use no sharper word—you are telling an untruth. You direct the reader, on page 36, to the *Anhaltine Chancery* <note>A reference to the "Anhaltische Kanzlei," a famous collection of intercepted diplomatic documents exposing the plans of Christian of Anhalt and the Protestant Union</note>; if he goes there, he will find nothing but foul-smelling vanity.

Our argument, however, was fashioned thus: The Dutch Estates have defected from their Lord; the Bohemians have done the same. Now, if the Union supports itself upon the very same foundation as the Dutch and Bohemians have done: *Therefore*, the Union and the Calvinist alliance were spun together only for this end: so that they themselves might rise up against their Lord and Emperor, and spread "Freedom of Religion"—which they call a privilege <note>original: "Priuilegia"</note>—by force. Do you see now, Plessi, that the Union, by virtue of this argument, was established against His Imperial Majesty? Not indeed because you know the secrets of the gods Jupiter and Juno <note>A metaphorical reference to high-level political secrets</note> (although such is also a good indication), but because the same principles and causes of such rebellion—as you yourself admit—are a common thing with the Union, which is one and the same with the disobedience of insurgents.

Furthermore, our argument—which you have reshaped to your own liking—is, according to these forms you describe, exactly like these: *The stick stands in the corner. Therefore, the Pope is the head of the Church.* Likewise: *Doctor Wilhelm Jocher was for several years an Assessor at the Imperial Chamber Court in Speyer; therefore he is opposed to the Count Palatine and takes pleasure in the fact that the Electoral Hat might come to the Chamber of Art in Munich.* In which [chamber], without doubt, it would be displayed among other beautiful

> arts, or sins of nature <note>original: "Sünden der Natur," likely referring to "lusus naturae" or natural curiosities/monstrosities often found in cabinets of wonders</note>. I beg you, dear Plessi, do not take such great pleasure in your eloquence or subtlety. For such a parable, conforming to your gossiping fables, has never been heard from our side. However, so that you might not have cause to complain against me, as if I were refuting all your arguments, I will allow your own tested metaphors to pass in this instance. For in both

He argues that the Union's alliances with foreign powers and rebels prove their goal is to overthrow imperial authority under the guise of religious freedom.</summary>

your examples; both consequences are not inconsistent with the truth, regardless of whether they were derived from a limping <note>original: "hinckenden" — meaning a flawed or weak logical premise</note> or a correct antecedent. Therefore, the third consequence which you attribute to us—and indeed compare to the eternal truths—is also similar to the truth: namely, that the Union was established against the Imperial Majesty. You may now, Plessius, recognize and confess whichever you wish; for if it is a metaphor, then the Union, according to the form of your argument, belongs to the rebels; but if it is not a metaphor, then you wrong us by suggesting we cannot correctly form a syllogism <note>A form of logical reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two premises.</note>.

## P L E S S I U S.

Regarding what the compiler suggests about the legation <note>A diplomatic mission</note> in Hungary, no one can deny that the Lord of Plessen was dispatched to Hungary for the Diet <note>A formal deliberative assembly</note> held there in the month of July in the year 1620. He was sent to the Prince of Transylvania <note>Gabriel Bethlen</note>, a free prince who is subject neither to the Holy Roman Empire, nor the House of Austria, nor the Crown of Hungary—but he was not sent to the Hungarian Estates (with whom he had no dealings). However, it is in no way admitted that he acted to the harm or detriment of the Holy Roman Empire or Christendom during this mission; the burden of proving such a claim is publicly and justly pushed back onto the compiler's own chest. Of the same sort is the compiler's assertion on page 25, where he writes as if Stephen Corlati, the Hungarian envoy to Constantinople, had delivered letters to the Sultan in which the surrender of the fortresses—namely Villeck, Novigrad, Waitzen, Genee, etc.—was promised. The kind-hearted reader should take careful note of the words "are said to have surrendered," which conclude nothing certain, much less prove anything. Is this how the accused are to be convicted with credible evidence and their own handwriting, especially in such a highly important matter? This is what the boastful compiler promises everywhere. Indeed, it seems more likely to be true what the Hungarians say: that Caesar Gallus and Nicolaus Starzerus, both Austrian officers, had of their own accord offered the Turkish Emperor the surrender of many places, solely for the purpose of inciting him to make war against the Bohemians. Likewise, it should be well noted here that when the Prince of Transylvania mentioned the "Germans" in the Constantinople writings, he did not speak of all Germans in general, but solely and only of the Austrians; the kind-hearted reader will easily understand this from the context of each passage, in what precedes and follows.

->F A B I U S.<-

# FABIUS.

Von Plessen <note>Volrad von Plessen (1560–1631), a high-ranking statesman and diplomat for the Elector Palatine</note>, because it was already known from discovered letters, confesses to the Transylvanian embassy; yet he does so with the condition that he denies that which he believes to be still hidden. He traveled such a long journey through Hungary in total silence—so that he might not fall into suspicion—solely and alone to Bethlen Gabor <note>Prince of Transylvania and leader of an anti-Habsburg insurrection</note>, a free prince who is not subject to the Hungarian Crown. He claims he went for a far different reason than to deliberate with him on anything contrary to the welfare of the Holy Roman Empire and all of Christendom. Of this, the "steadfastness" of the Calvinist conscience is a certain witness; if anyone wished to provoke it with contrary speech, he would instead succumb himself and be convicted of falsehood.

But if we should lack for words, the Calvinists relieve us of a twofold labor. For firstly, the reader to whom the Calvinist spirit is even slightly known will easily guess, without the need for many arguments, that nothing is so harmful to the common weal that it would be too much for Calvinist presumption. For they are already prepared for mischief, and wherever they can find opportunity, they put it into action. Furthermore, the sorrowful outcome has proven what a highly damaging legate and negotiator von Plessen was in Transylvania. For he petitioned for help in the robbery of the Hungarian Crown against the common enemy, the Emperor <note>Ferdinand II</note>. There, the caressing serpent-tongue obtained something, since immediately thereafter the war was spun in Hungary, which soon after extended into Austria. This occurred when Gabor, parading in the stolen Hungarian Crown, and the rebellious, warlike Count von Thurn <note>Heinrich Matthias von Thurn, a main leader of the Bohemian Rebellion</note>, displayed more triumphs than victories almost at the city walls of Vienna.

Just as von Plessen perorated so excellently (backwards) at the Transylvanian court, so too did Stephan Corlathian <note>István Czinka/Corlathian, a diplomat for Gabor</note> act with the Turkish Porte <note>The Ottoman government</note>. If he had promised several Hungarian fortresses to the Turkish Emperor through writing, not only he, but also all others who were the cause of such a compliant generosity, could—and should—not only be accused of an exceedingly great presumption, but also well be condemned to death, because they were convicted of such a vice-ridden deed. But von Plessen exerts himself with words in vain; he would have preferred to have caused a bloodbath beforehand, if a happy outcome had followed according to his heart's desire. Therefore, you Plessen-ites, since you appeal to the original letter: well then, take it and read it through.

## Most Illustrious and Most Mighty Emperor / Most Gracious

> Lord, my most humble, most faithful, and most ready services are ever at Your Imperial Majesty’s disposal. Although I previously informed Your Imperial Majesty on the 7th of February, via a private courier, of the stable and inviolable peace established among us—concerning which the High Commander <note>original: "Obriste Bezier," likely referring to a regional Ottoman official or Beylerbey</note> of this place assured me through many friendly letters, as well as through a Chiaus <note>term: Chiaus (Turkish: Çavuş). An Ottoman messenger, sergeant, or diplomatic agent.</note>—nevertheless, on that very same day, I experienced, not without particularly great astonishment, a major change and instability. This occurred when the Mufti <note>term: Mufti. A supreme legal authority in Islamic law; here referring to the Sheikh ul-Islam in Constantinople.</note> summoned Your Imperial Majesty’s agent, Michael Starzer, to appear before him. Among other things, he announced that the Hungarian Legate, in the presence of Stephan Corlathi, had promised his Most Mighty Emperor—through letters from the high Estates—not only the fortresses in Hungary, but also those in Bohemia, Moravia, Silesia, Upper and Lower Austria. This was done with the certain consolation that he would easily obtain everything from Your Imperial Majesty (relying on I know not what friendship). However, since I do not have the slightest doubt regarding Your Imperial Majesty’s most gracious intentions, and that you will nonetheless never consent or agree to this—and since the Turks commonly make great promises while gradually preparing themselves for war—I wished to warn Your Imperial Majesty in good time out of most humble reverence, commending myself most humbly to your Imperial favor and grace. Given at Constantinople on the 25th of February, Anno 1620. *

->Ludovicus de Molart.<-

***

That is quite enough reading, and the puffed-up Calvinist spirit has good reason to be ashamed of it. You say the Hungarians made such promises to the Turk through **Caesarem Gallum** <note>original: "Cæsarem Gallum," likely referring to a specific diplomatic intermediary</note> and **Starzer**; I wish to hear it from them when you can present witnesses to me. But I will have no Calvinists as witnesses, or else I shall cry out:

->**Pless** <note>Refers to the Calvinist polemicist Volckmar Pless, the target of this critique.</note> present other witnesses to me,<-
->Who speak the truth a little better.<-

And it is just as credible when you say that the Transylvanian <note>Refers to the Prince of Transylvania, likely Gábor Bethlen, who was allied with the Protestants.</note>, when he mentions the "Germans" to the Turk, means only the Austrians, so that you and others of your kind would not be included. But you attempt in vain to pull the wool over another's eyes; we are the children of the Eagle <note>A reference to the Imperial Eagle of the Habsburgs, symbolizing keen sight and legitimacy.</note>, and we see that you flee from the name of shame, yet love the shame itself nonetheless.

<summary>This page contains a translated diplomatic letter from Ludovicus de Molart in Constantinople (1620) warning Emperor Ferdinand II of Ottoman and Hungarian maneuvers, followed by a sharp Catholic polemic against the Calvinist Volckmar Pless.</summary>
<keywords>Diplomacy, Ottoman Empire, Thirty Years' War, Constantinople, Calvinism, Hungary, Transylvania, Habsburgs, Volckmar Pless, Michael Starzer</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the polemical debate regarding the "Anhalt Secret Chancery," a collection of intercepted documents used as propaganda during the early years of the Thirty Years' War. # PLESSIUS.

But is it not evident from this—and from many other places in the Anhalt Secret Chancery—that the Prince of Transylvania <note>original: "Fürst in Siebenbürgen," referring to Gabor Bethlen</note> is burdened with the title of a Turkish vassal, revealing the unjust proceedings of the frivolous and utterly inconsistent compiler? For, look you: now the Prince of Transylvania is called an obedient Prince of the Empire and, in the settlement reached at Nikolsburg <note>The Peace of Nikolsburg (1621) between Gabor Bethlen and Emperor Ferdinand II</note>, the very best prince of all; likewise, if the Imperial contributions were to follow as hoped, he was promised aid against the Turks, and even the Order of the Golden Fleece. Yet soon thereafter (as time will testify), he is again scolded as a renegade, perjured Mamluk <note>term: "Mammeluck," used here as a derogatory term for a turncoat or someone who has betrayed their faith</note> and a Turkish vassal. Furthermore, where the compiler mentions the Duke of Bouillon, he remains stuck in generalities, just as he did in the *Unrites* <note>likely a reference to a specific section or pamphlet titled "The Unjustices"</note> on page 21. In my opinion, only when the Devil ascends to Heaven will he be able to prove and demonstrate that anything adverse against the Imperial Majesty or the whole Holy Roman Empire was undertaken by the Count Palatine <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate, the "Winter King"</note> with the aforementioned Duke of Bouillon. At this very point, he also draws the King of Great Britain <note>James I</note> into the game, making him likewise suspicious to the Imperial Majesty. I beg the reader to take diligent note of this: for as soon as he continues in the Chancery (pages 305, 306, 307, 334), he will notice glorious praise for the King of England—namely, that he would not approve of or sanction the injustices and the Count Palatine's actions, especially regarding the Bohemian affair. From this, the reader can easily conclude that no credit is to be given to one who cites such contradictory matters.

## FABIUS.

The defender of the Prince of Transylvania flies into the hair of the author of the Anhalt Chancery; but his anger is so impetuous that he strikes himself with his own words. He says the author acts quite inconsistently—that is, now this, soon another thing—and that he receives Bethlen <note>Gabor Bethlen, Prince of Transylvania</note> now with honors, now with insults. And yet he nevertheless explicitly writes that in this place, as in the entire Anhalt Chancery, Gabor is called a Turkish vassal. Now, if the compiler maintains this consistently everywhere, how then does he act inconsistently? Have you ever seen, Plessius, that one who always stays on one...

Fabius counters by pointing out that if the author consistently calls Bethlen a Turkish vassal, the charge of inconsistency is logically flawed.</summary>

...remains in the same footsteps, leaping about, and yet is seen now here, now there? These wonders—since no others possess them—we do not begrudge the Calvinists <note>The author uses "Calvinist" here as a polemical label for the Reformed political faction.</note> at all. We wish, however, to further examine the fickleness of [Gabor] Bethlen: for without doubt, before this war, he was a vassal of the Turk <note>The Ottoman Sultan</note>, since with his help he conquered Transylvania <note>original: "Siebenberg"</note> and subjected that province—which belonged to the King of Hungary as a better Christian lord—to the Turk. Was this done so that he might more securely possess the property of foreign lords, or because he was somewhat closer to his new master in matters of religion? From this, the war against His Imperial Majesty arose, as he [Bethlen] imagined he would become a *Hercules* <note>A reference to the mythical hero, implying Bethlen's delusions of grandeur</note> with great terror, though after a few months he retreated again to safe places with little glory. 

Shortly thereafter, when he had been made a Prince of the Empire through previous *Treaties* <note>original: "Pacta"</note> and had received several provinces by way of peace, he set his old mind to it again. He attacked Moravia more violently and hostilely than ever before; and, so that men might know he was an extraordinary Calvinist, he led the Turks into the land for such an abominable devastation. By this, he has left an eternal shame to dear posterity, because he established the first example in Germany which is contrary to Christian love and befits only the Calvinists. Since he has now, against all truth and faith, broken the alliance and stood by the Turk, we could not unjustly honor him again with his old title (as you teach us) of a deserter and Turkish vassal; nor would he find our words contrary to him, whose defense and weapons we have had to experience with particular pain through the devastation of land and people. 

Regarding the Duke of *Bouillon*, that example is just like that of *Gabor* [Bethlen]: for if I had no other testimony, the Calvinist fruit would give me sufficient evidence. And when you wished to make war upon His Imperial Majesty, you undoubtedly sought no help from His aforementioned Majesty through such a Duke. You act a little more rationally than to deny it. We have neither made the King of England suspicious, nor shared any counsel with him against His Imperial Majesty. You write knowingly against your conscience. But this we say: that you, *Plessis* <note>Likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader</note>, have good knowledge of the dealings carried out with the King of England. You should not be indignant at other people; rather, you are not unjustly hated by us because you instigated all such mischief. You assisted him with highly damaging counsel: you used all possible diligence to create a large following for yourselves, and thereby to become greater lords. The deeds will...

<meta>This page continues a polemical dialogue or legal defense regarding the political legitimacy of the Holy Roman Emperor and the actions of the Bohemian estates. The speakers Plessius and Fabius represent opposing viewpoints in this diplomatic dispute.</meta>

Something more to be considered: when one has a great company of followers attached to oneself. That the King belongs to you is certain; but that you belong to him, of that Pilate writes nothing. Rather, it is undeniable that you have been exhorted to desist from your unlawful proceedings. Therefore, if you wish to praise this King elsewhere according to equity, we are by no means contrary to ourselves, but go solely and only against you yourselves, who have sounded the alarm everywhere. But you say here, just at the right time, that no faith is to be placed in one who brings adverse things onto the path. That is certainly so, but much less should faith be placed in one who lies; and in this case, you are occupied with both types of such actions.

### P L E S S I V S <note>Likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot writer and diplomat.</note>

On the previously cited 36th leaf, the author begins that which was undertaken against the currently reigning Imperial Majesty, concerning the actions that took place at Frankfurt during the Imperial election. There, it is primarily to be considered that, although the author—who has hitherto expressed himself in generalities—now wished to show something specific, this could by no means be cited as a particular plot or deceit against the Imperial Majesty. This is because His Majesty had not yet ascended to the Imperial highness at that time, and thus was not yet the Head of the Roman Empire. Likewise, the fable on the 37th leaf, consideration 26, regarding the coins minted at Nuremberg, is sufficiently refuted in the treatise *The Mystery of Iniquity* <note>original: "Mysterio Iniquitatis", a famous work by Philippe de Mornay attacking the papacy</note>, to which the favorable reader is referred for the sake of beloved brevity.

### F A B I V S

You should, Plessy, have rightly called to mind here that, although His Majesty was not elevated to the Imperial dignity at that time, it was nevertheless unseemly for you to undertake such highly damaging and adverse plots in Bohemia against him. For it was not unknown to you that His Majesty was a most prominent member of the highly praiseworthy College of Electors <term>Churfürstlichen Collegij: the group of princes responsible for electing the Holy Roman Emperor</term>, and you knew what was contained for his security in the Imperial Golden Bull <term>Gulden Bullen: the fundamental law or constitution of the Holy Roman Empire issued in 1356</term>. On the contrary, you attempted to occupy the Imperial election with typical Bohemian frivolity, so that if His Majesty were elected Roman Emperor, you would not be held as rebels because of it—all of which has only a lovely appearance and name, but in deed is hostile and malicious. For in truth, you undertook various swift plots against His Majesty, both before his Imperial election and also after-

<meta>catchword: -wards (men-)</meta>

<summary>This page records a heated debate regarding the legality of actions taken against the Emperor before his election, specifically focusing on the Golden Bull and the Bohemian revolt. --- Page 58 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion of political provocations and the "first" offenses; this page continues the argument regarding the "last" and most severe actions taken by the opposition. The catchword "PLES-" likely refers to a proper name or location appearing on the following page.</meta>

men, of which the last—even if the first were equally swift and hostile in pursuing you—we can in no way allow to pass. This is because such actions properly belong to enemies and rebels, in that you have hostilely snatched for yourselves the crown of the elected Roman Emperor, or indeed, have expected and accepted it with great longing from those who did so. This act is in itself highly punishable and sufficient to make other misdeeds seem like mere trifles or child's play. 

For instance, special coins were struck at Nuremberg with the inscriptions: **FrIDerICVs reX roManVs. 1622.** <note>A chronogram where the capitalized letters (I, D, I, C, V, X, M, V) add up to the year 1622.</note> *Frederick, Roman King, 1622.* **FrIDerICVs Imperator aVgVstVs. 1623.** <note>A chronogram for 1623.</note> *Frederick, Augustus Emperor, 1623.* <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate, the "Winter King," whose acceptance of the Bohemian crown helped spark the Thirty Years' War.</note> This endeavor, however, met with an unfortunate end. Just as the most powerful and wealthy King Croesus <note>A legendary king of Lydia known for his wealth and his fall from power.</note> was suddenly pushed from his throne and fell into misery, so too has Frederick been no Emperor, no King, and no Elector. 

If you now regard this prophecy as a fable or child's work, it was you yourselves who first honored your Count Palatine Frederick with such fables. But if you consider it earnest, you cannot mock the opposing side for making such coins public to everyone—coins through which you so openly displayed your senseless arrogance. In this, you imitate those people who constantly devote themselves to robbery and murder, shooting their arrows from hidden dens at passers-by. When they have caused harm, they are accustomed to excuse themselves to the traveler with friendly words, saying they only meant to give them a friendly fright. 

In exactly the same way, the Calvinists are accustomed—when they have attempted to snatch the Imperial crown and majesty for themselves, not only secretly but also publicly, and their schemes are revealed—to call it mere child's play and a fable with which they only joked and trifled. Such feigning and mocking is in no way to be tolerated. You might as well be earnestly malicious, though in that regard we have felt no lack of effort from you, but have rather received much public damage and misfortune at your hands. 

Furthermore, I am referred by you in vain regarding this point to the *Mysterium Iniquitatis* <note>Latin: "The Mystery of Iniquity," a theological term often used in the Reformation to refer to the Antichrist or the corruption of the Church.</note>—the mystery of malice—for while I am arguing with you, I have no intention of distancing myself from that subject for other reasons. I have not read the specific *Mystery* of which you write, yet I, along with all of Christendom, have felt its effects painfully. You would have done much better to explain the aforementioned coins at this point, though it pains you greatly that we can understand them perfectly well even without your interpretation.

# Page 56

...had and enjoyed peace in prison / as some, in a forgotten and highly punishable manner, have already considered and built; but it would be only fair that these same people / according to their earned reward / should themselves inaugurate and occupy them. Indeed, he who left the negotiation of the <term>Calvinists'</term><note>Followers of John Calvin's reformed theology, often viewed with suspicion by both Catholics and Lutherans at this time.</note> peace to chance, has truly entrusted the sheep to the grim wolf. For my part, no such peace is acceptable which is directed solely toward war and harm. The second error is that the Catholics allowed the Royal Danish ambassadors to depart from Vienna with their business unfinished. Regarding this, those who heard his petition could give a more correct answer than I, since I did not attend the Imperial Council. However, I know at least this much: that he desired peace at a time when it was not the season to establish peace, but rather when the punishment of the rebels was at the door. If <term>indulgence</term><note>original: "Indulgenz"; here meaning leniency or formal pardon.</note> and mercy were to be granted to wrongdoers every time after a crime was committed, others would be all the more incited to such acts. And indeed, one might well ask why this ambassador stayed away so long, and only then prayed for peace when the <term>Count Palatine</term><note>Frederick V, the "Winter King," whose acceptance of the Bohemian crown triggered the Thirty Years' War.</note> was still in his power and status, while His Imperial Majesty, by contrast, stood in great danger. Should more and higher importance then be placed on the welfare of the Count Palatine than on that of His Imperial Majesty? Truly, His Royal Dignity of Denmark would have attained immortal and eternal praise if he had undertaken / right at the beginning, while everything was still in good standing / to establish peace and unity between the parties, and thereby place the Kings in safety. Especially since this matter concerns all Kings in common; for if it is permitted to depose Kings from their realms by force, then His Royal Dignity in Denmark likewise has such a thing to lose, especially among so many who strive for it—just as no Regent has ever strangled or removed his successor to the Empire from the way. But if anyone takes this rejection and repulse of the Danish ambassador deeply to heart, let him consider that the outrage committed by the Count Palatine against His Imperial Majesty went much more poignantly to the heart—to say nothing of the fact that His Imperial Majesty justly refused that which even the one for whom the <term>intercession</term><note>original: "Intercession"; formal mediation or pleading on behalf of another.</note> was made would have rejected. Even if His Royal Dignity [of Denmark] were worthy of such a thing, the Count Palatine is not worthy to obtain it, as he, in such great misery, has yet shown no sign of any repentance and persists in his evil intention. For he who seeks destruction, perishes therein.
