### The Third Chapter.

# That the United Estates have undertaken many different
# schemes against the Holy Roman Empire and
# its Constitutions.

->Information and Answer.<-

->P L E S S I V S.<-
<note>Plessius likely refers to Philippe de Mornay, Lord of Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot diplomat, or a pseudonym used in this dialogue-style defense.</note>

It is evident from the content of the established Union alliance, both in the letter and the spirit itself, that the aforementioned Union alliance was not intended either against the Holy Roman Empire or to instigate any unrest within the same. Although the Author <note>The "Author" refers to the writer of the Catholic polemic this text is refuting.</note> cites evidence to prove the contrary, claiming that the United Estates—or three "Superiors" as he calls them—negotiated and concluded an agreement with the well-known Prince <note>Likely referring to the Elector Palatine.</note> regarding whether they could grant him the third Vicarship <term>Vicariat: the office of an Imperial Vicar, who governs the Empire during an interregnum or in the Emperor's absence</term> in the Empire. However, it is answered in response that such an offer was not necessary, since he was already a Vicar of the Holy Empire and had inherited such a Vicarship from of old from his ancestors, as is known to everyone. Thus, it cannot be regarded as a particularly high vice or crime that a search was conducted by command in the Heidelberg Archives <note>The administrative records of the Electoral Palatinate</note> to see if different Vicarships were to be found within the Holy Roman Empire and outside its borders. The Author should rightly have known, however, that there are not three Vicarships in the Holy Roman Empire, but four; as can be read in the *Thesauro Politico* <note>original: "Thesauro Politico", a famous collection of political treatises published in Milan</note> in the Italian language, which states that Emperor Maximilian I gave the Vicarship in the Frisian Provinces to his son as a Prince and Lord of the Netherlands. But how the granting of such a Vicarship was brought to light at that time, and whether it was established with the consent of the Imperial Estates, and what its current status is, we must rightly expect a report from the Author.

->F A B I V S.<-
<note>Fabius represents the opposing or questioning voice in this political dialogue.</note>

<summary>This page defends the Protestant Union against charges of constitutional overreach, specifically addressing the legality of the Elector Palatine's claim to the office of Imperial Vicar. It cites historical precedents and archival research in Heidelberg and Milan to justify the distribution of vicarious power within the Empire.</summary>

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the formation of the Protestant Union and the initial diplomatic maneuvers between the Calvinist and Catholic factions. # FABIUS.

I certainly cannot deny that in the instrument and documentation of your established **Union** <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states formed in 1608.</note>, nothing but honey-sweet, sugar-sweet, and many holy words are to be read—far more than you **Calvinists** are in reality. For not everything that is discussed at home in secret, or in private gatherings, is committed to paper; rather, that which is suspicious to publish is instead recorded in the memory. And he who undertakes to deceive another is accustomed to keeping his counsels secret and to <term>simulate</term> <note>original: "simuliren"; to feign or pretend</note> something other than what he has in his heart. Even the enemy often shows himself to be very God-fearing so that he may deceive others. And indeed, one must confess that your public writings, which you have brought to light, appear to be without blame, just as your words themselves are so fashioned that the whole world wonders where such friendliness among the **Calvinists** originated. 

But you are best aware of what secrets you have kept hidden under such a cover; we, for our part, did not hear you until you broke forth with force and open war. Likewise, your secret writings are best known to you because you wrote them yourselves—though we now know them as well, having read them to your great indignation, vexation, and shame. Even if it is esteemed very lightly in the **Anhaltine Chancery** <note>The administrative office of Christian of Anhalt, a lead advisor to the Elector Palatine and a key figure in the Protestant Union</note>, this matter is on the other hand scolded and highly resented, because the **Roman Empire** <note>The Holy Roman Empire</note> was thereby to be <term>agitated</term> <note>original: "exagitiret"; disturbed or stirred up</note> and disquieted. 

And indeed, if I accuse someone of a theft before the authorities and discover the theft with certain evidence, he cannot escape the accusation by proving that he behaved piously in one or two other places. Thus, the **Calvinists** may be entirely innocent, holy, peaceable, and quiet in their public writings—which is rightly to be praised—but nevertheless, their secret writings, hidden deceptions, and practices against the common good, established by them solely for harm, cannot be approved. 

In the meantime, you should give a proper answer to this: that although by virtue of your alliance no help was to be called for from **Saxony** against the **Catholics**, you nevertheless, regardless of this, desired such help through writings and dispatched legations; and to obtain such help, you enticed them not only through the promise of the **Vicariate** <note>The office of Imperial Vicar, who ruled during an interregnum</note> but also with even greater honors. Thus, it cannot be justified that you turn this **Vicariate** into a mockery, as if it were a mere name and title inherited by such a Lord from antiquity; for if you were aware of that, you offered him in vain that which he already possessed. But such a proposal of yours was based on—

<summary>Fabius accuses the Calvinist Union of hypocrisy, claiming their peaceful public documents mask secret "Anhaltine" plots to destabilize the Holy Roman Empire and illicitly court Saxon support.</summary>
<keywords>Protestant Union, Calvinism, Anhaltine Chancery, Holy Roman Empire, Imperial Vicariate, Saxony, diplomacy, political deception</keywords>

<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion of the legal claims regarding the Imperial Vicariate; this page continues the rebuttal against the Calvinist faction's interpretation of that office.</meta>

was viewed differently / and a different Vicariate <note>The office of a deputy or representative of the Emperor</note>, namely the third in the Roman Empire, was determined thereby / but you might deceive children with such a bare pretense of yours / but not sensible persons / to whom such cunning and deceit are already manifest. Should it really be such a great vice and a wicked undertaking / to offer the Vicariate to such a high Princely person? Did not the Emperor Maximilian I also take part in such a thing / in that he appointed his son Philip as Vicar of the Empire in Friesland? In this pretense now, let the favorable reader note the Calvinist's cunning cloak with which he beautifies such deceit. The Emperor had appointed his son as Vicar in Friesland / Therefore, [you claim] the Count Palatine / or the United Triumviri <note>A reference to the leaders of the Protestant Union</note> also have the power to appoint a Vicar of the Empire / and indeed, you yourself before this / while your Lord was still living / would not have permitted / that another Prince / or even a lesser person in your lands / should have called himself a Vicar / and indeed / whoever ordains a Vicar for me during my lifetime / tears my Empire and power out of my hand. It belongs to me / upon the occurrence of an appropriate occasion, to ordain a Vicar / why do others wish to intrude into my office and power? From whom have you Calvinists obtained such impertinence / and pride / (I will not say / such power) to ordain a Vicar for the Roman Empire / has such a thing perhaps been granted and permitted to you through an orderly decree of the Electoral College / or with the consent of the common Estates of the Empire? Indeed, the very example of Emperor Maximilian cited by you is against you. Inasmuch as he, as the reigning Roman Emperor / was able by right to grant and hand over those provinces / which he could not govern in person / to others to administer / just as in former times the old Emperors likewise did / when they traveled across the Alps / into Italy / and more recently Emperor Charles V, when he went to Spain / left behind his brother Ferdinand as Vicar in the Empire.

The Vicariate, however / which formerly belonged to you / as it is also still due to His Electoral Grace of Saxony / only takes place / when the Roman Empire stands vacant without an orderly Head and Emperor / but during the lifetime of a Roman Emperor / no one has power in the same / except him to whom His Majesty has expressly relinquished and commanded such. If I am not even bound to tolerate a Vicar and Governor in my own house / why then should His Imperial Majesty have to tolerate another / appointed by you / in the Empire? You have therefore from this the reason / why at that time a Vicar, or Governor, was ordained in Friesland / to be understood / which His Imperial Majesty is not minded to reveal to you / if it did not happen out of mercy / because you are robbed of all reason.

# PLESSIS <note>Philippe de Mornay, known as Plessis-Mornay, a prominent Huguenot leader and advisor often used as a persona in these polemical dialogues.</note>

The author proceeds on page 11 to the Roman and Bohemian Crowns, reporting that the same were offered to the well-known Prince <note>Likely referring to Charles Emmanuel I, Duke of Savoy, who was a candidate for the Imperial throne.</note>, of whom he treats in various places, such as on page 68, and recounts the Prince of Anhalt’s <note>Christian I, Prince of Anhalt-Bernburg, a key diplomat for the Protestant Union.</note> journey to the aforementioned Prince. Likewise, on page 69 he mentions his memorial, and on pages 94 and 98 a protocol, from which he draws various points to prove that the United <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states.</note> had undertaken to transfer the Empire and other kingdoms to others at their pleasure. On pages 43 and 50, he alleges various practices were undertaken to act against the Constitution of the Holy Empire; however, he lacks proof. And should the favorable reader consider the memorials, instructions, and protocols cited by the author somewhat more diligently and without prejudice, he will then find that the Elector Palatine <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate, later the "Winter King" of Bohemia.</note>, in the negotiations with the known Princely person, aimed at four particular points, which he also composed in the established pacts for the preservation of his honorable reputation:

First, to avert hereditary succession to the Roman Empire <note>The Protestant princes opposed the Habsburg practice of treating the elective Imperial title as a de facto hereditary possession.</note> (page 69, at the end). Secondly, he restricted the article concerning the Roman Crown solely to the case of the Roman Empire being vacant and unoccupied, and the occasion of a new Imperial election (pages 69 and 113), where the express words are to be read: *after the death of Emperor Matthias* <note>original: "nach absterben Kaysers Matthiæ"</note>, etc. Thirdly, that he freely reserved his vote for himself until the occasion arose (page 70). And fourthly, he took special care of the Electoral Oath, in which he roundly confesses that he would name and elect that person who would be most useful to the Roman Empire for the preservation of its privileges and its sovereignty (page 70).

->FABIUS<-

Yes, certainly, everything proceeded magnificently, majestically, and cordially with the Count Palatine, in that this "new Atlas" <note>A sarcastic reference to Frederick V taking the weight of the world/Empire on his shoulders.</note>—unasked—desired to help the "Supreme Jupiter" <note>The Emperor</note> carry the heavy burden of heaven and government, and let the Roman Empire be so highly a concern to him that it would indeed be shattered and torn apart. Although you, Plessis, refer the reader to various places, he will find none of them pure and unstained, just as you have dared to prostitute the Bohemian Kingdom—which is a lawful wife and not at all a widow <note>A metaphor arguing that the Bohemian throne was already legally occupied and not "available" for Frederick to take.</note>. That the hereditary succession in the Empire so highly displeased your heart will be scolded by no one, but rather held against him as a proud, ambitious young Prince.

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion of the Elector Palatine's political ambitions; this page continues the legal and moral defense of his actions regarding the Bohemian throne and the Imperial succession.</meta>

just as no one would scold a thirsty man if he wanted to lead all the well-water alone into his dwelling. It is, however, undeniable that the Count Palatine <note>Frederick V, Elector Palatine, also known as the "Winter King."</note> was plagued by a heavy thirst, since he dug a well in a foreign Kingdom of Bohemia, only to drink destruction and final ruin from it. Thus, the Count Palatine is not alone in his refusal to suffer a hereditary succession <term>Succession</term><note>The practice of inheriting the throne by birthright rather than election.</note> in the Empire, as all those who highly love and defend free election do; for this reason, the highly laudable College of Electors <term>Churfürsten Collegium</term><note>The group of princes entitled to elect the Holy Roman Emperor.</note> was established by the ancestors, so that from among all, one heart and prince would be chosen to rule over all others.

But if now, from a Princely House in which the government has continued for a long time, more persons are found who are capable and worthy of it, the free election is not thereby taken away from the Electors. Rather, it is a special praise and glory to such a Princely House that so many persons worthy of such high dignities are found within it; for they would not be elected if they were not capable and worthy of such high honors. Even if we were to grant you the second and third conditions by which the Count Palatine confirmed the promise or given hope of the Imperial Crown <term>Kayserliche Cron</term>, has he then, in your opinion, named the fatal departure of His Imperial Majesty and the resulting vacancy <term>Vacantz</term> or clearing of the Empire <term>Kayserthumb</term>? Or rather, did he not only fail to wait for that opportunity, but even sought to arrange it himself, in order to fulfill his made promise all the sooner and more conveniently? Just as the troops dispatched into Alsace were aimed at no one else but His Imperial Majesty, so too was the army <term>Armada</term> commissioned in Bohemia intended and aimed at His Majesty.

->PLESSIS<- <note>Likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader and diplomat.</note>

Therefore, the slanderers <term>Calumnianten</term> may attack the cited treaty according to their pleasure and pull it through the flax-comb <note>A metaphor for harsh criticism or "raking over the coals."</note>; nevertheless, the four mentioned circumstances and reservations will easily excuse and protect the Count Palatine among all Estates of the Empire <term>Reichs Ständen</term> as well as other foreign impartial parties. Nor can it be criticized by the author or anyone else, since hereditary succession in the Empire is hated by everyone and is intolerable; it is also expressly forbidden in the Imperial Capitulation <term>Kayserliche Capitulation</term><note>The contract between the Emperor and the Electors limiting his power.</note>. In truth, all and every Roman Emperor who is elected nowadays is accustomed, through a special instrument with their own hand-subscription and the attachment of their own seal—and thus through a powerful oath—to abjure such hereditary succession, and to swear that they nevermore, and upon...

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal and political debate regarding the rights of Electors to discuss imperial succession during the lifetime of a reigning Emperor, specifically defending the actions of the Elector Palatine against accusations of treason.</meta>

some wise men wish to undertake the same; they obligate themselves before God and in the presence of the entire Empire. But on the other hand, who would wish to strip the <term>Count Palatine</term> <note>German: Pfaltzgraf; the ruler of the Electoral Palatinate</note> or any other <term>Prince-Elector</term> <note>German: Churfürst; the high-ranking princes responsible for electing the Holy Roman Emperor</note> of his right, so that in the event of a vacancy in the Imperial throne, he might consider a capable, worthy successor useful to the Empire—even during the lifetime of a Roman Emperor—and communicate about this with others? Such a thing is forbidden to the Electors neither in the <term>Golden Bull</term> <note>The 1356 decree which functioned as the constitution of the Holy Roman Empire</note> nor in any other Imperial Constitution. Indeed, Elector Augustus of Saxony, during the lifetime of Emperor Ferdinand, communicated and negotiated with another Prince of the Empire (who was not even an Elector) regarding the future Imperial election. In the instructions given to Dr. Crambius, a professor at Leipzig, this is called the <term>Prerogative</term> of the Holy Electors and the "sole jewel" remaining from the freedom of their ancestors. It would also set a bad example, and speak ill of the Imperial Majesty and the highly praiseworthy House of Austria, if—according to the author's opinion in the *Apology* regarding certain intercepted letters—they were permitted to transfer the Roman Empire to their own lineage and House, even though the <term>Imperial Capitulation</term> <note>The contract signed by an Emperor-elect before coronation, limiting his powers</note> clearly shows the opposite. Conversely, it would be wrong to strip all foresight from an Elector and "Pillar of the Empire," who by his office is bound to maintain the freedom of the Holy Empire and its Estates, preventing him from considering a capable person as successor in the event of a vacancy, preserving the free election as a precious pledge of liberty, and thus averting hereditary succession.

### F A B I V S .

Plessis <note>Likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader</note> makes many vain words and disputes in this place, after the manner of wicked, gossiping women, yet he touches no one with them. For we do not deny that both the election and the deliberation for the Imperial Dignity always remain within the power of the Electoral College, provided only that the meetings and deliberations are not directed against the person already reigning. For such a thing is a highly punishable undertaking, which has been actively attempted by you <term>Calvinists</term> as well as other irresponsible acts. As I have already stated above, the entire highly praiseworthy House and lineage of the Imperial Majesty was targeted by such actions, and their downfall sought—not only through your counsels, but also with open violence and military force, even during the lifetime of Emperor

<summary>This page debates whether the Prince-Electors have the legal right to discuss future imperial candidates while an Emperor is still alive, arguing that such foresight is a "jewel of liberty" intended to prevent the Empire from becoming a hereditary monarchy under the House of Austria.</summary>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion of the political maneuvers surrounding the Imperial succession; this page continues the critique of the Protestant Union's attempts to recruit foreign allies against the House of Austria.</meta>

# Page 63

Matthias <note>Emperor Matthias, r. 1612–1619</note> of highly blessed memory, whom they likewise would have undoubtedly suppressed, if they had been able to ruin and overthrow his most praiseworthy House—according to the undertaking they had already begun. Furthermore, it was not done for the purpose—according to your false boasting—of restoring the Roman Empire, which was already leaning toward a fall, or bringing it into a better state through a foreign Prince; rather, it was done to patronize it for yourselves. Thus, it is also a pure deception to entice others with great promises for your own benefit. For you also gave the aforementioned Prince <note>Refers to Charles Emmanuel I, Duke of Savoy</note> hope for the Kingdom of Bohemia, but in deed performed nothing, although he was highly pleased by such a prospect, even though another already held it in possession. Likewise, the Imperial Crown was promised to him so that the matter might be achieved; but it would, without doubt, have adorned not the head of the Savoyard, but rather that of the Count Palatine <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate</note>. Thus, the Savoyard would have expended his costs and help in vain. It is indeed the common custom of the Calvinists here to make great promises when they stand in need of help; they also wish to offer great guarantees, even when they have nothing. Had the Prince thus set out full of vain hope, rendered his aid, manfully attacked the enemy, and perhaps even gained the upper hand—to whom would the benefit of such a victory <note>original: "Victori"</note> have belonged? Without doubt to the Count Palatine, who sought after the Imperial dignity <note>original: "Kayserthumb"</note> through such practices. And the Savoyard was not summoned with the intention that he should remain; unless he wished to undertake a new war, he would instead have to accept the situation as a petitioner and rather dispose himself back to his own land than remain among a hated people. Considering he is of a foreign nation and language, of another religion, and of foreign customs and habits—entirely distinct from the Germans <note>original: "Teutschen"</note> over whom he was meant to rule—the Germans, who are unaccustomed to foreign rule, would not accept him as Lord. Or, if they were forced to accept him, it would have no permanence and would be full of danger. He might thus dispose himself to his own land with great praise and glory after having obtained victory in such a manner, having overcome and weakened the most praiseworthy House of Austria. This would serve his everlasting glory, which praise—unlike worldly empires and principalities—could not be lost; for indeed, such a loss would happen with greater shame than the glory with which it was previously conquered. He should, therefore, justly desist from this before fickle fortune turns; for many have lost much despite the high renown and great titles of honor for which they so vehemently strove. Now, if the aforementioned Duke wished to decline this advice and press more vehemently upon the promises made to him, the answer to him would be—

word had been given—that although he was granted hope of attaining the **Imperial Dignity**, the freedom of choice in the election was always reserved in the treaty. The Count Palatine <note>Frederick V, Elector Palatine</note> had made the promise in such a way that nothing of his own would be taken from him. As is the custom of the **Calvinists**, they frame their promises so that they cannot be challenged for them.

What could the aforementioned **Duke of Savoy** <note>Charles Emmanuel I, who sought the Holy Roman Empire's crown</note> undertake against this? Should he use open force? He had already expended almost all his utmost power and might; the majority of his soldiery had perished through sickness and in battle. The remainder were not strong enough against the Calvinists, nor could he obtain new reinforcements from home, as it lay too far away with other lands in between. In those lands, there were more enemies than friends, whose minds had already turned; they would permit no foraging, but would attack his people secretly and openly, wear them down, cut off all necessities, and so distress him that if he did not withdraw, utter ruin was before his eyes. In his retreat, everything was equally insecure; yet it was much more advisable to depart from that uncertain, unfaithful land than to perish with it.

This, then, was the "great reward" for such demonstrated knightly service! And indeed, that the Calvinists deliberated in this way to deceive this mighty Prince and to bring everything belonging to the **Catholics** under their own control is evident from this: the Count Palatine and others who had knowledge of such secrets gave written admonition to the envoy when he wished to travel to Savoy. They stated that one must treat with the Duke with such sharp-witted cunning and craftiness that he would understand the war must be waged against the Catholics, but in no way against the **Religion** <note>The Catholic faith</note>. They intended to root out the faith, but dared not let this Catholic Prince notice it, otherwise he would have granted the envoy no audience.

This deceit, however, could by no means remain hidden for long, as the Duke of Savoy noticed it and left the Calvinists to their own ruin in this chaos, where he leaves them still at this time. Thus, the Calvinists deliberated on the reasons for setting up a foreign Prince as Emperor—not that they actually intended to raise him to such high majesty, but so that they might thereby create an access to the **Empire** <term>Kayserthumb: The office and authority of the Holy Roman Emperor</term> for themselves. Their goal was not merely to turn the scepter and power away from one lineage and House, but to bring it upon themselves. This was the true aim of their boasted "liberty." Thus, their great complaints—that the **House of Austria** <note>The Habsburgs</note> has hitherto been granted such great and supreme honor—are made against and without regard for the House's great merits and special virtues, through which they are worthy of the Empire. All these complaints from the Calvinists...

<meta>This page continues a political and historical argument regarding the evolution of the Holy Roman Empire, contrasting the meritocratic elections of the past with the religious and military turmoil of the early 17th century (Thirty Years' War era).</meta>

their origin; so that in former times, regard was not paid to high lineage and ancestry, but rather to great and distinguished experience, wisdom, martial virtues, and princely high understanding. Often, when votes were split between two equal persons, the honor was bestowed upon the higher and nobler one according to his virtue. In this manner, the first Emperor from the House of Austria, Count Rudolph of Habsburg <note>Rudolf I (1218–1291), the first Habsburg King of the Romans, often cited as a model of rising through merit and stability.</note>, was elevated to the Empire. He brought his lineage to such high standing through the aforementioned Imperial virtues. The same occurred with Emperor Adolph of Nassau and Count Henry of Luxembourg <note>Henry VII</note>, both of whom came from outside a princely line, being only of countly family. They received such great power not from themselves or their subjects, but as stemming from the Roman Empire itself. Because of the obedience of the Imperial Princes, they were powerful and were terrifying and feared by their enemies.

But now, it is a much different time and circumstance. Nowadays, virtue alone is laughed at if it is not larded with its own great force and power. Because the Imperial Princes are also divided in religion, they show the Emperor little of what is his due. Especially the Calvinists <note>A major Protestant faction during the Reformation and Thirty Years' War, often viewed by Catholic writers as the primary instigators of rebellion.</note> daily trim away all of His Imperial Majesty’s strength and injure His Imperial Majesty with their disobedience and rebellion. If you were to compare the current state with the old, you would find many fit for government; if it were only a matter of ruling subjects who remained in obedience as they did before, and if other help were available, fortune might turn and the government could be distributed as desired.

But at present, His Imperial Majesty must live in the midst of his unfaithful rebels and enemies, and cannot remain safe except through open force and weapons. You Calvinists have now made the Roman Empire and the Imperial dignity very expensive; whoever wishes to buy it must spend countless millions of goods upon it. Likewise, those princes who might have had the courage and opportunity to take it up are heavily restricted and hemmed in. Similarly, the Prince Palatine <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate, the "Winter King," whose acceptance of the Bohemian crown triggered the Thirty Years' War.</note> himself, however high-minded and ambitious he might be, would never have dared to set himself against His Imperial Majesty if his father-in-law in England <note>James I of England</note>, his mother’s brother in Holland <note>Maurice of Nassau</note>, the Huguenots in France, the rebels in Bohemia, Bethlen Gabor in Transylvania <note>Prince of Transylvania and anti-Habsburg leader</note>, and the Union in Germany <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states</note> had not been so fooled by the deceit of the Calvinists. They let them dream of and imagine nothing but mountains of gold—indeed, omnipotence itself—and even to this day they hope for it, which we may well allow them. If, following all this, any freedom has been taken from the Electors <term>Churfürsten: the elite group of princes responsible for electing the Holy Roman Emperor</term>, it was done by those who now complain about the causes of it, so that they might obtain that same freedom for themselves. But the Almighty...

It is forbidden by no right, by no law, or custom, to desire that dignity and honor of which I am worthy and which is given by others; and when I have attained the same, to likewise desire to pass it on to my children and descendants. Indeed, it is even permitted to apply all diligence and to work toward that end through all virtuous means. For that which is common <note>held in common by the Empire</note> should not be made the thrall of a single person. Therefore, the House of Austria and its highly praised Princes, when they apply themselves to this, confess that they in no way commit an outrage. If, however, they should undertake—by law, custom, or open or secret force—to pull that which is common solely to themselves, they would only then violate the Imperial Constitutions, forfeit their sworn duty, and be justly accused by the Calvinists—if indeed the Calvinists were capable of doing anything else, as they are accustomed only to scolding what is good.

Finally, because you also believe it was free for the Count Palatine <note>The Elector of the Palatinate</note>, as an Elector <note>term: "Churfürst" - one of the high-ranking princes who elected the Holy Roman Emperor</note>, to consider a successor to the Imperial Throne during the lifetime of His Imperial Majesty—which I also do not deny—you may now aptly remind him of this. For he is otherwise idle, and will not easily be rushed by the Electoral Assembly; nor will anyone appear too early who will never appear at all.

men, that it was suggested by Archduke Maximilian to be disclosed to the Elector Palatine <note>The Count Palatine of the Rhine, one of the high-ranking Prince-Electors of the Holy Roman Empire</note> and the Duke of Saxony. In his "Opinion on the Succession in the Empire," written at Prague on February 16th in the year 1616, he advised His Imperial Majesty Matthias to urge the succession upon the Electors in such a way that, while the election remains free to them, His Imperial Majesty should nevertheless designate a successor to the Empire. What was sought through this is not difficult to perceive. For, in the first place, the Electorship is thereby opened up, as it were, to hereditary succession; and there is no doubt that His Imperial Majesty would name a person from his own lineage. Secondly, the election would not be truly free, but rather confined within certain boundaries, if the Electors were bound to His Imperial Majesty’s specific designation and lacked the power to name another qualified person. Thirdly, this would be entirely contrary to the Electoral Oath, in which they swear to elect the person they deem most fit for such Imperial dignity, regarding neither favor nor gifts. For if it were left to His Imperial Majesty to name a successor to the Empire, the Electors would have to choose not the one they deem most suitable, but whoever was designated by His Imperial Majesty—whether he be fit for it or not—and thus they would elect against their oath and duty. Therefore, we highly exhort all and everyone who still carries any desire for the truth and the freedom of the Fatherland to keep a diligent eye and watch over where these <term>Consilia</term> <note>Latin: counsels or plans</note> and opinions are directed, and what outcome will follow, so that they do not allow themselves to be captivated by this "Bavarian Book." In the same way, you have also understood how to knit together various royal crowns through marriage by means of your <term>suffragia</term> <note>Latin: votes or formal expressions of support in an election</note> and voices; and you would much rather be kings and lords yourselves than advise others toward such a station. Nor is it easy to find someone who, being virtuous himself, would appoint a wicked man as his successor. The second particular point is: that the Princes of the House of Austria established a treaty and agreement among themselves in the year 1616 (which was subsequently signed and confirmed by Pope Paul V and the lately deceased King in Spain) without the prior knowledge of the then-reigning Imperial Majesty Rudolph II. In this treaty, the provision for future <term>Succession</term> <note>The right of following into an office or inheritance</note> in the Empire was expressly included, ensuring that the Imperial dignity continued within their House of Austria. ->P L E S S I V S.<-

This, however, would be entirely new and unheard of—indeed, comparable to a most singular wonder in the Roman Empire—that His Imperial Majesty should have possessed a right to the Imperial Crown prior to the election. Neither the Imperial Councilors and servants, nor that which is necessarily forced from the author's words, will approve of this: namely, that the House of Austria <note>The Habsburg Dynasty</note> should claim some right to the often-mentioned Imperial Crown either before or after the election. For there is a great distinction between the elected Roman Emperor and the House of Austria; the right of an Emperor is personal <note>original: "personal," meaning attached to the individual</note>, refers to the person, and passes away with the same. However, the rights of the House of Austria are material <note>original: "realia," legal rights attached to property or land</note> and extend to the following and succession itself. Thus, the author allows himself to be led astray by blind zeal to make the Elector Palatine and the United <note>referring to the Protestant Union</note> more hated by everyone, just as he displays even more palpable errors and resulting absurdities in this article beyond the previously cited mistakes. Alliances are established, of which the Bavarian Princes—specifically Duke William and Duke Ludwig—give sufficient testimony themselves. Regarding this, Thuanus <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou (1553–1617), a famous French historian and statesman known for his monumental history of his own times.</note> writes in the second book of his *Histories* in the following words:

> Accordingly, several years ago, when Emperor Charles <note>Emperor Charles V (1500–1558).</note> (in order to keep the Empire within his lineage and House) had his brother Ferdinand proclaimed Roman King at Cologne, the Electors and other Princes of the Empire strongly opposed this, as it ran contrary to the Imperial Golden Bull <note>The Golden Bull of 1356, the fundamental law or "constitution" of the Holy Roman Empire which dictated how Emperors were to be elected.</note>. They declared that such a thing could not happen without the consent of the Electoral College. For that reason, the Elector of Saxony, the Landgrave of Hesse, and both William and Ludwig, Dukes of Bavaria, established a secret alliance with King Francis in France <note>King Francis I of France (1494–1547), the great rival of Charles V.</note> for the preservation of the liberty of German lands. To this end, the King deposited one hundred thousand crowns with the Duke in Bavaria, so that the same would be ready at hand in case of emergency.

With the aforementioned King Francis, Landgrave Philip of Hesse also established an agreement and thereby obtained a certain sum of money from him, with which he dispatched an army and reinstated the Duke of Württemberg back into his Principality—without which, perhaps, neither he nor his descendants would have recovered it. Furthermore, it is known to everyone what kind of alliance was established 71 years ago between Henry II, King in France, and Elector Maurice of Saxony, Margrave Albert of Brandenburg, John Albert, Duke of Mecklenburg, and William, Landgrave of Hesse, for the preservation of German liberty. Regarding this, Thuanus likewise writes in his sixth book as follows:

> To the aforementioned conditions it was further added: that it would be useful for all parties if the King, as soon as possible, were to take possession of Metz, Toul, and Verdun in Lorraine, and retain them under the title of a Vicar of the Empire <note>original: "Vicarii deß Reichs." An official deputy or representative of the Emperor; here used as a legal pretext for French occupation of German-speaking territories.</note>. Likewise, he should start a war in the Netherlands, so as to attack and weaken the Emperor in different places. Finally, it was promised by the allies that they would regard the King, on account of such great favors, as a particularly high friend and father, and would in turn be helpful to him in the taking and keeping of his lands which had been taken from him or his ancestors. They would strive with the greatest diligence so that he might be elected Roman Emperor, or indeed, if that could not happen, that someone should be elected who was pleasing to him, remained in friendship with him, and would confirm these pacts with an oath.

And in the said book:

> The King shall pay two hundred and forty thousand crowns for the first three months, and sixty thousand crowns for each following month.

# FABIUS.

It would be entirely new and unheard of if the House of Austria <note>The Habsburg dynasty</note> wished to claim a right for itself prior to the election. You cannot extract such a meaning from the **Author’s** words, even if you were to attempt it in an entirely mad and irrational manner, as you say here: "which is necessarily perceived from the **Author’s** words," when in fact nothing of the sort is perceived or squeezed out of them, other than that you have lost your mind, and I am wasting great effort in vain to restore it. You might well teach others the difference between the Emperor and the House of Austria, since these **axioms** <note>original: *Axiomata*; self-evident principles</note> and rules are known to us: the right of an Emperor is **personal**, refers to the **person**, and perishes along with the same. However, the rights of the House of Austria are **real** <note>original: *Realia*; relating to things or property rather than persons</note> and extend to those who follow. This is without doubt a "high understanding"—for indeed, the Imperial rights also pass to the **successors**. What else would you put forward for your lost, dilapidated cause other than mere words?

But that the **Author** brings forward many palpable errors, from which great defects, **absurdities**, **inconveniences**, and faults arise—as you claim—you have not yet proven. Rather, you yourselves find in deed and in your long-lasting **exile** and misery that you have committed such defects and faults. Thus, no **examples** will testify that it was permitted to establish alliances against the Imperial Majesty, unless perhaps the Emperor sets aside his Imperial mind and acts to the public detriment of the Empire. 

When, therefore, **Charles V** <note>Holy Roman Emperor, r. 1519–1556</note> recognized his brother **Ferdinand** as Roman King <note>The title *Rex Romanorum*, designating the successor-apparent</note> in Cologne without the consent of the **Electoral College**, the Electors were justly able to **disapprove** <note>original: *improbiren*</note> and reject such an act, and maintain their right through the assistance of external help. In this matter, however, you, **Plessis** <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot writer</note>, along with your **historian Thuanus** <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou, a French historian</note>—who learned the history of events in Germany from fellows of your kind and deceived you, who were already wandering in error—have been far too unkind to the highly praised Emperor **Charles V**. For Emperor **Charles**, having so many stately kingdoms under him, could not give himself entirely to the German Empire; he was forced to travel now to **Italy**, now to the **Netherlands**, now to **Spain**. So that no harm would come to the common good and the Fatherland through his absence, he presented **Ferdinand** to the **Electoral College** in Cologne and highly **commended** him, so that they might accept him as Roman King, that he might be his **Vicar** <note>original: *Vicarius*; a deputy or representative</note> and **Governor** <note>original: *Statthalter*</note> in his absence. Although the Electors much preferred Emperor **Charles**...

...to keep Charles in the country; however, because His Majesty persisted so urgently, they—following the advice given (as Sleidanus <note>Johannes Sleidanus (1506–1556), a historian famous for his chronicle of the Reformation</note> reports at the end of the seventh book)—on the fifth of January, in the year 1531, renounced and accepted Ferdinand as the Roman King <note>The title "King of the Romans" designated the successor-elect to the Holy Roman Emperor</note>.

From this, you can recognize your own error and that of Thuanus <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou (1553–1617), a French historian whose work is being critiqued here</note>, and how you have allowed your emotions to deceive you. For Emperor Charles merely proposed his brother's person in this matter and did not elect him himself, much less declare and renounce him on his own authority; rather, this was done by the collective Electors <note>The prince-electors of the Holy Roman Empire</note>. To be sure, the sole Elector of Saxony protested against it through his son, as he was already occupied with the Schmalkaldic War <note>A conflict between the forces of Emperor Charles V and the Lutheran Schmalkaldic League</note> against the Emperor. But to give your argument more appearance of weight, you also include the two Dukes of Bavaria, Duke Wilhelm and Duke Ludwig, in this alliance. For this deed, you truly deserve great honor: since, aside from you and your author Thuanus, no one has been so industrious—not even the Bavarian princes themselves, who never entered into such an alliance and remained loyal to the Imperial Majesty throughout their entire lives. Likewise, only your Thuanus mentions sums of money being deposited (in secret) so that no one could see them, just as they were received by no one.

In addition to this, you mention two distinct alliances established with the King of France, in one of which the Landgrave <note>Philip I, Landgrave of Hesse</note> restored Duke Ulrich—who had been driven out by his own people—back into his land, and several others who thereby sought help and protection against the Imperial Majesty. Now, just as all of this is contrary to the Imperial Constitutions and the Imperial Recesses <note><term>Reichs Abschied: the final resolutions or decrees passed by an Imperial Diet</term></note>, it is likewise approved by no one to whom the statutes, orders, and laws of the Holy Empire are dear. And indeed, if an unlawful force is inflicted upon someone, it may well be repelled by another, whoever they may be; but when impatience is awakened among those unaccustomed to obeying authority by a regime conducted somewhat too strictly, there is no need for your own help or the help of others for your defense, save only that you render obedience: through this, either no war will arise, or it will be ended in a very short time. # PLESSIS

When the matter of this <term>Confederation</term><note>Referring to the diplomatic alliance or union currently under legal scrutiny</note> is compared with the negotiations involving the Duke of Savoy—which have been so vehemently exaggerated in the Bavarian Chancellery—a great inequality is found. For in this instance, nothing more than a mere, slight, and incomplete communication was held with a Prince of the Empire; whereas in the former case, a complete alliance was concluded with a foreign potentate, the King of France. This included all necessary formalities and oaths sworn by both interested parties, as can be seen in the aforementioned sixth book of Thuanus <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou (1553–1617), a famous French historian and author of 'Historiae sui temporis'</note>. Furthermore, nothing followed from our negotiation, while a significant military campaign resulted from the aforementioned alliance.

In addition, the Elector Palatine never attempted anything against the person of His Imperial Majesty. In contrast, Elector Maurice of Saxony and his confederates attacked Emperor Charles <note>Emperor Charles V (1500–1558)</note>, forced him into flight, and plundered everything they found in the Imperial court of that time. Moreover, he promised his confederates to strive with the utmost earnestness to ensure that the King of France would be elected Roman Emperor. In that agreement, the clause regarding a "future vacancy or opening of the throne"—as was used in this negotiation with the Duke of Savoy—was by no means included <note>The author argues their deal was conditional on a vacancy, whereas the historical precedent was an active plot to replace a living Emperor</note>.

Beyond all this, not only was the Imperial election promised to the King of France at that time, but it was also promised that if such an election did not please him, then someone would be elected Roman Emperor who was in friendship with the Frenchman and who would confirm this concluded alliance with an oath. From this, the favorable reader can clearly see how the confederates in that negotiation with the Frenchman went much further than what was negotiated here with the Duke of Savoy. Yet, no one in the Empire then or thereafter accused them of the crime of <term>lese-majesty</term><note>original: "læsæ maiestatis"; the crime of violating majesty or high treason</note> because of such an established alliance or the military expedition undertaken against Emperor Charles. Nor were they accused of undertaking secret, deceitful, and harmful practices against the Holy Roman Empire and His Imperial Majesty.

The author himself likely would not do so either, unless he wished to expect ill will from the Electors of Saxony and Brandenburg and the Landgrave of Hesse, or be sued by all the Protestants for <term>injury</term><note>original: "actione iniuriarum"; a legal action for damages resulting from insult or injury to reputation</note>. For it is known to everyone that through such an alliance with the King of France and the subsequent military campaign (without whose help the burden would have been too heavy for them), the liberty of the Roman Empire was preserved and the highly renowned Peace of Religion was brought about.

<summary>The author defends the Elector Palatine's negotiations with the Duke of Savoy by comparing them favorably to the 1552 rebellion of Maurice of Saxony, arguing that the latter's alliance with France was far more "treasonous" yet resulted in the celebrated Peace of Augsburg.</summary>

# FABIUS.

In vain do you trouble yourself and the reader; the Elector of Saxony, along with other Princes, out of an all-too-great desire for German Liberty <note>The "German Liberty" (Teutsche Freyheit) refers to the traditional rights and autonomy of the Imperial Estates against the centralizing power of the Emperor.</note>, and with the encouragement of other Princes, allowed themselves to be enticed into spinning an internal war. Even if that example were in any way praiseworthy, you would not desire to follow it. Although your cause is in itself much more wickedly fashioned, since you were incited to such an undertaking not by an overly harsh regime, but by a great lust for power. And you have sought foreign help against the defenseless, unarmed, and innocent Catholics, in order to ruin them, to snatch away other lordships, lands, and kingdoms for yourselves, and have deceived those whom you lured to your side with promises of great dignities. 

And although it might be permitted for you, when oppressed, to call upon others for help, such a thing is not appropriate in this case, when you venture to oppress others with injustice—especially against the Imperial Majesty. For when he was deposed by his rebellious subjects from the Kingdom of Bohemia, he was openly hindered and attacked by your Prince with the force of war; just as your Prince also assumed and ruled for a short time—supposedly—the Royal Scepter and Throne, which had been vacated through highly punishable rebellion. This now is the vice with which your Prince has offended the Imperial Majesty, having committed the <term>crimen laesae maiestatis</term> <note>Latin: "the crime of injured majesty," or high treason.</note>, for which reason he has been driven from the land and is cursed by everyone in the entire Empire. 

It is therefore right to erase everything you have written about this. There is also absolutely no comparison between the actions of the Elector of Saxony and your outrages; because yours were directed toward the despoiling and robbing of pious, innocent persons and the destruction of German lands, while the former directed their plots (in their own opinion) toward its welfare. Therefore, we cannot be accused of injuries by any Prince, for our writings were already aimed at you Calvinists, because you also attacked us with your secret cunning and open violence. Yet, by the righteous judgment of God alone, you have felt the harm, and vengeance is directed solely at the enemy; for we regard no one as an enemy who does not place himself in that number.

It is no injustice to attempt to expose those who intend to plunge all of Christendom into ruin, since even the love of the Fatherland did not suffice for such a task—unless you consider the entire Holy Roman Empire to be your Fatherland, just as you have exerted yourselves greatly to bring it entirely under your power. you were not at all oppressed with servitude, and your eternal religion—even if it was not included in the **Peace of the Empire** <note>original: "ReichsPacification"; likely referring to the Peace of Augsburg (1555) which initially excluded Calvinists</note>—was nevertheless left free and secure. # Page 82

The Estates have highly opposed and exerted themselves greatly to ensure that nothing was concluded there which might be useful for the preservation of the Imperial Majesty <note>original: "Kays. Mayest." referring to the Holy Roman Emperor</note> or the welfare of the Holy Empire. You have always been among the **objectors** <term>Refraganten: those who vote against or oppose imperial decrees</term> (I had almost said among the **insurrectionist rebels**), the foremost and most prominent of them. You even considered it a great praise when you showed yourselves to others as the **ringleaders** <term>Rädlinssührer: a derogatory term for the leaders of a conspiracy or faction</term> and adversaries. 

Nor can you prove that you were ever once an initiator or founder of obedience toward the Imperial Majesty or of unity among the Estates. For you have at all times incited others to disobedience against the Imperial Majesty and held them back by pretending that his commands were entirely heavy and unbearable. Indeed, I have already described your **grievances** <term>grauamina: formal complaints or legal objections filed by the Estates against the Emperor</term> above—those which you called your grievances, but which actually lay upon our necks and were imposed by you. 

If, however, you believe that you were too heavily burdened by them, you may cast the load off from yourselves and roam freely and unencumbered outside the Roman Empire. And if any understanding remains in you, do not come back into the Empire, lest you be burdened with new loads; for within the Roman Empire, that just obedience—to which you have become entirely unaccustomed—is required.

As for what concerns the **Heidelberg counsels** <note>Refers to the political strategies and diplomatic maneuvers originating from the Electoral Palatinate in Heidelberg, a center of Calvinist opposition</note>, it is now well known to you how they are being criticized by everyone. A good portion of them are attributed to you, and you have well deserved such judgment and the disgrace of common rumor in Germany <note>original: "Teutschland"</note>, because you much preferred to be highly regarded abroad than to remain unknown at home. 

We, indeed, judge you—since you are ruined regardless—not by the outcome, but rather by your reckless beginning; because from such a violent impulse, nothing other than such an outcome can follow. Although in former times some **heathen kings** <note>original: "Heydnische Könige"</note> seized foreign lands and empires for themselves, did they for that reason achieve their intent or possess them peacefully for a long time? Christian princes, however, should rightly have a loathing for robbing others of their realms. 

Beyond this, I can find no reason why you advised such a deed, unless perhaps it happened because you yourself are a **bad Christian**; which I will gladly believe to please you, if you call yourself a bad Christian. But if such plots have been discussed among you for seven years now, then the other counselors who were before you were just like you. Indeed, you yourself confess—which we otherwise could not have extracted from you even through great **torture** <note>original: "Marter"</note>—that for so many years now you have been pregnant with our destruction and directed all your deliberations toward it. We, however, wish to show ourselves grateful in return: and although for the greater part of these four years we have repaid you in kind with force and military power, nevertheless, if we still owe you anything further, you can...

remind us of this; we want to pay you in cash: according to the proverb, he who gives quickly, gives double. You have cooked your counsels against us far too slowly, and just as you have cooked and brooded so slowly, so too have you been slow to execute and carry them out; you are still waiting for that long-absent Fortune, just as the Jews wait for their Messiah. Regarding the distinguished Imperial Council, which you praise so highly, they have stung you shamefully—though out of gross lack of understanding you refuse to notice it—namely (according to my interpretation) that everything cited in the *Anhalt Chancery* <note>The "Anhalt Chancery" refers to a published collection of secret documents belonging to Christian of Anhalt, which the Catholics used as propaganda to prove a Protestant conspiracy.</note> is mere, void, worthless foolishness. If the reader believes these are newly spun plots, they were in fact begun long ago, even during the lifetime of Duke Casimir <note>John Casimir of the Palatinate-Simmern (1543–1592), a staunch Calvinist leader.</note>; the Calvinists have by no means lacked the will or spirit to start new unrest, but have only lacked the opportunity. This is the correct understanding of the Imperial Council, through which you Calvinists can be appropriately recognized: as those who are notorious not only for the magnitude of your misdeeds but also for the long duration of time you have spent pursuing them.

# That the United <term>Unirten: Members of the Protestant Union</term>, especially the Count Palatine

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the word "men," likely referring to "Böhmen" (Bohemians) or "nehmen" (to take), establishing the context of the ongoing political and military defense of Bohemia.</meta>

The consideration to be seen here comprises two noble parts: First, a proposal pertaining to the military campaign, namely how the Bohemian military forces are to be used for defense and distributed to various locations. Indeed, this is a private proposal, mixed in <term>obiter</term> <note>Latin: "by the way" or "incidentally"</note> merely within a familiar missive. This should not, and cannot, be interpreted—contrary to the author's intention or the express letter of the text—as if they had, out of great ignorance, begrudged the Bohemians a fair, secure, and lasting peace. For according to the opinion of the famous military commander Hannibal, found in the writings of Livy: *A certain peace is much better and safer than a victory for which one still stands in hope.* <note>original: "beym Liuio, ein gewisser Freund viel besser / vnd sicherer sey / dann die Victori, deren man noch in hoffnung stehet." The translator notes the author likely meant "Fried" (peace) rather than "Freund" (friend) based on the Latin context of Livy.</note> Indeed, at that time there was much more a concern that the Bohemians, in their state of defense, might be overtaken unawares by an error, thereby handing the victory to the enemy along with the loss of their religion and freedom—as subsequently occurred.

In the second part, there is a particular <term>cautela</term> <note>Latin: "cautionary provision" or "legal safeguard"</note> which the Bohemians were found to use in grasping for a gracious truce. Regarding this, the author on page 60 adds this parenthesis: *(at this point one can see again what the United <note>Referring to the Protestant Union</note> sought under the beautiful cloak of a gracious truce).* 

However, this is all a twofold error: Firstly, that he believes the gracious suspension of arms, or truce, was proposed by the Elector Palatine <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King"</note> or his councillors for the benefit of the Bohemians and to the prejudice of the opposing side. Since it is beyond all doubt (as the records testify, and the councillors of several princes will not deny) that the Elector Palatine did not proffer, propose, or see such a suspension and gracious truce, but rather the same was brought forward by others. 

The second error is that the author suspects (for he does not know for certain) that something dangerous against the Imperial Majesty was sought under such a truce. Since it is well known that at that time the Bohemians had a strong military force at hand, and therefore the aforementioned truce was all the more harmful to them, but useful to the opposing side to strengthen themselves in the meantime and to practice all sorts of schemes under such a pretense; for these reasons, it was highly desired by them. 

It is thus manifest how this parenthesis was dragged in by force, as well as through error and ignorance of all the circumstances. As for the rest of the mentioned letter—namely, that the Imperialists hope to obtain much more through such a gracious truce than through open violence, and therefore might perhaps allow themselves to accept the peace treaty—this is to be understood as referring to the simulated and fabricated, deceitful peace negotiations. These were accepted so that during such time the Bo-

### F A B I V S.

In vain do you undertake to catch me with your perverse words / for I am already accustomed to your deceit / and although your words / according to your custom / are larded with fraud and falsehood / you may not through them bring the rope over my horns <note>A proverbial expression meaning "to lead someone by the nose" or "to trick someone."</note>. We have written with truth / and write it still / that you never directed your counsels toward making peace in Bohemia / but rather took every opportunity from that same unrest to awaken new war / and in case this occasion had failed you / you would have awakened the like for yourselves: In support of this, we wish here to examine the writing of the Count of Solms / the Grand Steward <term>Großhoffmeister: the highest administrative official of a princely court</term> at Heidelberg / and Privy Councilor: wherein he writes in the year 1618 / under the date of the 25th of December / while Emperor Matthias was still alive / among other things: If the Elector of Saxony refers himself to the Duke of Bavaria / it is to be feared / that he of Bavaria / might finally be moved to make peace (which the Emperor highly desires) and the Count Palatine <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King."</note> would be more vehemently pressed. From this it is to be seen / how much your crafty hearts feared peace / in that you falsely simulated peace / which you nevertheless highly hate. Upon this we will [examine] Christopher Dona / who is likewise fed with Calvinist grips <note>A metaphor for deceptive tactics or maneuvers.</note> / [who] in the thought year and month / on the fifth day of the same / writes: he had read to the Count Palatine the writing of the Prince of Anhalt / in which he opines that things in Bohemia are proceeding happily / and that so soon no peace would come. These words of Anhalt and Dona you can [examine] yourself / and will find / how they both opine / that this is a happy state of affairs in Bohemia / when all hope for peace is either abolished / or would be drawn out into a long duration: All to the end / because war is so pleasant / lovely / and desired by you from the heart. Thus the Calvinists who came from Heidelberg to Prague / were accustomed to address the people in their sermons in the following manner:

"Well then, you dear Bohemians / cast off all obedience toward the King / take heart / show your magnanimity / reach for your weapons / and follow your one-eyed / and afterwards entirely blind Colonel Ziska <note>Jan Žižka (c. 1360–1424), the famous Hussite military leader known for his tactical genius despite his blindness.</note> / who was an enemy of the [Catholic] religion / a destroyer of the Fatherland / after whose example attack your Lord and King with war and sword / hunt all peace far from you / so long as you shall persist in your fury / madness / and rebellion..."

things will go happily and well for you. In the meantime, we want to hold the Emperor off with good words, dazzle him, and—along with other Princes—lead him around by the nose. We will exhort you to reverence, obedience, and humility toward them and your King; we will admonish you to turn away from all war and toward peace, and employ other such reasons and hypocrisies—not with the intention that you should understand us <note>i.e., take the calls for peace literally</note>, but so that the Catholics, our and your common enemies, may not understand us. By this means alone can they be overcome: when their conquerors and enemies are unknown to them. Let us keep everything secret and hidden, so that before we undertake anything publicly, they already truly feel our attack and deeds; for otherwise, even a swift deception is easily revealed.

You would indeed like, Plessis <note>Refers to Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader</note>, to attribute your actions and dealings to other people, but your deeds are much sharper and better known; therefore, you apply all your intellect and wit to invent a different meaning for them. In your rebellious letter which you sent to the man from Anhalt <note>Christian I, Prince of Anhalt-Bernburg</note> in favor of your comrades in Bohemia, against Emperor Matthias of most blessed memory, you have primarily either entirely denied two distinct points or attempted to force them into another meaning by the hair <note>An idiom meaning to force a logic that doesn't fit</note>. Now, if you wish to be so *familiar*, friendly, and sociable while you spit out all kinds of weapons of war—pikes, halberds, muskets, and large pieces of artillery—against the Emperor, I desire your friendship and company not at all, and would much rather do without it. In that little word *incidentally*, you pass over the truth "incidentally," just as you say such advice was written by you in passing. We, however, understand your writings neither against the clear letter nor against your own opinion and *intent* <note>original: "Intent"</note>; we understand from them how you wrote such hostile practices against the Imperial Majesty, because you have shown yourself to be an enemy of the Imperial Majesty. You also, without doubt, wished for peace for the Bohemians, but in no other way than if the Emperor's government and rule over the Crown of Bohemia were suppressed and His Majesty were entirely driven out from there; this would be, to you, a fair and lasting peace. And indeed, if you had—according to the little proverb cited from Hannibal—regarded a certain peace as better and more secure than an uncertain victory which one must still hope for in the future, you would have advised the Bohemians to accept peace much more than to [proceed] with...

violent opposition and open war, you should further irritate and provoke His Imperial Majesty <note>Latin: *Kayserliche Mayestät*</note> and bring greater danger upon the land. But what kind of freedom and privileges were then in danger, I cannot see; indeed, the madness and fury of the Calvinists <note>A reference to the Reformed Protestant movement, often used pejoratively here by the Catholic or Lutheran author</note> want more freedom, place, and room to spread their insanity. The Bohemians lacked nothing other than good understanding and reason. Even if some injustice had befallen them, why should it concern you, a private and, in that place, insignificant person? If the welfare of another land was so important to you, why did you advise them of such a great deficiency (as you call it), or why did you not show them a better and more useful way to seek what they desired peacefully and in patience? For he who seeks his demands with open violence commonly does not trust in the Law; it is also a great vice that subjects wish to hide their rebellious spirits and demands under the lovely name of freedom.

Your other loophole is the author's parenthesis, in which he admonishes the reader to observe how careful and peace-loving you people of Heidelberg <note>Refers to the court of the Elector Palatine in Heidelberg</note> wished to appear until all unrest was amicably settled. Thus, the Count Palatine's <term>Pfaltzgraff</term> <note>The Elector Palatine, Frederick V</note> desire and wish are evident from this, even though it does not agree with your words. > We doubt whether the Bohemians will be moved to a truce unless this suspension is established on both sides for a period of time, as happened a few years ago in Austria; to which end we then, as we also did with His Imperial Majesty through our...

The delegates have had this brought forward, wishing to show every willing promotion of the cause. Your heart undoubtedly writes this with truth, which makes your lies manifest: the Author has written correctly that you have pretended to seek a suspension of the war with words, while you were nevertheless constantly preparing for war. This was done all the more—as you yourself admit—because the Bohemians were then well-prepared for war, and therefore any delay would have been harmful to them; meanwhile, the Emperor <note>Holy Roman Emperor Ferdinand II</note> could bring about greater assistance to the great danger of the Bohemians.

In addition to this, you presume to fortify the error and ignorance that you attribute to the Author with a new lie. In your letter cited in the Anhalt Chancery, you state among other things: It is to be feared that even if the Bohemians do not lay down their arms, the Emperor will not reject the <term>Composition<note>A legal settlement or mediated peace agreement</note></term>, because he hopes to achieve more through it than through open violence. How "praiseworthy" your affection, loyalty, and obedience toward your sovereign, His Imperial Majesty, truly is, can be judged by everyone; for you are deeply distressed by the fortunate state of His Imperial Majesty and fear that his affairs might continue to prosper, while on the other hand, you assist his enemies with counsel and deed. 

However, so that you might appear to be doing too much for both sides in the meantime, you trim your words somewhat differently, as if you had said: "When I wrote that the Emperor hopes to obtain more through an amicable settlement than through open military force, I understood this only as a deceptive settlement." Rather, I understand that you set down your words deceptively; you have also carried great anxiety that His Imperial Majesty might accept peace, whereby your malicious hopes would come to nothing. And indeed, if His Imperial Majesty believed he would receive better benefit from peace, how could he then have simulated peace, unless he were eager for war and hoped for better benefit from it than from peace? If you were of a sincere mind, you would speak freely and roundly without doubtful, double-tongued words, and not—as you do now—place everything on uncertainty and <term>screws<note>A metaphor for "twisting" words or using evasive language</note></term>, so that you yourself cannot understand your words; although in such a case, we only learn to understand your craftiness and doubtful words all the better.

# FABIUS

It is indeed well known in what manner the settlement of the Bohemian disorder was handed over to the mentioned Electors and Princes to be negotiated <note>original: "transigiren"</note>. However, the reason why His Electoral Highness in Bavaria <note>Maximilian I, Duke of Bavaria, a key Catholic leader.</note> did not wish to undertake such a heavy burden is unknown to you. Perhaps His Electoral Highness (whose purity of intent is better known to neither you nor others) foresaw that not only the Bohemians, but also the Heidelberg Calvinists—whom peace never pleased unless the Bohemians thereby obtained their nonsensical desires, which match the Calvinists' own fanaticism—would resist and not give way to fairness. Furthermore, such a delay by His Electoral Highness in Bavaria was very agreeable to you; you would have lamented it much more had His Electoral Highness hurried, because your actions would have been blocked thereby. You indeed had peace in your mouth, but in your hand you carried the sword and open war, which always pleased you better, as the way to obtain the Crown of Bohemia was opened through it. I have also already mentioned above that His Imperial Majesty wisely rejected entrusting the settlement to the Austrian Estates and other Confederates, as they would have established such a peace as they themselves wanted, whereby the masters would have become servants, and the servants masters. I wonder, however, why you did not likewise offer your services in this matter: although you were perhaps sent to Bethlen Gabor <note>Prince of Transylvania and opponent of the Habsburgs.</note> for that reason, to negotiate a general peace, since you are indeed His Imperial Majesty’s

You are not an enemy, and it is sufficient that you make others into such enemies.

   Just as children, when they cannot answer for themselves, bring forward all sorts of questions to trouble others, so it is with you. Did not His Imperial Majesty offer peace to the Bohemians? Why was Renius dispatched to them from Vienna? Did he come to Prague like a special herald to renounce them? What you mention here regarding the Pope and the King of Spain, I can hardly understand; I have scarcely seen a snake that entangles itself in more twists than you do in your words. Or perhaps you wished to say that because the Spaniard and the Pope did not show themselves so earnestly toward this war, they gave the Bohemians all the greater courage, so that they allowed themselves to be moved to peace all the less.

   However, so that the favorable reader may better understand the aim of the aforementioned letter, it should be known that *Balthasar de Zuniga* <note>A Spanish diplomat and influential advisor to Philip III.</note>, who was in Prague many years before the Bohemian unrest, wrote to the late King of Spain following certain improprieties: that the Kingdom of Bohemia stood in great danger, and if the Estates thereof should perhaps elevate one from their own midst, or if the Kingdom should fall into the power of one of the Protestant Electors, great danger would eventually arise for the Netherlands provinces, the County of Burgundy, and finally also the Duchy of Milan and the Kingdom of Naples. Von Plessen was perhaps aware of this letter and therefore mentioned it in his missive, yet in a much different sense and in a different manner than is set down here by the author. Since from the French original to which one refers, such a meaning clearly appears: that according to the above-stated report of *Don Balthasar de Zuniga*, it was of such great importance to the King of Spain to keep the Crown of Bohemia with the House of Austria, and therefore it is to be presumed that the King would apply **green and dry** <note>original: "verd & le ser" (vert et le sec). ## FABIUS.

The more you strive to reveal your intentions more clearly / the less I can understand you : nor can I know from where you obtained the copy of such a letter from Zuniga <note>Don Baltasar de Zúñiga, a Spanish diplomat and influential advisor</note> to the King in Spain : or perhaps you had already begun at that time to intercept other people's letters / which / if done by you / was a blasphemous and wicked act. But even assuming / that everything was written by Zuniga to his King exactly as you report / it still cannot benefit your shaky cause: especially since you have undertaken every hostility and violent action against the Imperial Majesty in one way or another / and have committed <term>treason</term> <note>original: "crimen laesae maiestatis" — the crime of violating majesty or high treason</note> / in that you have not only incited the Bohemians to all manner of madness and military violence / but have also instructed and directed them. Even if the King in Spain was eager for peace / because it was directed to the benefit of the Imperial Majesty / and also convenient for the future / to better subdue the wild Bohemian horse / how can you say / that the Imperial Majesty was not serious about a settlement? For no one seeks such a peace in that manner / in order to enjoy it / just as I am likewise feigning / when I am not serious in my question to you.

A common proverb in everyday use says: *Negotiations do not suspend arms* <note>original: "Le prattiche non sospendono le armi" (Italian) and "tractationes arma non suspendant" (Latin)</note>, meaning that amicable discussions do not halt the progress of war. This was proven by the outcome in this very place. For when the amicable negotiations failed to proceed, and the King of Spain <note>Philip III</note> took notice of this, he sent powerful assistance in both money and troops to His Imperial Majesty <note>Ferdinand II</note> against the Bohemians. This aid was promoted with great diligence by the aforementioned Don Balthasar Zúñiga <note>A prominent Spanish diplomat and advisor</note>—who had sent the previously mentioned letters some years ago—following his return to Spain as a distinguished member of the Royal Spanish Council.

->FABIUS<-

Regarding your speech, my dear Squire Pless—God knows what kind of color you try to paint it with—it is seditious and greatly contrary to His Imperial Majesty. Neither the Bohemians nor the Unionists <note>The Protestant Union</note> had anything to fear from the Spaniard, especially at a time when he preferred peace over war. But when the Bohemians refused to listen to any reasonable terms, and the warlike god Mars stormed the doors of justice, only then did the King of Spain provide assistance. If you, Plessy, had intended to say that, it would have been well spoken; however, you have written poorly in teaching that the Bohemians took up arms against His Imperial Majesty while setting peace aside.

->PLESSIUS<-

Concerning Pope Paul V (who is being discussed here), it is certain that at that time, and right at the beginning of the war when Plessy's letter was written, he did not have a very great desire to contribute to the Catholic League. This was because he had been <unclear>advised</unclear> and had understood from the letters of the Prague Capuchins <note>A reform branch of the Franciscan order</note> that the Roman Catholics in Bohemia, both clergy and laity, were not being burdened in the slightest by the new King <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King"</note>, nor were they being offended in the performance of their divine services. However, when he was subsequently left with no peace, and told it would cause a great scandal if he were to abandon the Catholics in Germany, he finally promised to contribute 20,000 crowns monthly. Yet, he could not be persuaded in the slightest to reach any deeper into his treasury in the Castle of Sant' Angelo <note>The papal fortress and treasury in Rome</note>. Nevertheless, to show some favor to the Catholics in the Holy Roman Empire, he imposed certain tithes <note>A 10% tax</note> upon the lower clergy, because of which they then (as is seen outwardly)...
