# FABIUS.

arose due to the prohibition of the **Augsburg Confession** <note>The primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church, presented in 1530.</note>. Therefore, the pious old gentleman, **Lazarus von Schwendi**, counselor to Emperor Maximilian in the year 1572, gave advice—foretelling like a prophet all the things that have occurred in Austria in these recent years—and thus faithfully warned His Most High Imperial Majesty against such harmful uprisings. Where, then, were the **United** <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states formed in 1608.</note> and their leaders hidden fifty-two years ago, to whom the Compiler <note>The author of the document being refuted by this text.</note> attributes the beginning and origin of all the unrest that has arisen in Austria and neighboring provinces? As for the **Bohemians**, their old bitterness has been evident for many years now, and it is publicly known that they made various military preparations for their defense; they also established an alliance with neighboring kingdoms and provinces with the knowledge and consent of **Emperor Matthias**. It is likewise notorious <term>notori: well-known or a matter of public record</term> that the recent uprisings arose only from righteous pain and patience transformed into fury, so that nothing premeditated can be alleged here. To which end **Simeon Partlitz von Spizberg**, in his book which he titled *Mundum furiosum* <note>original: "Mundum furiosum" — The Raging World</note>, says:

> The Bohemians, who in earlier times were conquered by Emperor Rudolf I, have fallen away under Matthias and Ferdinand because of the arrogance of the Presidents.

These words, "because of the arrogance of the Presidents," are well worth noting: in this case, the same thing happened to the Bohemians that previously befell the **Netherlanders**. Following burdensome and overly heated counsel, the desperation of the people and finally the defection itself followed, as **Thuanus** <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou (1553–1617), a French historian known for his monumental history of his own times.</note> beautifully discourses upon this in the preface to his *Histories*. Beyond this, the cause of such Bohemian disorder is noted quite plainly in a short Latin writing by **Cardinal Klesl** <note>Melchior Khlesl (1553–1630), a powerful bishop and chancellor to Emperor Matthias.</note> (which everyone at the Imperial Court has at hand):

> That Klesl gave the Bohemians cause to riot. Indeed, further on, these words are expressly to be found in the *Response to the points spread in Rome against His Highness of Bavaria* <note>original: "Nella risposta à ponti sparsi in Roma contra S. A. di Bauiera"</note> under the last article: namely, that the Emperor's officers, either in Bohemia or at the Imperial Court, were the principal causes of the Bohemian tumult and calamity, and that they vainly wish to accuse other innocent people of it.

Nevertheless, the Compiler, being little mindful of all this, wishes—out of forgetfulness, I suppose—to load such guilt upon

->the necks of the United.<-

# FABIUS.

That the Calvinists have been the cause of the manifold unrest that has arisen throughout Europe until now, you refute with no other argument than the youth of your Union <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states formed in 1608</note>, which has lasted only a very short time. But, my dear Plessy <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a prominent French Protestant figure often called the "Huguenot Pope"</note>, those tumults over which we fought with one another in the Anhalt Chancery <note>Refers to the "Anhaltische Kanzlei," a collection of secret diplomatic documents seized after the Battle of White Mountain which exposed the Palatinate's interventionist plans</note> are much younger: for it has been only six years since this raging war publicly emerged from the Calvinist dungeon and opened its doors by force. The Austrians, of whom you speak, have without doubt impatiently desired more license for a long time prior; but because it was not beneficial to the common wealth, such was not permitted to them. Regarding Mr. Lazarus von Schwendi <note>A famous 16th-century imperial general and diplomat</note>, he was indeed well-versed in matters of war, but inexperienced in religion; likewise, his prophecies did not reach their hoped-for conclusion. What he considered good, he also wished for others, because no tumult arose from the boundary stone set for the new religion, but rather because the reins were allowed to slacken too much—especially where, through clever secrecy, the loathsome Calvinism gradually crept in. And from this, such calamity arose: here it became Raging: here it stirred up a highly damaging and ruinous war, especially when you, Plessy, bravely and freely sounded the alarm. And it is precisely through you that this Bohemian blaze has been maintained for these six entire years, and its sparks were sown here and there by the Palatine Councilors.

However, for several years now, the Bohemians have carried a desire for war and provided themselves with instruments of war; indeed, they established alliances with the provinces bordering the kingdom, which Emperor Matthias knew well and permitted them, as you say. What advantage, Plessy, do you gain from that? Are the Bohemians to be esteemed as heroes because of this? Or, as I might better say, have they long been rebels? That the Emperor knew this well is not unknown to me either; that he also permitted it, I likewise know very well. It would be better for you if we knew less of you: for one often permits many things which cannot always be averted. Yes, you have also—with the prior knowledge and permission of this Emperor—recruited a large army for his destruction, which he also permitted you for a time. But when he found himself well-prepared, he struck the sacrilegious <note>original: "Kirchenrauberische," literally "church-robbing" or "sacrilegious"</note> sword from your hands, and the tame lions to the...

taught to obey the Eagle <note>The Imperial Eagle, symbol of the Holy Roman Empire</note>. But, my dear **Pleß**, among whom—to use your own words—was it ever notorious that such a rebellion arose from "justified pain"? Namely, among those who are servants of such Fury, and among whom you, **Pleß**, were the colonel, or at the very least, not the least among them. Is it a "lawful concern" when one is required to hear the lawful judgment of the Presidents, to release oneself from the obedience of another, and to yield to customary justice? I only laugh at your **Simeon von Spißberg**, and at you yourself, who boast of such a great witness. For he says the Bohemians departed from their bounden obedience because of the arrogance of the Presidents: *Therefore*. You say the same: *Therefore*. But I deny it: *Therefore*: and the Presidents themselves deny it: *Therefore*. What shall come of this now? If you hold the upper hand with such an argument, then we hold it too. Indeed, we all the more so, because you assert it, while we, on the contrary, deny it. You require proof, yet you have none; but we, so that we lack nothing in that regard, do have it, because we—not you—were the ones injured and insulted. 

We descended from the Prague City Hall without the use of the stairs <note>A sarcastic reference to the Defenestration of Prague (May 23, 1618), where Catholic regents were thrown out of a window</note>; we had to leave our property in the lurch; we were driven into misery; we suffered persecution and would have had to suffer the very worst, had not God the righteous Avenger and the invincible hand of a lawful war stood by us. 

But now, your little book—in which **Cardinal Khlesl** is accused of supposedly giving the Bohemians cause to riot—is entirely suspicious and repugnant to me, because it was undoubtedly written by a <term>Calvinist</term> <note>A follower of John Calvin; used here as a derogatory label for the religious faction opposing the Catholics</note>, who would act quite foolishly if he wanted to shift the cause of such a war onto his own people. **Cardinal Khlesl** was in Hungary during that restless time when the Royal Councilors in Prague were plunged out of the windows, and he heard of your Fury by hearsay, but not by deed. 

As for what response was given in Rome to the speeches running contrary to the **Duke of Bavaria**, that is unknown to me, but it matters little. Do not seek to prove the injustice done to you with vain <term>Scartecken</term> <note>original: "Scartecken" - a term for worthless pamphlets or scurrilous writings</note> and sought-out words, but rather consider the matter itself somewhat better. When the newly-begun work was forbidden to you at **Brünn** <note>Brno</note>, in the territory of another Lord, you became indignantly enraged at such a just judgment and interpreted it as an injustice, because you sought innovation and, by every opportunity, took great pleasure in war.

# PLESSIUS.

As for what finally concerns the Hungarians—who would dare to suggest that Bocskai <note>Stephen Bocskai (1557–1606), Prince of Transylvania, who led an uprising against the Habsburgs.</note> was incited by the United <note>The "United" likely refers to the Protestant Union or the allied estates.</note> to rise up against the tyrants Georg Basta and Belgiojoso? <note>Giorgio Basta and Giacomo Barbiano di Belgiojoso were Imperial generals known for their brutal methods in Hungary.</note> Is it not rather known throughout the entire Kingdom of Hungary that the aforementioned Basta’s and Belgiojoso’s Machiavellian <note>A reference to Niccolò Machiavelli; used here to imply cynical, ruthless, and amoral political power.</note> rule gave Bocskai and the Hungarians the cause to take up arms and to wring fair conditions of peace from the Austrians? What a grand, intentional, shameless thing and injustice it is, then, that people now want to attribute solely to the United—through slander—that calamity which was caused in Bohemia by the arrogance of the Presidents and their burdensome, overly-heated counsel; by the prohibition of the Evangelical Exercise <note>The practice of Protestant worship.</note> in Austria; and by the Machiavellian, immoderate desire to rule in Hungary? But we shall now proceed to the most prominent article and main subject of the Bavarian-Anhaltine Chancery.

### FABIUS.

How imprudently you bring these examples to light, Plessius! For Georg Basta and Belgiojoso, who were perhaps somewhat too strict as military commanders, only took up the war when the territories of the Empire were being touched. Truly, the beginning of the Hungarian rebellion was just like the Bohemian uprising. The Calvinists, who could not patiently endure the yoke of their hearts but instead strived for immoderate freedom, filled everything with weapons and the terror of the same. Bocskai raised the "blood-banner" for you Calvinists, who were already pregnant with war. For whatever riots have been stirred up within the last sixty years, you yourselves have been either the causes or the instigators. The rebellious *Bocskai* <note>Stephen Bocskai (1557–1606), Prince of Transylvania, who led an uprising against the Habsburgs.</note> was very well inclined and devoted, and this was not without particular distress to those who were aware of their own guilt. This also troubled the *Chamberlain* <note>original: "Camerarium"; likely referring to a high-ranking administrative official.</note> not without reason, as seen from his letter which he sent from *Wolfenbüttel* on the 4th of *March*, *Year 1606*, to his Princes. This letter, which one of my good friends translated into the Latin language, reads as follows:

> Most Serene Elector, my most humble and ready service to Your Electoral Grace beforehand. Gracious Elector and Lord, I recognize myself as being entirely bound to write in all haste to Your Electoral Grace what the Serene Duke of *Brunswick* has had his Chancellor indicate to me: namely, that a few days ago an Imperial Envoy named *Anthoni Schlieff*, through Imperial intercession and public *letters patent* <note>term: "Patenten" - official documents or warrants granting a right or privilege.</note>, obtained so much from the *Magistrate* of the city of *Northeim* (in the *Brunswick territory*, located no further than 8 miles from here) that he took into captive custody *Johannes Bocatius* <note>A Hungarian poet and diplomat loyal to Bocskai.</note> from *Kaschau* in Upper *Hungary*, along with his companion. The aforementioned *Captain*, in the presence of several members of the Council, *examined* <note>interrogated</note> them, recorded all their belongings, sealed them, and kept them with him. Your Electoral Grace may learn more of this from the attached letters marked *A.B.C.D.E.* (which were *communicated* to me and which I have transcribed in all haste). However, since I have gathered from the completed *examination*, as well as from the *inventory* itself, that Your Electoral Grace is significantly *involved* <note>original: "interessiret"; implying legal or political entanglement.</note> in this, and that such matters—in these difficult times and dangerous course of events—could result in particular harm and shameful disadvantage to you, especially because such matters have already spread further, and the present Imperial *Commissioners*, along with others, have spread various and inconsistent rumors about the Envoy, this has troubled me not a little. It remains a matter of great concern to me at this time, so that Your Electoral Grace might not fall into harm thereby. I have therefore inquired with the highly esteemed Duke of *Brunswick*, asking that he would not only reveal to me what should be done in this matter, but I have also further diligently petitioned His Princely Grace (as I have heard for certain that there is a very insistent demand upon the Council at *Northeim* that the aforementioned *Bocatius* be transported to *Prague*) that he should, without any delay, make arrangements so that the accused would either not be sent from there at all, or at the very least not before I have reported the whole affair in detail to Your Electoral Grace and received a reply from the same. And although His Princely Grace is exceedingly busy with the confused affairs of *Brunswick*, so much so that he hears and speaks of almost nothing else, he nevertheless gave me the answer that he [would] not fail to do what would serve this cause...

<meta>This page continues the diplomatic correspondence regarding the detention of Johannes Bocatius, a scholar and diplomat caught in the crossfire of imperial politics. The previous context likely involved his initial arrest in Northeim.</meta>

# Report on the Detention of Bocatius

He wished to state that even if the Council of Northeim were inclined to hand over the prisoner Bocatius <note>Johannes Bocatius (1569–1621), a humanist poet and diplomat serving the Hungarian cause</note>, he would nonetheless necessarily have to be escorted through Her Princely Grace’s territory, which could not occur without Her Princely Grace’s consent. In the meantime, I have further understood that the Council of Northeim has the power not only to arrest in criminal matters but also to carry out executions; however, for a full legal determination of the case, Her Princely Grace’s presence and consent are required. And indeed, because the matter is still unresolved, the Council will apply all possible diligence to maintain their rights and justice during the processing of the legal records. They would not otherwise have complied with the Captain <note>likely a military official acting on behalf of the Emperor</note> so hastily—considering that neither the evidence nor the law itself supports him <note>original: "ihme Patrociniret," meaning to advocate for or support his legal position</note>—in taking Bocatius into captive custody. In my opinion, said Magistrate would not have detained the frequently mentioned, ensnared Bocatius as a favor to the Emperor, had the favorable opportunity to exercise such judicial authority not presented itself at that time.

Furthermore, I do not believe that the Magistrate of Northeim will escape unpunished, because he not only imprisoned Bocatius based on meager evidence but also excluded the District Administrator <term>Amptmann: a high-ranking regional official representing the sovereign's interests</term> of Northeim from the interrogation. I hope, therefore, that the Illustrious Prince will make arrangements so that the matter might be postponed until the accused is sent back again. Additionally, I have humbly petitioned the Princely Councilors—and will not cease to do so further—to obtain from Her Princely Grace herself, as well as her Councilors, an assurance that Bocatius and his associates are not handed over to His Imperial Majesty; although I have not the slightest doubt that all the legal records have already been dispatched to Prague <note>The seat of the Imperial court under Rudolf II</note>.

Given the nature of these circumstances, Your Electoral Grace will now very easily perceive whether a letter should be written to His Imperial Majesty and the Elector of Brandenburg. This would be to investigate—both in Your Electoral Grace’s letters to the Elector of Brandenburg, to Bocskai <note>Stephen Bocskai, Prince of Transylvania and leader of an anti-Habsburg uprising</note>, and to the Hungarian Estates, whose resolution they delivered to Bocatius and where all matters were found in the original documents—whether perhaps something secret was found in them which might be shared with the Duke of Brunswick. Your Electoral Grace should then decide without delay what should be written to the aforementioned Duke in the meantime, and what should be requested of him. However, this must happen without any delay; for that reason, I have hurried so in my writing, and I hope that because of such diligence, I may not be drawn into any suspicion.

Finally, I have written to the highly esteemed Duke of *Brunswick* <note>original: "Braunschweig"</note> regarding this opportunity, and have requested a more gracious resolution of our common affairs, specifically concerning the *Hungarians* as well as the day of our assembly prior to the Imperial Diet <term>Reichstag: the deliberative assembly of the Holy Roman Empire</term>. Indeed, I could wish for nothing else than that your *Electoral Grace* <note>A formal title for a Prince-Elector of the Empire</note> would promote the matter and omit nothing; this shall occur when you propose these matters to your counselors and write their opinion back to me. All of which I did not wish to hide from your *Electoral Grace* in humble submission: humbly commending the same to the highest Divine protection for joyful physical health and a long-lasting reign. Dated *Wolfenbüttel*, the 4th of *March* 1616.

->*Your Electoral Grace’s*<-
->*Most Obedient*<-
->*Ludwig Camerarius.*<- <note>A prominent advisor to the Elector Palatine and a key figure in Protestant diplomacy leading up to the Thirty Years' War.</note>

Thus far *Camerarius*; which is a public indication that *Bocatius* <note>Ioannes Bocatius, a Hungarian scholar and diplomat</note> brought such an answer to *Hungary* which *favored* <note>original: "fauorisirt"</note> the rebels, but was on the other hand highly damaging to the *Imperial Majesty* and the commonwealth. For why was *Camerarius* so greatly afraid? To what end did the great diligence applied to the Duke of *Brunswick* serve, that he presumed to block the public road to the *Imperial Majesty*—who desired a harmful man—and to open it to a traitor? But that he exerted himself so greatly in vain was publicly proven by the unfortunate outcome, in which all writings, as well as *Bocatius* himself, were sent to *Prague*, where everything was then discovered. Accordingly, as this matter was also of the highest concern to the *Count Palatine*, he wrote to the Prince of *Anhalt*, Governor at *Amberg*, with orders to investigate all the rumors circulating in *Prague*; just as this Prince, according to his custom, diligently fished out the confession of the ensnared *Bocatius* and wrote of it on the 9th of *April* in the year 1606 to his *Count Palatine*, just as it was transferred to me by my aforementioned good friend.

Beyond this, it was written to us from *Prague* that a distinguished *Italian* recounted, among other things, how he had understood from the Imperial counselors—under the rose <note>original: "vnder der Rosen" (sub rosa); a Latin-derived idiom meaning "in confidence" or "secretly"</note>, as they say—that among the writings of *Bocatius* the following were also found: as if *Your Lordship* had given counsel to your master (namely *Bocskai* <note>Stephen Bocskai, Prince of Transylvania and leader of an anti-Habsburg uprising</note>) that he should not negotiate with the *Imperial Majesty* for the sake of peace, but should continue in the other [course of action].

So far the Prince of Anhalt, from all of which we can very well recognize the alarm-blowing trumpet of the Hungarian uprising. Now Plessis <note>likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader</note> runs further and ascribes such excesses to the gentle and brave military commander Basta <note>Giorgio Basta, an Italian general in Imperial service</note>, and also punishes Belgioioso's <note>Giovanni Giacomo Barbiano di Belgioioso</note> cautious procedure. Because they desired equitable things, they have made the Calvinists their enemies; they would otherwise have been their good friends if they had desired unjust things. But you might say here that the Italian's narratives and accounts were not entirely similar to the truth and were not to be believed. Perhaps, because he knew of some grudge against the Imperial Majesty, he did not hope that you would undertake such cruel things. But that such an Italian prophesied the truth is proven by your fear and anxiety. He who writes honest things that are beneficial to the common wealth does not fear its rulers and Emperor. Therefore, Basta and Belgioioso are relieved of the vice which clings to you Calvinists in this case. You need not reproach us with Machiavelli's practices <note>referring to the political pragmatism of Niccolò Machiavelli</note>, since we would know nothing of them if you yourselves did not teach them to us, and with no other school-fee than the rightful downfall of your affairs.

## The Sixth Chapter.

# That the Electoral Palatinate, by accepting the new Bohemian election, taking actual possession of the Royal Throne in Prague, permitting and receiving the Coronation, and accepting the oath of allegiance, has offended against His Imperial Majesty, and was the great cause of all the misfortune that has arisen in said Kingdom, neighboring provinces, and within the Empire.

### Information and Response.

### PLESSIUS.
<note>Likely referring to Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot diplomat and writer whose works influenced Protestant political thought.</note>

That the Bohemian Kingdom—in the view of the Elector Palatine <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King"</note> and perhaps other Princes and Estates of the Union <term>Protestant Union: a coalition of Protestant German states formed in 1608</term>, as well as the Electoral servants and others—consists in an election and not in an inheritance, and that the right to elect a King (as the Golden Bull states) has been attributed to the Estates of said Kingdom for the reasons explicitly detailed therein, should not appear so strange or "Spanish" <note>A contemporary idiom meaning "foreign" or "incomprehensible," often used pejoratively against the pro-Habsburg/Spanish faction</note> to anyone. And firstly, because both the Hungarian and Polish Chronicles are full of accounts regarding the election of the Kings in Bohemia, just as the Silesian Chronicles and many ancient Bohemian examples testify to the same. But who would have dared to dream previously that "election" is nothing other than any manner of obtaining a kingdom, or who could have imagined that even hereditary succession could encompass the word "election" within itself? Especially since such an interpretation only became known after the great defeat outside Prague <note>The Battle of White Mountain, 1620</note> from the treatise regarding the hereditary rights of the House of Austria, which Sigismundus Latomus printed at Frankfurt in the year 1621.

Whether this interpretation is consistent with Law and History, let the reader judge for themselves, namely when they recall that all kingdoms and principalities are acquired either through election or hereditary succession; also, that election and hereditary right cannot exist simultaneously; indeed, that

<summary>This page presents a legal and historical defense of the Elector Palatine's acceptance of the Bohemian crown, arguing that the Bohemian throne is elective rather than hereditary. It challenges the Habsburg claim of "hereditary succession" by citing historical chronicles and the Golden Bull.</summary>
<keywords>Elector Palatine, Frederick V, Kingdom of Bohemia, elective monarchy, Golden Bull, House of Austria, Protestant Union, Battle of White Mountain, Sigismundus Latomus</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues a dialogue regarding the legal nature of the Bohemian throne, specifically debating whether the kingdom is elective or hereditary, following the previous arguments concerning the rights of the Estates.</meta>

with the assertion of Election, the hereditary right is completely and utterly abolished, and vice versa.

### ->FABIUS<-

We dispute in vain with one another, Plessi <note>Likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot writer whose arguments were used by the Bohemian Estates</note>: since such a disputation does nothing but cast a blue mist <note>An idiom meaning to deceive or hoodwink</note> before the reader's eyes. Even if it were granted to you (so that you may know what useless chatter you engage in) that the Bohemian Kingdom is an elective kingdom, what help does that provide to you and your cause? In an elective kingdom, when the same is vacant, one has good reason to elect; but when it is not vacant, one must elect no one—which, however, the Bohemians have done, through the instigation of you and your ear-whisperers <note>Flatterers or secret agitators</note>. But lest I fail to strike back at your historical weapons with which you stir up this conflict: well then, draw your sword! For I am ready to catch all your arrows, so that I might not have the appearance of being unable to defend myself.

And first, indeed, as you say, the Bohemian, Silesian, Hungarian, and Polish chronicles are filled with the election of the Kings in Bohemia; which I myself do not deny. But they speak, or should speak (as the author of the booklet entitled *Hereditary Justice and Lawful Succession in the Kingdom of Bohemia of Ferdinand the Second* interprets the words of Emperor Frederick) not of the free election by any mortal men, but of the lawful admission and entering into the kingdom according to the laws and previously binding customs. You mockingly laugh at these words, which you have distorted, by saying: "Who could have previously imagined that 'election' should signify every possible way of acquiring the kingdom?" No one has done such a thing, nor acted so foolishly, except for you. The lawful way to obtain the kingdom, according to law and previously practiced custom, is what the aforementioned author called the "Bohemian Election," just as:

> "he says, the hereditary princes are also accepted and received by the provinces,
> "and are led into possession; which acceptance can be called an 'election' 
> *abusively* <note>original: "abusiue" - a legal term meaning the improper or non-literal use of a word</note> and through misuse of the term."

One may see more on this in the aforementioned author, about which you leave the judgment to the reader—and indeed not unwisely, because your argument is worth nothing. But I marvel that you remind that very same reader, and make him believe, that there are only two ways to obtain a principality: namely, through election or hereditary succession; and you yourself meanwhile forget the third way used by you: namely, robbery, which you have made very [well known] throughout all Europe.

<meta>catchword: known</meta>

<summary>Fabius argues against Plessi, claiming that historical "elections" in Bohemia were merely formal ceremonies of acceptance for hereditary heirs, rather than true free elections, and accuses the opposition of seizing power through robbery.</summary>
<keywords>Bohemia, Ferdinand II, elective monarchy, hereditary succession, legal terminology, Thirty Years' War, political propaganda</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues a legal and historical debate regarding the nature of the Bohemian throne—specifically whether it is elective or hereditary. The speaker in the first section is responding to an opponent's narrow definitions of sovereignty.</meta>

you have made known. But now, back to the matter at hand. With what narrow measures do you wish to trap me, so that I might not, through the law, compare these kingdoms in ways other than those you have prescribed? How many principalities have been retained through the right of war? How many have been given away as gifts? For when the Empire was established among the Germans by Charles <note>referring to Charlemagne</note> in former times, the Emperors assigned principalities to virtue, but withdrew them from those of evil character. I will remain silent for now on the fact that provinces can also be exchanged and purchased. Your division is quite childish, as is that which you further add: namely, that "election abolishes hereditary right, and conversely, hereditary right abolishes election." For both can otherwise be mixed, if you wish to interpret "election" somewhat more broadly. For although I may be a natural blood heir in this or another kingdom, I am only so in such a way that I do not rule lawfully unless I have first been accepted through the unanimous voice and justice of the subjects, who by custom have only this right: to love the heir and not to drive him away.

->PLESSIUS<-
<note>Likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot writer and diplomat.</note>

Secondly, because at all times neighboring as well as other kings and princes have striven for the Bohemian Crown and thereby considered it an elective kingdom: thus also the Kings of Poland have, at various times, promoted their sons and brothers to the Bohemian Crown by means of a regular election, as can be seen in the histories of Cromer <note>Martin Cromer (1512–1589), a Polish historian</note> in the description of the lives of Casimir and Sigismund, and elsewhere. Likewise, Charles, the 7th King of France of that name, after the death of King Ladislaus (to whom he had promised his daughter in marriage), dispatched a stately legation <term>legation: a formal diplomatic mission</term> to the Bohemian Estates, who were assembled at that time for the election of a new king. He urged the Estates that they should choose either one of his sons, or at least the one to whom he would marry his daughter; as we read in the Bohemian History of Aeneas Sylvius <note>Aeneas Sylvius Piccolomini, later Pope Pius II</note> and have clear testimony that the King of France considered the Bohemian Kingdom not as a hereditary one, but as an elective kingdom. Nevertheless, Matthias Corvinus, King of Hungary, also exerted himself for so long to obtain the aforementioned Bohemian Crown until he obtained it from Pope Aeneas Sylvius, or Pius II, who—as one to whom Austrian affairs were well known—also [supported] Frederick III and the...

House of Austria, he would never have done so <note>original: "nimmermehr gethan hette"</note> if that Kingdom had been hereditary and subject to the House of Austria. Indeed, it is found in even greater abundance in Jovius <note>Paolo Giovio (1483–1552), a famous Italian historian and physician</note> in his 30th Book, that Duke William of Bavaria desired this very crown alongside Ferdinand I: a fact which should be remembered in Munich all the more often, rather than noting the Elector Palatine’s acceptance of the Kingdom of Bohemia so shamefully. Thirdly, the aforementioned Jovius has left this for memory in his said 30th Book:

> "The Bohemians," he says, "are by no means accustomed to choose a King from among their own natives,
> "but rather from foreign nations, whom they believe to be lovers of virtue and justice, according to ancient custom;
> "so that now to the Hungarians, soon to the Poles, and, as we see in our own day,
> "sometimes even to the Germans, such a Kingdom is offered."

Fourthly, it is found in the confession of Tengnagel <note>Sebastian Tengnagel, counselor to Archduke Leopold and imperial librarian</note>, counselor to Archduke Leopold, which he himself composed in writing and delivered to the Bohemian Estates, that he likewise held Bohemia and Hungary to be <term>elective kingdoms</term> <note>original: "Wahlkönigreiche"; kingdoms where the monarch is chosen by a vote of the nobility rather than by bloodline</note>. Fifthly, Count Palatine Wolfgang Wilhelm (and with him, without any doubt, many other Princes) also held Bohemia to be an elective kingdom, as he addressed His Imperial Majesty in the heading of his congratulatory missive as an *elected King in Bohemia*, and offered his congratulations under the name of that election.

->FABIUS<-

That neighboring Princes strove for the neighboring Kingdom of Bohemia—there were perhaps reasons for that. For otherwise, other kingdoms are often sought after, but they are not thereby made free for the purpose of me being able to claim them for myself: kingdoms are acquired by right, and not by desire. But what you bring to the floor regarding the Kings of Poland, Casimir and Sigismund, there you strike yourself with your own sword. For King Casimir desired the Bohemian Crown in the name of his consort, who was the sister of the deceased King Ladislaus. With the same right of close kinship, King Sigismund also sought after such a crown, because he was Casimir's grandson. But leave aside what you write of Charles, King of France <note>Likely referring to Charles VIII or Francis I's earlier ambitions</note>, since he, as a King inexperienced in Bohemian law, dispatched his ambassadors to apply for the Bohemian Crown.

The character Fabius counters these claims by arguing that such ambitions were based on dynastic kinship rather than the "freedom" of the kingdom to choose any leader.</summary>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues a legal and historical argument regarding the elective versus hereditary nature of the Bohemian crown, specifically addressing the period following the death of King Ladislaus the Posthumous.</meta>

at the suggestion of Zdeněk von Sternberg <note>original: "Snenconis von Sternberg," a prominent Bohemian nobleman and leader of the Catholic nobility</note>, who preferred that a Catholic prince be placed over the fatherland, rather than—as he perhaps feared—that George Poděbrady <note>original: "Georgius Bodiebradius"</note>, the Hussite <note>A follower of the pre-Reformation reformer Jan Hus; the author uses this as a term of religious distinction</note>, a lord famous for his military power and the faction of Rokycana <note>referring to Jan Rokycana, the Hussite Archbishop of Prague</note>, should occupy the royal throne for which he strove. But because you allow French authority to weigh so heavily upon you—which maintains that the Crown of Bohemia should be an elective kingdom—we will instead look to the neighboring princes, who at that same time were of a much different opinion. For King Casimir of Poland, says Dubravius <note>Jan Dubravius, Bishop of Olomouc and historian</note> in his 30th book, to whom King Ladislaus's sister was married, and William, Duke of Saxony, who had the other and elder sister in marriage, asserted that such a kingdom belonged to them by right of succession. Do you see now, Plessis <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot writer whom the author is addressing</note>, that this kingdom was held to be hereditary, and that even Dubravius, Bishop of Olomouc <note>original: "Ollmütz"</note>, was himself of this opinion, as clearly appears from the words in the aforementioned place. But the King of France, he says, desired the Crown of Bohemia in a womanish manner <note>original: "frauvenlich," likely implying a lack of legal/martial right, or perhaps "fraudulently"</note>, not with right and proper claim, but with gold and riches—by which even previously acquired kingdoms are subdued—promising that he would pay all the debts of the kingdom with his own money, and reconquer all places alienated from the kingdom with his own military power.

I would therefore like to see that you, Plessis, take good note here that this King did not desire the Kingdom of Bohemia by right, and that you consider well that such a kingdom is not, as you wish, an elective kingdom <note>The German text says "Erbkönigreich" (hereditary) here, but the context of the argument against Plessis suggests the author is refuting Plessis's claim that it is elective, or correcting a slip in the opponent's logic</note>. For a kingdom which can be given voluntarily and by right to anyone, the same can also be desired freely and by right by anyone. But I have laughed at you for wanting to pull the Roman Pope—a name so hateful to you—onto your side: Pope Aeneas Sylvius, or Pius II, I say, who, as you say, procured the Bohemian kingdom for King Matthias of Hungary; which he certainly would have left undone had he considered it a hereditary kingdom. Yes, indeed, my dear Plessis, because Poděbrady was raised to such a crown by force (while those who desired the kingdom by right were rejected <note>original: "durch den Korb gefallen," literally "fell through the basket"</note>), and persecuted the Catholic religion in Bohemia, the said Pope (who, in such turbulent times, could place little hope in other Bohemian lords) commanded the then-brave war hero, Matthias Corvinus, to pursue the Bohemian King Poděbrady with military force.

<meta>This page continues the legal and historical argument regarding the legitimacy of the Bohemian succession, specifically addressing the transition from Pope Pius II to Paul II and the competing claims of the House of Bavaria against the Habsburgs.</meta>

# Page 108

Upon the deceased Pope *Pius* <note>Pope Pius II, reigned 1458–1464</note>, *Paul* the Second, a more vehement enemy of *Podiebrad* <note>George of Podiebrad, King of Bohemia, whom the Pope excommunicated</note>, soon followed. As *Dubravius* <note>Jan Dubravius (1489–1553), a Czech historian and Bishop of Olomouc</note> testifies in his 30th book of Bohemian Histories, he deprived this rebel against the Holy Empire of his royal dignity, placed the deprived man under the ban, and transferred the Crown to the King of Hungary—even though you repeatedly appealed, in vain, to the permission granted by Pope *Pius the Second*. 

That Duke William IV of Bavaria <note>Reigned 1508–1550</note> opposed Archduke *Ferdinand* of Austria <note>Later Emperor Ferdinand I</note> in seeking the Bohemian Kingdom is a matter left to memory by *Jovius* <note>Paolo Giovio (1483–1552), an Italian physician and historian</note>, the only *historian* <note>original: "Historicus"</note>—as far as I am aware—to do so; however, from whom he obtained this information remains uncertain. But even granted that Duke William was an opponent of Archduke *Ferdinand*: he brought the Estates, as the electors of the Kingdom, to his side at the very time when, after the miserable slaughter of King *Louis* <note>Louis II of Hungary and Bohemia, killed at the Battle of Mohács in 1526</note>, the royal throne stood vacant. 

But you have violently seized a Kingdom already promised to another and handed over with sworn duty, rather than seeking it by right; let no fog arise from such a Bavarian deed that might cover your presumption. I hold the words of *Jovius*, as a not unskilled *writer* <note>original: "Scribenten"</note>, in high regard—indeed, much higher than the narrative itself. He was, however, an *Italian* and lived somewhat far away; he wrote what he heard because he could not have the direct experience himself. At times the Hungarians, and at others the Poles, have drawn this Kingdom to themselves, because now a Hungarian, now a Pole, was somewhat more closely related to it by blood; thus the name of an "election" deceived the aforementioned *Jovius*.

In the same way, cease to introduce *Tengnagel* <note>likely a reference to a contemporary political figure or witness involved in the disputes</note> as a witness, who serves as a loathsome *example* <note>original: "Exempel"</note> of your cruelty. What one forces out of a person with fire and torture cannot be of much consequence. Furthermore, I doubt—not without reason—that such words ever fell from the lips of the said *Tengnagel*, for I hear no one else affirming this except you yourselves, who have already lost your *credit* <note>reputation or trustworthiness</note> beforehand. Nor does the title of the Duke of *Neuburg* help you: he congratulated King *Ferdinand* on his election—not on a "free election" as you boast of it, but, according to custom and habit, on an hereditary election. This prince, who loves truth and justice, is still alive; if you wish for him to interpret his own words for you, then ask him, and he will then answer you:

->what you do not wish to hear.<-

<summary>This page disputes the legitimacy of the Bohemian election claims by critiquing the historical accounts of Paolo Giovio and dismissing testimony obtained through torture. <keywords>Pope Paul II, George of Podiebrad, Jan Dubravius, Duke William IV of Bavaria, Archduke Ferdinand I, Paolo Giovio, King Louis II, Battle of Mohács, Tengnagel, Duke of Neuburg, Bohemian succession, hereditary monarchy vs. elective monarchy</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal and historical debate regarding the elective versus hereditary nature of the Bohemian crown, specifically referencing the Golden Bull of 1356.</meta>

# PLESSIUS.

To this is added, sixthly, the **Golden Bull** <note>The Golden Bull of 1356 was a decree issued by the Imperial Diet at Nuremberg and Metz, headed by Emperor Charles IV, which fixed important aspects of the constitutional structure of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>, which expressly grants the inhabitants of the Kingdom of **Bohemia** the right to elect a King (provided that the Kingdom is vacant and stands empty). From this it follows that such a Kingdom cannot simply be a hereditary Kingdom, but that the election must necessarily belong to the inhabitants of the Kingdom, at least in certain cases. And although the words "vacant Kingdom" could be made a matter of dispute, it is nevertheless in accordance with the Law that, in doubtful and unknown matters, that interpretation should be adopted which contains the least injustice and uncertainty, and which tends toward ensuring it might not be called an "evasive law" by the common rights. 

For if one wished to restrict the right of election only to the case as long as the lineage of Emperor **Charles IV** and his descendants remained, such would not unjustly be held as an unheard-of thing. For the descendants of Emperor Charles IV, sprung from the **Luxembourg** lineage, are spread among so many Royal and Princely Houses (since from there the Royal children in **England** and **Denmark**, and almost countless Princes in the House of **Brandenburg**, **Mecklenburg**, **Lüneburg**, and even in the **Palatinate** itself, take their origin) that even if we considered the mortality of men, nevertheless, according to this understanding, the right of election would not belong to the inhabitants of the Bohemian Kingdom for all eternity. But no one—unless he be a very poor *Vocativus* <note>original: "Vocatiuus"; a Latin grammatical term used here as a slur for a novice or someone who only knows the basics of language/law</note> in the Law—is unaware that it is an unheard-of thing to say that a Law (especially one so excellent as the Golden Bull is) should speak and dispose of a case which, in the nature of things <note>original: "in rerum natura"</note>, could never occur.

---

### FABIUS.

Now finally the artillery is being planted to utterly strike down the **Austrian Hereditary House** in Bohemia. The Golden Bull permits the Bohemians the right of election in the case, as you say, of a vacant Kingdom: I do not deny this, Plessius; the Law allows such a thing, but only in such a way that one uses the manner of such permitted Law. Such a manner, however, is this: that he whom the blood favors should not be bypassed. It is enough that the elected one demands the <term>Regalia</term> <note>Royal rights or privileges, such as the right to mint coins or collect certain taxes</note>, as they are called, from him according to customary usage. Thus the Law certainly allows it, provided that no Royal...

<summary>Plessius argues that the Golden Bull of 1356 guarantees Bohemia's elective status, as a hereditary interpretation would render the law meaningless; Fabius counters that election must still respect blood lineage.</summary>
<keywords>Golden Bull, Bohemia, elective monarchy, Charles IV, House of Luxembourg, House of Austria, succession law, legal interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal and historical debate regarding the succession of the Bohemian crown and the legitimacy of the Elector Palatine's actions. It follows a discussion on the interpretation of the Golden Bull and dynastic lineage.</meta>

lineage no longer exists: And this interpretation, which is founded and grounded in usage and custom, contains nothing foolish within it. But you hold that it could hardly happen that the lineage of Charles IV <note>Holy Roman Emperor Charles IV (r. 1346–1378), author of the Golden Bull</note> might go extinct in future years, as if many different families had not already gone completely to ruin. Inheritance does not extend to an infinite number of people: a certain limit is set for successors, since not every person attains the inheritance of every grandfather. Otherwise, if Noah’s Ark were still in existence, you would also inherit it, or at least rightfully claim a portion of it—provided you were not instead fathered from the stones of Deucalion <note>A reference to Greek mythology where Deucalion and Pyrrha repopulated the earth by throwing stones behind them; used here to mock the idea of an impossible or mythical lineage</note>. Therefore, my dear Pless, the Golden Bull disposes nothing regarding a case which could not occur in the nature of things—unless it perhaps disposes that your Count Palatine be King in Bohemia.

->P L E S S I V S<-

However, such a thing is not brought onto the path <note>brought up for discussion</note> to the end that the right of the Kingdom among the Bohemians should be argued here with particular diligence. For it is only being dealt with so that the reader might, as it were, feel with his own hands that the crime of Lese-majesty <note>term: Lese-majesty; from the Latin 'laesa maiestas', meaning an offense against the dignity of the sovereign or state</note> cannot and should not rightfully be attributed to the Count Palatine and others (who hold that the Kingdom of Bohemia is an elective kingdom and that free election is the right of the Bohemian Estates). For just as someone who comes before a court for a provable legal cause cannot be recognized as deserving the punishment of those who quarrel in vain: so also neither the Elector Palatine, nor any others who held Bohemia to be an elective kingdom, or are still of that same opinion, can be accused or convicted of any malicious fraud. Without such fraud, no crime—much less that of Lese-majesty—can be committed, and for that reason even the slightest cause excuses a person. Upon such a laid foundation, the sensible reader will now easily be able to consider for himself that the Electoral Palatinate, on account of this Bohemian business, was declared under the Imperial Ban <note>term: Imperial Ban; 'Reichsacht', a form of outlawry where a person is stripped of all rights and territories within the Holy Roman Empire</note> with the highest injustice. And firstly, indeed, because of the foundation just cited: that he is easily excused regarding any malicious fraud. Secondly, because of the lack of jurisdiction in the person of His Imperial Majesty. Who, in this private claim to the Bohemian Crown, is to be considered not as an Emperor, but as an Archduke of Austria—as many different treaties and writings have now sufficiently proven.

Now, an Archduke of Austria has not the slightest claim, jurisdiction, nor superiority over an Elector <note>The Electors were the high-ranking princes of the Holy Roman Empire who chose the Emperor.</note>. Thirdly, because it is publicly known that the Kingdom of Bohemia has nothing to do with the jurisdiction of the Holy Roman Empire, nor with the Imperial Chamber Court at Speyer, nor with the Imperial Aulic Council <note>original: "Kayserlichen Hoffgericht"</note>, nor with the Public Peace, the Ordinance of Execution, or any other Imperial constitutions. Upon what ground, foundation, or right can the declaration of the Imperial Ban <note>The "Reichsacht" or Imperial Ban was a form of outlawry where a person lost all rights and property.</note>—which was issued by the Imperial Aulic Council in the Bohemian matter against the Count Palatine <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King," who accepted the Bohemian crown.</note>—stand? In this manner, it would be as if someone committed an offense in the Kingdom of Denmark, Sweden, or France, and could be declared under the Imperial Ban for it.

## FABIUS.

Regardless of what opinion you ultimately dispute concerning the election of the Bohemian Kingdom, it will do very little for your cause. For even if we granted your simple-minded claim—that you would have been deterred from the religious cause had you known the Kingdom belonged to the heirs—you were nonetheless not unaware that another had already been elected previously, and that what the Bohemians offered was something quite different. This you truly knew well, and the Calvinists are otherwise rarely accustomed to stumbling out of ignorance. It helps to do evil, even if the whole world should go to ruin over it: why do you not just admit that you committed a grand deception? You have sinned against the law of *Lese-majesty* <term>Lese-majesty: from the Latin 'laesa maiestas', meaning an offense against the dignity of a reigning sovereign</term>, and there was no lack of jurisdiction in him who passed the judgment. Unless, of course, you mean to suggest that the Emperor is no Emperor. If he can place under the Ban an armed Prince who hunts other people from house and home, why then should he not also be able to proscribe the one by whom he himself is insulted and robbed? Do you wish then, you tricksters <note>original: "Pfiffi"</note>, that I should defend you against freebooters or highwaymen, and meanwhile not protect myself? Furthermore, by the time you began to rave, the Kingdom of Bohemia already belonged to the Emperor by both hereditary and elective right. Your distinction between the Emperor and an Archduke is also worthless. The person is injured: otherwise, even the Jews would be excused, who inflicted scorn and shame upon the crucified Christ, not as God, but only as a man who was accused of guilt.

<meta>The previous page ended with the catchword "sunter" (but). This page continues the legal and theological defense regarding the conflict in Bohemia and the Palatinate.</meta>

It is true that the Kingdom of Bohemia has been granted privileges by several Emperors, stating that they—the Bohemians—do not have to appear before the Imperial Court of Justice, but may instead administer justice at home. However, they have no privilege stating that they cannot be brought back to obedience by the highest authority when they overstep the established order. Furthermore, in our case, no judgment has been passed nor has the law been exercised against the King of Bohemia <note>referring to the legitimate Habsburg claimant</note>, but rather against the usurper of the Kingdom <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate, the "Winter King"</note>—specifically the one who had no legal title to invade the lands and people of other potentates in a hostile manner. In this regard, you seem quite ridiculous to me, acting as if you are wiser than a jurist by maintaining that a person cannot be proscribed <term>proscribed: officially denounced and outlawed</term> by the Sovereign's power simply because he has already fled the country and is now residing in a foreign dominion. Therefore, neither flight nor guilt offers an excuse; otherwise, you would be considered a saint.

## PLESSIS <note>Likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot writer</note>.

In finally accusing the Electoral Palatinate of the present misfortune and the harmful uprisings stirred up in Bohemia, Hungary, Austria, etc., and in the Roman Empire, they truly commit a malicious error which is entirely without foundation. For if one were to examine the causes of such unrest, anyone who confesses Christ not only with the mouth but with the heart knows from Holy Scripture that such major punishments, which befall entire provinces, do not happen by chance. Instead, they stem from certain Divine providence and disposition <note>original: "disposition"</note> to punish human wickedness, which increases more and more over time. Just as the Prophet says on this matter: there is no misfortune in the city which the Lord has not ordained. To this end, Thuanus <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou, a famous French historian</note> likewise says in his 59th book: the beginning of war is doubtful and its outcome uncertain, and it commonly does not incline toward the more just cause. Indeed, we see through daily experience that through such events, God—who, as we cannot deny without stubborn impudence, is angry with us—is accustomed to punish His own. We also know just as well from Holy Divine Scripture that discord between Princes and subjects is ordained by the righteous judgment of God. For we read thus in the Book of Judges, Chapter 9, verses 22 and 23: Abimelech therefore ruled over Israel for 3 years. And God sent an evil spirit between Abimelech and the inhabitants of Shechem, who began to [deal treacherously with him...]

to curse. From which it appears as clear as the sun that such a rebellion of the Bohemian people against the House of Austria and its rule was sent by God Almighty Himself; just as the following little saying by **Erasmus** <note>Desiderius Erasmus (1466–1536), the famous Dutch humanist and theologian.</note> is proven true by experience and the facts themselves, wherein he said: wars are common punishments for both parties. <note>original: "die Kriege sind gemeine Straffen beider Parthey."</note>

## FABIUS

The Count Palatine <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate (1596–1632), the "Winter King" of Bohemia, whose acceptance of the Bohemian crown helped trigger the Thirty Years' War.</note> has never muddied the waters, though other people may wish to lay the blame for so much heartache instigated in Germany at his feet. For, in the first place, he is not the primary cause of these highly damaging uprisings, but rather God, who disposes war and peace among mortal men. But who would want to challenge Him with words or resist His divine counsel and will? The Calvinist himself was terrified by his own words, which is why he added the words of the Prophet, stating that God ordains the misfortune of punishment: in which he is indeed somewhat more modest, or at least no better, than his master *Calvin*; because Germany has suffered not only the misfortune of punishment, but also the misfortune of guilt. God is not the author of this guilt, but rather the Count Palatine, Plessen, <note>Volrad von Plessen (1560–1631), a leading counselor to the Elector Palatine.</note> Camerarius, <note>Ludwig Camerarius (1573–1651), a statesman and head of Frederick V's government in exile.</note> and more of their kind. For just as God permits for a time the murderer's homicide, the robber's plundering, and the fornicator's fornication, and does not restrain the one who willingly stains himself with public vices—either because of merit or demerit, of this person or another—so too has He not restrained the swarming Calvinist youth from Heidelberg <note>A reference to the University of Heidelberg, the intellectual center of Calvinism in the Palatinate.</note> who hurried toward his own ruin and that of others; either so that those whom he warred against might be disciplined, or so that he who wished to ruin others might himself be ruined. However, God did not will it, nor did He command the Count Palatine to rage so, nor did He forbid others from stopping the madman. But what you cite from the Bible does not serve your purpose, since among many peoples, at all times, various vices are committed of which God is not the cause, but rather human wickedness, which the Lord will surely punish in His own time. For God does not choose to take revenge in every single moment, but is commonly accustomed to postpone the punishment. Regarding *Thuanus*, <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou (1553–1617), a French historian known for his "History of His Own Time."</note> he philosophizes in the manner of a wandering historian, and it is not unknown to us that something evil commonly follows a victory. But be that as it may, let one prevail with one or another battle; they are overcome by war,

## PLESSIUS <note>Philippe de Mornay, Lord of Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a prominent Huguenot leader and advisor</note>

If one now wishes to proceed to the other causes, the Electoral Palatinate <note>original: "Chur Pfaltz"</note> is supported by the lawful calling and election by the Bohemian Estates. And so that he, the Elector, might recognize such an election as lawful, he was moved to do so by highly important reasons—as we have mentioned above—so that at the very least the "putative title" <note>original: "Titul"; a legal term for a title that is believed to be valid but may have defects</note>, as the lawyers <note>original: "Juristen"</note> call it, excuses him. Against this election by the Electoral Palatinate, the Emperor has fallen into a controversy <note>original: "Controvers"</note> and discord with the Bohemian Estates, and has openly presumed to claim the Bohemian Crown as his inheritance, and the Bohemians as his hereditary subjects. They, on the contrary, were determined to place such a crown on another's head with no other title than that of election; also, because they had already taken up arms before the election and arrival of the Count Palatine, they would rather endure the extreme for their freedom and religion than consent to this. And indeed, fortune has been so contrary to them that they were forced to cease from arms for a time. The principal <note>original: "Principal"</note> dispute, however—namely, whether the Crown of Bohemia is an elective or a hereditary kingdom belonging to the House of Austria—has not been lawfully decided <note>original: "decidiret"</note> to this very day, but the dispute still rests upon the lawful verdict of the judge.

## FABIUS

Let the kind-hearted reader behold here Plessen’s godless proceeding <note>original: "procedere"</note>! For he attributes the primary cause of such an abominable riot stirred up in the Empire to God, but the other to his Count Palatine, who is supposedly entirely innocent in the matter because he was called to Bohemia by a stately embassy <note>original: "Legation"</note> and, after previous timely deliberation, recognized the same as lawful because it pleased him. He would have been of a much different opinion had it been placed on someone else's head. But what kind of "putative title" is this, Plessis? Were either you or others in the Palatine Court so hardened that you did not know that robbery is and remains robbery, or that you regarded the same as a gift? Never can it be a lawful election

be and help when I undertake to follow a rightfully elected King. It would also be an unjust thing if one were to promote a foreign prince to a vacant kingdom and, in the meantime, bypass the natural heir; for that reason, one could not drive out the previously enthroned King by any legal title. But you err with particularly great cunning when you write that His Imperial Majesty <note>Emperor Ferdinand II</note> fell into dispute because of this election of the Count Palatine <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate, the "Winter King"</note>—namely, whether the Kingdom of Bohemia were hereditary or not—as if the war had arisen for that reason, and the Bohemians had taken up arms under such a beautiful pretense.

The war, my dear Pless <note>Referring to Philippe de Mornay, Lord of Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot advisor</note>, did not arise from the dispute over the election, but from your wantonness and contempt for the Austrian government. You removed the Imperial Councilors from their dignity so that you might become capable of the same; those who remained steadfast to His Imperial Majesty, you drove out of the kingdom; and so that they might not perhaps arrive to punish your malice, you blocked their way and passage with recruited soldiery, so that you might also attack Austria itself in a hostile manner. And this—not the disputed question of the election—was the origin of such a Bohemian war.

Likewise, Ferdinand <note>Ferdinand II</note>, successor to Matthias <note>Emperor Matthias, d. 1619</note>, had no dispute with the Bohemians regarding the election, but only regarding the lack of orderly possession. For the elected one desires only that which was owed to him by virtue of the election; and, because the Bohemians kept no faith and gave to another what was already no longer theirs, he took up defense against the rebellious who were already found in arms. And, through an excellently maintained victory, he established such a notable example: that kingdoms are by no means given and taken back according to the whim of the subjects.

Nor has the dispute yet been settled, as you say, whether the right of election belongs to you Calvinists; for if you had such a right, you have conversely lost it through your rebellion. And as you have set sail, you have left everything behind you in the lurch to such an extent that you seek a lodging elsewhere as beggars; and in such beggary, you are no longer mindful of the Royal election. In this, however, you do not act wrongly by not wishing to claim the dispute of the election for yourselves: but you should also have given up the election itself—through which your Prince allowed himself to be fooled for the sake of a Royal title—then you would not now have to wander about in misery in this way.

But now I would only like to ask this: why should the Count Palatine be at fault for what Cardinal Khlesl <note>Melchior Khlesl, 1552–1630, Bishop of Vienna and influential advisor</note> and others had given the Bohemians cause to riot? Or that the Bohemians, because of the arrogance of the Presidents, had...

fallen—as we have indicated above: or why should the *Count Palatine* <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate, the "Winter King"</note> be slandered, claiming that the Austrian *Councilors* and *Officers*, through secret *intrigues* with *Homonnay* <note>Drugeth von Homonnay, a Hungarian nobleman and rival to Bethlen Gabor</note>—whom they wished to elevate to the Transylvanian Principality—stirred up Prince *Bethlen Gabor* <note>Prince of Transylvania and leader of an anti-Habsburg uprising</note> against themselves, and thus gave him the occasion to seek help in *Constantinople*? ->*FABIUS.*<-

In this case, you are beginning the third cause of the war, after the *acquittal* of the *Count Palatine*, for which *Cardinal Khlesl* <note>Melchior Khlesl, 1552–1630, a powerful bishop and statesman under Emperors Rudolf II and Matthias</note> and the arrogance of the *Presidents* are to blame. But why do you not also recount the deeds of *Khlesl* which stirred up such a *tumult*? Likewise, the *Presidents* did you no harm, but rather they were the ones offended. Indeed, you would have provided a useful answer if you had *specified* such *injuries*. He who does not trust his own case complains in *generalities*. Elsewhere you say they would be *reviewed*; I know it well: they should have been *censured* in the name of justice. For whatever is against your will, that is an *injury* to you. Oh, dear God, with how many *injuries* have you wretched *Calvinists* now been overwhelmed! Yet you are well-deserving of the fortune which you have made for yourselves.

Furthermore, the Austrian *Councilors*, in inciting *Homonnay* to innovation, obscured the light of day and thereby drove *Bethlen* into such a fear that he was forced to knock at the *Ottoman Porte* <note>The central government of the Ottoman Empire</note> to *save* himself. Looking backward, *Plessy* <note>Likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader</note>, the *Imperial* party forced *Bethlen* to seek help from the *Turks*! If he—as would have been fitting for him—had held his King in due *respect* as a *client* <note>A subordinate or dependent ruler</note> and had fully satisfied his duty as a subject Prince, then neither the *Turkish* help nor the war would have been necessary. For he devastated *Hungary* for the very reason that he might rule over it with the empty *title* of a King. But since we, my dear *Plessy*, have now come to this point, and so that it may become known and notorious to the entire honorable world who exactly [caused] the great rebellion...

## P L E S S I V S.

Regarding the remaining unrest in the Empire: after the defeat at Prague, it could have been very easily stilled through fair and peaceful negotiation. At that time, almost no province of the Empire was burdened with war except for the Lower Palatinate on the Rhine. Thus, it was the most secure opportunity and the best time to plant a secure, eternal peace—especially since His Imperial Majesty, after obtaining the victory at Prague, and his accomplices, felt an exceedingly great advantage. As the Lord de la Noue <note>François de la Noue, a famous Huguenot captain</note> heartfully teaches in his *Military Discourses*, final chapter, where he says:

> Namely, that the one party of the combatants can then establish peace at the very best and
<margin>„</margin>
> most secure time when they have deprived the opposing side of a great advantage.
<margin>„</margin>

<margin>„</margin>
> testifies in Decade 3, Book 3 <note>original: "Liuius Decad. 3. lib. 3."</note>: "Things well accomplished in
<margin>„</margin>

<margin>„</margin>
> neglect the time in which we can give more than we take,
<margin>„</margin>
> and yet peace could be struck, then I fear that our joy, being too proud,
<margin>„</margin>
> might eventually turn out badly for us."
And Marquis Spinola <note>Ambrogio Spinola, the Spanish general</note> took very good notice of such an opportunity when, a few years ago, he advised his King toward peace with the Dutch, and he himself, along with others, attended such peace negotiations. If His Imperial Majesty had at that time—when Baron Digby <note>John Digby, 1st Earl of Bristol</note>, in the name of the King of England, offered himself for negotiation and promised that his King [James I] would bring his son-in-law <note>Frederick V</note> to fair terms and proper submission...

For what reasons all peace negotiations were set aside, however, anyone can easily discern from the long-held counsels and schemes which <note>The author uses a personification here: "Truth is the daughter of Time."</note> Truth, the daughter of Time, has since brought to light through Divine help.

## FABIUS

As far as I can perceive, you suggest that the Emperor stirred up the unrest in Germany, he who was not inclined toward peace after obtaining the victory at Prague <note>The Battle of White Mountain, 1620.</note>. You imply he did this by well-considered counsel because, at such a favorable time, he could have settled and directed the peace according to his own pleasure. For how should the enemy, cast down to the ground, resist him much or refuse to agree? Those who are about to lose everything must also allow everything to be done to them. My dear Pless, you act somewhat more wisely in this instance because misery has made you more skillful than when Fortune—which has now overthrown you—falsely showed herself so favorable to you. 

But I ask you, since you believe your King <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King."</note> is bound by a heavenly chain against all attacks: did you ever even offer peace to His Imperial Majesty? Rather, you strengthened your greatly weakened military forces and attacked the nearest adjacent provinces in a hostile manner. Why then should the Emperor follow your example? Furthermore, there was no reason why the Emperor should offer peace to you, the defeated, because it would have been belittling to His Majesty to give to the rebels that which they had withdrawn from so many provinces. And why did you not rather—you who were in need—betake yourselves to the altar of mercy and petition for peace? We, as those who suppressed the enemy before Prague, were not in need of it; yet no cruelty would have kept us from peace, provided only that you had been minded to petition for it. 

You might say, however, that the King of England's ambassador, Digby <note>John Digby, 1st Earl of Bristol, who attempted to mediate the conflict.</note>, petitioned for peace negotiations and yet could obtain nothing. Yes, indeed, nothing was refused to anyone, except only to those who did not wish to enter into peace. For when Digby had made himself ready for his journey and awaited the Count Palatine's resolution, the Count Palatine wrote on <margin>October 14/24</margin> October, 1621, to the Duke of Zweibrücken, the Governor at Heidelberg, that he...

### The Seventh Chapter.

# That the United <note>The "United" refers to the Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states formed in 1608.</note> resolved to wage the war—which they had long intended—against the Catholics during the Nuremberg assembly in the year 1619: for the proof of which article, on page 203, the Compiler recounts a letter written by Volrath von Plessen at Nuremberg on November 27, 1619.

->Information and Answer.<-

->P L E S S I V S.<-

Regarding this letter, the kind-hearted reader is requested to take note: Firstly, that it was written at a time when both the United and the Catholics had their military forces at hand. The Duke of Bavaria was expecting more cavalry and infantry from Westphalia, while the Electors of Mainz and Trier were daily awaiting the Anhalt infantry, which their councilors and officers were meant to inspect. Why should it be taken ill of von Plessen that, in such a state of affairs, he dared to reveal his mind in a private letter by way of discourse? Specifically, regarding the location where the United's troops might be conveniently quartered, especially since winter was at the door? Furthermore, it has never been the custom in the Empire until now that a councilor or servant of a Protesting Prince must give an account of his affairs to the Roman Catholics in such cases. This is something the Protestants have never—as far as we can recall—demanded to know from the Roman Catholic councilors and servants.

->F A B I V S.<-

If von Plessen now has more sense in his "Capitol" <note>A play on words referring to both the Roman Capitol and the writer's head/intellect.</note>, he lets it be noted now, even if it should cost him his head. He wrote a letter which is well-deserving of proscription <term>Proscription: a legal decree of condemnation or exile, often involving the loss of property and rights.</term>, and
<meta>catchword: would</meta>

he would likely have cast the pen away from himself if he had feared even the slightest suspicion from Catholic eyes. But now, because the matter has been betrayed and the game is up, he wishes to cloak his shameful letter with a long and tangled explanation—yet all in vain. One fox recognizes another, and if they did not already know the art of cunning, they could certainly learn it from such a crafty master as this. We do not ask, *Plessis* <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a prominent French Protestant strategist.</note>, at what time you wrote the letter, but rather what you wrote; time covers no shame, and such matters meet their retribution at every moment. The Elector of Bavaria <note>Maximilian I, a key Catholic leader.</note> might have recruited more troops, as might the Elector of Mainz and still others, to prepare themselves for defense amidst so many tumults stirred up by the *Calvinists*: was it therefore permitted for you to pitch your camps on Catholic soil and become an enemy before they ever were? Was it proper for you to incite the Bohemians against the Emperor and to teach them those arts of war by which His Imperial Majesty might be brought to ruin?

But you venture, *Plessis*, to wipe away your presumption with two points, though again in vain. For when you call your letter a *private* writing, that is true if one looks only at the blank paper; but when one considers the writer, the subject matter, and the person to whom the letter was written, then it is all public and very useful for the times which you have troubled and filled with unrest. You, *Plessis*, were the counselor to the Count Palatine <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate, the "Winter King."</note> and the head of the *rebellion*; you provided the *material* <note>original: "Materj"</note> for war; you traversed all the provinces back and forth; you inflamed quiet minds and offered incense to *Bellona* <note>The Roman goddess of war.</note>—and indeed most diligently at the time when, full of worry and impatient of delay, you wrote this very letter so that the commonwealth might be all the more distressed. 

And who would not understand the "common good" of which you speak? To organize the armed forces and to lead those still somewhat inexperienced in war against their King—is such a thing a *private* matter? And the one to whom you wrote was one of the most prominent *agents*. For who has expended more labor and shed more sweat for so many years now than Prince *Christian of Anhalt* <note>A primary diplomat and military commander for the Protestant Union.</note>, who, acting as everyone’s messenger and sitting as if in *Triptolemus's* wagon <note>A mythological chariot drawn by dragons, used here to mock Anhalt's rapid travels across Europe to sow discord.</note>, wandered through a great part of *Europe*—not so that peace might be procured for the Empire, but so that the *Calvinists* might maintain the upper hand in the Realm.

Therefore, your letter was no *private* writing, except perhaps—if one might speak thus—in regards to its lack of righteous virtue and a pious, sincere mind. But your "freedom" cannot protect you so highly that—

by which you—on account of your words and deeds—are exempt from any other censure. A fanatic has the power to draw his sword, and he who is meant to lose his hair <note>likely an idiom for someone being shorn or punished</note> must leave his sword in its scabbard. So too, Plessis, God grant that however your pen may stain the paper—even if it should cost our harm and blood—it shall not be forbidden for us to go against you, and in turn to bring to light publicly with the pen what you have deserved with your poisonous quill.

### P L E S S I S <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a prominent French Protestant strategist.</note>

Secondly, because in the aforementioned letter not a single indication is to be found of such a hostile assault, or of violent oppression, or even the total expulsion of the clergy—which the compiler spreads abroad on page 27 and elsewhere—but rather, such a letter only makes mention of the quartering and maintenance of the Union's soldiery <note>referring to the Protestant Union forces</note>, so that one can rightly refer to the very literal text of such a letter.

### F A B I U S <note>Likely a pseudonym for a Catholic polemicist, possibly representing the Jesuit perspective.</note>

Since you yourself, Plessis, place so little trust in these words of yours—these absurd little war-ruses—you do not hold the matter in high regard, because you have written nothing of the general war and the oppression of the clergy. But this is so false that even in this place (since you have cast off the veil of shame) the letter itself must feel ashamed when it comes before the sight of other people. Among other things (where you teach the "bare broom" <note>an idiom for a desperate or blunt tactic</note> that one should persuade the Elector of Bavaria, with whatever words possible, to remain neutral, as they call it—which, as you hoped, could easily happen if one gave guarantees regarding the two or three bishops bordering Bavaria), you write thus: "If the Bavarian Prince wished to except the two or three nearest bordering bishops, his allies, then one must, in my opinion, look through one's fingers <note>to deliberately overlook something</note> so that one might have freedom with the others; and in this way, the Bavarian Prince will, in fact, be kept away from other Archbishoprics and Bishoprics." What do you admit here, Plessis? Were not the weapons for the subduing of Catholic Germany brought about by you—which you nevertheless deny? Did you write nothing of the ruinous downfall of the clergy in your letter? O, what a mouth!

already gazes with an open mouth toward the bishoprics <note>original: "Bißthumben"</note> and all ecclesiastical estates; even now you gnash and whet your teeth.
