->P L E S S I V S.<-
<note>Refers to Volrad von Plessen (1560–1631), a leading statesman of the Electoral Palatinate.</note>

Thirdly, von Plessen writes only his own opinion, and not the decree and declaration of the assembled Estates. The compiler <note>The author of the opposing pamphlet being refuted</note> should have taken good note of this, so that he might not so frequently conclude from this letter that the invasion of the Catholics had been resolved at the assembly in Nuremberg. For from this, a malicious and public error appears. In the oft-mentioned letter, page 204, it is expressly found: That the Estates had left the declaration of this point (whether one should quarter soldiers in Catholic lands) to the inquiry of the Count Palatine, as General, and the Margrave of Anspach, Lieutenant General of the Electoral Palatinate. These men were to consult with the war councils and those who lived closest to the Catholics, and could finally reach a conclusion regarding it. Which conclusion, however, was neither conceived at Nuremberg, nor did it ever follow afterward. Rather, the embassy dispatched to Munich intervened, and from there—after the meeting and assembly of the Leaguists <note>The Catholic League</note> at Würzburg had changed—the Bavarian answer was first awaited. Thereupon finally followed the Treaties of Ulm and Mainz. Thus, from such a "War Expedition" of the United <note>The Protestant Union</note>, of which the compiler makes such a great outcry in his Chancery, the Catholics—whether they be Spiritual or Secular Estates in the Empire—have seen not a single soldier upon their ground and soil.

->F A B I V S .<-

The <term>Compiler<note>original: "Compilator"; likely referring to the author of a collection of documents or a specific polemical work</note></term> has demonstrated and proven, not only from your own writing but also through other testimonies, that a resolution was passed against us at Nuremberg at the instigation of the **Calvinists**. For Count Johann Albrecht of Solms, Grand Chamberlain at the Palatine Court, wrote on November 13, **Anno 1619**, in his letter to Prince Christian of Anhalt, among other things, as follows: "During these days, matters have been handled quite masterfully in the Council, and it has seemed to everyone together that one can no longer hope for any treaty regarding the grievances, and that one should proceed most securely with action itself." This is indeed, **Plessi** <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader and diplomat</note>, plain enough English <note>original: "teutsch genug" (plain German); an idiom meaning "clear enough"</note>, and I marvel that you do not understand it. But you merely send your writings as if they were entirely innocent, and as if your speech did not betray your secret affairs.

For you mention, among other things, that the United Lords and Estates in the aforementioned assembly had left the war against the Clergy (whom you, according to your **Calvinist** habit, call I know not what kind of filth) to the discretion of the Elector Palatine and the Margrave of Ansbach, as well as other **Calvinist** colonels. This discretion, my dear **Plessi**, rested only upon time and opportunity; for the war pleased the Union assembly. And if we did not know this from elsewhere, it would nonetheless be notorious and manifest from the arrogant <term>Legation<note>A formal diplomatic mission or embassy</note></term> sent to the Duke in Bavaria. For that man was brought a command, and nothing was requested of him—and indeed with intolerable pride—to <term>execute<note>original: "exequiren"; to carry out or enforce a decree</note></term> the prescribed time as they saw fit.

Do you now wish, **Plessi**, that I should believe you have resolved upon no war, which you nevertheless announce to me regardless? Why is it not a public error, what the **Compiler** has written? But your vanity is revealed, especially in the <term>Parenthesis<note>A clarifying or digressive remark within a text</note></term> where you say: the Estates had only deliberated on the declaration of such a point (whether the <term>Soldiery<note>original: "Soldatesca"; mercenary troops</note></term> should be quartered on Catholic ground and soil), because in your writing you wrote not only of the quartering of the **Soldiery**, but of how one might wage war against the Clergy; which matter—when and how it should happen—was left to the war councils to deliberate. And it matters not at all that, immediately after this **Decree**, nothing hostile was undertaken; for at that time, one only sought a pretext <note>original: "Faͤrblein"; literally "a little color" or a gloss/veneer</note> for such things. To find this, you dispatched your envoys to Munich, seeking a cause for war for the sake of appearances, when you were already certain of a refusal. Thus, your presumption first delayed the **Legation**,

At the end of the oft-mentioned writing, page 212, the compiler quite craftily introduces the opinion of the Lord of Plessen, in which he suggests there was a fear that some Estates, under the pretext of paying their quotas <note>financial contributions to the Union's military fund</note>, might apply the money to their private benefit. In this, the compiler perhaps did not know the characteristics of the French language, or else did not rightly understand the sense <note>original: "sensum"</note>. For the words "under the pretext of their own defense"—which are of the substance <note>original: "de substantia," meaning essential to the meaning</note> and are found in the various Würzburg and Mainz translations, and the omission of which changes the sense significantly—he has entirely left out. We, however, publicly appeal here to the French original, to the Würzburg and Mainz translations, and to the true sense or understanding of the writing. And indeed, there is no other understanding than this: it was feared that the money deputized for the Union's formed army might decrease by no small amount if perhaps Landgrave Moritz (being neighbor to the Netherlands' borders), or the Margrave of Culmbach (because of the neighboring Kingdom of Bohemia), or the Margrave of Baden (who has the Lorrainers, Burgundians, and Austrians as neighbors), or even the city of Strasbourg (which must keep a watchful eye against Lorraine and France)—all of which the Articles of the Union permit in a certain way and measure—should recruit soldiers for themselves, and thereafter, under the pretext of their own defense (since these words were left out either due to recent haste or out of deceit), would want to keep their quotas. The compiler cites this quite hatefully and translates it as "applying to their own private benefit," with this addition: "What also from the Union..."

## FABIUS.

Since you, Plessis <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Mornay (1549–1623), a prominent Huguenot leader.</note>, have also insulted the eternal ones <note>Likely referring to the high-ranking princes or authorities.</note> in your writing, you now thunder against the translator of your letter, accusing him of being unskilled in the French language—as if everyone could easily understand what you have written so poorly! The translator can never be better than one who sticks to the words. But what was the reason you did not write in German? Among the Calvinists (where it is considered a shame not to know one's own mother tongue), everything is French: not indeed out of love for the language, which is otherwise to be praised for its excellence, but out of special goodwill toward the Huguenots, with whom the German Calvinists share their origin from a single Genevan egg. Whether they speak French or German, they are understood, and they always have a trick ready for deception. Therefore, Plessis, leave the dispute against the translator aside and protect yourself against the eternal ones. You have offended and angered them not a little, because you explicitly wrote that it was to be feared they might put the money granted to the general fund <term>Cassa: a public treasury or chest</term> into their own pockets. And even if the translator omitted the pretext of this hardly laudable matter, he did nothing wrong in that; rather, you sin by adding it (under the guise of your own defense), and you truly would have been wiser to remain silent about it. For why were you afraid (since the Princes and the city of Strasbourg, by virtue of the Accord, could well have secured the funds promised to the Union against the approaching danger) that they might keep the treasure in their chests under the guise of their own defense? Which the Princes, out of ancient sincerity, would not have done, except through fraud and to the detriment of the common treasury <term>Aerarij: from the Latin 'aerarium', the public treasury</term>. Therefore, the words which you believe to have been omitted, and about which you grumble, only increase the injury done to the Princes and the city of Strasbourg. Since this is the case, who does not notice now that you blaspheme the compiler when you say that you feared the Princes might turn the money owed to the common good to their own benefit? For what purpose does your fear serve? If they keep the money to protect themselves with it, then there is nothing to fear, since they do so by their right and for the Union...

to keep for the good: for as much as when the parties are protected, the Union itself is also shielded. Therefore, you fear something else, and indeed exactly that which you indicate with public words: that they would keep the money, not for *defense* <note>original: "defension"</note>, but for the appearance of *defense*, which is to say, to keep it for their own benefit. Stop speaking, then, for you entangle yourselves more and more, even though you are already overwhelmed with many *schemes* <note>original: "Practicke"; refers to political machinations or intrigues</note>.

->PLESSIUS.<-

Furthermore, let the kind-hearted reader examine page 211, where he will find no other example in which the *compiler* <note>The "Compilator" refers to the editor of the "Anhaltische Kanzlei," a controversial publication of seized diplomatic documents</note> is to be found in such equal presumption. For there, and in the translation of the often-mentioned letter, he mentions among others the attached *discourses* <note>original: "discursen"; likely referring to diplomatic reports or essays</note>, or letters, of *Arsenis*, *Plessen*, *Guextini*, and *N.* Of the first three names, he sets them down expressly; but in place of the fourth (who was the Lord of *Haraucourt*, *Governor* of *Nancy*), the letter *N* is added by him, so that he might—according to his old custom—diligently omit some. For in the other translation forged at *Würzburg* or *Aschaffenburg*, the Lord of *Haraucourt* is found, although even in that one, the word *Haraucourt* was mistaken by them for *Haninecourt* in a hasty reading. Why the *compiler* did not expressly set down *Haraucourt's* name is indeed unknown to us; however, it may well be that he feared the displeasure of either the Lord of *Haraucourt* or the Duke of *Lorraine* <note>original: "Lothringen"</note>. We will not, however, withhold from the reader that the Lord of *Plessen* has had good *correspondence* with the Lord of *Haraucourt* for many years now, with the prior knowledge of both Princes.

->FABIUS.<-

Oh, what a great error the *compiler* has committed by setting the letter *N.* in place of the omitted name! He did not wish to betray the writer. But as to why he did it to all the others, the reason is at hand: namely, your hollow pretenses <note>original: "holhippen"; likely a derogatory term for empty talk or deceptive maneuvers</note>, at which you are a master. And are you not ashamed that you waste so many words because of this single *N.*? You might, however, while rebuking your readers, make plenty of words, because they have lasted a long time.

->PLESSIUS.<-

Finally, concerning the *embassy* <note>original: "Legation"</note> to the Duke in *Bavaria*, which not alone

not only the United <note>The Protestant Union</note> but also all corresponding members—as many as were assembled at the Union Day in Nuremberg, either in person or through their ambassadors—dispatched [the mission] after mature and unanimous deliberation. Although our Compiler <note>The opposing author being critiqued</note> calls this mission now a "threatening" one and now an "armed" one, and does not shy away from attacking it most vehemently in his Chancery, it is nevertheless sufficiently clear from von Plessen’s writing that he undertook such administration unwillingly. This will be testified to by the legates of Saxony-Weimar, Lüneburg, Württemberg, Nuremberg, and others—besides those of the Palatinate—who were deputized for the deliberation of the instructions. Likewise, [they will testify] that he mixed in various warnings at the time and desired a different instruction, both in form and in substance. However, because both the Legates' Proposition and the Duke of Bavaria’s counter-answer are available through public printing, it is unnecessary to introduce anything further in this regard. Every good person is therefore referred there to inspect them according to their own judgment.

# FABIUS. <note>The persona representing the Catholic/Imperial perspective</note>

You, Plessen, defend the ambassadors dispatched from Nuremberg to the Elector of Bavaria because you were among them yourself. You were quite well-armed, not with swords, but only with naked words. You also threatened with the sword if that which the Calvinist gods wanted should not come to pass. And you indeed—as much as I have heard from other people—drew your sword quite modestly, which was nevertheless well-noticed. Some others, however, being somewhat more impetuous, did no harm to anyone but themselves, because your tongues betrayed arrows directed against us, so that we were necessarily forced to take up the shield. How unwillingly you took this legation upon yourself, you know well enough yourself; for even if you wanted to tell me, I have no desire to know it. But if you desired a different instruction, you held the one given by the Princes to be either fit or unfit. If it was unfit, then you haughtily rebuke the entire Princely Council <term>Consess: a formal sitting or assembly of princes</term>. If it was fit, why then did you advise that it should be changed? Did you perhaps desire a sharper one? We considered it as such, because in truth it was full enough of a puffed-up spirit. Let the reader whom you refer there now go and behold the cited place; he will find neither you nor your companions to be peaceable, because under the appearance of a legation, you declared war upon us.

# FABIUS.

Anger, my dear Plessen, puts the arrow into one's hand. If the Compiler rejoices over such a great defeat of the Hungarians, then you will either not be Plessen, or you will be an evil counselor to the rebels. The Bavarians have not claimed the Victory of Prague for themselves alone, nor can Don Verdugo attribute it solely to himself, because many more swords shimmered in that battle which was so ruinous to you. Certain modes of speech are used here which, while they attribute something to one person, do not take it away from another; and it is said that one man alone did such a thing because he was one of the foremost among all those who performed the brave deed. Nor is the Compiler a slanderer of the Hungarians, as you say: for because you wrote that the Hungarians greatly feared the Bavarian Prince, the Compiler added that experience itself had proven this, when the Duke in Bavaria drove them from the mountains into the Vltava. And that is his entire triumph, which would undoubtedly have been increased by those Hungarians whom you accuse of the longer delay. For had they not ended the Diet at Neusohl and hurried toward Bavaria, they would...

without any doubt, they would have started a different dance, and would have much rather seen the Bavarians jumping. But, my good friend, Bavaria was not as bare of garrisons at that time as you supposed, even though the Elector <note>Maximilian I, Elector of Bavaria</note> had sent his army into Swabia to meet the enemy. He did the one thing and did not neglect the other: he who leads a war must be prepared for a war. Otherwise, the Danube as well as the Vltava <note>original: "Moldaw"</note> are both good to drink from, for both waters quench the thirst.

->P L E S S I U S<-

At this point, the favorable reader must be reminded that the often-mentioned letter from Lord von Plessen <note>Volrad von Plessen (1560–1631), a high-ranking statesman of the Electoral Palatinate</note> was written on November 17, 1619, before the publication of the *Anhalt Chancery* <note>term: Anhaltische Cantzeley; a famous propaganda publication of the era that exposed the secret diplomatic plans of the Protestant Union</note>, and was sent in all haste from Munich to Würzburg, Aschaffenburg, Mainz, and Darmstadt. Consequently, in many places, people triumphed greatly over it and discussed it in various ways, as if—I know not what—secret matters or miraculous beasts had been found within it. Indeed, they even did this in Würzburg or Mainz: while the victory at Prague <note>The Battle of White Mountain, 1620</note> was still fresh, and with flattering imagination, they completely believed that it was now all over for the Palatines and that they could never rise again. For that reason, they often publicly broadcast Lord von Plessen’s letter with a libelous title. We, however, hold the complete hope that those—of whatever religion they may be—who knew von Plessen well will judge and speak of him much differently than the inventors and authors of such insulting and shameful titles do.

->F A B I U S<-

I do not recall that a feast day or a jubilee year was established in Würzburg or Darmstadt because of your letter being found and broadcast here and there. Instead, I believe that the minds of the readers were much more inflamed because of your faithless advice, which is highly damaging to the common good. And do you now wish to criticize the brave deeds recently accomplished? Let us now, in turn, rejoice a little: for you triumphed before the war and exulted before we had even fallen. For two years ago, when we met with an unfortunate encounter at Wiesloch <note>The Battle of Mingolsheim/Wiesloch, 1622</note> due to the unexpected hostility of a hypocritical friend...

falsely spread rumors everywhere, as if such a battle had been equal to that of Cannae <note>The Battle of Cannae (216 BCE) was a major Roman defeat; the author is mocking the exaggeration of Protestant victories.</note>. In some places, they rang all the bells and wailed in Calvinist churches so that the common people came running, congratulating one another on account of this battle and holding festivals of joy. England, in particular, held a great celebration over it; however, this joy—alas—did not last long, but was completely extinguished by the sad news that followed soon after. For not long afterward, the Durlacher <note>Georg Friedrich, Margrave of Baden-Durlach, a Protestant commander.</note>, who was defeated in a great field battle at Wimpfen, once again heavily distressed and discouraged those minds that had been rejoicing over their little bit of luck.

But we shall now, Plessis <note>Refers to Volrad von Plessen, a statesman for the Elector Palatine.</note>, proceed again to your writings and inspect the "libelous title" about which you complain. But what kind of title was it, and have you omitted nothing? It would have been proper for the reader to judge its fairness or unfairness, had you permitted him to read it. But I see that in this you wish to show your piety, give honey-sweet words, and promise peace, while beneath it you seek to cover your malicious, lying, revengeful, and warlike heart. The title truly does not adorn you; and you have hurried so that the title would not go astray, and you appeal in vain to witnesses of your innocence. For if they are Lutheran or Catholic, they testify to nothing; but if they are Calvinist, no one will place any faith in them.

# P L E S S I S

If von Haslang, the Bavarian Colonel, were still alive, he would without doubt make such people blush with shame. For when he was captured by the Hungarians near Klatovy and led into the Bohemian camp at Rokycany, von Plessen—who was staying there at the time—as well as other counselors and officers, showed him, von Haslang, every friendly service, despite him being unknown to them. This was done as much <note>original: "per termine di Cortesia, & per debito di Cavaliere"</note> out of courtesy and the duty of a gentleman (to use the Italian's words), as well as out of the most humble respect toward the Duke of Bavaria, who had interceded on his behalf, requesting that he might have a safe withdrawal upon his given word and faith until he had better attended to his health. This request, however, could not be satisfied at that time, just as Haslang himself—who daily petitioned that he might ransom himself from the Hungarians with money and remain in the King's custody—could not have his prayer granted.

may, because the Hungarian General Bornemissa <note>General János Bornemisza, a military leader from Transylvania</note> claimed that he had written to his lord, the Prince in Transylvania <note>Gábor Bethlen, Prince of Transylvania and a key anti-Habsburg figure</note>, stating that he had captured von Haslang: therefore, he could not release him without the prior knowledge of his Prince. Otherwise, every goodwill and friendship was shown to him, Haslang: for the King <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King" of Bohemia</note>, who summoned him into his chamber, spoke to him graciously; the councilors, officers, and other royal servants also showed him all honor and goodwill. Furthermore, because he arrived in the camp poor, having been robbed by the Hungarians of his money, clothing, and other necessary things, they were as helpful to him in this regard as possible, so that von Haslang himself wrote these very words from Rokycany <note>A town in western Bohemia</note> to the Bohemian camp: **I am being treated here as an ambassador.** <note>original: "Ich werde hier als ein Legat gehalten." Likewise, when he was not feeling well, and the usual fare of the Hungarians in the field camp (for he had to make do with them in a room that smelled badly of onions and garlic) was contrary to his health, arrangements were made so that several better dishes from the King's kitchen were brought to him daily. In the same manner, Dr. Rumpff, the King's personal physician, took better care of the patient by royal command and performed his office with such diligence that the aforementioned von Haslang (who passed away shortly thereafter near Rakovník) was so mindful of the kindnesses received that he bequeathed 100 ducats <term>ducat: a gold coin used throughout Europe</term> to Dr. Rumpff.

This friendship was shown, among others, by von Plessen <note>Volrad von Plessen, a high-ranking advisor to Frederick V</note> to the distinguished, secret, and imprisoned Bavarian councilor at that time, even though he was not certain whether (should he fall into similar misfortune as von Haslang) such friendship would be shown to him in the [opposing] camp. However, after von Plessen arrived back in the Palatinate from Bohemia, he had to learn, contrary to his hopes and with a very troubled spirit, that his letters of gratitude had been sent to Munich, Würzburg, Aschaffenburg, Mainz, and Darmstadt (so that he might fall into everyone's hatred and suspicion). These were also soon thereafter translated into other languages and printed in "Paxburg" <note>A satirical or fictitious place name, likely a play on "Peace-town"</note> or another unnamed place under a false and fabricated title, and thus scattered, as it were, through the whole world.

...every true and upright Christian—of whatever religion they may be—will very easily recognize this. Likewise, in verse 45: A good man brings forth good from the good treasure of his heart, and an evil man brings forth nothing good from his evil treasure.

### F A B I V S.

Because von Plessen <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a prominent French Protestant strategist</note> believes his witnesses to be doubtful, he appeals to the dead, especially to the now-resting Lord von Haslang, who was not only an excellently experienced military commander but also a lover of the truth. Let von Plessen, therefore, appear in the comedy: oh, with what long-past times will he then recount the friendship shown by von Plessen! But he will, in the concluding speech, give him brief thanks: for although you, Plessy, have already shown him all good will, you have truly not released him from his entanglement <note>likely referring to imprisonment or legal bonds</note>, which would have been the greatest kindness to him as he was then wishing to give up his spirit. Indeed, even the Elector's intercession had no effect, though he graciously took up the cause of the Lord von Haslang’s weakness and desired that he be released from custody <note>original: "Custodi"</note> only until he might regain his former health. And why should I take pleasure in royal finery, and the bitter drinks of the physicians <note>original: "Artzten"</note> in my entanglement? Do not speak, Plessy, of what is becoming to a man, but do it, and thereafter leave me in peace: I hunger less, and would rather drink the physicians' <note>original: "Medicorum"</note> potion elsewhere. I wish, you might say, that I had done it, if I had been permitted to do so: the unmerciful Hungarians were opposed to mercy, and I praise your good will, which I would have praised more had I experienced it. The remaining kindnesses shown to the poor old man, of which you make so many words, I praise myself, and thereby also recognize the nature of your nobility: just continue always with such good works, and you will come to a secure way of life, which I wish for you; and, if you wish, I will be your leader, insofar as a Calvinist can be led: but he is accustomed to leading. You have refreshed me, Plessy, with the Holy Scripture, in that you would gladly pass yourself off as a good man. But permit me that I may, from your own words, recognize you thus through logic <note>original: "Logica"</note>: If von Plessen were a bad tree (as he is shamefully accused by the Paxbergers), he would bear no good fruit. But he has borne good fruit, in that before the

...good care was taken for the weak and imprisoned hearts of Haslang. Therefore, he is not a bad tree. The conclusion, Plessi, is quite elegant, but let the "Mainz-style" <note>likely a pun or reference to the Archbishopric of Mainz's rhetorical style</note> also please you. If Plessen were a good tree, he would also bear good fruit; but he has borne no good fruit. Therefore, he is also not a good tree. You, Plessi, try to prove your minor premise <term>Assumptum: the second part of a logical syllogism</term> with a single—and indeed minor—kindness shown to the Lord of Haslang, the Chief Sergeant. I, however, prove my premise with many more examples. For, beyond that, you have incited more to rebellion with your poisonous pen; you have given advice highly damaging to the common wealth; you have also undertaken harmful embassies <term>Legationes: diplomatic missions</term> against the same. This very writing of yours, which you cannot cloak with any smooth, flattering words, is an indication that you have borne shameful fruit. Since you then wish to have me as your judge, and bring your fruits and actions onto the path as an argument for your innocence, I desire nothing else: for I have now recognized you for who you are.

->P L E S S I U S.<-

From all of which it clearly appears that von Plessen—as one devoted to the Protestant <note>original: "Euangelischen"</note> religion, who has served the most prominent Protestant Princes of the Empire for many years now, and who until now has had no adverse or hostile dealings with persons of high or low standing—has weighty reasons to take to heart that his good name has now been injured by the thoughtless and false superscription and defamatory title spread here and there against all decency. Therefore, one cannot remain silent at all; rather, it is permitted and allowed by right of law to act against the inventor and poet of the aforementioned superscription and defamatory title—whether they be in Würzburg, Aschaffenburg, Mainz, or wherever else they may be. Everything that has been spat out there from the poisonous hearts and lying mouths of the Calvinists to the contempt and disparagement of the Lord of Plessen is hereby publicly shoved back into their own lying throats and bosoms, for as long and as much until they can convict von Plessen, as an honorably living and credible nobleman, with sufficient grounds of something else: which they, however, will never be able to demonstrate or prove in all eternity.

->F A B I U S.<-

You may, Plessi, write and speak whatever may occur to your angry mind; and though you may indeed flatter with your mouth and behave with fawning manners—

...nevertheless, you have harbored hostile intentions against us in your minds and have also carried them out. Although you were not among the worst, you cannot be counted among the good, because you are not found among them. You have dealt as you pleased with the lovers of the true religion and wished to plunge both parties <note>The author refers to the two main religious factions of the time.</note> (understand: the Lutherans and the Catholics) into utter ruin, if only fortune had been favorable to you. As for the title which your patience wishes to defer to the more vehement adversaries, you labor in vain in this regard: such a collar <note>A "Halßband" or collar here implies a yoke of shame or a hangman's noose.</note> befits your own throat. They did not give it to you so that they might receive it back; it does not suit ancient lovers of virtue, but rather you and other alarm-blowers <note>original: "Lermenbläsern" — agitators or warmongers.</note> who think of war in their hearts and promise peace with lying words, not without a particular stain upon their nobility.

### P L E S S I V S <note>Volrad von Plessen (1560–1631), a statesman and counselor to the Elector Palatine.</note>

At this point, the favorable reader must be reminded in like manner that von Plessen is accused by the Compiler (page 202) of being one of the foremost counselors, instigators, and causes of the Bohemian affair <note>The "Bohemian affair" refers to the 1618 revolt that triggered the Thirty Years' War.</note>, and again on page 37 in the second section, that he, along with Dr. Camerarius <note>Ludwig Camerarius, a key figure in the Protestant Union.</note> and others, wished to persuade many more to join the Union alliance. To this, it is answered that von Plessen was neither a director nor an instigator of such a league in this matter, because he never attended the negotiations with the Bohemian Estates and the Duke of Savoy; nor did he travel with the King <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate, the "Winter King."</note> to Upper Saxony, Prague, or into Moravia and Silesia for the rendering of homage. Instead, at his most humble request, he was left in Heidelberg throughout the entire winter until May of the year 1620, and only then was he summoned to Prague.

### F A B I V S <note>Likely a pseudonym for a critic or a specific voice in this dialogue-style polemic.</note>

Paper is not as pure a virgin as my Lord von Plessen: he has done everything, denies everything, and swears to everything. He did not go to Bohemia to sound the alarm, nor did he travel to Savoy to incite that sovereign to take up arms; he spun no webs elsewhere and did not stir up the slightest rumor. Such would also be the confession of Camerarius, if one were to ask him, and of many others besides. Yes, indeed! Because it has turned out poorly for you <note>original: "auff ein Lami außgangen" — an idiom meaning to fail or end in a "lame" fashion.</note>, no one advised it; but had it gone according to your head, then everyone would have advised it. This is how we are accustomed...

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues a dialogue or legal defense regarding the actions of Volrad von Plessen and his involvement in the Bohemian Revolt and the Protestant Union. The catchword from the previous page likely introduced the speaker Fabius or the context of the Chancery.</meta>

Human affairs are such: in adverse matters, we do not recognize our own counsel, but in fortunate ones, we claim the counsel of others as our own. If I read nothing in the Chancery <note>original: "Cantzeley"; the administrative office where state documents were kept</note>, if I read nothing of Plessen's work elsewhere; if the Transylvanian Legation <note>original: "Siebenbürgiſche Legation"; referring to diplomatic missions to Transylvania, a key ally of the Protestant cause</note> were unknown to me, and if you, Plessen, had not been a Councilor of Heidelberg, I would hold you in higher regard. But as it is, I cannot imagine or recognize as good a man who has written evil, done evil, and sat as an assessor to evil counsel.

->PLESSIUS.<-

Which is not recounted for the purpose of burdening those who were with the King in Bohemia and Silesia, or who accompanied their lords there—for these same people, as we have indicated above, will easily clear themselves <note>original: "purgiren"</note> before pious and upright people—but rather because these things actually and truly occurred regarding the person of Plessen himself. Furthermore, von Plessen attended few of the meetings of the Union <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states</note>, as is known to everyone. He was also never chosen, either alone or together with Dr. Camerarius <note>Ludwig Camerarius (1573–1651), a central figure in the Palatine government and the "Winter King's" cause</note>, to persuade anyone to join the Union: although the Union was permitted in itself and was rejected neither by Emperor Rudolf nor by Emperor Matthias. Thus, in this article, the Compiler has heavily insulted von Plessen, because he attacks the innocent Plessen with a sharp pen on account of such instigations.

->FABIUS.<-

You act very unwisely, Plessen, in pouring such things out before crafty people. For if you hold yourself innocent of doing what you recount, how then shall those who actually did those things be innocent? And yet you say you do not recount this to burden them. It would vex me greatly if it were said of me what others recognize as a dishonor to themselves, even as they so stiffly deny their committed deeds. That you withdrew from the meetings of the Union is to be attributed to your cunning: you considered the uncertainty of the outcome of the matter, and regarded it as either a matter of praise or dishonor; the latter you do not desire, but to await the former seemed dangerous to you. Meanwhile, you supposedly satisfied your office by proposing your counsels in secret, which others—who hoped for a fortunate outcome—were meant to execute publicly.

<meta>catchword: PLES-</meta>

# PLESSIS.
<s>S</s>
<meta>catchword: Lands</meta>

In the cited writing, page 207, the compiler <note>The "compiler" refers to the opposing author Plessis is critiquing</note> strikes out at the Prince of Anhalt out of pure passion and envy. For even these words—that the "Bavarian Prince looks to his own security and to the preservation of his subjects, lordships, and revenues" (praise which indeed befits a cautious and sensible Prince)—he does not let pass without punishment, but adds this poisonous parenthesis: "(The Prince of Anhalt and his faction, however, look toward foreign lands and kingdoms)." But the Prince of Anhalt will know well how to defend <note>original: "defendiren"</note> himself against such slanders in due time. And we cannot, nevertheless, leave untouched—though only briefly and in passing (as a rooster runs over glowing coals)—what was specifically negotiated in Munich during the ongoing Truchsess-Cologne War <note>The Cologne War (1583–1588), triggered by Archbishop Gebhard Truchsess von Waldburg's conversion to Protestantism</note>, so that several noble places belonging to the ancient Archbishopric of Cologne might be appropriated by the House of Bavaria and incorporated into it forever. Whether this does not also mean "striving after other lordships," and whether such a thing is consistent with the foundations, laws, and liberties of the said Bishopric and its Estates, the sincere and impartial reader may judge for himself. Furthermore, it is not unknown to the compiler that what has just been recounted can be proven with original Bavarian manuscripts and in certified <note>original: "vidimirter"</note> form.

### FABIUS.

So that von Plessen might not only deal with his own affairs, he comes somewhat to the aid of the crumbling reputation of the Prince of Anhalt regarding the desire for other lordships and kingdoms. But how does he undertake to erase this stain? "The Prince of Anhalt will know well how to defend himself against these slanders in due time." At what time, however, my dear Pless? This present moment demands your defense! At this time, be your patron's advocate if you can, and speak to the matter. For will you be able to deny that you have striven after other lordships and seized them? There were even more provinces remaining, which you, with insatiable and inhuman greed, would have swallowed long ago, had not a sword thrust into your maw postponed the supper. It is said that one of the envoys dispatched to the Electorate of Bavaria <note>original: "Chur Bayern"</note> in the year 1619 let these meddlesome words slip: "My Lord has so many young lords and ladies; it is therefore truly necessary that he also has more..."

<summary>Plessen accuses the House of Bavaria of territorial greed during the Cologne War, while Fabius counters by mocking the Prince of Anhalt's failed ambitions and the "insatiable greed" of his faction.</summary>
<keywords>Prince of Anhalt, House of Bavaria, Cologne War, territorial disputes, political polemic, 1619 diplomacy, Protestant Union vs Catholic League</keywords>

...lands (by which he understood the Bishoprics) / over which he might place so many young lords. A fine display! Either—whoever you may be—beget no children / or else nourish those you have begotten / but do so from your own means. And indeed, whether the matter was thus / or otherwise / it is unknown to no one / that the Prince of Anhalt <note>Christian I, Prince of Anhalt-Bernburg (1568–1630), a key advisor to the Elector Palatine and a leading figure in the Protestant Union.</note> sought after other people's goods / and thereby lost his own. But so that the blame <note>original: "das Bad" (the bath); a metaphorical expression meaning to pour the blame or trouble onto someone.</note> might not be poured solely into the shoes of the Prince of Anhalt / the Duke of Bavaria <note>Maximilian I, Duke of Bavaria (1573–1651), leader of the Catholic League.</note> is also summoned into the company of the covetous / because in his council it was previously discussed / how namely a good portion of the Cologne Lordship <note>The Archbishopric of Cologne, a vital ecclesiastical territory.</note> might be appropriated to the House of Bavaria / and eternally incorporated. But where shall I turn in this case? What appearance of excuse shall I offer for the presumption of such things? The great hand / the great seal of the great Prince lies in my way. Yes indeed, my dear Pless <note>Refers to Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a prominent French Protestant writer and diplomat.</note> / I desire to set neither the hand / nor the signet before me / but rather the words / which were strengthened by that hand and signet. At that time a very violent war was waged / and among other things / was also approved by the Heidelberg Council. Therefore the Duke in Bavaria / alongside the King in Spain / with great war expenses / checked such disorder / and so that he might become capable of the same again / he desired this / or that security / or even to purchase a portion of the land / which he might take against debts / so that perhaps such an Electoral Hat <term>Churhut: the hat or cap symbolizing the status and dignity of a Prince-Elector of the Holy Roman Empire</term> would not remain forever stuck in debt / and the Bavarian Prince always have to advance money: for no one is accustomed to serve others at his own pay. Therefore, leave off your babbling / about the unlawful seizure of the land / since no one in Munich has made many words of the same / and you truly show yourselves to be quite poorly armed / in that you protect yourselves with lies.

### The Eighth Chapter.

# That the Elector of Heidelberg, together with the Bohemians and other allies, through the mediation of the Prince of Transylvania, undertook to make themselves tributary to the Turkish Emperor and to introduce him into the Holy Roman Empire.

->Information and Answer.<-

->P L E S S I V S.<-
<note>Plessius likely refers to Philippe de Mornay, Lord of Plessis-Marly, a prominent Protestant diplomat and writer.</note>

In order to answer this false accusation with few words, I ask the reader to inspect the instructions given to Johann von Cöllen, the legate sent to Constantinople by the King <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King"</note> and the Bohemian Estates, as well as the incorporated and confederated provinces. In those instructions, he will find nothing regarding any tribute, and nothing that would tend toward the ruin of the Holy Roman Empire, or anything contrary to reason and the customs which the ambassadors of other Kings and Christian Princes are accustomed to maintain at the Ottoman Porte <note>The central government of the Ottoman Empire</note>. The form of these instructions can be found at the end of the booklet entitled *The Mirror of the Ban*, number 18. Indeed, the favorable reader will be able to grasp—as the saying goes—with his own hands how utterly groundless this accusation is when he runs through the articles of the alliance established between the Prince of Transylvania on one side, and the Bohemians and their associates on the other, and when he reads the public protests against such claims. For it is found therein that the Bohemian, Moravian, and Silesian Estates promised the aforementioned Prince <note>Gabriel Bethlen, Prince of Transylvania</note>, in addition to the usual aid, a further 50,000 Thalers for the protection of the border fortresses built against the Turks in Hungary. Whether the Turks are being drawn into the Holy Roman Empire because of this alliance is something everyone may judge for themselves.

->F A B I V S.<-

I am not, dear Pless, of such a blunt and foolish understanding—

<summary>This page defends the Elector Palatine and the Bohemian Estates against charges of treasonous collaboration with the Ottoman Empire, citing diplomatic instructions and financial records as proof of their innocence. It argues that their alliance with Transylvania was intended to strengthen Hungarian border defenses rather than invite a Turkish invasion.</summary>
<keywords>Elector Palatine, Frederick V, Bohemia, Transylvania, Ottoman Empire, diplomacy, Thirty Years' War, Johann von Cöllen, Gabriel Bethlen, Hungary</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the catchword "provided," leading into this discussion of political deception and secret alliances.</meta>

that your maneuvers should deceive me; and you exert yourselves—though a blush of shame would be more fitting for you—so that you might push away from yourselves that shameful friendship with the Turks, which is unbecoming of Christians. You exert yourselves, indeed, but in vain. For although there might be nothing to criticize in your so highly praised and public *Instruction* <note>A formal set of diplomatic orders or briefing documents</note>, there was without doubt something blameworthy in the secret instruction known only to the Ambassador; there was also something commanded only by word of mouth, which was to be presented orally to the Turkish Emperor. I know your ways and your craftiness, for I have often been led into the dark by you. If we have read nothing, we have nonetheless seen much, and have—to our great misfortune—become well aware of it.

For it was that very Transylvanian <note>Likely referring to Gabriel Bethlen, Prince of Transylvania, who allied with the Ottomans against the Habsburgs</note> whom you, as an ambassador, advised toward warlike arrogance; he not only called upon the Turks for help but also led a great army, gathered from the Hungarian garrisons, to the devastation of Moravia and to our ruin. I shall remain silent for now regarding von Thurn <note>Count Heinrich Matthias von Thurn, a leader of the Bohemian Revolt</note>, that plague of Christendom, who for so long a time stayed in Constantinople with the Turkish Emperor to lobby for help in the shedding of innocent Christian blood and the destruction of the common Fatherland.

You are betrayed, dear Plessis <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader</note>, and indeed by your own selves. You draw the sword against me and kill me, and yet meanwhile cry out with unheard-of impudence that you have nothing to do with the sword: have the weapons terrified you? Thus are all Calvinist rebels infatuated, who denied that they called upon the Turks for help, and while they deny it, they nonetheless murder in a most gruesome manner their fellows who were baptized with one baptism and one name. Let the favorable reader run through the recently published Heidelberg Consultations; he will find therein arguments befitting a Turkish scribe and, as it were, a permitted tyranny. But what was in those very documents? If you bear no guilt in this, why then did you dispatch your ambassadors to the Turk? Is it proper for you, contrary to the Ordinances of the Holy Empire and in contempt of the Emperor, to either form alliances or establish good friendship with the Turk? The Emperor is permitted, for the good of the Empire, to establish alliances or to protect rights weakened by war against the enemies of Germany. "Calvinist" is a private name, and has been tolerated until now only by petition; it is not fitting for him to boast because of his fortune and to step into the master's footsteps, when he is hardly to be esteemed as more than a servant. In general, however, it is forbidden to everyone that those subjected to another's scepter should ally themselves with foreigners,

<summary>The author accuses Calvinist leaders and diplomats of secret, treacherous alliances with the Ottoman Empire against the Holy Roman Emperor, specifically citing the actions of Transylvanian rebels and the Count of Thurn. He argues that such diplomacy violates Imperial law and threatens the unity of Christendom.</summary>
<keywords>Calvinism, Ottoman Empire, diplomacy, treason, Thirty Years' War, Gabriel Bethlen, Count von Thurn, Philippe de Mornay, Heidelberg, Holy Roman Empire</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the polemical defense against accusations of conspiracy between the Protestant estates, Transylvania, and the Ottoman Empire during the early stages of the Thirty Years' War.</meta>

...unless the Supreme Prince <note>original: "Obrist Fürst"</note> consents to it. He who flatters my enemy cannot be my friend. Let anyone who serves a master elsewhere pack up and leave my house.

But you, so that you might escape such shameless suspicion, presume to boast of your "eternal" foresight regarding the Hungarian border fortresses. To turn away the Turkish military power, 50,000 Thalers were promised from these "eternal" funds: Dear God, help us, what a great treasure! You could hardly buy a midday meal for a well-formed army with that. We shall now take note of your generosity—not indeed how great it was, but rather where it was intended to go. You did not have to fear the Turk as your good friend, but rather Emperor Ferdinand, against whom you presumed to strengthen such camps so that he, being excluded from all sides, would either have to enter into the peace which you yourselves desired or else abdicate his kingdoms. And in such a way, the Turk is undoubtedly "lured" into the Empire, as you say, namely when he sees that the pass is blocked for the Emperor everywhere but opened for him: since you did not call upon him so that he should come to your aid while the gates were locked.

->P L E S S I V S.<-
<note>Refers to Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a French Protestant writer and diplomat often cited in these polemics.</note>

With similar falsification, the Compiler's account is wrapped around the young Prince, Stephen Bethlen of Ictar, the cousin of the Prince of Transylvania <note>Gabriel Bethlen</note>. This particular detail is taken from those gossipy pamphlets <note>original: "waschhafftigen Scartecken"</note> which were spread through public print under the title of *The Post-Rider*. For the aforementioned young Prince, at that time barely 12 years old, was sent to Heidelberg with only two tutors <note>original: "Præceptoribus"</note> and 3 or 4 servants—who were mostly students of Holy Scripture—so that he might stay there for a time and from there travel to France and Italy; indeed, all things for such a journey had already been prepared. But that the alleged political business or correspondence was handled by the mentioned Prince (whose age was far too young for him to be capable of such dealings) or by his servants (whose profession kept them from such things), is absolutely denied; the Compiler must demonstrate and prove such a thing. Of equal "truth" is the story that this same Prince traveled to Frankfurt and was drawn into the electoral proceedings by the Electoral Palatine Councilors: since the Electoral Councilors and...

and other honorable members of the nobility present, as well as other credible persons, can further testify that this young Prince was never seen in the Elector Palatine’s <note>The Elector Palatine was one of the high-ranking princes of the Holy Roman Empire who had the right to elect the Emperor.</note> lodgings; rather, he stayed in Frankfurt for no more than two days, but as soon as he had shown his reverence to His Imperial Majesty, he departed for Marburg to visit the university there.

### FABIUS.

I knew well, Plessy, that you were too clever to admit that the young Bethlehem <note>Referring to the son or relative of Gábor Bethlen, the Prince of Transylvania.</note> stayed with you for a time on account of general business. For what—you might perhaps say—could a boy have negotiated? He did what he was commanded to do, acting as a sort of hostage between the Transylvanian <note>original: "Siebenbürger"</note> and the Count Palatine, and as a sign of confirmed friendship. The rest was carried out by the regents placed over such youth, whom you call "Preceptors" <note>term: Preceptors (Latin: praeceptores) — private tutors or teachers responsible for the education and conduct of young nobles.</note>. These, however, were theologians. Your custom is well known to me, as is this: that your theologians are dedicated to the god Mars <note>The Roman god of war; a critique suggesting these religious men were actually promoting conflict.</note> and both learn and teach iron doctrines. And what if they had used the study of the liberal arts as a pretext, so that they might all the better exercise and practice with weapons? A murderous dagger can hang beneath the hypocritical cloak of your theology, and it is your habit that you wish to be regarded as different fellows than you actually are. If the boy Bethlehem avoided your inn at Frankfurt, he did so out of the cunning of others, so that he would not have to betray the great friendship; meanwhile, however, you did not bar the door to his Preceptors, who visited you on account of the commission they held. They were meant to know everything, so that they might report everything back to the elder Bethlehem.

### The Ninth Chapter.

## That it has been clearly proven that in Bohemia it was not about Religion, but rather about the Region and the Territory.

->Information and Answer.<-

->P L E S S I V S.<-
<note>Plessius and Fabius are likely pseudonyms or representative personas used in this polemical dialogue to represent the Protestant and Catholic viewpoints respectively.</note>

**I**n the 32nd leaf, in the verse beginning "in the same manner," etc., in the second main section, almost identical words are to be found. We would, however, dearly like to know how, when, and by whom such a thing was proven from the fundamental facts: since in the Bavarian-Anhaltine Chancery <note>The "Anhaltine Chancery" refers to a famous collection of captured diplomatic documents from the Protestant Union, published by the Catholics to expose their enemies' secrets</note> no such fundamental proof is found; rather, the opposite and the truth of the matter itself is brought to light by the following words. First, that of the four articles which were presented by the Duke in Bavaria to the assembled Estates at Würzburg in the year 1620 for deliberation, the first was: That it was namely to be feared that if the Bohemian Kingdom were withdrawn from the Roman Catholics, the entire business of the Catholics in the Empire might take a particular hurt and fall into decline; also whether, how, and in what manner the Catholics should take up the Bohemian matter. This is also indicated to some extent in that letter which he sent at almost the same time to Dr. Camerarius. From this it clearly appears that the Roman Catholics have always strived, and even at this time still intend, how they might namely maintain their religion in Bohemia, but keep the Bohemian Kingdom itself under the obedience of the Roman See <note>original: "Römischen Stuls"</note>; and that in this case they have looked solely and only upon religion, but have never considered whether the matter was right or wrong.

->F A B I V S.<-

You, Plessius, are the cleverest and most beautiful among all men in that you wish to persuade the reader that you [acted] in Bohemia not for the sake of the region or the territory—which you would have gladly withdrawn from the Imperial Majesty—but for the sake of religion,

<summary>This page presents a dialogue debating whether the Bohemian Revolt was motivated by religious freedom or territorial power, citing the 1620 Würzburg assembly. The Protestant speaker (Plessius) argues that the Catholic side was solely focused on maintaining the Pope's authority over the kingdom regardless of justice.</summary>
<keywords>Bohemian Revolt, Thirty Years' War, Anhaltine Chancery, Duke of Bavaria, Würzburg, Roman Catholic Church, Dr. Camerarius, political vs religious motivation</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the initial accusations regarding the causes of the Bohemian Revolt; the catchword "verder" (further) suggests a continuation of the legal and moral defense of the Catholic League's intervention.</meta>

half / creates a tumult / and draws us in as witnesses: For the Elector of Bavaria <note>Maximilian I (1573–1651), a key figure in the Catholic League</note> exhorted the Würzburg Convention <note>A meeting of the Catholic League in Würzburg to coordinate military support for the Emperor</note> with arguments, among others, to come to the aid of His Imperial Majesty: namely, that religion in Bohemia and other rebellious provinces stood in danger, and could even be eradicated, if one were to permit the Calvinists such wanton robbery. In this, His Electoral Grace prophesied truly. But what thoughts do you conceive regarding this? Have you perhaps—because religion was a matter of concern to us—disregarded religion for that very reason? What a fine piece of chatter you have whistled. You challenge me to combat out of pure wantonness / and while I protest / that I appear on the battlefield for the protection of my good name / you are able to say / that this combat arose for you because of your dissension. This indeed was my intent / but your own grim and frivolous spirit incited you to such an uproar.

We have said / that you took up arms / partly because you were weary of your Lord <note>The Holy Roman Emperor, Ferdinand II</note> / partly weary of the obedience due to him / and partly weary of lawful freedom / in that you strove for another's rule / for another Crown <note>Refers to the Elector Palatine Frederick V's acceptance of the Bohemian throne</note> / and thereby excited such great heartache: Since the Count Palatine <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King"</note> would not have plunged himself and his House into such great danger with such great waste of his resources for the sake of his Gospel <note>The Protestant/Calvinist faith</note> / had he not desired to be a King. So too the Bohemians themselves had sufficient cause / that they / for the sake of a few minor Calvinist churches / should cast such an excellent Kingdom before the enemy like a piece of prey: Moreover, they themselves presumed to gain more in this case / in that / after such robbery / they wished to further propagate the Genevan religion <note>Calvinism</note> / but on the other hand abolish the Catholic ceremonies. 

The desire for the territory came first / but religion followed in the rear / and the dutiful respect toward His Imperial Majesty / who at that time was being treated unfairly / girded the weapons onto the Catholics: the endangered religion / and other provinces of the Holy Roman Empire would have been devastated by the raging sword. Therefore, you do not shoot far from the target when you say: We had resolved to protect our religion in Bohemia. But in this you commit a great error / when you further add / that it never entered our minds to consider whether the matter were right or wrong: We have considered such many times / and the more often we have done so / the more just we have found it to be. For who would stand in doubt / that he does right / when he lends aid and assistance to his Lord who has been driven out by rebels? And when he sees that his religion / and fellow believers are pursued with the sword through the presumption of restless heads? When he himself...

<summary>This page presents a polemical defense of the Catholic League's intervention in the Bohemian Revolt, arguing that the Protestant "robbery" of the crown was motivated by political ambition rather than religious piety. The author asserts that the defense of the Emperor and the Catholic faith was a legally and morally justified response to Calvinist aggression.</summary>

<keywords>Thirty Years' War, Catholic League, Elector of Bavaria, Frederick V of the Palatinate, Bohemia, Calvinism, Imperial Authority, Counter-Reformation</keywords>

is closest to the ruin of dissolute people? Among yourselves, you have not considered whether you had a legitimate cause to begin the war. Desire forces no counsel together: it deliberates on nothing: it wills, it proceeds, and it maintains; but it loses alongside this as well, and indeed with a very unequal loss.

### PLESSIS <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a prominent French Protestant figure often used as a character in such polemical dialogues.</note>

If now, secondly, the matter of religion in Bohemia is not being dealt with, why then have so many reforms and changes been undertaken following the suffered Prague defeat? Why have so many preachers been driven into misery <note>original: "Elend," referring here to forced exile or banishment</note>? Why have so many churches been barred to them? The reply—namely, that only a few Hussite <note>Followers of Jan Hus; a pre-Reformation Christian movement in Bohemia.</note> preachers were banished, not solely because of religion but also because they had participated in the rebellion and promoted the new election—does nothing at all for the case. For one can respond quite elegantly to that with a question: Oh, what then have the obedient and peaceable Lutherans committed as a crime, that in Prague and other places more, their churches and schools have been closed to them, and their preachers likewise banished into misery?

### FABIUS

The second conclusion, Plessis, is this, which however proves nothing regarding the started religious war. When you were driven back by force from your unlawful possession, we came to the aid of the religion that is now suffering shipwreck. Therefore, did you not take up arms and weapons to hunt for other lands and people? Nowhere will it hold together with you, and I do not deny that changes were undertaken in Prague, because you overturned everything; nor can anyone follow the Calvinists without seeing a change. If other Evangelical <note>term: "Evangelical" (Evangelische) here refers generally to Protestants, specifically Lutherans in this context.</note> preachers, alongside your Calvinist preachers, were banished from the land, this arose neither from the war nor the end of the same, since it was of primary concern to the Catholics how they might confirm the Imperial Majesty's <note>Refers to the Holy Roman Emperor, Ferdinand II.</note> Empire, maintain religion in its essence <note>original: "in ihrem esse," using the Latin philosophical term for being/existence.</note>, and tame the rebelling Calvinists. After the conquest of the Bohemian Kingdom and the subduing of the destroyers of peace, the Emperor is permitted to do what is also allowed to other princes in their dominions, especially when those deserve it who now complain about their banishment. I ask nothing about the Hussites, because the Empire owes them nothing: they have now [held] Bohemia more...

<summary>This page features a heated dialogue between Plessis and Fabius regarding the religious persecutions in Bohemia following the Battle of White Mountain, specifically questioning why Lutherans were punished alongside rebellious Calvinists. Fabius defends the Emperor's right to restore Catholic order and punish those who destabilized the peace of the Empire.</summary>

->PLESSIUS<-
<note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Protestant diplomat and writer.</note>

And why then, thirdly, (if it was not a matter of religion in Bohemia) did Emperor Matthias have an express letter written to the Count of Bucquoy <note>Charles Bonaventure de Longueval, Count of Bucquoy, a commander of the Imperial forces.</note>: namely, that he should depart from the Netherlands in all haste and join the army which he already had assembled under the banners, whereby he would serve the Roman Catholic religion as well as the House of Austria very well. This letter, published through public printing, is still in almost everyone's hands.

->FABIUS<-

You are always playing on the same violin <note>An idiom meaning "harping on the same point."</note>. His Imperial Majesty wrote to the aforementioned Count of Bucquoy in Bohemia, among other things, that a good opportunity was at hand to serve religion and the House of Austria well. Therefore, [you argue] the Bohemians did not take up arms because of the territories destined for their plunder.

->PLESSIUS<-

And what is more, fourthly, if religion was not taken into account in Bohemia, why then did Father Sabinus, a Capuchin monk and Viennese court preacher, in the congratulatory sermon which he delivered regarding the battle and the victory obtained before Prague <note>The Battle of White Mountain, 1620.</note>, admonish the highest authorities that they should—among other punishments well-deserved by the Bohemians—take back the Letter of Majesty <term>Letter of Majesty: A 1609 charter granted by Emperor Rudolf II guaranteeing religious freedom to Bohemian Protestants.</term> and tear it into small pieces? Should these mere words not be sufficient to prove the thesis: namely, that in Bohemia it was indeed a matter of religion? And whether the Bohemian affair was ultimately about religion, let the favorable reader see what Dr. Matthias Hoe <note>Matthias Hoe von Hoenegg, a prominent Lutheran court preacher in Saxony.</note> has further written about this, which letter has been added below at No. 5.

->FABIUS<-

### The Tenth Chapter.

## The Protestants in the Empire need not fear a foreign government—whether of the Pope, the Spaniard, or the Roman Catholic power—but should and could safely place their faith in their sincere declarations and promises to maintain both spiritual and secular peace.

->Information and Answer.<-

->P L E S S I V S.<- <note>Likely referring to Philippe de Mornay, Lord of Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot writer, or a pseudonym used in this controversy.</note>

The Compiler <note>The author of the opposing treatise being critiqued.</note> sells this very point in many places in his chancellery, and the matter indeed has a particular appearance of truth in itself. However, the favorable reader will easily notice the deceptive equivocation <note>The use of ambiguous language to hide the truth.</note> in his doubtful words, like a snake hidden under the weeds. On page 11, at the verse "Therefore etc.," in the first section, the Compiler declares that the Roman Catholics have sought, wished, and desired nothing else than to maintain what is theirs, as well as the statutes of the Holy Roman Empire and both spiritual and secular peace; and that they do not wish to disturb or burden any Estate adhering to the Augsburg Confession <note>The primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church.</note> in its territory, jurisdiction, and justice, much less (as some heated counselors are said to have spoken and written) to drive them into exile or ever rob them of their land and people. But let the kind-hearted reader pay somewhat closer attention to these words. First, he says the Catholics sought nothing other than "what is theirs"—namely, those bishoprics which were taken after the Peace of Religion <note>The Peace of Augsburg (1555), which established the legal basis for Lutheranism and Catholicism to coexist in the Empire.</note> and have been held until now by the—

<summary>This page disputes Catholic claims of peaceful intent toward Protestants, specifically critiquing the "Compiler's" argument that Catholic authorities only seek to reclaim their rightful property without disturbing Lutheran estates.</summary>
<keywords>Religious Peace of Augsburg, Matthias Hoë von Hoënegg, anti-Calvinism, Catholic-Protestant relations, Holy Roman Empire, ecclesiastical territories</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion of foreign influence; this page continues the argument regarding the Protestant princes' possession of ecclesiastical territories.</meta>

the Protestant <note>original: "Evangelischen" — referring to the Lutheran and Reformed princes of the Holy Roman Empire</note> Princes / under the titles of Bishops / and Administrators / have possessed; among which are to be understood the Bishoprics of Magdeburg / Bremen / Minden / Osnabrück / Halberstadt / Verden / Lübeck / Ratzeburg / Schwerin / Kammin / Hersfeld / etc. These [the Catholics] hold to be their own Bishoprics / and recognize / in these possessions / no right / nor justice / just as they do not recognize mere continued occupation. This is seen in the more extensive letter from the three Spiritual Prince-Electors / to the late Vice-Chancellor of the Empire, Stralendorff (regarding the point of the Administrator of Magdeburg's request to Emperor Matthias for protection and preservation): In which they also maintain / and fully hope / that his Imperial Majesty will relieve them, the clergy, of this great burden (for these are their formal words <note>original: "verba formalia"</note>) at the earliest opportunity / that is / to restore their Bishoprics to them again. Indeed, the current Prince-Elector of Cologne / said several years ago to a protesting Prince / of the Empire / (who is still alive to this very day, expressly) / that it had now come so far / that the Protestants must restore all ecclesiastical properties (making no distinction between Archbishoprics / Bishoprics / Monasteries / or other church assets / nor even regarding the Elector of Saxony himself) / and give way to the Catholics / or else / together with their children / they must convert to the Catholic religion. And indeed, this declaration from Cologne requires no evasion / even though the doubtful words of the Compiler <note>The "Compilator" likely refers to the author of a specific legal or diplomatic collection being debated</note> might seem different at first glance: although even these / through the doctrine of equivocation <term>Equivocation: the use of ambiguous language to conceal the truth or avoid commitment</term>, can easily be reconciled. For the Compiler says / that the Catholics desire nothing else / than what they hold to be their own / and what was due to them / by right and by the statutes of the Peace of Religion <note>The Peace of Augsburg (1555), which attempted to settle the religious conflict in the Empire</note> / and finally that they did not desire to offend the Protestants in their own lands: But the Bishoprics were by no means the territories of the Protestants / but belonged / by virtue of the Peace of Religion / to the Catholics / and would therefore / as the Prince-Elector of Cologne says / be rightfully demanded back. Therefore, this declaration / and the Compiler's words / by means of equivocation / are in effect of the same meaning. Therefore, let the Protestants / who possess ecclesiastical properties / well consider / whether more faith is to be placed in the aforementioned Cologne's round and express declaration / or rather in the doubtful and hidden words of the Compiler.

It is also true that we have kept the peace with those related to the **Augsburg Confession** <note>The primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church, established in 1530</note>, and have not violated the faith we pledged. Indeed, we have not offended you **Calvinists** <note>Followers of John Calvin; at this time, they often lacked the same legal protections as Lutherans under Imperial law</note> either secretly or openly; on the contrary, we have been frequently offended by you, both secretly and openly. There is no "snake in the grass" <note>A metaphor for hidden treachery</note> among us, unless perhaps some Calvinist has hidden himself within our ranks. For when have we broken the spiritual or secular peace? We have indeed waged war, but only when we were forced to do so. 

And furthermore, what injustice is there when we demand back what is ours? You make a catalog of those **bishoprics** <note>The office or jurisdiction of a bishop</note> which, by right, are ours and belong to us by virtue of the **Treaty of Passau** <note>A 1552 agreement guaranteeing religious freedoms to Lutherans, which the author argues also protects Catholic property</note>. Perhaps you wish to annul and abolish such a treaty, just as you respect no pacts. Yet we have permitted the possessors to keep our lawful bishoprics for a long time now out of German peace-loving connivance and oversight, being accustomed to acting with law and justice. If our gall had overflowed out of "Calvinist heat," and we had preferred weapons over laws, who could have stopped us if we had reclaimed what was ours? 

The Calvinists apply themselves diligently, and through great robbery, to seize the property of other people. And who would now dare to deny that such a thing is a sensitive grievance to the **Catholics**? You complain constantly about I know not what great grievances, and yet in the meantime, you grieve us. You reject a judge of the Catholic religion, and yet you yourselves have abandoned that very religion? Should we therefore step down from such a long-standing possession so that you might take it over? We understand the plan, but we do not accept it. 

With boisterous shouting, you demand impartial assessors at the **Imperial Chamber Court in Speyer** <note>The highest judicial body in the Holy Roman Empire</note>, and yet you have made them partisan yourselves. Should we then vacate the seats of honor so that you might occupy them? From ancient times, we have had the right to send a certain number of councilors to the Imperial judicial bench; why should we now diminish that number so that yours might be increased? Increase your own from your own side, and we will keep what is ours. If you suffer any damage, you bring it upon yourselves. Come back to us instead, since it was you who separated from us. We have remained steadfast in the same footsteps for many hundreds of years past; but you have set your foot elsewhere. Stay now, if it pleases you, in your place, and we will stay in ours—which we occupied by right in times past, still possess by right, and with right—

The speech of the Most Serene Prince-Elector of Cologne is entirely straightforward and without evasion, but your speech, set upon screws <note>meaning: slippery or unstable speech</note>, is accustomed to deviating from the truth quite often. What His Electoral Grace of Cologne may have said is unknown to me; and I would not believe your Prince even if he reported it himself—now I regard what you recount in this case however I please. You make much of it, and consider it such a great matter when I say this: I only repeat my own point: You have striven after the property of others and made it your spoil. Princes are often accustomed to say something in jest, and commonly to the end that a further-spreading greed might be kept in check to some extent. And in such a way, the Compiler does not fight with the Elector with words, but rather you fight with both of them, so that you might be overcome by both.

->P L E S S I V S.<-

On the 44th leaf and in the second main section or point, the Compiler introduces similar equivocations <note>original: "æquivocationes"; the use of ambiguous language to hide the truth</note>: namely, that it is not the intention of the Catholics to prescribe anything to the Estates adhering to the Augsburg Confession <note>The primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church, presented in 1530</note> within their own lordship or jurisdiction. But he does not say that the Roman Catholics would leave the Bishoprics to the said Estates, but rather this: if only one would allow the Catholics to maintain their ancient and laudable customs of the Empire (that is, in his opinion, the Lower Saxon Bishoprics which, by virtue of the Peace of Religion <note>The Peace of Augsburg (1555), which legally established the principle of "whose realm, his religion"</note>, belonged to the Romanists <note>A polemical term for those loyal to the Pope in Rome</note>), through the preservation of which the Empire had always flourished (that is, if these men were to have more votes in the Imperial Diets and, without distinction, held the administration of justice in their hands in all places of the Empire), and if they were not continually drawn into dispute one after another.

->F A B I V S.<-

*The Compiler has used no equivocation, but you are the one who does and uses the same. In saying that the Protestants would have peace from us if only one would leave to the Catholics what is theirs, you insert this parenthesis: (that is, in his opinion, the Lower Saxon Bishoprics which, by virtue of the Peace of Religion, belonged to the Romanists). Do you speak rightly and well of the matter here, Plessius? You accuse me of an equivocation, and yet you provide me with the very same thing yourself. The Compiler, in the passage translated and perverted by you, understood only that which is ours in truth itself, but not what belongs to us by right.*

### FABIUS.

Because we have promised true friendship to the Protestants, provided they make use of what belongs to us without hindrance, and that not first one thing, then another, be pinched away. These words, says my Plessius, could better rhyme with the Catholics: but for what reason? In the lands of the Protestants, various innovations are desired by the monks, Jesuits, Capuchins, and other Roman-superstitious orders. But how dare you bring such a thing to the floor against the public, sun-clear truth, when your vanity and futility are openly revealed? Among the Protestants, we count many Princes and many Imperial Cities. Be bold and show me a single Jesuit, or Capuchin, or any other priest in the Princely territories? And what kind of innovation do such people seek in those places where they are neither present nor tolerated? Where are their desires executed? Is this done by them in writing, or do they dictate laws to the preachers <note>note: original: "Prädicanten" - a term often used for Protestant ministers</note>? Other Imperial Cities are entirely Lutheran; others are mixed with Catholics, and have neither Jesuits nor monks among them, just like the Princes. In these matters, the Monks, Jesuits, and Capuchins <note>The Capuchins are a reform branch of the Franciscan order, known for their traditionalism during the Counter-Reformation.</note> remain according to Catholic laws, and are helpful either to themselves or to their fellow believers, or otherwise devoted to holy divine service. If they ever begin any innovation (which is seldom customary among them, as they retain the ancient Church ceremonies), they do so among their own people: but how does this concern the opposing party? Do the Calvinists then presume to prescribe ceremonies to us? Do they wish to have the Spiritual Directory over us—namely, when and how we should hold the Early Mass and other Masses, or when we should sing the **We Praise Thee, O God** <note>original: "Te Deum laudamus," the ancient Latin hymn of joy and thanksgiving.</note>? We ourselves know the right time: we wish to sing the same when the Calvinists have finally vacated the Empire. Let us be allowed to manage our own affairs, even among those who disagree with us on account of religion. We make use of our rights, and because of this, no power shall be diminished from the preachers <term>Prædicanten: a term often used by Catholics to refer to Protestant ministers</term>, nor shall anything be withdrawn from their ministry. Indeed, if these men themselves ventured to change something daily in their ceremonies, our people would not interfere with them, but would all the more observe the inconstancy of other people, which at another time would grant their authors a better mind. And indeed, that the Calvinists frequently seek innovation, you people of Heidelberg are a notable example. For how often in our time have you changed your religion? Now you have swallowed, without any distinction, sometimes an unleavened morsel, sometimes a salty one, because you believe nothing and, like gods, accept nothing.

### FABIUS.
