If the son showed himself obedient to the Emperor, what did he prove by it? He rather leaped to the aid of his father—the Emperor's enemy—with deed and counsel, and was minded toward His Imperial Majesty in the same way as his father, even venturing to destroy him, had he not been nearly defeated unto the head <note>An idiom for a total or crushing defeat</note> at Wimpfen <note>The Battle of Wimpfen (1622), a major Catholic victory in the Thirty Years' War</note>. Precisely that justice which restores to each man his own, that which is due to him. ->P L E S S I V S<-
<note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a prominent French Protestant writer and diplomat often called the "Huguenot Pope."</note>

As for what finally concerns the Pope, and what the Estates adhering to the Augsburg Confession <note>The primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church, presented in 1530.</note> have to fear or not fear from him, it is sufficiently well known that not all Popes have been equally zealous against the Lutherans and Calvinists, as they are called. For some wished to exterminate the heretics by force, as Pope Pius, the fifth of this name, did; others, however, undertook to convert them through gentler ways and means, which was the opinion of Gregory XIII and Pope Clement VIII. Moreover, experience has testified that these latter achieved more through the aforementioned means than Pope Pius V. The current occupant of the Papal throne, however, is held by everyone to be a modest, good, and discreet gentleman. He indeed wished at first to be regarded as a universal father, and as impartial, not wishing to interfere in the factions of the King of France and Spain. But because the Spaniards noticed this, they easily brought him over from such modest discretion to their side, and proposed a marriage between the wealthy Princess of Venosa and his brother's son—who was somewhat over 11 or 12 years old (otherwise called Count Nicholas)—and thus completely won over the good Pope. 

But because the good old gentleman feared he might perhaps be delayed by the time-winning Spaniards (for they had similarly led Pope Paul V by the nose until his death through various delays regarding his grandson and the same Princess—who is being raised in a convent in Naples and has 100,000 crowns of annual income), he has urgently pressed the Spaniards to bring the matter to an end and send the bride to Rome. Whether the Spaniards will keep faith with him, or whether they will lead the old, greedy raven <note>A derogatory metaphor for the Pope, suggesting a scavenger or someone easily tricked by shiny prospects.</note> on a fool's rope as they did his predecessors, time will very soon bring to light. Since the Pope is then so thoroughly pro-Spanish, who would now any longer doubt—

To whom is it unknown with what great earnestness it was enjoined upon the King in France, by Rome, that he should make war against the Huguenots <note>French Protestants</note> and force them into obedience? So much so that the Pope, when the peace concluded in France was broadcast in Rome, complained vehemently about it to the French Ambassador (who is called the Commander de Sillery) and gave enough to understand how much such a thing vexed him. Beyond that, it is sufficiently known from the intercepted Italian letters what Cardinal Ludovisi <note>Ludovico Ludovisi (1595–1632), nephew of Pope Gregory XV</note>, the Pope’s brother’s grandson <note>likely meaning nephew/relative in this context</note>, wrote concerning the suppression of the Elector in Heidelberg <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate</note> and the transfer of the Electoral Hat <note>the dignity/office of Prince-Elector</note> to the Duke in Bavaria; namely, that from this foundation the power of the Protestants in Germany would be considerably weakened, and in time the rest could well be conquered.

->FABIUS.<-

O, how in vain you trouble yourself, my dear Plessis <note>Refers to Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader</note>; for had the Popes driven the Protestants into armor <note>incited them to war</note>, there would have been sufficient war everywhere long ago. They desire that among us the religion, but not the Empire, be changed; to which end they use no weapons, but rather—according to ancient Apostolic custom—they make use of doctrine. Now, the godly zeal of Pope Pius V may vex you, and on the contrary, the friendliness and mildness of Pope Gregory XV and Clement VIII may please you: when has he ever used better means to convert you? Since the Catholics have never preached to you with pikes and muskets, but have rather fought with the mouth and with writings. You praise Pope Gregory XV so that others might scold him for the same: he died with the same modesty with which he entered the Papacy, yet he is called a "greedy raven" by you. The marriage hoped for from Pope Paul and obtained from Pope Gregory XV—what has that to do with the Empire, or how does it serve the propagation of Roman rule? You help yourself with idle chatter just so that you might have something to say. The Italian nuptials will give birth to nothing for the German land, because such a *Venusina* <note>original: "Venusina" - a reference to a Venus-like figure or Helen of Troy</note> was not carried away to the Trojans by force, such that a war should arise elsewhere because of it. But what messenger has made known to you that the Pope was indignant over the peace made with the Huguenots in France?

And even if that were the case, what does it matter? Will Germany perish because of it? Cardinal Ludovisi <note>Ludovico Ludovisi (1595–1632), a powerful Cardinal and nephew to Pope Gregory XV</note> did not write those things for the reasons you describe here regarding the Protestants; rather, you write this so that you may ensnare the Lutherans with lies. Prove these words to me: namely, that by the transfer of the Electoral Palatinate <note>The "Chur Pfalz" refers to the territory and voting rights of the Elector Palatine, a key Protestant leader</note> to the Duke of Bavaria, the power of the Protestants in Germany would be significantly weakened and, in time, could be gradually subdued. That these words were written is known to everyone; yet it is also no secret to anyone that you are accustomed to writing untruths instead of choosing the truth.

### PLESSIS <note>Likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot writer</note>

From which it follows that, just as no one should doubt the Pope's will, he also does not lack the power. The treasury in the Castel Sant'Angelo <note>The Papal fortress in Rome</note> is well known, as is the Pope's successful progress since the beginning of the Papacy, which—through various accidental cases <note>original: "casus"</note> and other extraordinary income—has been so increased that it extends to several million in gold. And the compiler need not portray the Spaniards to us Evangelicals <note>The term "Evangelical" here refers to the Protestant estates of the Holy Roman Empire</note> in the Empire, since their counsels and practical desire to rule are well known throughout all of Germany, and one need not dwell on them much here. From the aforementioned, however, the Evangelical reader will easily notice for himself how poor the caution against foreign domination <note>original: "Dominat"</note> is, which was previously set forth by the compiler and called a "sinceration" <note>A formal justification or clearing of one's reputation</note>. For truly, the Papal Nuncio in Vienna, in his letter given to Father Hyacinth on October 20, 1621, used no evasion—as the compiler does here—but explained well and clearly how the affairs of the Empire would turn out. Namely, he considered that if the Palatinate were taken, then neither the Protestants could come to the aid of the Dutch, nor the Dutch to the Protestants; and then the King of Spain would become a lord and patron of the Netherlands, while the Emperor would become lord in Germany. Thus, he certainly used no evasion: for here the oft-mentioned Nuncio portrays a model of Spanish rule or sovereignty in the Netherlands and of Imperial rule in Germany. We therefore protest that we presume nothing evil of the Imperial Majesty, but would rather believe that such a model would not please Carafa <note>Carlo Carafa, the Papal Nuncio to Vienna during the early Thirty Years' War</note>.

->FABIUS<-

# FABIVS.

Every action among you follows from another: you continue your ill will against the Pope <note>original: "Bapst"</note> and not he himself against you. For what war has the city of Rome sent upon your necks throughout all these years of the Gospel <note>referring to the years since the Reformation began</note>? Not even a little hare has been taken from you. You increase the Papal treasury more with your pens than the Romans do with silver. For you yourselves said above: that very little money could hardly have been obtained from the Pope for this war—and yet you now point to so many millions of gold which were supposedly used against the heads of the Protestants <note>original: "Evangelischen"</note>. You await the Spanish Monarchy with much greater fear than the Catholic King himself desires it. For which German has he, even to this very day, attacked with war, where he was not himself first attacked? If the rod <note>a metaphor for punishment or discipline</note> pleased you, you would have it: time has discovered in whom there was more sincerity. You were the very first, without any given cause, to break the peace; is that then dealt uprightly enough? So be now as you would not have us be, and we have put on a mask so that we might see another.

The Papal Nuncio wrote his letter to Father Hyacinth <note>P. Hyacinthus, a prominent Capuchin diplomat</note> on the 20th of October in the year 1621, after which time anything could have been written, because you were already enemies by then. We owe no sincerity to enemies, regardless of the fact that the aforementioned Nuncio wrote correctly and thereby offended no one: For it is certainly true, since the rebellious Count Palatine <note>Frederick V, whose acceptance of the Bohemian crown sparked the war</note> was humbled, that the power of the Dutch is somewhat diminished, and the Emperor would have Germany in his power. For what other highest Prince do we have in the Empire, Plessius, than precisely the aforementioned Supreme Imperial Majesty? One knows now, O you Calvinists, the Imperial power, and it is also praised, as is not unjust, and you alone diminish it far too much. But I must laugh at your protestation, by which you do not wish to appear as though you presumed anything unjust of His Imperial Majesty. Would to God that your enemies likewise presumed the same of you.

->FABIUS.<-

Since you, Plessi, have forfeited your good name through your high treason <note>original: "Perduellion," a legal term for treason or taking up arms against the state</note>, why then should we have spared it? And what kind of god, Jupiter, should forbid me from saying those things that you yourself practice? Your deeds have dishonored you: you have rebelled against us and fallen upon what is ours. We have only revealed those tragedies with which you have long been pregnant, so that they might finally be brought to light—as has, unfortunately, sufficiently occurred. I will protect your honor neither in this nor in any other book. You have lost it for all eternity: for who would think well of such evil people? Even though you reveal "justice" to the world—which I do not deny, though I mean *our* justice—for who is unaware that you are the instigator of such great mischief? Had you tended to your own affairs at home, renounced the kingdom of another, and preferred peace to war, you would be far more fortunate in your old, happy prosperity. But since you are now so ruined that even the victors take pity on you, you sit there barely clinging to life. You would have done to us everything that you yourself must now suffer, had you not been prevented from doing so.

->PLESSIUS.<-

Now, from the previously mentioned words of the Nuncio Caraffa <note>Carlo Caraffa (1584–1644), a papal diplomat active during the Thirty Years' War</note>, one could, if one wished, take this argument: The King in Spain rules in the Netherlands despotically <term>despotisch: ruling with absolute power, as a master over subjects</term> and by hereditary right. Therefore, the government in Germany is also despotic and hereditary. This conclusion rests on the fact that, in Nuncio Caraffa’s opinion, things of the same origin are also equal and of one kind. But the Imperial Councilors and servants will not—if we are guessing the truth—allow this axiom <note>original: "axioma," a self-evident principle</note> and objection of Caraffa’s to please them; time will eventually reveal this, etc.

### F A B I V S.

Who could possibly restrain themselves, Plessi, from great laughter at such fables of yours? The words in the Apostolic Nuncio's <note>A papal legate or diplomatic representative of the Holy See.</note> letter read as follows: "The King (in Spain) will have the Netherlands, and the Emperor will have Germany in his power, when the Count Palatine <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King," whose removal from power triggered a major constitutional crisis.</note> is deposed, and the Duke of Bavaria is taken in his place." Can you, Plessi, argue even more absurdly than you are doing now? The King of Spain rules in the Netherlands despotically and by hereditary right: therefore, shall the Empire in Germany also be despotic and hereditary? You speak correctly on this point, Plessi, namely that the Imperial servants will not approve of this argument. I, however, am somewhat kinder in this matter, and simply demonstrate that such a thing is an indication of your folly.

### P L E S S I V S.

Furthermore, the compiler says: It is not the case that the Protestants have anything to fear from the Pope or the violent introduction of Popery. However, regarding the response given to Verospio, the Papal Extraordinary Nuncio in Vienna, in the month of February, in the year 1622, the author says right at the beginning: that the Pope, moved by highly important reasons, has requested of his Imperial Majesty that he should immediately grant the Electoral dignity <term>Churfürstliche Dignitet: the status and right of the prince-electors to choose the Holy Roman Emperor</term> and title—now stripped from the proscribed Count Palatine—to the Illustrious Duke of Bavaria, for the propagation of the Religion in the Empire. And he adds thereafter, that for this very purpose (namely to propagate the Roman religion in the Empire), the prudence of the Empire has set the aforementioned goal, etc. How, and in what way, can the Roman Catholic religion be propagated in the Holy Roman Empire, other than through the total suppression and subjugation of the Evangelical Estates <note>The Protestant territories and princes within the Empire.</note>, or by leading them to the Roman service under the yoke of that same See? Or at the very least when, due to military force and instilled fear, the Protestants are compelled to the point that they must restore all ecclesiastical properties to the Romanists? And indeed, the creator of such an answer continues further, in that he so explicitly pretends that his Imperial Majesty shares a goal with the Pope; which, as we are not unwilling to believe, we also not unfairly let the author of such an answer justify it, if only he can: just as it is no less the duty of the Imperial Councilors and scribes to explain how they...

...certainly wish to interpret those points found in the Latin letter written to Mr. Balthasar de Zuniga <note>Don Baltasar de Zúñiga (1561–1622) was a key Spanish diplomat and advisor to King Philip IV</note>, in which the primary "little flowers" and axioms are taken from Scioppius's *Classicvm Belli Sacri* <note>original: "Classico belli Sacri"; referring to Caspar Schoppe's 1619 work 'The Bugle Call of the Holy War,' which incited Catholic princes against Protestants</note>. Such words, however, read as follows: "Through the transfer of the Palatine Electoral Highness, the Catholic cause shall greatly increase." If, then, this cause is to increase, it must necessarily follow that the Evangelical <note>Protestant</note> cause shall fall into decline. By this, the "sincerity" of the Compiler—which he so highly praises—is completely overturned; namely, his claim that the Evangelicals have no danger to fear from the Papacy. However, both the Roman Catholics and the Compiler himself may settle this dispute among themselves, to see if they might perhaps wiggle out of it by means of an equivocation <term>equivocation: the use of ambiguous language to conceal the truth</term>.

### FABIUS.

That the Compiler wrote the truth—namely, that the Protesting estates <note>Protestants</note> had no danger to fear from the Catholics—is witnessed by times past, in which the Catholics lived in good rest and peace with the Lutherans. Point out to us with your finger even a single hardship inflicted upon them; indeed, we have often washed away our own received wounds with the small cloth of patience. The answer given to the Papal Nuncio <note>The Pope's diplomatic representative</note>, of which you speak here, is unknown to me. But let it be so: that the Electoral Palatinate promised to the Duke of Bavaria was intended for the propagation of the Roman Catholic religion. You yourselves would have weakened that same religion; you would have even completely and utterly uprooted it, had Fortune been favorable to you, and that outcome is only prevented through [our] support. 

Because you ask, however, how the Catholic religion might be preserved in the Empire? To that I answer: Through the driving out of you and all rebels; but not, as you say, through the suppression of the "Protesting ones," among whom neither you nor your kin are counted <note>Fabius distinguishes between "peaceful" Lutherans and the "rebellious" Calvinist faction he is addressing</note>. We have desired to use these weapons to preserve what is ours, not to increase it; and you may wander about on such a monstrous journey for so long until you—having finally been made wise by an unexpected misfortune—may repent for the destruction of the peace. At the end of the chapter, you begin again to speak in a Calvinist manner, in which you recount the words written by His Imperial Majesty to Mr. Balthasar de Zuniga:

### The Eleventh Chapter.

## That the Union’s Treasury fell into decline early enough, and the business gradually turned around, though they were highly praised by foreigners among the United.

->Information and Answer.<-

->PLESSIUS.<-

To prove this thesis, the compiler of the Heidelberg Chancellery <note>The administrative office of the Electorate of the Palatinate</note> cites the letter written to von Pless on page 201, and stabs here and there at the poverty of the United <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states</note>. Indeed, Theonestus Cogmandolus (who wrote a commentary on the Bavarian Chancellery, which Justus Justinopolitanus, however, bravely and according to his merit countered and correctly refuted in his *Mystery of Iniquity* <note>original: "Iniquitatis Mysterio"</note>) did not hesitate in the least to proclaim the United as entirely poor and spendthrifts. Therefore, that the costs of war first fell somewhat heavily upon the United is something no one should wonder at so greatly. And who is unaware that war is the swallowing up of money? A distinguished Councilor of the King of France says in Thuanus <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou, a famous French historian</note>, book 49 of his *Histories*: In war, it can

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the financial burdens of the Thirty Years' War, shifting focus from the Protestant Union to the exhaustion of the Catholic League and the Spanish Habsburgs.</meta>

# FABIUS

In war, no one can calculate their accounts so precisely that the costs do not exceed all human foresight and hope. And it is not only the United <note>The Protestant Union</note> who have experienced this well; for we recall that the same has frequently happened to the mighty and wealthy King in Spain. Indeed, at this very time, Milan and Naples have left Spain so poor in money that even in the State of Milan, all churches have been despoiled of their gold, silver, and all other precious church ornaments, which were distributed among the poor soldiers. In these parts, matters have reached such a point that, as they say, the same measures are to be undertaken in Spain and the Kingdom of Naples.

How much money and war munitions remain for the Leaguers <note>Members of the Catholic League</note>, especially the Elector of Mainz and the Rhenish Confederates, the kind-hearted reader may perceive from the grievance-script which the Elector of Mainz gave in response to the proposal made by Licentiate Rihel, the Bavarian envoy. This is also evident from the letter sent to Archduke Leopold (both of which are to be found in the repeated and expanded *Prodromus* <note>A "Prodromus" is a preliminary publication or "forerunner" text, likely referring here to a contemporary political pamphlet</note>). 

That the so-often-boasted Munich treasures have either flown away entirely or have been drained to the very bottom can be clearly understood—literally, as they say—from the little letter to the Elector of Cologne, dated Munich, November 9, Anno 1621. In this letter, he confesses quite roundly and plainly that he has already been forced to pawn his own possessions. Indeed, during this time, this Duke has had to take up loans from several private individuals in Nuremberg, and that in pieces and in small sums; the Bishop of Würzburg has done the same. And the gold guilders collected long ago by Bishop Julius <note>Julius Echter von Mespelbrunn, Bishop of Würzburg (reigned 1573–1617), known for his administrative thrift</note> have, as it is said, already disappeared as well.

From this it appears that the Leaguers are in no position to reproach the United <note>The Protestant side</note> regarding an abundance of money and other things necessary for war. If one wished, one could easily—following the custom of the *Compilator* and *Cogmandolo* <note>Likely references to contemporary polemicists or satirical writers known for sharp critiques</note>—mockingly and scornfully "pluck" at the poverty of the Leaguers, especially if there were something to be said about their various members. However, although we would be well-authorized and have sufficient cause to do so, we shall let the matter rest with what has been said.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue or polemic regarding the financial and military failures of the Protestant cause during the early stages of the Thirty Years' War, specifically addressing the "Fabius" figure or faction.</meta>

# FABIUS.

We did not reproach you for the deficiency of your treasury <note>original: "Ærarij," referring to the public funds or state treasury</note> to your detriment, but rather for your great presumption; for you possessed no great resources, and yet nonetheless provoked so many strong fists against you. Either another acts quite unwisely, or I do so myself, if I were to march before the eyes of a more powerful enemy with such very meager forces. And indeed, that you had very little in reserve in your coffers at the beginning of the war—specifically in the ninth month before the defeat at Prague <note>The Battle of White Mountain, November 1620, a decisive Catholic victory</note>, and especially on the 8th of January, 1620—is testified (to say nothing of others at this time) by the letters of the Count Palatine <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King"</note> himself. These letters, which he sent to his Governor, Count Palatine John, propose various ways and means to scrape money together: namely, to demand minted coinage from the subjects, to take up loans from the servants, and to look into every person's assets. Likewise, he suggested that the inheritances of orphans <term>Pupillen</term> <note>legal term for wards or minors under guardianship</note> should be scraped together, several bailiwicks sold, and help sought from the Huguenots <note>French Protestants</note> in France and from Hungary, along with whatever else was necessitated by such untimely poverty.

In the time that followed, when there was nothing left of your own property nor of foreign aid, and that most rare treasure—the golden bowls—had been melted down, these golden rivers were mixed with such inferior metal that even the mint-master himself had to be ashamed of such "beautiful" work. This blush of shame, however, was driven away by the writings of Peptikus regarding the Calvinist conscience, which, translated by my good friend, I intended to pursue further as the war continued. There is also not the slightest doubt that you would have betaken yourselves to France, had not the discord there reached its end through the death of the Marquis d'Ancre <note>Concino Concini, a favorite of Marie de' Medici, assassinated in 1617</note>. And would to God you had only come marching up, since you are accustomed to staying and presenting a memorable example to the French.

Regarding the war between the Catholics in Friuli, it is certain that the Calvinists were not very diligent in its settlement; for they would have preferred that it had never begun than that it should have reached its conclusion. It was exactly the same situation with the War of the Montferrat Succession. The Duke of Savoy, at your request and deception, sent you troops, which he certainly would have refrained from doing had he only known you sooner.

<meta>This page continues the polemical debate between the figures "Plessius" and "Fabius" regarding the origins of the Bohemian Revolt and the involvement of Reformed (Calvinist) allies.</meta>

### PLESSIUS.

Writes on the 21st leaf, and 2nd section, that one had indeed doubted the help and assistance of the Zwinglian-Calvinist <note>Followers of the reformers Huldrych Zwingli and John Calvin</note> Swiss. How he has now guessed this so accurately, experience has brought to light: since the Swiss villages never sent even a single soldier to aid the United <note>The Protestant Union</note>.

### FABIUS.

What fault has the Compiler <note>The author of the text being critiqued</note> committed in affirming that one never doubted the help of the Swiss? I do not think that he "guessed" it; for although they did not come to you, you nevertheless did not doubt their help on that account. Indeed, you have not doubted many others in the slightest, and yet the truth has testified that you were found to be false. Who among you ever doubted that Bohemia would remain yours for many years? And yet Bohemia is as little in your power as it is true that you are under the power of another.

### PLESSIUS.

On pages 2 and 3, in the second principal point, he says: The Calvinist spirit compelled the Bohemians to use the first act of violence, such as throwing the Lords of the Land down from the window <note>The Defenestration of Prague in 1618, which triggered the Thirty Years' War</note>, firing pistols in a hostile manner, and making a beginning to the war. Answer: These things, all together, have absolutely no basis: For through such a throwing down from the windows, nothing premeditated was undertaken; rather, it is certain that at that time—when the Bohemians visited the Directors and Royal Officers deliberating together in the chamber—such a process had not yet been formulated in the slightest. Instead, the great threatening words of the Directors, especially those of Martinic <note>Jaroslav Borzita of Martinic, one of the officials thrown from the window</note>, the great injustice of the matter, the justifiable pain, the frequently violated patience, and the "first movements" (which, as the Philosophers <note>original: "Philosophi"</note> maintain, are not within our power) moved them to anger. In what way, then, were the Bohemians instructed and incited by the United and the Calvinists, as the Compiler pretends?

<summary>This page debates the culpability of Calvinist influence in the Bohemian Revolt, specifically arguing whether the 1618 Defenestration of Prague was a premeditated act or a spontaneous outburst of anger against oppressive officials.</summary>
<keywords>Defenestration of Prague, Thirty Years' War, Calvinism, Bohemia, Jaroslav of Martinic, Protestant Union, Swiss mercenaries</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the polemical dialogue or critique directed at the Protestant faction during the early stages of the Thirty Years' War, specifically addressing the events in Prague.</meta>

# FABIUS

I believe that your adversity has caused your memory and all your senses to grow numb. For when the Compiler states that the affair was first spun by the **Calvinist spirit**, and that the Royal Officers were cast down from the windows <note>A reference to the Second Defenestration of Prague in 1618, where Protestant lords threw Catholic officials out of the palace windows, sparking the Thirty Years' War.</note>, pistols were fired in a hostile manner, and weapons were seized: you then add these words: *that none of this has the slightest basis in truth.* Mightily fine, my dear **Plessy** <note>Likely referring to Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader and writer, or a representative figure of the Reformed party.</note>, for in your entire book nothing more resembling the truth is to be found; for, in truth, everything that you have committed in such a rebellious and hostile manner certainly has "no basis": since if you had acted with wit and reason, your King’s authority and our beloved Fatherland would still stand, which, through such a raging **rebellion**, have been so miserably ruined.

I am also pleased by that which you append to the previous statement: namely, that through such a "hurling from windows," nothing premeditated was undertaken. Yes, indeed, Plessy, you speak the truth: nothing premeditated was undertaken. For had you possessed the understanding that would have served to reflect on the matter, then all senseless rioting would have been avoided, for no one with reason rages so cruelly.

But the great threats of **Count von Martinitz** <note>Jaroslav Borzita of Martinice, one of the Catholic governors thrown from the window.</note> drove you into your armor: which I also believe. For gunpowder placed near a fire is ignited by even the smallest spark. It was a **Calvinist** wantonness which drove you to such a **tumult**; a festering abscess is also terrified by even a slight touch. You did not perceive mere threats, but rather murder and homicide. Why do you wish to reproach those with "threats" whom you nevertheless intended to destroy in a tyrannical fashion?

You have a convenient excuse: in the *first impulse* <note>original: "primo motu" — a philosophical/legal term for an involuntary or immediate reaction.</note>, which is no sin, all these things occurred. We, however, have done everything in the *second impulse* <note>original: "andern motu" — a deliberate, reasoned action.</note>, and have sinned less than you, because we have punished the sinners. Furthermore, we ourselves confess that the *first impulse* has taken the upper hand with you, because for many years now, whatever misfortune has occurred, the *first impulse* is always found with you. Just as we cannot be persuaded that you first began such a **tragedy** in **Prague** by mere chance, so we also do not believe that no counsel was given for this from **Heidelberg** <note>The capital of the Electoral Palatinate and a center of Calvinist influence; the "Winter King" Frederick V was the Elector Palatine.</note>. He counsels a crime who desires to govern in a criminal manner.

<summary>The author critiques a Protestant defense of the Defenestration of Prague, mocking the claim that the violence was unpremeditated and blaming the "Calvinist spirit" and influence from Heidelberg for the rebellion.</summary>
<keywords>Defenestration of Prague, Calvinism, Count von Martinitz, Rebellion, Philippe du Plessis-Mornay, Thirty Years' War, Heidelberg, Political Polemic</keywords>

# PLESSIUS

Page 37. In the second section <note>original: "membro," referring to a structural division of a book</note>, as well as in other places in this book, the Compiler <note>likely referring to the author of a Catholic polemic being refuted</note> tries to flatter the Imperial Cities <note>original: "den Fuchsschwanz streichen," literally "to stroke the fox's tail," an idiom for sycophantic flattery</note> and presumes to prove that the greater load and burden of the Union <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states formed in 1608</note> rested upon them alone, and that the High Estates <note>the Princes</note> had kept a "lion’s share" partnership with them <note>an allusion to Aesop's fable where the lion takes all the spoils, leaving the partners with nothing</note>. But it has happened to them in the same way as those who, in other places where it is not appropriate, wish to be overly careful, observant, and subtle. For even if he had the accounts of the Union in his very hands, he nevertheless does not understand them particularly well; nor can he actually know how this or that Treasury <term>Ærarium: a public treasury or exchequer</term> was managed, which were quite differently constituted at Nuremberg and Heidelberg. He errs greatly, as the kind-hearted reader may note from the *Mystery of Iniquity* by Justus Justinopolitanus <note>A pseudonym for a polemical author, likely referring to a specific counter-argument text</note> in the 21st Reflection. He rightly should have known the Imperial Cities better, and not held them to be so simple-minded, as if they were so poor at business that they would allow themselves to be deceived in monetary accounts.

->FABIUS<-

The "fox’s tail" <note>flattery</note> does not please us, Plessius, because we do not love those animals (of which that tail is a part), the Calvinists. We recount the flatteries which you use in the assemblies of the Cities, and we prove that you were in agreement with one another in the business: for some contributed the money, while others spent it. Whoever doubts whether the Cities' Treasury suffered damage, let them consider it; it does not benefit us, nor does it benefit the Cities who squander their own. They soon ceased their generosity because they became wiser, seeing that you were not believed and were much more foolish than those you took them to be. However, as to how many Treasuries you had, we do not ask much; it is enough that the bottom in all of them was not deep <note>meaning the funds were quickly exhausted</note>.

### The Thirteenth Chapter.

## That the Calvinist spirit is weary of its associates and desires to have the precedence and rule everywhere: and is otherwise the cause and instigator of all mistrust, calamity, and rebellion.

-> Pages 7, 8, 9, and following in the second main section. <-

-> Information and Answer. <-

-> P L E S S I V S. <-
<note>Likely referring to Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader and writer.</note>

Firstly, the compiler in the entire Bavarian-Anhaltine Chancery—but especially in the second main section—heavily insults many honest people in the Empire under the hated name of "Calvinists," even though they profess the Augsburg Confession <note>The primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church, presented in 1530.</note> with both mouth and heart. Secondly, he calls all members of the Union "Calvinists," and thus, in his opinion, the Count of Hohenlohe is also a Calvinist. Thirdly, the compiler considers all those to be Calvinists who have acquired bishoprics or ecclesiastical properties since the establishment of the Peace of Religion <note>The Peace of Augsburg (1555), which legally recognized Lutheranism in the Holy Roman Empire.</note>, or who were made bishops or administrators through the Lutheran chapters.

Fourthly, all those who do not allow the majority of votes at the Imperial Diets <note>The Reichstag, the deliberative body of the Holy Roman Empire</note> to be applied indiscriminately to matters of both spiritual and religious affairs. Fifthly, also all those who complain about the administration of justice, both at the Imperial Court and at the Imperial Chamber Court <note>The Reichskammergericht, the highest judicial body in the Empire, then located in Speyer</note>. Seventhly, all who venture to recognize the course of the world and what happens outside the Empire—such as in Italy, Constantinople, and Hungary—so that they might not be required to believe all things with <term>implicit faith<note>fide implicitâ: believing something blindly based on the authority of another without personal understanding</note></term>, as they say; a practice which is not only masterfully commanded and imagined in such assemblies and Diets, but also advised. ->FABIUS.<-

How many are held by us ignorant folk to be Calvinists? And indeed, if that is so, it is with little fruit; we call those Calvinists who *are* Calvinists and who also *act* in a Calvinist manner. But if this so-hated name is perhaps vexing to one or another associate, why then did he join such a hated company? We know well that not all the United <note>Referring to the Protestant Union</note> were Calvinists, therefore we do not accuse them; but since those same people were deceived by the Calvinists, they began to mistrust us because they trusted the Calvinists. It is also an untruth that we count the Protesting Bishops among the Calvinists: you invent that so that you might make them hateful to us. For you yourselves admit that, by our agreement, the Calvinist name is quite hateful, and yet you take pleasure in it. Furthermore, we do not consider those to be Calvinists who prefer many votes over the few, nor those who desire equal judges or assessors at Speyer <note>The seat of the Imperial Chamber Court</note>, much less those who accuse the justice of the Imperial judicial bench and wish to be mindful of German Liberty <note>Teutsche Freyheit: the traditional rights and autonomy of the Imperial Estates against the centralizing power of the Emperor</note>, regardless of whether the Calvinist Doctors formulated their legal proceedings during such complaints—to which the Calvinists remained silent and will never say anything more. That in such a number are also included those who [handle] the matters...

->P L E S S I V S.<-

It is even more to be wondered at, because it is well-known and rumored that the Duke of Bavaria <note>Maximilian I, Elector of Bavaria</note> also has his scouts and frequent news, primarily from England through those Englishmen who reside both in Munich and elsewhere. In this, you will perform for us a pleasant service, and one well-pleasing to God, etc. From Neumarkt in the Upper Palatinate, 20 October 1621, etc." The instruction which the Duke of Bavaria desires from this Vice-Provincial in England truly does not concern English school matters or Jesuit colleges and schools (the knowledge of which properly belongs to the Rector of the Munich Society), but is rather directed toward inquiring of the King of England <note>James I</note> what he intended to do for the protection of the Lower Palatinate; so that he, the Duke, might then know how to guide himself accordingly, as if by a plumb line, and might occupy the said Lower Palatinate all the sooner. For such things can be learned best of all from the English Jesuits, because in the morning they know what is being discussed toward evening in the most secret Royal Cabinet: Yes, I say, they know not only what the King is already doing, but what he will yet do. Therefore, the English Jesuits residing in Rome are also accustomed to deliver the letters they receive weekly from England to the Pope: so that in Rome even the most secret English matters are so well known in the manner indicated, that one might not unjustly marvel at it.

# FABIUS

If those who maintain scouts <note>original: "Kundschaffter" - spies or intelligence gatherers</note> in foreign kingdoms are to be called Calvinists, then the Elector of Bavaria <note>Maximilian I (1573–1651), a staunch Catholic leader during the Thirty Years' War</note> must also be guilty of that name and will not be able to escape it; for even from Bavaria, which is situated so far from England, he is able to obtain such intelligence through the Jesuits. This was first learned from the *Panegyric* <note>A formal speech or text of high praise</note> which he dedicated to the victorious hero, the Duke in Bavaria, in which that thing is said which is not unknown to the whole world: namely, that the father-in-law <note>King James I of England</note> was displeased by the misdeeds of his son-in-law <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate, the "Winter King," who lost his lands to Bavarian forces</note>, and that the latter could comfort himself with no help from England because they were prepared for none there. Oh, what mighty secrets those are! And yet they have been spread abroad so that the entire world has seen them.

Afterward, the Elector of Bavaria exhorted the Jesuit Blondus <note>likely Giuseppe Blondo or a similar Jesuit figure active in diplomatic circles</note> that if he should learn anything for the good of the Religion <note>meaning the Catholic Church</note>, he should then make it known to him, the Elector. Therefore, does the Elector of Bavaria have secret observers in England who send everything they "fish up" elsewhere? The Bavarian Prince is—if you do not know it—much dearer to the English people than you fawning sycophants <note>original: "Fuchsschwäntzer" - literally "fox-tailers," meaning flatterers or hypocrites</note> are to the King. Your heresy, which is hateful to Catholics, often puts our oppressed fellow believers into the dark, takes their lives, or banishes them into misery. And such has often been imposed upon them out of "mercy," though sometimes they have taken it upon themselves. Therefore, many of them, fearing such tyranny, have left behind their belongings and property and had to betake themselves to various places in Europe, where they obtained safety among other people.

Toward such innocently hunted exiles, he <note>the Elector</note> shows himself quite beneficent and takes pity on their hardship; for he has experienced in truth that his charity would not be poorly invested, and therefore he does not shrink from showing further kindness. And because Blondus attributes such things to so excellently well-deserving a Prince—and thereby the Catholic cause, for the good of the Religion, has again begun to prosper—and because such a Prince exhorted him to write further of such things, now to Plessen <note>likely Volrad von Plessen, a counselor to the Elector Palatine</note> every place seems full of spies. He imagines they are found even in the most secret Royal bedchamber, hearing everything, betraying everything, and even revealing future matters. If this had not happened in years past, the Duke in Bavaria would not have captured the Palatinate <note>The Rhine Palatinate, seized by Maximilian of Bavaria in the early 1620s</note> so quickly; for he would have had to fear the King in England,

->P L E S S I V S.<-

Since the Duke of Bavaria, as well as the Pope himself, strive with such great diligence to investigate all the secrets of the King of Great Britain—and without any doubt, those of other Evangelical <note>Protestant</note> Princes as well—in the manner just indicated: Oh, on what grounds does the Compiler <note>The author of the opposing pamphlet being critiqued</note> dare to so highly reproach the Palatines and others, and reckon it against them as a crime committed against the Empire, that they also apply themselves to inquire into what might be happening in the world?

->F A B I V S.<-

The Compiler never intended to accuse you as if you had undertaken something against the Empire, or as if you intended to exercise your providence in far-off lands. For what has he to do with your diligence? You might traverse the great Ocean and all the kingdoms of Neptune, and you would carry away the name of a careful man, but not a wicked one. It is free for every man to look after his own affairs. But it is another thing which we have to reproach you with, and we complain not without reason, that to the ruin of the entire human race, you are insidiously rebellious rebels. You investigate secrets only to the end that you may harm people, and that with your spying eyes <note>original: "Kundtschafferischen Augen"</note> you might bring the innocent into a snare. We must look out for such Arguses <note>A reference to Argus Panoptes, the hundred-eyed giant of Greek mythology, used here to mean all-seeing spies</note> scattered here and there in Europe, so that we might recognize your footsteps, which you have directed toward our destruction. The malice of other people makes us more cautious. For what pleasure should it be to us if we wanted to investigate the reign of the god Jupiter in Crete? Every man may boast in his own court; we attend to our calling, but can hardly protect the same against the cunning, secret practices of the Calvinists, no matter how we watch. Yet these, amidst such great calamity, provide us the benefit of making us more clever.

->P L E S S I V S.<-

After this, it is a pleasure to see how the Compiler on page 20 and following makes himself so merry with heated disputing, only so that out of pure hatred and envy against the Calvinists, he might make the Electoral Palatinate and others hated, and attribute to them what...

It would hardly even occur to them in a dream; yet everything together—whatever was initiated against the Empire by foreigners, their servants, and zealous Roman Catholics—was settled and judged by those same people. For those riots which arose to the general ruin of the Grisons <note>original: "Grawbündten"; a region in Switzerland</note> were instigated by Pope Paul, the fifth of this name, out of pure hatred and thirst for revenge against the Venetians, so that their ruin and downfall might be promoted. Subsequently, these were propagated by the Spanish servants and finally strengthened into the present extremities. Regarding the civil war recently stirred up in France, the Protestants did not begin it; rather, it was decreed in the new Council of Conscience in Paris, undermined and persuaded from Rome, which is notorious and well-known throughout all of France, and can be proven by the vote of a Jesuit which he proposed in the Council. But why does he lay the discord between the King of France and the Queen Mother at the feet of the Calvinists, and thus secretly stab at the United <note>The "United" likely refers to the Protestant Union</note> through the fence? The boys running in the streets in France know how to sing and speak of the origin of such disunity: namely, the hatred and mistrust between the Royal Widow <note>Marie de' Medici</note>, her adherents, and the Lord of Luynes and his following. What also do the Calvinists have to do with the war waged in Friuli, from which the dispute between the Uskoks <note>term: Uskoks; irregular soldiers of Habsburg Croatia</note>, Venetians, and Austrians arose? Whether the Constable Lesdiguières <note>original: "Ledigeirius"</note> promoted the war in Montferrat is not yet so certain at this time, and he will know how to save himself as necessity demands. The United, whose counsel was not requested, neither advised for it nor against it; but why the Duke of Savoy was granted the recruitment of German infantry has been sufficiently explained above in the first chapter.

->FABIUS<-

Now we wish to proceed to other disturbances in the world, of which the Catholics are the cause of all; for the Calvinists have not angered or chased away even a little mouse in the whole world. Pope Paul V, out of hatred against the Venetians, stirred up the revolt among the Grisons, which the Spaniards promoted with great help, and from which the ruinous calamity in Montferrat followed. I would like to see your mouth, Plessis <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader</note>, to see if it would turn red with shame; and how dare you accuse the Papal Majesty regarding the unrest of the Grisons? For he is [the cause] of that same uprising—

...such a cause existed that even the inhabitants of the Valtelline <note>original: "Teliner thal"; a strategic valley in present-day northern Italy, then a subject territory of the Grisons</note> publicly complained in their adversity—which the Calvinists had inflicted upon them—that they were being abandoned by the Common Father <note>The Pope</note>. And what is more, the Pope initially declared that, to prevent greater misfortune, everything should be restored to its former state. The Calvinists were the first to sound the alarm among the Grisons <note>The Graubünden or Grey Leagues</note>. For when they had established a league with certain members of the Union <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states</note>, and several captains and other officers had been recruited at the expense of the Germans, so that the secure pass into Italy might be blocked for His Imperial Majesty as well as for the King of Spain, and help might be given to the princes of their sect in Germany, they set their sights on the Valtelline, which had been the seat and fatherland of good Catholics. But so that this may be placed more clearly before one's eyes, the adversaries of the Grisons shall not find it burdensome to read the following letter, which a German prince sent to the Count Palatine <note>Frederick V, Elector Palatine</note>, who at that time made use of another scepter in Bohemia <note>A reference to Frederick V's brief and controversial reign as the "Winter King" of Bohemia</note>. Which, according to another translation, reads as follows:

> Most Serene King, because Your Royal Dignity received a written report on the affairs of the Grisons not so long ago and was also informed orally by the Lord of Erlach, we remember the answer received: that you wished to have such business deliberated upon more maturely at the next Diet in Prague, to which you had deferred the matter. However, because of the aforementioned business, two citizens have arrived here with letters from the Three Leagues of the Grisons, to whom an answer is being given regarding the Nuremberg Legation <note>A diplomatic mission from the city of Nuremberg</note>, which they had directed to the same in the name of the Union concerning the blocking of the pass. When these men arrived at Heidelberg, having completed their legation to Duke John, Count Palatine, and having been sent by him to us, they personally revealed their opinion and that of their people—after obtaining an audience from us—almost in these words: Namely, that they had received letters from the Union for the third time, and had understood from them that their primary intention was how the pass might be blocked to the enemies; they would therefore, as is proper, gladly grant such a request, especially since the nature of their location contributed much to such a plan. In such a manner, with a modest force of soldiers and light equipment, they intended to occupy both the Valtelline and the Grison territories themselves (from whence the enemy could otherwise move from Milan into the Tyrol within two days).

Accordingly, it would be a most welcome service to them if they were to ally themselves more firmly with one another, and if one of their own, and likewise one from the Union <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states</note>, could be heard and maintained at the Royal Court at common expense. Since it sufficiently appears from this that this general cause flows from Divine providence, and also that the Grisons are to be held in some esteem (as much for their known zeal for our religion as for their manliness and, most importantly, the nature of their location): we should not and could not fail to diligently weigh their request among ourselves. From this we also sufficiently understand that their godly zeal toward us is not to be rejected in the least, but rather accepted with special thanksgiving, and to hasten forward with it; because, without any doubt, if the Milanese army were to notice this, they would desire nothing more than to rush forward, just as the Roman Catholic sectarians in these parts would themselves hinder this work to the best of their ability and report it to their own party. When, therefore, negotiations were held with them by our people, it was desired to know from them whether they would be satisfied—while the main business was being handled—if three companies <note>original: "Fähnlein," literally 'little flags,' a military unit of infantry</note> of foot soldiers, each consisting of 200 men, were chosen from among them in the name of His Royal Majesty, the Union, and the three Grison Estates, mustered, and maintained to keep the roads of their territory secure..."

We do not doubt in the least that His Royal Dignity will take this business to heart, especially since it will serve the particular benefit of the **Kingdom of Bohemia**, as well as His Royal Dignity, just as it will also serve our fellow believers <note>original: "Religionsgenossen"; referring to the Protestant allies</note> to profitable advantage. 

We therefore request quite dutifully that His Royal Dignity would address this at the next **Diet** <note>term: Landtag (a regional or national assembly of estates)</note>, so that we might become capable of understanding his opinion. In the meantime, so that we might sense the faithful and sincere spirit of those places, we are willing to pay 2,000 Guilders on the condition that they appoint capable, experienced Colonels and remain patient until the main matter can be negotiated and resolved. 

Furthermore, and so that we might respond to our previous letters, they have urgently requested this. Meanwhile, we did not wish to neglect to comply with their petition; in return, they would then be willing to risk "property and blood" <note>A common early modern idiom for total commitment to a cause</note> as necessity demands, and would also contribute more soldiery for the propagation of the **Religion** <note>The Protestant faith</note>. 

Because they further let it be known, among other things, that if the two cities of **Zurich** and **Bern** could be moved to drive the **Spaniards** from their dominion with united help, they would then leave nothing undone on their part to capture the **Gotthard** <note>The St. Gotthard Pass, a vital strategic mountain pass in the Alps.</note>. We have written to our cities of **Baden** and **Strasbourg** so that the matter might be taken in hand at the earliest opportunity, and thus the business with the **Grisons** <note>The inhabitants of the Graubünden region in Switzerland.</note> might reach its successful conclusion all the sooner. In our estimation, it would also be a great advancement and promotion of the matter...

This is what this Prince writes: from which the kind-hearted reader will become better informed regarding the beginning and progress of the Grison uprising. For as soon as the legates brought news of these preceding affairs home, troops were immediately recruited using a different purse <note>funded by a different source</note>, and the Catholics were attacked therewith. Because the Catholics could not tolerate such violence, war broke out in the meantime; however, it did not turn out for the best for those who caused it, since they were defeated in various places. They would have even lost their freedom entirely, had others not taken up their cause through their own prestige.

Similarly, in recent times, the King of France has harassed the innocent **Huguenots** <note>French Protestants</note> with military force, all at the instigation and prompting of the Catholics. For in Paris, out of sheer hatred against the Religion <note>The Reformed faith</note>, and upon the deed and suggestion of a **Jesuit**, war was started without cause. His voice <note>The Jesuit's influence</note> is known to the one from **Pfeffen** <note>Likely a reference to a specific polemicist or local figure</note>, according to his claim, and yet he cannot name the man: just as if the whole world did not know that the Jesuits have no seat in such a Council! The conflict between the **Most Christian King** <note>A traditional title for the King of France</note> and his Lady Mother <note>Marie de' Medici</note>, and who awakened it, is something all of France is full of; it proves your innocence, which will, however, be slow to arrive. But since you have led me into France, I now see before my eyes the beautiful fields upon which, in the year [16]17, the hostile army contested the King.

**Colonel Gent**, your fellow believer, who recruited cavalry through your help and favor, had free passage and welcome provisions everywhere among you, so that he would not be hindered and the King might see his enemy all the sooner. And indeed, that you wished to overwhelm the King with force—the very one whom you now so diligently call upon for help—is testified to by **Peblitz's** account: since the **Count Palatine** <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King" of Bohemia</note>

### The Twelfth Chapter.

## An account of the grounds and reasons why the United <note>The Protestant Union</note>, and indeed each in their own place, were moved to enter into the Union.

->Page 1. 2. & following in the second Main Member.<-

->Information and Answer.<-

### PLESSIS <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader and writer.</note>.

Since all and every member of the Union approve of this Bavarian writer's assertions, and are not minded to pull back out the defamatory sting inserted against them and all posterity, they are themselves clever enough to weigh such things well. However, one should not withhold from the reader in general, and for the sake of general truth, that it fares with the United members just as Livius <note>Titus Livius, the Roman historian Livy</note> once left behind for posterity when he said: **"Common fear is the best bond of unity."** <note>original Latin: "Communis metus est maximum unitatis vinculum"</note> For both common fear and danger caused them to have to step together, because otherwise no other means remained to hinder the course and violence of the Bavarian Execution <note>The military enforcement of an Imperial ban</note>, which had gained such a successful beginning with Donauwörth <note>The 1607 occupation of the Free Imperial City of Donauwörth by Bavaria, a key trigger for the formation of the Protestant Union</note>. Because of this, the Electoral Palatinate and several united members have been made so hated at Munich,

that the Bavarians primarily held the view that—as we might say—this <term>Syncretism</term><note>A term used here to describe the merging or union of different religious factions, specifically the Protestant Union.</note> and unification must have been more diligently promoted by them. And if one wishes to reflect more deeply on the matter, the Lower Saxon and other Protestant Estates have the "United" <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states formed in 1608.</note> to thank for the fact that they have been left in peaceful prosperity for some 15 or more years now. For the Roman Catholics directed their counsels and projectiles against the United, as their nearest neighbors. Therefore, in the lands of Upper Germany—where the Papists along the Danube, Rhine, Main, and Moselle had all their military power gathered close together—matters were first approached with earnestness and weapons were taken up; at that time, both types of Saxon Estates <note>Referring to the Lutheran circles of Upper and Lower Saxony.</note> were either passed over or perhaps set aside. They are already beginning to turn their banners toward the Saale, Weser, and Elbe rivers.

### FABIUS

Fear drives both princes and subjects toward alliance: For because the enemy easily carries the victory against any single opponent, he tends to hesitate when facing many. Therefore, lest the Catholics overwhelm first one and then another until finally no one is left, the Calvinists have come together and have also brought several Lutherans to their side. <insert>And should one pay with his own money for what the people of Donauwörth have forfeited?</insert> If the higher Estates wish to consider it a disgrace to redeem the pledged city of Donauwörth, then those of lower rank may do so; but if it does not please either of them, it certainly pleases the Elector <note>Maximilian I of Bavaria.</note> to keep the city he has occupied. You fear the "Munich Chalk" <note>A metaphorical reference to the debts or "bills" run up by the Bavarian war effort.</note>; indeed, not without reason, because while everything is entrusted to fickle fortune, great debts are incurred. And will you not pay more than a million when you visit the Rhine again, as an eternal guest? War, your own offspring, is a beast which has a great maw and swallows vast armies and many mountains of gold. He who begins a war must also fill such a great pit again, or else be swallowed by it himself.

<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion of the Imperial Ban and the political tensions surrounding the city of Donauwörth. The catchword "PLES-" likely refers to a subsequent mention of a place or person, possibly Plessenburg or a related name, appearing on the following page.</meta>

# The Restoration of Donauwörth

Just as he desires to refill the Imperial Cities <note>The Free Imperial Cities were self-governing cities that sub-ordinated only to the Holy Roman Emperor.</note> once again, he will not cast away the city of Donauwörth in vain. The Electoral Hat <note>The ceremonial cap symbolizing the status and rights of a Prince-Elector of the Holy Roman Empire.</note> has now been purchased, at the extreme loss of yours and of eternity; however, his chest is not empty because of it. He has acquired the same through his brave deeds, and because he has deserved so highly and well of his Imperial Majesty. What we have spent, we shall place that same amount upon your account, which account we intend either to deduct from you or to withdraw from your lands. He who inflicts damage should also bear it, or else you cannot extricate yourselves from this matter without lies. For you say that those same citizens were robbed of their temporal goods; what was taken from you, I do not know, and you simply lack the truth. Who took the spoils at Donauwörth? You knowingly deceive the reader, and in a manner hardly noble. 

So too have they lost their physical freedom—unless you would consider yourselves to be bondservants. You who obey the Prince of Conscience, what do you fear? Every man has his own property with him, unless perhaps the <term>Calvinists</term> <note>Followers of John Calvin; at this time, they were often viewed with suspicion by both Catholics and Lutherans.</note> have none. If the people of Donauwörth are abandoned by all men, then they are also abandoned by you, unless you are not men—which I do sometimes believe. The absolution from the <term>Imperial Ban</term> <note>A form of outlawry where a person or city lost all rights and protection under the law.</note> accepted those citizens into grace, but not into liberation, so that they should owe nothing to the one who has expended such great costs on their behalf. 

The <term>Electoral College</term> <note>The group of princes entitled to elect the Holy Roman Emperor.</note> has exhorted the Bavarian Prince <note>Refers to Maximilian I, Duke of Bavaria, who executed the ban against Donauwörth.</note> that the poor people of Donauwörth should not be harassed on account of their religion. I know this well, but who has been injured? The complaints of fugitives carry no weight with just judges; and it is just as free for me to denounce the authority which I hate; but I suffer because I refuse to be civilly obedient. 

But why do you babble about a somewhat more secret writing from the Spiritual Electors? <note>The Archbishops of Mainz, Cologne, and Trier.</note> Do you dare to burst forth so boldly, claiming that such Princes have written secretly and publicly so that they might lead His Electoral Grace in Saxony <note>The Elector of Saxony, usually the leader of the Lutheran estates.</note> into the light? <note>An idiom meaning to deceive or mislead someone.</note> Simply produce such secret writing publicly, or confess openly that you speak in the "good Calvinist" manner. Why does the city of Donauwörth cause you pain, when it does not even pain the city itself? Oh, how much more blessed is she than you! She lives now, under such a pious Prince, quite peacefully, while you now inhabit another land, enjoy another air, and lick another plate like soup-gluttons. The bricks have long since been doubled for you <note>A biblical reference to the Israelites' labor in Egypt (Exodus 5), implying increased hardship.</note>, but where is, and where remains, Moses? Perhaps a Moses will come,

->but he will destroy the plagued Egyptians,<-
->who are hateful to the people of God<-
->(among whom you also are to be counted).<-

<meta>This page continues the polemical dialogue between Plessius (representing the Reformed/Calvinist view) and Fabius (representing the Catholic/Jesuit view), focusing on the character and reputation of the Jesuit Order.</meta>

# PLESSIS.

Now that the Compiler has filled his book everywhere with the title of the "Calvinist Spirit" and—if it pleases the gods and does not snow <note>A sarcastic proverbial expression meaning "if all goes according to plan."</note>—has painted the same in vivid colors: the kind-hearted reader might perhaps wonder why the "Jesuitical Spirit," which is the "cock in the basket" <note>An idiom meaning the favorite or the one in charge.</note> among the Roman Catholics, has been silently passed over? However, so that the same spirit is not cheated of its due testimony and praise, and so that nothing is withheld—against all right and equity—from the learned and "grave" Jesuits (for so they are called by Mariana <note>Juan de Mariana (1536–1624), a Jesuit historian known for his controversial political theories.</note> in the book *On the Instruction of a King*, which was printed at Mainz with the permission of the superiors), well then, we wish to share just a little something of this Jesuitical spirit with the kind-hearted reader.

# FABIUS.

The Compiler filled his book with the Calvinist spirit for the reason that you yourselves provided the material for it: For what else should I transcribe from so many of your letters, other than a Calvinist spirit? Well then, proceed in like manner and fill your book with the Jesuitical spirit: But what will you write? No pikes, no halberds, no pistols, nor any rebellious-warlike counsels and plots; we employ an irreproachable pen and life. Our paper is filled with ink, not with blood: Our hand never grips a sword, except when it is itself attacked. For to whom have we done violence during this entire time of such highly damaging religious unrest? From whom have we stolen anything? From whom have we stripped away their justice? You accuse the innocent in this matter so that you yourselves might not be viewed as the guilty ones. But go ahead, paint the Jesuits: Because they are so learned, they will recognize their own likeness; and because they are also "grave" <term>gravitetisch: from the Latin 'gravitas,' implying dignity, seriousness, and moral weight</term>, they will answer your worthlessness with silence. And were they not so grave, they would only laugh at it. Mariana called no one learned or grave; rather, it was the Provincial <note>The regional superior of the Jesuit Order.</note> who permitted such a book to be printed and employed three censors for that purpose.
->Nevertheless, I confess that the Jesuits<-
->are grave, especially to the<-
->Calvinists.<-

# PLESSIS

The Jesuits, following the conclusion and publication of the Council of Trent <note>The Council of Trent (1545–1563) was the formal Roman Catholic reply to the doctrinal challenges of the Protestant Reformation.</note>, exerted all their power to solidify their possession of German lands. Within fifty years and more—namely since the year 1563—their Society has increased to such an extent that in Germany and the Netherlands they now number over 2,423. This is testified by Adam Contzen, a Jesuit from Mainz, in his *disceptation* <note>original: "disceptation" — a formal debate or treatise</note> regarding the secrets of the Jesuits. Pope Pius V, whose zeal was far too heated, contributed greatly to their advancement; he undertook to reform the clergy in the Empire and urged the Prince-Electors and other Bishops to introduce the Synods and the decrees of the Council of Trent.

However, several Electors and Bishops in Germany (who somewhat postponed such dealings) exercised a particular modesty and caution in this regard, the more easily to prevent the mistrust of the Protestants. Among these, the two Electors of Mainz, Archbishop Daniel <note>Daniel Brendel von Homburg, Elector of Mainz 1555–1582</note> and Archbishop Wolfgang <note>Wolfgang von Dalberg, Elector of Mainz 1582–1601</note>, were not the least; they employed persons adhering to the Evangelical <note>term: "Evangelical" — at this time, a general term for Protestants, particularly Lutherans</note> religion not only in consultations, but also in official positions, court business, and chamber administrations. Likewise, other Bishops and Prelates maintained Evangelical servants without any burden to their consciences, a fact well known and celebrated in the Rhenish district and Franconia.

And indeed, at that same time, the meetings of the Collegiate Circles <note>term: "Circles" (Reichskreise) — administrative groupings of states within the Holy Roman Empire</note> and others organized by the four Rhenish Electors were conducted in a quite friendly, peaceful, and quiet manner. Indeed, the Spiritual Electors themselves maintained good neighborliness and correspondence with the Electoral Palatinate, the Duke of Zweibrücken, the Landgrave of Hesse, and other neighboring Evangelicals, without any distinction of religion. The sole reason for this is that these same Spiritual Electors, their councilors, and servants, were not drawn from the Jesuit school, but from the ancient, upright German school.

But now, since Jesuit pupils are being drawn by the Roman Catholics into all offices, both spiritual and secular, the direction of affairs—even in the Circle meetings, as well as at home in council deliberations and in the governorships of the provinces—is given to those same people (who are dependent upon the Jesuits, by whom they, as their creatures, are promoted to office...

This is the very same reason / why in former times the Electors and Princes of the Empire had good correspondence with one another: sincere customs brought such things about on both sides / but because the Calvinists gradually stained them / friendship has also declined. You attribute the guilt to the Jesuits in vain:

# PLESSIS

After the conclusion and publication of the Council of Trent <note>The Council of Trent (1545–1563) was the formal Roman Catholic reply to the doctrinal challenges of the Protestant Reformation.</note>, the Jesuits exerted all their power to confirm their possession of German lands. Within fifty years and more—namely from the year 1563—their Society has increased to such an extent that in Germany and the Netherlands they now number over 2,423. This is testified by Adam Contzen, a Jesuit from Mainz, in his *disceptation* <note>original: "disceptation" — a formal debate or treatise</note> regarding the secrets of the Jesuits. Pope Pius V, whose zeal was far too heated, did much to promote them; he undertook to reform the clergy in the Empire and urged the Spiritual Electors <note>The Archbishops of Mainz, Cologne, and Trier, who held the power to elect the Holy Roman Emperor.</note> and other Bishops to introduce the Synods and the decrees of the Council of Trent.

However, several Electors and Bishops in Germany (who somewhat postponed such dealings) exercised a particular modesty and caution in this regard, the more easily to prevent the mistrust of the Protestants. Among these, the two Electors of Mainz, Archbishop Daniel and Archbishop Wolfgang <note>Daniel Brendel of Homburg (r. 1555–1582) and Wolfgang von Dalberg (r. 1582–1601).</note>, were not the least; they employed persons adhering to the Evangelical <note>Protestant</note> religion not only in consultations, but also in official positions, court business, and chamber administrations. Likewise, other Bishops and Prelates maintained Evangelical servants without any burden to their conscience, which is well known and rumored throughout the Rhenish district and Franconia.

And indeed, at that same time, the meetings in the Collegiate Circles <note>Administrative groupings of states within the Holy Roman Empire.</note> and others arranged by the four Rhenish Electors proceeded in a quite friendly, peaceful, and quiet manner. Indeed, the Spiritual Electors themselves maintained good neighborliness and correspondence with the Electoral Palatinate, the Duke of Zweibrücken, the Landgrave of Hesse, and other neighboring Evangelicals, without any distinction of religion. The sole reason for this is that these same Spiritual Electors, their councilors, and servants, were not taken from the Jesuit school, but from the ancient, upright German school.

But now, since Jesuit pupils are being drawn by the Roman Catholics into all offices, both spiritual and secular, the direction of the Circle meetings—as well as domestic deliberations and the governorships of the provinces—is given to those same people (who are attached to the Jesuits, by whom they, as their creatures, are promoted to office...

Then, even if many are drawn from that same school <note>referring to Jesuit colleges</note> into the government, this does not happen to the detriment of the common wealth. No one suffers harm from this, unless you consider it harmful simply because not everything is within your power. In public assemblies, you play "hide and seek" <note>original: "hinder dem Hütlein spielen," an idiom for acting deceptively or secretly</note> with them; and because you desire unjust things from them, you take offense when they do not immediately wipe away your concerns <note>original: "auffwischen," meaning to settle or clear a matter instantly</note>. Furthermore, they do not become more weary because of Jesuit teaching, but rather because of your great presumption, through which you hope to drive the Catholics into a corner <note>original: "in Sack zutreiben," literally to drive into a sack</note>. I have said above that we find nothing new either among the Lutherans or among you. If, according to your opinion, we decree something new, we decree it for ourselves; there is also no difference in religion between the old Catholics and us. However, a differing zeal exists among different people: some are more heated, some more modest. For many hundreds of years, there have been no equally zealous followers of religion. And perhaps in your Calvinist sect <note>term: Sect - used here in the 17th-century sense of a religious faction or party</note>, some are ignited with greater flames than you are heated—though you have fire enough, for you have set many lands ablaze. The words of His Electoral Grace in Saxony <note>referring to John George I, Elector of Saxony</note> are harmless; for as princes sometimes hear, so they also often speak—and yet you speak differently, even though you hear differently.

->P L E S S I V S.<-
<note>Likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader and writer.</note>

These recounted matters should now give all sincere Evangelicals <note>term: Evangelicals - at this time, a general term for Protestants, particularly Lutherans</note>, but foremost to many persons of high and low station in the Upper and Lower Saxon Circles <note>administrative regions of the Holy Roman Empire</note> (who until now have taken great pleasure in the Bavarian-Anhaltine Chancery <note>note: A reference to the "Anhaltische Kanzlei," a famous propaganda publication during the Thirty Years' War involving intercepted secret documents</note>), cause and guidance to open their eyes. They should diligently take care so that, while they believe they are finding some sort of political secrets in such a Chancery, they do not—according to the old proverb—find coals instead of a treasure. For the sole purpose of that book, as we have also detailed above, is this: that it might lead the Evangelicals (otherwise pious and sincere people) astray through the various practices of the Jesuits and words set on screws <note>idiom for ambiguous or twisted language</note>, and might incite them against good, sincere patriots and companions of the faith and religion. Now the compiler makes out of the advice of said good patriots—which originated solely from a love of freedom and the dignity of the common Fatherland—exceedingly great and public crimes against the Emperor, the Empire, and its statutes; but he does so entirely without foundation or ground.

For even assuming that perhaps an excess occurred and an error was committed, it is nevertheless known that the laws, in such cases, excuse anyone from malicious fraud—without which such crimes cannot be committed—based on the slightest of reasons.

->FABIUS<-
