I am also, Plessi, well satisfied with you on this point, and I myself lament that, due to political secrets, traps are found in the Anhalt Chancery <note>The "Anhaltinische Kanzlei" refers to the secret diplomatic archives of Christian of Anhalt, which were captured and published by Imperial forces to expose Protestant war plans.</note>; they are, however, entirely similar to your black, evil customs. We deceive no one in such a book, but rather prove that you yourselves have deceived. You have a friendly face and a hostile heart, which was pregnant with misfortune and would have fallen upon the Lutherans just as well—whom you nevertheless call good, upright patriots so that they should also consider you as such. One knows you, Plessi, by your own pen and deeds. The compiler forges no vices, but brings those forged by you to the light; and I pray you would cease trying to demonstrate your innocence to others, which you yourself do not see—even though you begin to confess an "excess," yet one which is supposedly very minor and no malicious fraud. I, however, would be guilty of a malicious fraud if I believed it. Is it a minor excess to you, Plessi, to presumptuously stir up all of Europe? To take kingdoms from another by force? To rob other provinces? And to devastate the common Fatherland with the plundering of such a dangerous war? Why do you so often excuse malicious fraud? You are also a deceiver in this case.

In sum, what exceptions and excuses are drawn up against the Aschaffenburg treaty, the kind-hearted reader can—

noted in the letter which His Electoral Grace of Saxony dispatched to His Electoral Grace of Mainz and His Princely Grace Landgrave Ludwig of Hesse on October 30th, in the year 1622.

->FABIUS<-
<note>Fabius likely represents a Catholic or Imperial viewpoint in this constructed dialogue.</note>

You rightly recount the hardships of war, but all such things fall upon your own heads. For who let the Furies out of hell to trouble the world so? We, as those traveled for that purpose, have girded on our weapons and donned our armor, and have dealt the blows back to you. From this has arisen the devastation of lands and the plundering of citizens; from this has followed the wasting of provinces, the loss of domestic prosperity, and the uttermost poverty in all things. Many could not be spared because you inflicted damage upon us everywhere. No man was promised a remission that was not sincerely kept; we would indeed have abstained from several lands if only something had been left for the more righteous enemy <note>original: "gerechteren Feind"</note> where the army could have kept its winter quarters. Those who previously either had nothing, or have now already lost their own, have waged war with borrowed money. And where, in such great cold, should one have led the soldiery? In the Palatinate <note>The Electoral Palatinate, a central territory of the conflict</note>, the uncultivated, hungry land has hunted out its own inhabitants; how then should that same land nourish foreign people? Do you think then, Plessis, that the soldiery can be led to the neighboring regions of the moon, or pitch their tents in the winter mountains? You Calvinists will have to thank those who, through the quartering of the soldiery, have become either rich or poor. The Treaty of Ulm <note>A 1620 treaty of neutrality between the Protestant Union and the Catholic League</note> has been sincerely kept, for when has anyone ever been attacked with weapons against the same? We have pursued the Calvinists with these [weapons] alone, because they preceded us with such. If damage has been done thereby, let those restore it who either did such things, or were the cause that we did them.

->PLESSIUS<-
<note>Plessius likely represents the Protestant/Reformed perspective, possibly named after Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly.</note>

The Duke of Bavaria, as is known to everyone, concluded the peace at Ulm with the Un-erring <note>The Protestant Union</note> and expressly included the Palatinate therein, under his word of faith and honor (which promise, as the legal scholars teach, is equal to a physical oath), promising that the Palatinate would be attacked neither by his forces nor by the soldiery of the Catholic League <note>original: "Bäpstlichen Ligæ"</note>—

...should be. But when, thereafter, contrary to such highly-sworn promises, he occupied the Upper and Lower Palatinate <note>The Upper Palatinate (Oberpfalz) and Lower Palatinate (Kurpfalz) were the territories of the Elector Palatine, Frederick V</note> in a hostile manner, it is said that this excuse was invented in Munich: That the Emperor is always excepted, or is understood to be an excepted party, and therefore the nature of the matter was changed after the declaration of the Imperial Ban <term>Achtserklärung: a legal process in the Holy Roman Empire where a person was stripped of all rights and their lands were forfeit</term>.

But what a void invention is this? For the High Chancellor of Bavaria, during the negotiations at Ulm <note>The Treaty of Ulm (1620) ensured neutrality between the Protestant Union and the Catholic League</note>, expressly allowed it to be stated that no evasion should hold weight against the aforementioned Ulm treaty; which both the Duke of Angoulême <note>original: "Hertzog von Angeloume"</note> and other French ambassadors, who were the arbitrators of said transaction and promoted it, can testify. Now, if this evasion were to be used to nullify this well-founded treaty, secured with so many clauses: what then should happen to the manifold sincere words and doubtful explanations—which could serve them as a Delphic sword <note>A metaphor for an ambiguous or double-edged argument</note>—with which the books, writings, and reports of the Romanists' <note>A term used here for supporters of the Catholic/Imperial cause</note> ambassadors are so tediously filled? How easily could they be extended and glossed over through mere perceived meanings and mental reservations, according to the Jesuit teachings (just as years ago in Halle in Saxony, the younger Granvelle <note>Antoine Perrenot de Granvelle, a powerful advisor to the Habsburgs</note> deceived the elder Landgrave), of which sufficient examples are at hand?

### FABIUS.

What do you say, Plessis? Should the Elector of Bavaria have practiced deceit and been unmindful of his word? He promised peace to the peace-loving members of the Empire; you have torn apart the bond of that treaty, so why should the Duke of Bavaria remain bound by it? I have told you elsewhere that von Mansfeld <note>Ernst von Mansfeld, a mercenary general for the Protestant cause</note> also attacked the Duke of Bavaria in a hostile manner in the Palatinate and ravaged the Bishopric of Speyer. Why do you accuse me of the broken peace, which you yourselves have broken? The excuse which, as you say, was supposedly invented in Munich, you smear with your pen in vain: We could have raised such objections if we had lacked other causes for war, because you in the Palatinate could promise nothing to anyone, since your Prince had forfeited his right: especially since promises are void if I promise that I will not render obedience to my superiors. I can indeed promise you what is mine, but what I owe to my Sovereign, that I cannot [give] to him, because...

--- Page 197 ---
<meta>This page continues a dialogue between Fabius and Plessius regarding the political and religious tensions of the Thirty Years' War, specifically focusing on the legitimacy of the Treaty of Ulm and the shifting loyalties of the Duke of Bavaria.</meta>

what is not mine, I cannot withdraw in the slightest: nor can any law compel me to pay my debts with another man's goods. For this reason, in this Princely Assembly at Ulm, the Highness of the Imperial Majesty <note>original: "Kays. Mayest." referring to Holy Roman Emperor Ferdinand II</note> remained undisturbed; likewise, the observance of the highly esteemed Princes toward the supreme Head has remained steadfast on both sides. Therefore, you speak somewhat shamelessly here, claiming that the Elector of Bavaria promised something at Ulm which was meant to abolish the required obedience toward the aforementioned Imperial Majesty. The Imperial mandate has been obeyed at all times, yet you say excellently: the Bavarian Chancellor let words slip at Ulm which you never heard from his own mouth. But so that I do not detain you longer, such Ulm Pacts <note>The Treaty of Ulm (1620) saw the Protestant Union agree to remain neutral while the Catholic League invaded Bohemia.</note> are known through public print: show me, then, what you are so maliciously stabbing at in this case. When I read it for myself, I will pass a different judgment upon you. We Catholics do not help ourselves in treaties and other pacts with any equivocation <note>original: "æquiuociren" - the use of ambiguous language to hide the truth, a common accusation against Jesuits at the time</note>, since your practices cannot please us; nor are you so simple that you should not understand what is said, since you are so excellently well-practiced in deception.

->P L E S S I V S.<-

When, in the winter of the year 1622, the soldiers of Mansfeld, Brunswick, and Bavaria were recruited with great earnestness, and at the same time the Evangelical <note>Protestant</note> Princes and Cities sought capable soldiers for every emergency and for their own defense, the Duke of Bavaria—I know not what evil he dreamed of—immediately dispatched his High Chancellor to Stuttgart. Through him, he proposed to the Duke of Württemberg, among other things, how the highest necessity required the laying down of highly harmful weapons and the consideration of peace or a truce. He claimed to have no other wish at that time than how, according to everyone's desire, one might reach an amicable settlement of matters; he therefore requested His Princely Grace most diligently to graciously promote such a highly useful work, as he was well able to do. However, shortly thereafter (as all things, especially in war, are prone to turn around), fortune changed, so that the Spaniards and Bavarians had the advantage in the Palatinate over others. Consequently, the Bavarians remained entirely silent about peace. Indeed, in Italy, and especially in Rome, much greater schemes were repeated, and the Pope was relentlessly urged to elevate the Duke of Bavaria to the Electoral Dignity <note>The transfer of the Palatine electoral vote to Maximilian of Bavaria, a pivotal and controversial event of the war.</note>.

->FABIVS.<-

<summary>Fabius defends the Catholic League's integrity regarding the Treaty of Ulm, while Plessius counters by accusing the Duke of Bavaria of feigning a desire for peace only until military fortunes favored the Catholics and he could secure the Electoral title from the Pope.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the polemical dialogue between Fabius (representing the Catholic/Bavarian interest) and Plessius (representing the Protestant/Palatine interest) regarding the events of the Thirty Years' War.</meta>

# FABIUS.

And what, Plessy, would be necessary for the Bavarian actions to please you? The Elector of Bavaria, in his good fortune and your misfortune, dispatched his envoy to the Duke of Württemberg so that he might present himself as an illustrious mediator for peace and restore the same to our dear Germany. The more carefully and diligently he desired this, the more he is to be praised; his heroic spirit was not in the least puffed up by so many victories achieved, nor did he behave tyrannically toward the conquered. But that he shortly thereafter let the desire for peace drop—your unfaithful friendship was the cause of that, which nearly plunged us into utter ruin as we relied upon German sincerity. For the Margrave of Durlach, with a newly recruited army gathered from all sides, hostilely attacked the Bavarian General, Count Tilly <note>Johann Tserclaes, Count of Tilly (1559–1632), the primary commander of the Catholic League's forces.</note>, who nevertheless, at Wimpfen <note>The Battle of Wimpfen (May 6, 1622) was a major Catholic victory.</note> in a battle disastrous for you, stained the field with your blood. Who would then still desire peace among such enemies? Peace may be brought by the one who wishes to uphold it. If we allowed new recruitment to proceed in Rome, as you write, we did so because you repeated your rebellion, and it appeared as though the Duke of Bavaria deserved the Electoral Hat <note>The symbol of the Prince-Elector's office and status.</note>, which has now been stripped from your Count Palatine <note>Frederick V of the Palatinate, the "Winter King," who lost his lands and electoral title after the Battle of White Mountain.</note> on account of his offending the Imperial Majesty. If you cannot bear that we should possess this forever, then you should have kept it through virtue; we receive what you lose with shame.

### PLESSIUS.

When, shortly thereafter, the city of Heidelberg fell in a most negligent manner into the power of the Bavarian General, the oft-mentioned Duke of Bavaria sent the world-famous library <note>The *Bibliotheca Palatina*, one of the most important collections of Renaissance manuscripts and books.</note> as a gift to Cardinal Ludovisi. For the collection of this library, Leo, a Greek (the Superintendent, as it is said, of the Vatican Library), was chosen. Through this highly prestigious gift, the Duke of Bavaria hoped for more favor and advancement regarding the Palatine Electorate from the College of Cardinals. In the meantime, the Imperial Majesty’s General, Count Tilly, must have carried this out, even though hardly a single soldier under his command <note>original: "vnder seinem Commando"</note> earns the pay of the Imperial Majesty. It is much closer to the truth, and not unjustly so, that this army is called the "Soldiery of the Leaguers" <note>The Catholic League, a coalition of Catholic German states.</note>, which also the King of Spain and—

who gave this name sufficient weight in his recent letter to the Infanta <note>Isabella Clara Eugenia, Governor of the Spanish Netherlands</note>, when he said: *The arms of the Catholic League, which are in the Lower Palatinate.* <note>original: "Cas armas de la Liga Catholica, quæ schallain el Palatinado inferior."</note> That is: The Catholic League’s army, which is in the Lower Palatinate.

### FABIUS

O, how greatly you would rejoice if not only the conquest of the city of Heidelberg, but also other Catholic victories could be kept silent! Such things gnaw at your weak heart and daily oppress your conscience, which you yourself burdened through this war. Was Heidelberg then, as you claim, taken in a "careless" manner? What kind of careless manner is that? Did it happen through the negligence of the protectors, or through brave soldiers? You do violence and injustice to your own soldiers, who for a long time quite manfully drove back the enemy attacking from all sides, and were finally subdued by the sword while defending the city walls. The highly renowned General, Count Tilly <note>Johann Tserclaes, Count of Tilly (1559–1632), commander of the Catholic League's forces</note>, alone broke through the fallen defenses and subdued the city, which had grown proud due to long-lasting prosperity. As for the Library <note>The Bibliotheca Palatina, one of the most important Renaissance libraries</note>, the most distinguished of the spoils, the Elector of Bavaria <note>Maximilian I</note> undoubtedly sent it to the Pope so that he would not diminish the Pope's favor; but not, as you write, merely to secure the Electoral Hat <note>term: Electoral Hat (Churhütlein). The symbol of the office and dignity of a Prince-Elector of the Holy Roman Empire</note>. For the Duke of Bavaria did not act as a merchant, bartering books in exchange for such an Electoral Hat; rather, he attained it through his heroic and brave deeds, such that many wished him such honor, some demanded it, and the Emperor finally granted it. The soldiery serving under the Bavarian Elector belongs as much to the Leaguists as to the Emperor, because they were recruited for the service of His Imperial Majesty; for you rushed upon them with so many arms and weapons that they were forced to protect themselves and their own, not only with their own weapons but with those of others. You may title this army however you wish, but you should know that it has overcome you.

### PLESSIUS

In many other matters as well, another contradiction and distinction of inner thoughts and inclinations tends to entangle itself among the Papists. For example: Who is there who does not know how highly the

The Elector of Saxony (provided that one wishes to look at his outward manners and appearance) is respected by the Roman Catholics; and yet, how contemptuously the Papal Nuncio Caraffa wrote concerning His Electoral Grace to the Bishop of Patras, the Nuncio in Brussels, on October 20th, in the year 1620. In that letter, he writes among other things: *That he is not a prince of spirit, nor of such strength that one ought to fear him.* <note>original: "Che non è principe di spirito, ne for ze tali, che de uagrau. fatto temerti di lui."</note> That is to say: The Elector of Saxony is neither a heroic prince, nor is his power so great that one has cause to be afraid of him, etc. Such a Nuncio—who never saw His Electoral Grace of Saxony with his own eyes, much less ever conversed with him—writes this, without any doubt, not out of his own brain. Rather, he has received this from those who have diligently associated with him and have well-investigated his actions, power, military apparatus, and wealth. But as to who those people are—who have been sent to Dresden from elsewhere under the guise of great friendship and honor, but in effect and truth only to investigate and spy, or who have invented a pretext for such a journey themselves—this has been learned in those places from which, as they say, the Nuncio surely obtained his information: namely, as no one can prophesy better, in Dresden.

### FABIUS.

At home, you have learned with what colors the Calvinists <note>A major branch of Protestantism following the teachings of John Calvin, often at odds with both Catholics and Lutherans.</note> are painted, and you imagine we should paint you in just such a manner. What difference is there among us in words, gestures, thoughts, and intentions? Can you—who wander about in misery among the Dutch—pay attention to our gestures, or even (which belongs to God alone) investigate the secrets of the heart? We have our mind on our tongue, and our tongue in our mind, or heart. Be gone with your example, which you repeat so often merely to make us hated.

Perhaps one of all the Catholics might have written about the Duke of Saxony, who was unknown to him, in a way that might be interpreted poorly. Does it follow, therefore, that other Catholics—to whom the heroic virtue and bravery of the highly esteemed Duke are well known, and who declare themselves for His Electoral Grace—should therefore be mistaken or deceptive? Where now is your "contradiction," in which you pride yourself so much in this example? But you say: the Papal Nuncio was an Italian, who neither sent for the Elector of Saxony, nor...

also was known by the same: Should he therefore pass such a judgment on such an Elector? But, my dear Plessy, if the Nuncio knew nothing of this, then he can be all the more easily forgiven; for he would have judged differently of a potentate who was better known to him. Can you show me that anything adverse was said of His Electoral Grace by the German Catholics? I, however, will prove to you that you have written many abominable things against such an Elector. One part was recently published in the *Consultations* <note>original: "Consultationibus"; likely referring to the *Consultatio Cassandri* or similar political-theological treatises of the era</note>, but another part they wished to keep silent out of respect for the Electoral Dignity and Worthiness. In the meantime, you frequently throw such a free-spirited censure in our faces, and belittle us all together because of a minor statement, as if we were complicit in it; claiming that we report what we understand to the Nuncios <note>Papal ambassadors</note>, and that we wish—even backwards and outwards—to go into Saxony as spies, so that we might learn of His Electoral Grace as well as his secrets, and then report what is known to us to the Roman Nuncios or others? How presumptuously you fabricate such things, and yet you want to make the good-hearted reader believe that His Electoral Grace in Saxony is little esteemed by us: You, Plessy, will not be held so worthy that the reader should grant you belief.

->P L E S S Y<-

From all of which it clearly appears what marvelous judgments the Catholics have passed, both privately and publicly, concerning the Elector of Saxony <note>John George I, who maintained a complex neutral/pro-Imperial stance during the early Thirty Years' War</note>, who nevertheless hopes for much better from them. And although our compiler, in his chancellery, boasts everywhere of a special devotion and Bavarian reverence toward the Elector of Saxony, yet something quite different—and indeed the very opposite—is to be heard from the letter of the elder Duke William in Bavaria <note>William V, "the Pious"</note> to his son, the Archbishop of Cologne <note>Ferdinand of Bavaria</note>, dated November 9, Anno 1621: in which he mentions, among other things, that in his opinion, far too much honor and respect is shown to His Electoral Grace in Saxony: so that the internal affection does not agree in the slightest with the outward words. But the good old man (indeed, backwards) is perhaps to be excused by us, because fatherly love toward his first-born son, Duke Maximilian <note>Maximilian I of Bavaria</note>, and the impatience of a slower progress—namely in the transfer of the Electoral Palatinate <note>term: "Chur Pfaltz"; the dignity and lands stripped from Frederick V and given to Maximilian of Bavaria</note>—has so far enraptured him.

-># FABIUS<-

You are, Plessi, an unskillful washer <note>original: "ungeschickter Wäscher"; a metaphor for someone who tries to "clean up" or spin a story poorly</note>: For you are able to make so many words out of a single, somewhat absurd word from a foreign person, and—just as in a comedy—act out an ill-considered judgment regarding the Electorate of Saxony. You have passed wonderful and abominable judgments upon the same, which are not fit to be openly revealed. You have, as your own hand shows, written such things about them: And yet we have violated their honor neither with words nor with the pen, although you maliciously interpret some harmless words of the elder Duke in Bavaria <note>Maximilian I, Duke of Bavaria</note> in the worst possible way. For he only wrote this: *That in this place, Saxony is also respected far too much.* That is to say: many are casting a special eye upon His Electoral Grace, namely concerning the transfer of the Electoral Palatinate <note>The "Transferirung der Chur Pfaltz" refers to the controversial transfer of the electoral dignity from the Protestant Frederick V to the Catholic Maximilian of Bavaria</note>, which you then, according to your custom, "beautifully" distort. You claim that far too much honor and respect is being shown to the Elector of Saxony: Why must you incorporate such great honor into his record? No one has ever written, or even held the opinion, that His Electoral Grace is honored too much, but rather that he is perhaps somewhat more feared: which is so little against His Electoral Grace’s highly praiseworthy name that it rather exalts him, so that everyone might know that His aforementioned Electoral Grace is so respected by the Catholic Princes that everyone considers that perhaps too much is being done in the matter. We have not unjustly rendered our services to His Electoral Grace as much as we were able, because he, as a prince who loves justice, joined us in stilling the destroyers of equity through war.

### P L E S S I V S.

There we have yet another contrariety or contradiction to note in the Bavarian "sincerities." For in the Bavarian *Apologia* <note>A formal written defense</note>—concerning several intercepted letters—the writer (that is, as many believe, the compiler of the Bavarian-Anhalt Chancery) affirms that the Duke in Bavaria, in these dangerous courses of war, looked solely and uniquely toward the common good. However, how much he was concerned with private gain (that is, the Palatine Electoral Hat <note>The "Churhut" or Electoral Hat symbolized the status and voting rights of an Elector of the Holy Roman Empire</note>) is clearly to be understood from the cited letter, in which impatience so overcomes the good old man that he even becomes somewhat harsh toward the Electorate of Saxony on that account. This is further to be seen from the letter of the Papal Nuncio, dated Vienna, October 20, Anno 1622, in which it is found that the Duke in Bavaria

Bavaria has written to the Imperial Majesty stating that the entire Upper Palatinate has been brought under the authority of the Imperial Majesty by him. On account of this most loyal service, he claimed to have earned so much that what had been promised to him (meaning the Electoral Hat <note>The "Churhut" or Electoral Hat symbolized the status and voting rights of a Prince-Elector of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>) should be upheld. Furthermore, the Imperial Majesty had heard the opinion of his advisors, who unanimously—these are their formal words—concluded that such a thing was right and just. Therefore, anyone can easily perceive what a beautiful harmony and agreement exists among the Roman Catholics between their sincere professions, words, hearts, and thoughts.

### F A B I V S.

In calling our speeches "adverse," Plessy, you recount the eternal ones. The author of the "Line" <note>Likely a reference to a specific political pamphlet or "Strich" (line/stroke) of an argument.</note>, regarding whom your suspicion greatly deceives you, writes truthfully that the common good moved the Bavarian Prince to take up arms. However, when the war was for the most part accomplished, and the Imperial Majesty had promised him the Electoral Hat as an honor for his loyal services, he could not unjustly demand and expect the same. For this reason, the Imperial Court Councilors judged rightly and well. A promise is a debt, especially to those who have served so well.

### P L E S S I V S.

From all these things recounted thus far, the kind-hearted reader—unless they wish to be partisan—can easily conclude that the compiler in his writing has far overstepped the mark. This is especially true where he proclaims as highly damaging and public vices those useful counsels, proposals, and actions which were all aimed together at the preservation of the Protestant Religion <note>original: "Evangelischen Religion"</note> and the freedom of the Empire, especially of the German lands, as well as the suppression of hereditary succession and servitude as their principal goal. We therefore wish to publicly reserve for all and everyone, of whatever rank or being they may be, all due legal rights and other means to proceed against him.

->F A B I V S.<-

Yes, indeed, from all such accounts the kind-hearted reader will notice that your Calvinist <note>The author uses "Calvinist" here as a polemical label for the Reformed party</note> counsels, deeds, and actions have been highly damaging, because all of them were intended for the extermination of the Catholics and the ruin of the Empire. Therefore, do not choose to call your wantonness a "Freedom of the Evangelical Religion" or of the Empire. What kind of spirit has driven you is notorious. And what does the freedom of your Gospel concern us? The sect of the Calvinists is condemned among us, and should we abandon our own so that yours might have a place? No citizen lacked his freedom before you took the freedom of Kings away. And do you still think an injustice was done to you because your own letters betrayed and convicted you? We have brought your writings to the light; as they are now, so you also wrote them. Are they harmful? Certainly. One way of managing things is to learn to recognize the harmful ones. Are they harmless? Why then do you complain and even want to summon us before a court? This should be done to you; you are the guilty and the accused. When we both stand before the court, we will place excellent witnesses before your eyes: namely yourselves, your writings, your hands, and seals. What will we have to fear? We do not tremble at a slander, because we have slandered no one—unless one wanted to forbid us from writing, which is exactly what you have publicly done and wished to do.

---

->P L E S S I V S.<-

Furthermore, we—acting as those who are somewhat more cautious and modest, as it seems to us—wonder not a little why the Compiler, Cogmandolus, and Urbanus Freydenreich in his *Panegyric* <note>original: "Panegyrico"; a formal public speech of high praise</note>, as well as many others, triumph and rejoice so horribly simply because the state of the Evangelicals <note>Protestants</note> in Bohemia and the Palatinate has been somewhat unfortunate, and why they lay all the blame at the feet of the Palatine Councillors, as if they had not previously considered the matters sufficiently.

->F A B I V S.<-

Do you not grant us, Plessius, the right to rejoice over the victory we have achieved? You triumphed *before* the victory. And what is it now but justice?

Did you not also hold a festival of joy when that lying rumor spread some two years ago of I know not what victory? But you are vexed by the speech that is everywhere annoying to the Palatine Councilors, for people openly speak ill of them because they gave evil counsel. The Count Palatine <note>Frederick V (1596–1632), the "Winter King," whose acceptance of the Bohemian crown triggered the Thirty Years' War.</note> was driven by his fortune, age, and desire, but you were driven by another spirit, and you have far, far overstepped the bounds of prudence. And could you, Plessis <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a prominent French Huguenot strategist and advisor.</note>—if I may speak with you as a well-considered man, practiced over many years, experienced in many outcomes and cases, well-aware of envious Fortune, yet forgetful of equity—have advised the young Count Palatine to venture it? To trust himself and his own to fickle Fortune, to strive after other estates, and to build upon uncertainty, so that he might lose his own? But because I hear you preaching thus yourself, I will now remain silent.

### PLESSIS.

Firstly, as far as the conduct of war is concerned, it is well known that such a thing is in itself fickle and inconstant, especially since—as the proven axiom <note>original: "probirte axioma"</note> states—nowhere does the outcome correspond less than in war. To introduce the specific details here and the administration of the same would take far too long; rather, that remains to be answered in its proper place. The political foundations, concerning both the justice of the matter and the possibility of the matter, have been approved and sanctioned by those of high as well as low standing, both foreign and domestic. Thus, there is not the slightest doubt that the Bohemian cause is the cause of all Evangelical <note>Protestant</note> Bohemians. However, the fact that those who have hitherto taken up the defense in Bohemia did not succeed in the past years, and that they yielded to the opposing side—that is truly not of such a nature that it could be concluded that the cause was either unjust or that the matter had been managed imprudently. For the old rule is still valid today: that one should not judge before the outcome of the matter, and that often the best thoughts turn out for the worst. Paul de Foix <note>Paulus Foxius (1528–1584), a French diplomat.</note>, the secret and well-experienced councilor of King Henry III of France, said very well in De Thou <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou (Thuanus), a famous historian; the reference is to his "History of His Own Time."</note>, in the 49th book of his Histories, that all human affairs, especially those concerning the conduct of war, are subject to fickle Fortune. So it also commonly happens that those things which have been well weighed and deliberated upon commonly gain an unhappy outcome; on the contrary, however, vain and rash...

<meta>This page continues the defense of political advisors following a military defeat, likely in the context of the Thirty Years' War and the Bohemian Revolt. The catchword "but" (aber) from the previous page leads into the discussion of how public opinion judges counsel based on outcomes rather than merit.</meta>

# 201

...well-considered advice turns out quite fortunately: it is then customary that the common man, who is more mindful of the outcome than the nature of the matter, praises the [fortunate] result, but conversely despises and belittles even the cleverest and wisest counsel [if the outcome is poor]. I do not deny that a just cause commonly meets with an uneven result. Have not other Kings, Princes, and Emperors, who have waged war, experienced fickle and adverse Fortune in their war councils and affairs in times past? Have you then forgotten how easily the great, almost invincible Spanish Armada was overcome and scattered a few years ago, during the time of Elizabeth, Queen of England? Who then said, or would even dare to say now, that the Spanish advisors had acted without foresight at that time, and that the blame for such a defeat should be attributed to them? Is the memory of that ambition already extinguished, in which Philip II, the elder King of Spain, sought the Crown of France after the death of King Henry III <note>Henry III of France, assassinated in 1589</note>, and brought the matter so far that he did not hesitate in the least to call the city of Paris "his city" (*la bonne Ville de Paris* <note>French: "the good city of Paris"</note>)? Nevertheless, he was afterwards repelled with great insult and mockery, as well as the loss of several millions in gold—which would have been sufficient to purchase many Electorates—yet it was never heard that such a cause was blamed on the Spanish advisors. Did not likewise the King of Poland <note>Sigismund III Vasa</note>, along with the Senators of the Realm, have to flee from Sweden and thus leave the Kingdom in the lurch (as they say) even unto this day? Who then spread so many lampoons <term>Pasquillen: anonymous satirical scurrilous pamphlets or posters</term> as has happened regarding the Bohemian defeat, and who has ever heard that the Polish advisors were accused and despised on that account? And finally, was not Archduke Maximilian himself elected King by several Polish lords, but a short time later overcome and taken prisoner by the Poles? Who then posted lampoons against him, and thus further troubled one who was already troubled?

### FABIUS.

Wisely enough, Plessis <note>Likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot advisor</note>, but yet not in all things. Fortune is fickle and inconstant in war: why then, O fool, did you trust Fortune so much? But your foundations and reasons—namely, why the war pleased you—were approved by persons of both high and low standing, both domestic and foreign. According to those same...

<meta>The previous page ends with the catchword "schla-", likely completing the word "schlagen" (to strike or defeat) or "berathschlagen" (to deliberate).</meta>

But I do not ask you, Plessi <note>Refers to Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent French Protestant advisor</note>; it is with you that I must now contend. No one since the beginning of the world has started such an unjust war without attempting to justify its causes. Various judgments test various things: a wise judgment allows a few things to pass, a greedy one allows many, and a foolish one allows everything to please it. Now, as to which kind of judgment you possessed, I will not dispute with you at this moment. Certainly, the first kind was not among you; for while you gazed with wide eyes toward another crown, you brushed past justice like a blind man. For what kind of justice do you actually possess? Did the Kingdom of Bohemia <note>original: "Böhäymb"</note> not previously have its King, and the King his Kingdom? By what right, then, do you now desire that which belongs to another? And by what right do you accept that which could not be given to you unless it were first stolen from another? If one wishes to rob another’s property and "rightfully" grant it to someone else, then even a Rhadamanthus <note>In Greek mythology, a judge of the dead known for inflexible justice</note> cannot be compared to the fairness of you Calvinists.

Furthermore, Plessi, I am not unaware that your unjust cause is not being blamed for the defeat suffered by the opposing side. Fortune is often fickle and oppresses those whom it ought to have elevated for the sake of their virtue. In the chronicles, many sad examples are to be found where, if we consider it rightly, Fortune was unjustly opposed to them. God bestows fortune and victory, and sometimes gives them to one who has not earned them, so that the one who deserved it more might not lose through victory that which he had well earned; or else He is accustomed to punish the better side through a murderous army because there was a worse evil elsewhere. But be that as it may, God is not always ungracious to the pious, yet He is never gracious to the wicked. You will recognize neither man by one or two deeds, but by many; for he cannot have God as a friend who has been struck so often by His right hand, as the Calvinists have felt in this war. For when has it ever gone fortunately for them? And what lucky star, such as Helice <note>The Great Bear constellation, used for navigation</note>, have they looked upon all this time? What Castors and Polluxes <note>The Gemini twins, regarded as protectors of sailors</note> have appeared to them so that they might sail? Only Helena <note>Helen of Troy; here used as a metaphor for a stolen prize that brings ruin</note>—stolen by force and with premeditation, and now angry at her captors—sits and drowns the hated people in her ill-fated ships, which sank in their own bath. For the war they started to our detriment, they have now exchanged for their own ruin.

As for what you bring up regarding the misfortune suffered by certain Kings and Princes, count that among those who, though they deliberated <insert>at the right time—</insert>

# Page 203

...defeated; however, they met with an unfortunate outcome. The King of Spain <note>Philip II</note> wished to avenge himself against England due to injuries suffered; Archduke Maximilian was elected King by those to whom it was due, and thereafter marched into Poland. You, however, to whom no injustice had been inflicted, and since no one had called upon you in a lawful manner, have presumptuously attacked another kingdom. Thus, your hardship is not unjustly regarded and named a punishment for presumption and folly. The Spanish defeat, however, as well as the unfortunate outcome for Archduke Maximilian, are to be counted among the fearsome judgments of God, many of which are hidden, but none of which are unjust. I will believe in the greed of King Philip II only when you present witnesses other than yourselves, you Calvinists. That same King sent an army into France at that time, not so that he might seize that kingdom, but rather to come to the aid of the suffering Catholics, who desired foreign help and power for their own protection. You say, however, that no mocking poems <note>original: "Carmina"</note> or lampoons <note>original: "pasquillen"; satirical anonymous attacks</note> were written against the unfortunate designs of the Catholics. Yes, indeed, as you say: but the descriptions of such misfortunes which were carried about at that time were not much better than lampoons, and they lacked only the bare name. But let us suppose that were the case.

Those blinded by human nature also laugh at a reckless undertaking which Law and Reason have already begun to oppose! You began yours without right, without reason, presumptuously, faithlessly, contrary to gods and men, with insolence, ostentation, and threatening words, leading to the ruinous downfall of all Europe. And indeed, because you cried out so defiantly, fiercely, and puffed up at that time—namely, that you wanted to conquer everything—you have returned either as nothing or with bloodied heads. Therefore, the common man has begun to open his mouth, to laugh, and to mock such heroes who even wanted to overcome Mount Olympus. Who, then, would want to stop everyone's mouth here, or refute every writing? We sing of that which you have committed.

->PLESSIS<-
<note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a prominent French Protestant writer and diplomat.</note>

And indeed, in this case, one should repeat what Paulus Aemilius <note>A Roman general famous for his victory at Pydna</note> warns in Livy, Decade 5, book 5, where he says: "You see a memorable example of the change in human affairs; therefore, in times of happy prosperity, no one should advise anything puffed up or violent, because one cannot know..."

->FABIUS<-

Well then, my dear Plessi <note>Likely Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader</note>, why should your adversity go to our hearts, when you do not let ours concern you? Why should I apply myself to modesty, when you have forgotten the same? For though you have already been conquered, scattered, and put to flight, nonetheless you have—even in misery—retained your hostile minds, and you venture even to this day to begin a war, if you had not already begun it. I certainly have sufficient pity when I see a poor, troubled, and needy person before my eyes. But which among you does not still have such a spirit as the enemy has? Who troubles themselves about that which you have all committed in a malicious, intentional way? Indeed, it pains you that it did not [successfully] happen. Why then do you now call me to the oration of Aemilius <note>referring to Lucius Aemilius Paullus, the Roman general who defeated Perseus of Macedon</note>? Such a speech serves the Calvinists: let me also now speak somewhat with that Roman commander, who pointed out the captured King Perseus, and point with my fingers at a prince well known to you. You Calvinists have a memorable example of human change before your eyes. The Count Palatine <note>Frederick V, the "Winter King"</note> is that very one—the Elector, namely—who flourished sufficiently in wealth and age. But because he, through the negligent advice of his counselors, desired far too much, he has meanwhile lost everything, has been driven out of his own fatherland by weapons, and must elsewhere endure the misery and wretchedness <note>original: "miseriam"</note> which he hoped to inflict upon another. Therefore, O you Heidelberg counselors, in good prosperity you should advise nothing inflated or violent against another, especially so that a lawful king might not perhaps be driven out, and it is uncertain what (because favorable Fortune is fickle, and not to be trusted, even as you had painted it for yourselves as if in a comedy) the evening might bring with it. Certainly that same thing is uncertain, and you Calvinists have seen an evening that baptizes you all in opposition. O how painfully you have to think of such an evening, on which your army at Prague <note>The Battle of White Mountain, 1620</note>—all your hope—went to ruin as if in a moment: you should have considered this evening before it came, so that it might not have come.

--- Page 210 ---
You comfort yourselves with the thought that the sun of all days has not yet set! But what shall the sun help you, Plessen <note>Referring to Philippe de Mornay, Lord of Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader</note>, who are rushing toward your own ruin? The sun rises and sets: you have fallen, and you did not know whether your fortune would rise again. We Catholics have no sun to fear: she has often been a good friend to us until now, and will hopefully be even more of a friend to us in the future; for she stood still and shone so that you could be defeated before the darkness fell <note>A biblical allusion to Joshua 10:12-13, where the sun stood still to allow victory in battle</note>. Therefore, cease from your fury and wrath; for even if you throw chairs and benches in the way, you will achieve nothing with your martial spirit. For although you have war in mind (as we well know), I do not concede that virtue will return to a place where it has never truly been.

->P L E S S I S<-

But so that we may proceed to the foremost article of the Jesuit spirit, we must also remember this: although our compiler, in the second main point, throws many accusations at the Calvinists, these are nothing compared to the actions of the Jesuits. These actions are recounted by Thuanus <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou (1553–1617), a French historian known for his "History of His Own Time"</note> in his History (especially in the place where he relates the reasons for the Jesuits' banishment from the Lordship of Venice and records them for posterity). To this we add what the city of Riga in Livonia wrote to Duke Christopher Radziwiłł <note>A powerful Lithuanian noble and military commander</note>—a letter printed in Wittenberg in the year 1622—namely, that the Jesuits would not permit any progress of a Universal Roman Reformation unless the city of Riga were first destroyed (in the same manner as happened to the destroyed cities of Wolmar and Wenden). From this, the Jesuits' particular "affection" for Germany can be noted; and if you know one, you know them all. They do not take to heart the misery of the ravaged provinces or the ruin of the poor subjects; rather, they are much better pleased by this: *A ruined country is more pleasing to us than a damned country* <note>original: "Piu tosto paese ruinato, che paese dannato."</note>. Experience in France, Germany, and Switzerland sufficiently testifies to this. Furthermore, the compiler, on page 45 and throughout that section, taunts the Calvinists, claiming that the Emperors, Electors, and Princes, as well as other Estates of the Holy Roman Empire, lived in harmonious unity according to the Peace of Religion <note>The Peace of Augsburg (1555), which attempted to stabilize religious conflict in the Empire</note> until the Calvinist innovation arose. However, it has been proven above that all such innovations are to be attributed solely to the Jesuits.

->FABIUS<-

# FABIUS

That you, Plessis <note>Philippe de Mornay (1549–1623), Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent French Protestant writer often called the "Huguenot Pope."</note>, have fabricated everything regarding the Jesuits is proven by this: you cannot recount any of the mistreatments of which you accuse them, but instead dismiss the reader to Thuanus <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou (1553–1617), a French historian and statesman known for his monumental history of his own times.</note>. If you do not know it, Thuanus was as fickle in his religion as he was with his pen, so much so that I have scarcely been able to correctly discern his true faith. Nevertheless, he recounts some things about the Jesuits which are quite similar to Calvinist mistreatments, and almost surpass them. But which people have they ever made war upon? What sedition have they instigated? To which King or Prince have they denied due reverence? Against which authority have they either started a rebellion or continued one? Thuanus says nothing of this, not even in the Venetian uprising <note>Referring to the Venetian Interdict of 1606–1607, where the Jesuits were expelled for siding with the Pope against the Republic.</note>, from which the Jesuits were expelled by the magistrate not because of any vice, but because they found themselves duty-bound to obey the Pope.

From there, you summon me to Livonia <note>A historical region on the eastern shores of the Baltic Sea, modern-day Latvia and Estonia.</note>, such a far-distant province, so that you might better fabricate stories without witnesses. For that the magistrate of Riga should have written such a fabricated fable to Prince Radziwiłł <note>Likely Krzysztof Mikołaj "the Thunderbolt" Radziwiłł or his son, prominent Lithuanian nobles.</note>—no one believes such a thing, except for one who desires it to be true. Furthermore, those in Riga have the same affection toward the Jesuits as you do; and in writing much about them that does not befit a truthful man, you are bold, agitated, and bring them to your side. Yet be mindful that like speaks to like, and they speak ill of good people.

You say: "If one knows one Jesuit, one knows them all." Therefore, if one or even more of them wishes for the ruinous downfall of those in Riga, do they for that reason also wish to ruin Germany? The Jesuits living in Germany are Germans, and they care for the air of their Fatherland just as much as the Calvinists—indeed, much more than those who ravage Germany. For who has ever caused so many conflagrations? Who has so often ravaged the fields and ruined so many magnificent lands, cities, and villages, if not the Calvinists? And do you dare to attribute such things to the Jesuits in France and Switzerland? There is no Jesuit in Switzerland or the Grisons <note>The easternmost canton of Switzerland.</note> yet living who could have done what you have been able to write. Because the compiler wrote correctly that the Emperor and other Princes of both religions lived in harmonious unity until the wretched origin of the Jesuits, it is false what you have "proven"—that all uprisings in the Empire are to be attributed to the Jesuits—unless you wish to credit them with your own "virtues" <note>The author uses "virtues" sarcastically to mean the vices or destructive actions of the Calvinists.</note>.

# PLESSIVS.

In conclusion, he burdens the Calvinists <note>Followers of John Calvin; here used as a polemical label for the Reformed party</note> with words, but in actual fact, he burdens the United <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant states formed in 1608</note>—indeed, the greatest part of the Protestants who ever subscribed to the Union—in four ways. The first reads word for word as follows: "The Calvinist spirit now attributes to the Imperial Majesty the honor, right, and sovereignty due to him; but soon he interprets such things only as outward appearance and a mere name." Answer: The Compiler <note>The author of the opposing document being critiqued</note> remains here in generalities, which we expressly do not concede to him. As soon as he ventures to prove and cite specific details, we will then provide him with a proper response.

# FABIVS.

The Compiler does not accuse all the United, but only the Calvinists, as the primary instigators of all the schemes and rebellions they have committed; and he does so not without justification. For first, it is certain that you have diminished the honor and power of the Imperial Majesty, and have grudgingly granted him his mere name. You say, however, that the Compiler remains in generalities: be that as it may, you have recorded many specific details yourself, so that it was not necessary to write what is seen happening daily before our eyes.

# PLESSIVS.

The second: "That now, almost all justice is being withdrawn—sometimes in few matters, sometimes in many, and finally by consequence—from the Emperor and the Imperial Chamber Court <note>The *Reichskammergericht*, the highest judicial body in the Holy Roman Empire, located in Speyer</note>, especially in those matters which are disputed between the two religious estates." Answer: The United do not presume to diminish the honor and power of either the Imperial Majesty or the Imperial Chamber Court, nor to deem the Imperial Majesty worthy only of his name; for they are not unaware that it is not fitting for them to do such things. For they only pray and desire that, according to the Ordinance of the Chamber Court, the distinction between matters and judges remains unchanged, and that what belongs to the Chamber Court should also be adjudicated there, and not all be shifted together—as is currently the practice—to the Imperial Aulic Council <note>The *Reichshofrat* in Vienna, which was more directly under the Emperor's control than the court in Speyer</note>. By this, the Imperial Sovereignty is not diminished in the slightest. Since the judgments, both at Speyer and at the Imperial Court, are published in the name of the Imperial Majesty; and one must not [deviate] from such an Ordinance of the Chamber Court, which was once [agreed] between the Imperial Majesty...

and [it was] resolved by the Estates, that one should not deviate by a finger's breadth without the consent of both parties; the highest necessity also required that such matters be presented clearly to all Estates at the general Imperial Diets <note>The Reichstag, or Imperial Diet, was the highest deliberative body of the Holy Roman Empire</note>, weighed by each and all after mature deliberation, and finally decided in a fair and equitable manner.

->FABIUS<-

That you rob His Imperial Majesty <note>original: "Kays. Mayest."</note> of his justice is proven by your words as well as by the deeds themselves, whatever you might answer to this, God grant. For by what ordinance of the Empire is it forbidden that His Imperial Majesty should not just as well administer justice at his own place and Court, as is accustomed to happen at the Imperial Chamber Court at Speyer? <note>The Reichskammergericht in Speyer was the highest judicial body in the Holy Roman Empire, often at odds with the Emperor's personal court</note> Whatever justification is settled at the Imperial Court, and whatever is performed in the name of the Emperor at Speyer, the same can also be performed elsewhere in his name. It was indeed resolved between His Imperial Majesty and the Estates so that what is decreed at Speyer might be recognized as law. However, it is not fitting that what the Emperor decrees in other places should be considered unjust. He is Judge in all places, but, due to the great multitude of litigants, he does not have his judicial seat in all places of the Empire. You need not worry about future Imperial Diets or bring your counsel to light: for if one should occur, you, as Calvinists, will not be heard at it, but will perhaps hear something else entirely.

->PLESSIUS<-

The third accusation is that the Calvinists, or the United <note>original: "Vnirte," referring to the Protestant Union</note>, sometimes want this specific judge, but at other times, in certain matters, want no judge at all. Answer: The United, or Calvinists, are not so wild and barbaric that they should not know or recognize that neither a house, nor a city, much less the Roman Empire, can exist without justice. They allow any judge, provided only that he is competent <term>Competens: having the legal authority to hear a case</term>, not suspect, or an open enemy. The Compiler, my good friend, is accustomed to pointing out such a judge in the Roman Empire at this time and in these current affairs.

->FABIUS<-

It pleases me well, Plessius, that you do desire such a judge in the Empire; but it does not please me that you do not desire the one whom you ought to desire. But do you desire an impartial and competent one? Who then is he? Is he a Calvinist? Neither the Lutherans <note>original: "Lutherauer"</note> nor the Catholics desire such a one, as they have the preference in law. Which

What more do you desire then? Who is not suspect or under suspicion? Who is not an open enemy? But who is that person? A Calvinist: under which name neither the Lutherans nor the Catholics would permit such a companion; for he is always an enemy, and one who is only an inhabitant of the Empire by way of petition <note>The legal status of Calvinists was precarious as they were not officially included in the Peace of Augsburg (1555) until the Peace of Westphalia (1648)</note>. Now, from ancient times, the Imperial Chamber Judge <term>Keyserliche Cammerrichter: the presiding judge of the highest judicial body in the Holy Roman Empire</term> has been a Catholic; why then should he now give way so that a Calvinist might succeed him? And yet you desire here from the Compiler <note>Likely referring to the author of a legal collection or the opposing advocate</note> that he should not provide you with a suspicious or hostile judge today; and this indeed not without cause, because the Compiler will hardly be able to do so: for you, acting as enemies, have made the Bishop of Speyer into an enemy. Do you then want me to become your friend, when you deal with me as an enemy? He would be a friendly judge, if only you were not enemies. Tame your wild spirit and apply yourselves henceforth to friendliness, and we likewise will do the same. And even if you persist in such abominable obstinacy, we shall nonetheless not be found unjust, because we well know that even an enemy retains his right to justice, and it is not unknown to you yourselves what we owe to rebels.

->P L E S S I V S.<-
<note>Refers to Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent French Protestant writer</note>

So that we might be better understood through examples, let us inspect the Imperial Chamber Court at Speyer somewhat: where it is known that the Roman Catholic Assessors <term>Assessorn: associate judges or legal advisors who sat on the bench</term>, and especially the Presidents, were raised in the school of the Jesuits and are their disciples, and that they have, as it were, swallowed whole the axiom and command of Pope Pius V (as is read in Girolamo Catena’s <note>original: "Hieronymo Cathenæ"</note> description of the life of Pope Pius V) namely: that above all else, everything must be settled according to religion. The "good intentions" of the Princely Judge and Director of the Imperial Chamber Court toward the Un-erring <note>Sarcastic reference to the Catholics</note> and the Protesting <note>Protestants</note> is far too well known, and it is no different with the Imperial Court Councilors and Presidents. And although some Evangelical <note>Lutheran</note> Assessors are admitted to Speyer—as perhaps one or another Protesting Councilor is also admitted to the Imperial Court Council—this happens much more *pro forma* <note>original: "pro forma" - for the sake of appearance</note> than because it is owed to them. For in the final decision itself, the direction and administration of justice commonly turns out in favor of the Jesuits and their disciples, who must judge and settle everything, as indicated, according to the Pope's will, so that they [remain] under the...

the Pope's power and favor might remain: Who would then advise the Protestants to justify themselves before such judges? ->F A B I V S.<-

He who has a bad cause, my dear Pless, fears the judge. The assessors <note>term: Assessors (legal officials who sit beside a judge to advise on law)</note> who administer justice at the Imperial Chamber Court <note>term: Cammergericht (the highest judicial body in the Holy Roman Empire, located in Speyer)</note> are hated by all because of the religion instilled by the Jesuits, and are terrifying to the Calvinists, who have a guilty conscience. But if we were to follow your lead in this matter, we could complain of the very same thing. The assessors present at Speyer, who assist the Chamber Court with their counsel, have soaked up their religion—which is hated by Catholics—from the preachers. What then have we to hope for from suspicious and hostile people? As if it were necessary that those who are of unequal religion must also be divided in justice and destroy the common good. If more Calvinists sat at the board <note>meaning: held seats on the judicial council</note> there, they would perhaps work toward the goal of sinking the rights and justice of the Catholics into the very ground. Now, however, they can do very little harm: The Lutherans hold equity much dearer than to act contrary to justice, nor could they say that they have been insulted by us, although some might consider it somewhat difficult that fewer Protestants than Catholics sit in such a session. But they should know that we have not increased the number of Catholic assessors in that aforementioned place. From the first order we send some; the Lutherans send a certain number. And why should our votes stay behind today, so that others should increase all the more? What you bring back onto the track regarding Windeck, Ungersdorff, Gottlieb, and Schoppen, those are the speeches of common people, who have nothing to say against the common good,

to speak or to write, are also not a hindrance to the administration of justice. For although you are somewhat hated among the eternal <note>likely referring to the Catholic clergy or the "eternal" Roman Church</note>, nevertheless, the judge—should the law be on your side—would grant you the victory in the dispute. But we must, in all things, conduct ourselves according to religion by the command of Pope Pius V. I can hardly restrain myself from crying out and [addressing] you <term>etc.</term> <note>The "etc." here indicates an abbreviated rhetorical outburst in the original text</note> but the emotion is better [suppressed] <term>etc.</term> Do you then dare to blurt out that Pope Pius V commanded us to settle everything according to religion? Should we then regard all Protestants—who are contrary to our religion and seek justice with us—as nothing, and for that reason overturn all law and equity? Yes, certainly; otherwise, one would have to settle and judge all things according to religion: that is, according to the counsel and admonition of our good conscience, so that we might act in nothing against the laws, but rather be mindful of God and of equity. You, Plessis, if you will not conduct yourself according to religion, then you must help yourself with lies.

->P L E S S I S<-

Since it is therefore entirely contrary to natural equity and common reason to force the Protestants to submit themselves to be condemned by suspicious Roman Catholic judges, the King of France has ordained for his Protestant subjects *Les Chambres mi-parties* <term>Bipartite Chambers: special courts composed of an equal number of Protestant and Catholic judges to ensure fairness</term>, and in the same has appointed assessors of both religions, so that before them the Protestants might receive law and justice and share it with others. Indeed, even the Pope himself, in consideration of natural equity, has publicly permitted his subjects in the County of Venaissin <note>A papal territory in France around Avignon</note>—specifically all those of the Reformed faith—to introduce and prosecute their legal cases in the French Bipartite Chamber of Nîmes, as may be read in Thuanus <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou, a famous historian</note> at the end of the 86th book. With what right or color <note>legal pretext</note> do the Roman Catholic Estates in the Empire now wish to deny the free Evangelical Estates—over whom they have not the slightest authority—that which the King of France permits to his hereditary subjects, and which the Pope himself grants to the subjects of the Roman See in consideration of natural equity? In short, whichever way one turns the matter, if no greater equity and fairer temperament <note>moderation or compromise</note> is maintained here, it can hardly happen that peace and quiet might be newly planted and maintained in existence <note>original: "in ESSE"</note> within the Empire.

->F A B I V S<-

<summary>This page features a debate between "Plessis" and "Fabius" regarding whether Protestant subjects should be judged by Catholic officials, highlighting the French "Bipartite Chambers" as a model for legal equity. It argues that even the Pope has recognized the necessity of fair representation for Reformed subjects in legal disputes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Fabius and Plessy regarding the legal standing and judicial rights of Calvinists within the Holy Roman Empire and France.</meta>

# FABIUS.

->FABIUS.<-

The King of France has also granted the Calvinists <note>Followers of John Calvin's branch of Protestantism</note> Calvinist judges; not because he believed it ran contrary to natural equity for a Catholic to pass judgment on a heretic, but rather so that he might simply have peace—especially among those who were weary of Catholic equity and who were quite accustomed to, and as if born to, hating virtue. Let the judge say what he will, it is deemed unjust simply because a Catholic has delivered the verdict. A similar situation exists with the Pope, if what you say of him here is true. But be that as it may, the Pope and the King of France allowed such judges so that they might reserve for themselves the supreme right of appeal. And will such a thing then, my dear Plessy <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot leader</note>, be contrary to natural equity? And since the Pope and the King of France are suspect judges to the Huguenots <note>French Protestants</note>, why then should they present themselves to be condemned? But you ask by what right the Catholics refuse to allow the Calvinists a Calvinist Council? Answer: by the law of nature, by which it is my duty to keep what is mine and leave to another what is his. What the King of France has done, he has done for himself; and the Emperor will also do what he will do for himself. But what that shall be, you will see. I suspect, however, you will see very little, because the Calvinists have received enough if they are left in peace within the Empire. If lawful equity were denied to you, you would indeed not complain unjustly; but the inequality of persons <note>The religious difference between judge and defendant</note> takes nothing away from the Law. Furthermore, peace in the Empire is not broken by the judges, but by those who cannot tolerate equity and who ought to be judged. The Catholic religion teaches that one should observe justice; whoever transgresses this acts against his own religion, so that no one has anything to fear from our religion. One has more to fear, however, from the Calvinists than from the Catholics, on account of their evil inclinations.

->PLESSY.<-

The fourth accusation, Leglichen <note>Likely a reference to a specific legal or conversational partner</note>, is this: namely, that all Calvinists and United <note>The "United" likely refers to the Protestant Union or allied Reformed estates</note> wish to have religious matters decided not at the Imperial Court or the Imperial Chamber Court <term>Cammergericht: The highest judicial body of the Holy Roman Empire</term>, but before all the Estates, or their deputies, in equal number—

<summary>Fabius argues that Catholic judges are inherently just and that Protestant demands for their own judges in the Empire are unnecessary, while Plessy begins outlining the Protestant demand for legal parity in religious disputes.</summary>
<keywords>Calvinism, Catholicism, religious law, Huguenots, King of France, Imperial Chamber Court, natural equity, judicial reform</keywords>

<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on the legal resolution of religious disputes; this page continues the debate between Fabius and Plessius regarding the jurisdiction of the Imperial Chamber Court and the validity of the Peace of Passau.</meta>

number of each party, should be amicably settled. Answer: This is a <term>fallacy of composition and division<note>original: "fallacia Compositionis, vnd diuisionis"; a logical error where one assumes what is true of the parts is true of the whole, or vice versa</note></term>, since it confuses private disputes concerning religion with the public grievances of the Protestants. For as far as private disputes are concerned, the United <note>referring to the Protestant Union</note> will gladly allow them to be discussed in the Imperial Chamber or Court of Justice, provided that they do not have suspicious <note>biased</note> judges in either court (in cases belonging to the Imperial Chamber).

### F A B I V S.

We, my dear Pless, confuse nothing; rather, you confuse everything with your rebellion. The grievances you call "common," you leave to the general Estates; private matters you do not withdraw from the Imperial Chamber Court, provided only that equal judges from your own side might be added to it. Oh, what <term>Chrysippean subtlety!<note>original: "Chrysippischen Spitzfindigkeit"; a reference to the Stoic philosopher Chrysippus, known for complex and hair-splitting logic</note></term> Or what new forms of justice, by which neither law is felt nor given. Well then, let us now deal a little with one another concerning a single monastery which you have taken from us, contrary to the established German faith. What will we then obtain in the presence of these "equal" judges? While our side demands what is ours, your side will grant us nothing: And what end will it finally take? The end that is sought is namely that you may possess what you have alienated from us. If a judge were to be admitted in equal number who is not a <term>Calvinist<note>A follower of the Reformed tradition of John Calvin</note></term>, he would be deemed "suspicious"; there would be no judgment and no law, except for a Calvinist one, by which what is mine belongs to another.

### P L E S S I V S.

However, the public grievances of the Protestants are by no means to be settled privately as a particular quarrel, but rather in the general <term>Imperial Diet<note>original: "Reichstag"; the deliberative assembly of the Holy Roman Empire</note></term>, or else (as happened in the year 1552 in the <term>Treaty of Passau<note>original: "Passawischen Vertrag"; a treaty providing religious guarantees to Protestants</note></term>) laid to rest with an amicable <term>settlement<note>original: "Transaction"</note></term> and contract. For such words as "through amicable negotiation and settlement," etc., are a thorn in the eye of the Catholics, who would like to spit upon the memory of such a treaty: which is truly puffed up and arrogant enough: since the kind-hearted reader will find in the entire Anhalt-Bavarian Chancery <note>A reference to the published diplomatic correspondence and polemics between the Protestant Prince of Anhalt and the Catholic Duke of Bavaria</note>

it will be found that the United <note>The "United" refers to the Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant states formed in 1608.</note>, but especially the Calvinists, have sought no peace or rest, but only war, rebellion, and unrest. The compiler himself says that only the Calvinist spirit wanted to settle the matter in a friendly way, even though it is notorious and well-known that all Protestants in general desired such a treaty, just as His Electoral Grace in Saxony <note>John George I, Elector of Saxony, who often attempted to mediate between the Emperor and the more radical Protestant factions</note> urged Emperor Matthias that he should promote the same. ### FABIUS.

Everywhere, either your grievances, or rather ours, are placed before our eyes, insofar as the disaster suffered by the Holy Roman Empire might be settled either through an amicable treaty or through other means. And in this case, I cannot remain silent about the fact that such a "Composition" is similar to the Treaty of Passau <note>The Peace of Passau (1552), which guaranteed religious freedoms to Lutherans but notably excluded Calvinists</note>. I cry out once again: O, what a head! Doubtless exactly as you might wish it to be. The treaty was broken by you, and you have taken much from us against your promise, and yet you now wish to take from us what we have left? We were barely satisfied with your treaty, and yet you nevertheless want to have it upheld: it is enough that the Catholic alliances hold together so that the Calvinists might not have even greater freedom. The compiler said rightly that you Calvinists sought neither peace nor rest. For you would have had equal peace with us if you had left behind everything that was not yours. And what do you hope to achieve with such a transaction? Do you take what is mine and still want to be in the right when I summon you before the court? I do not quarrel with you over what is mine; what is mine is mine: give it back to me now, and the matter is settled. You hope to obtain everything through the peace negotiations, yet you do not desire to restore anything at all. The Elector in Saxony desired to hear you out, but through equity, he did not wish to take anything from the Catholics: and so it happened, what His Electoral...

[Your] Grace has desired it. You have been heard, and indeed explicitly enough, because you have desired what is ours against all right and equity.

->P L E S S I V S<-

We wish, however, hereby to have all Protesting parties <note>The "Protestants" or those aligned with the Union</note> quite kindly exhorted again, so that they might not allow themselves to be deceived by the Compiler with such a book, from which they perhaps presume to draw a good doctrine—wishing thereby to carry away the praise of a good Politician <note>term: Politici (Politicians). ->F A B I V S<-

->P L E S S I V S<-

In the last paragraph, which is as it were a conclusion of the Bavarian Chancery, the Compiler—as if in a small bundle—comprises the following accusations:

First: That the Calvinist Spirit wished to persuade all men as if its actions were directed toward the overturning <note>original: "evertirung" (evertion/overthrow)</note> of the Austro-Spanish Monarchy.

Secondly: That under this fabricated appearance, it sought nothing other than a subversion of the Holy Roman Empire and its statutes.

Thirdly: That it presumed to introduce a new Calvinist government.

Fourthly: That it wished to introduce a Calvinist, and finally a Turkish dominance and tyranny, so that it might shove the most holy Christendom into the maw of the Turkish hereditary enemy of the Christian name, into servitude. To which this instruction and answer is given.

->F A B I V S<-

The Compiler wrote such things because you have done such things: Now we shall hear how you intend to defend such a lost cause.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Plessius and Fabius regarding the political motivations of the Protestant Union and the House of Austria.</meta>

# PLESSIUS.

->PLESSIUS.<-

To the first accusation, it is answered: that it is not denied that several few estates of the Union <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of German Protestant states formed in 1608.</note>, who are devoted to the Reformed Religion <note>Calvinism</note>, have, along with the other United members, most faithfully concerned themselves with the preservation of the ancient liberty of the Holy Roman Empire, and have undertaken to prevent, according to their ability, any foreign domination or monarchy, and everything that might prepare the way for such a thing. But that this Union was specifically directed against this or that domestic or foreign prince is expressly refuted in the Protocol of the Union (which is to be found at Munich). And indeed, the compiler quite absurdly mentions the Austro-Spanish Monarchy by name: because it is sufficiently known that if either the Austrians, or the Spanish, French, Poles, or any of the neighboring kings and princes should strive for a dominion or monarchy within the Empire, then all the United members—the Lutherans as well as the Reformed, indeed all (none excepted) Protestants in the Empire—would not reject any hereditary dominion of the same, nor any danger, so that they might thus preserve the ancient liberty <note>original: "vhralte Gefahr" - likely a typesetter's error for "vhralte Freyheit" (ancient liberty) given the context of the preceding sentence.</note>, even with the loss of their property, yes, even of life itself, and propagate it to their worthy posterity and descendants.

->FABIUS.<-

I fear greatly that, as beautifully as you speak, you will find it difficult to shield yourself with the truth. I do not deny that the Union <note>original: "die ewige" - likely a typo for "die Vnion"</note> undertook to exclude Spanish dominance, for which even the Catholics themselves have felt no desire. But you Calvinists have belittled us before the Protestants with untruths, as if we wished to introduce and propagate Spanish dominance. But with what foundation, Plessius, do you wish to prove such a thing? The government which we have had of old in Germany, that same government we have wished to maintain in its **BEING** <note>original: "ESSE" (Latin: existence/essence)</note> and prosperity. We ask, just as you do, for no foreign rule: every land has its scepter: if these remain in their **BEING**, then they are simply not Calvinists. If the others, who were united with your Calvinist religion, believed that we strived for another dominance, then they believed such things from false mouths. Let the Empire, the Empire, I say, remain in its prosperity, and let the Calvinist remain outside in his misery.
