<meta>This page continues a polemical dialogue between Plessius (representing the Reformed/Calvinist perspective) and Fabius (representing the Catholic or Imperial perspective) regarding the political intentions of the Protestant Union.</meta>

->P L E S S I V S.<-

Secondly, we by no means admit—and indeed we do so with public contradiction—that the United <note>The "United" refers to the members of the Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states formed in 1608.</note>, at the prompting of a few Reformed members, or that the Reformed themselves, have undertaken such a thing as to overturn the Holy Roman Empire and its highly laudable statutes. And because the Compiler <note>The "Compiler" likely refers to the author of a specific anti-Protestant tract being refuted here.</note> in his chancery has hitherto proven this with no appearance of truth (let the upright reader judge for himself in this case), a further and more thorough proof is therefore required of him (if only he is able).

->F A B I V S.<-

Regarding the second point, which the Compiler reproaches you with, you answer quite absurdly. He says: You have, under a fabricated pretext, sought the overturning of the Holy Roman Empire. You say: that one publicly says the opposite, that such a thing was sought by the United. However, Plessius, nothing of this sort has been cast as an accusation against all the United, but only against the Calvinist United, to whom it has been said what they have done, and with the testimony of the entire honorable world, what they intended to do.

->P L E S S I V S.<-

Thirdly, that the Reformed <note>term: Reformirte (Reformed). In this context, it specifically refers to the Calvinist branch of Protestantism as distinct from the Lutherans.</note> intended to introduce a new Calvinist government <note>term: Regiment (Regiment/Government). Refers to the political and ecclesiastical administration.</note> can neither be proven nor even presumed. For the very Articles of the Union <note>original: "Vnion Formular"</note> testify according to the letter (as was indicated above in the 3rd Chapter) that they maintain that such things should not have given cause for any renewals or other innovations in the Empire. And it is indeed not so very unbelievable—or rather, it is entirely credible—that such few Reformed members would have neither undertaken to introduce a new government against the order of the Union, nor ever taken such a thing into their minds. Considering they could not perform such a thing in the slightest without the consent and help of the other United members, and against the express will of those same United members; to say nothing here of the fact that all other Protesting parties <note>term: Protestirende (Protestants). Specifically those adhering to the Protestation at Speyer.</note> in Germany would have opposed them. Therefore, the Reformation is beneficial for all Protestants, and the Compiler will have to prove such a thing. To this, the Compiler might perhaps say: should such a new Calvinist government be like a Monarchy, Aristocracy, Democracy, or perhaps an Oligarchy? Truly, whatever he will answer us on this, he will renew himself <note>likely meaning "entangle himself anew"</note> and stick himself into a dangerous labyrinth: since at all times...

<summary>Plessius defends the Protestant Union against accusations of plotting to overthrow the Holy Roman Empire or establish a radical Calvinist government, arguing that such innovations would be impossible without the consent of all Protestant estates. Fabius counters by distinguishing between the general Protestant members and the "Calvinist" faction, whom he accuses of subversive intent.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the polemical debate regarding the political structure of the Holy Roman Empire and the alleged secret ambitions of the Calvinist (Reformed) faction. It follows a discussion on the legal nature of imperial authority.</meta>

It will remain true and blameless, what Thuanus <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou (1553–1617), a famous French historian and statesman known for his monumental history of his own times.</note> writes concerning the Roman Empire in the second book of his *Histories*, shortly after its beginning, where he says: "From the aforementioned points it is manifest that the common wealth of Germany—which might be so named in such a fashion—is one where, although the Emperor is foremost, then also the other Princes, and lastly the Imperial Cities also have their own lordships, rights, and customs over their subjects, including the right over life and death; yet simultaneously all subjects of the Empire, as well as the Emperor as the Head of the Empire, are subject to the statutes of the Empire, and thus all together and individually are subject to a common utility." <note>original Latin: "communis utilitas," referring to the "common good" or "public interest" of the state.</note> Such a government and type of common wealth is pleasing to the United and Reformed <note>The "United" likely refers to the Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant states formed in 1608.</note>; but let the Compiler <note>The "Compilator" refers to the author of the opposing text being refuted here.</note> bring a new or different form onto the track, or else defend such a thing, if only he can.

### FABIUS.

The other United members would undoubtedly not have allowed the new Calvinist government to please them if we had remained silent and the Calvinists had revealed themselves; for both their words and their writings, as certain indications of their secret counsel, have frightened the others away from this. And the smallness of their number will not move me to believe otherwise, because, under an honorable title and name, they were in need of other people's help and assistance; for after the suppression (which they had spun against the Catholics), they would have also subdued their own helpers and allies.

To answer the question with which you think to hope for a future government for the Calvinists, no one will be able to answer more aptly than you: a semblance of a common wealth? For if I were to answer that, I would answer regarding a rotten matter—not sluggishly or maliciously—that it would have been most harmful if (after the subduing and rejection of the Lutherans and Catholics) both religions were to be abolished according to ancient tradition and order. We know the Roman Empire even without your Thuanus: he describes it as he either saw it to be in the old manner or believed it to be, but not how you wished to change it. In this, however, you desire quite in vain that the Compiler should provide proof, as if you wanted to introduce a new form of governing, because you have sufficiently proven such a thing with the devastation of almost all of Europe. Did you at that time

at that time, did you not have it in mind to clear the Spiritual Electors <note>The "Spiritual Electors" were the Archbishops of Mainz, Cologne, and Trier, who held significant power in the Holy Roman Empire.</note> out of the way, and to add four others to the four remaining? Did you not have it in mind to conquer the Bishoprics, to force the Princes under your yoke, to plunder churches and cloisters, and to make everything Calvinist? New "wonder-beasts" in the Empire are also accustomed to introducing a new form of government.

### PLESSIS <note>Refers to Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a prominent French Protestant writer and diplomat.</note>

Regarding the fourth accusation finally, as well as its 8th Chapter, we have made mention of these above, where we have sufficiently refuted them. But because the Compiler, in the preceding verse or paragraph—*Along with and with the Collective*, etc.—repeats his old song, we find it necessary to answer: namely, whether the Calvinist spirit has taken counsel with the Turk <note>The Ottoman Sultan</note> and his creature (as he speaks), seeking help and protection there; and also whether the introduction of the Turks and Tartars was threatened by the Count of Hohenlohe at the Saxon Court? Firstly, it is not known whether the Count of Hohenlohe either threatened or protested this. Indeed, it is not in the least to be doubted that the aforementioned Count, as necessity demands, will quite well excuse himself in that regard.

Secondly: indeed, such a Count is no Calvinist. Thirdly: the deeply troubled Palatinate, as well as the subjects of the Rhine district and the inhabitants of Silesia, well remember that no one has cause to protest the introduction of Turks, Tartars, Cossacks, or Croats <note>original: "Crabaten." Light cavalry units often associated with the Imperial/Catholic side, known for their ferocity.</note>? Rather, it is more credible that they themselves would have much preferred the Turks than to have permitted these others; namely, from whom they might hope for a greater mercy and compassion. However, so that we might discover and completely refute the practice which the Compiler presumes to saddle upon the good-hearted reader at this place, we wish to faithfully cite the principal article of the instructions given to Johann von Cologne <note>John of Cologne, a diplomat</note> near Constantinople (which can be read in the supplement of the *Mirror of the Proscription* <note>original: "Spiegels der Achtserklärung." A reference to legal documents regarding the Imperial Ban.</note> No. 18, and is simultaneously preserved among the hung-up Prague Acts at Munich), which reads as follows:

"We therefore wish to have dutifully petitioned the Most Mighty Ottoman Emperor, so that He might render no help against the Kingdom of Bohemia and its incorporated provinces, nor permit hostile incursions of his soldiery or the Tartars; but rather maintain good neighborliness, friendship, and peace with our Serene King, his incorporated lands, as also with the aforementioned Royal Majesty's most mighty Father-in-law, the King in Great Britain, and His Majesty's uncle, Prince Maurice..." <note>Refers to Maurice of Nassau, Prince of Orange.</note>

<summary>This page refutes claims that the Calvinist nobility conspired with the Ottoman Empire to overthrow the Holy Roman Empire, arguing instead that diplomatic contact was intended to ensure peace and prevent invasions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the legal and political defense of the Elector Palatine and the Bohemian Estates against accusations of treasonous alliances with the Ottoman Empire during the early stages of the Thirty Years' War.</meta>

### FABIUS.

"...and intended to maintain with other Netherlandish provinces. And lastly, His Majesty’s willing service and good neighborliness will be demonstrated to the Great and Mighty Turkish Emperor <note>The Sultan of the Ottoman Empire</note> with the same customs, words, and praises as are used by the orators <note>diplomatic envoys</note> of the French, English, and Netherlandish, as well as other Christian kings and peoples, etc."

From this cited passage, it is now manifest what the primary request in this case was, and what compliance followed from it. Firstly, nothing else was requested than good neighborliness and friendship. One cannot find a single syllable, let alone a word, regarding requested help, protection, or various alleged "extremities" (which the compiler <note>The author of the opposing pamphlet being refuted here</note> baselessly attempts to tap into in the aforementioned article).

Secondly, this request was made so that the Turkish Emperor would not perhaps provide military aid to the opposing party against the Kingdom of Bohemia and its incorporated provinces, and so that his people, or the Tartars, might not invade in a hostile manner. Now, have not the Emperor, the King of France, the Netherlands, and other Austrians requested the same—and indeed in much more significant points— at the Ottoman Porte <note>The central government of the Ottoman Empire</note>? Yes, they have exerted themselves much more to the end that they might send the Turks and Tartars at the throats of the Bohemians, and this through various means and great promises; for both the Hungarians and Bohemians say this publicly and desire to prove it.

Furthermore, the Bohemian Estates, along with their Confederates, requested such friendship and neighborliness from the Sultan in Constantinople as he is accustomed to maintain with the King of England and the Netherlandish Estates. But who has ever dared to say or boast that the said King and the aforementioned Netherlandish Estates have placed themselves under the Sultan's protection and shield?

In addition, the offer which was to be proposed to the envoy—in both form and substance—has a harmony with the style <note>The formal diplomatic protocol</note> of the French, English, Netherlandish, as well as other Christian kings and princes. From all of this, it appears as clear as day that the Elector Palatine, as well as the Bohemian Estates and their Confederates (among whom there are few Calvinists, as they are hatefully called—and if one were to compare them with the Lutherans, one could hardly find ten for every hundred), are being treated with violence and injustice when it is claimed, as the compiler and other Roman Catholics pretend, that they wish to ally themselves with the Turks.

<summary>The author defends the Bohemian Estates and the Elector Palatine against charges of treason, arguing that their diplomatic contact with the Ottoman Sultan was standard practice mirrored by other Christian powers and intended only to ensure neutrality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the polemical defense of the Catholic cause against the accusations of Philippe du Plessis-Mornay, specifically addressing the charge of inciting the Ottoman Turks against Christian powers.</meta>

# FABIUS

That, Plessis, it is true what the compiler has written—namely that so many thousands of Turks have raged against the Christians—sad experience, alas, bears witness to that. Now, Plessis, you further deny that the Turk was incited and petitioned by you to our ruinous harm. It is also not to be wondered at that one man let a word of loyalty slip, since everyone had consented to and advised it. The Count of Hohenlohe, although no Calvinist, nevertheless serves the Calvinists and knew well of their counsels, so that he, according to your opinion, threatened us with the military power of the Turks and Tartars. That your Rhinelanders make no distinction between the Turks, Cossacks, Tartars, and Croats <note>original: "Crabaten"; refers to Croatian light cavalry units</note> is no wonder, because they have not sufficiently felt both of them. It was enough for them that they noticed the Croats, who did not come to you to help you in the vineyards. You have committed hostilities elsewhere, therefore they have repaid you like for like. That part of the *Instruction* <note>A formal diplomatic briefing or set of orders</note> given to Johann von Cöllen when he traveled as an embassy to Constantinople, you recount in vain; for it has already been said above that one thing is written, and when one has deceit in mind, another is spoken. It does not behoove you to keep friendship and neighborliness with the Turk. Whoever desires to be his friend is a bad Christian. The Emperor and free Kings have the power to establish alliances with the Tartars and the Turk for the sake of general peace, but not the Calvinists, who are subject to a higher Prince, and for whom it is a shame to petition the Turk that he should not come to the Emperor's aid. And those are the Emperor's "fine clients" who, so that they might utterly ruin their Lord, first withdraw aid from him and give the Christians a "fine example," which even displeases the Turk himself, who is more upright than the Calvinists. What Starzer and others requested in the name of the Imperial Majesty is unknown to me, and what you have written is without doubt false. But let it be that, against such cruel rebels, one has sought help and troops from all sides: what kind of dishonor would that be? If I can protect what is mine in no other way, I will call upon every arsenal for help. He who helps me stay alive is dearer to me than he who stabs at me so that I may not live. If violence had been done to you, if you had had to suffer the extreme, then necessity would somewhat excuse you. But now, such an abominable deed can expect no more mercy, so that you [oppose] not the regime, nor your welfare, but the Emperor, your Lord, and all of us Catholics...

<column-break/>

<summary>The author accuses the Calvinists of treasonous negotiations with the Ottoman Empire and Tartars against the Holy Roman Emperor, arguing that while a Sovereign may make treaties for peace, subjects committing such acts are "bad Christians."</summary>

<keywords>Ottoman Empire, Calvinism, Count of Hohenlohe, diplomacy, treason, religious warfare, Croats, Tartars, Holy Roman Emperor</keywords>

--- Page 227 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion of the ruin brought upon the Catholic cause; this page continues the debate regarding the alleged alliance between the Reformed (Calvinist) party and the Ottoman Empire.</meta>

Catholic cause / you have established the very ruin in which you yourselves now lie prostrate / and you shall not rise again / except through the kindness of those / whom you would have gladly seen fallen.

-> P L E S S I V S <-
<note>Plessius likely refers to Philippe de Mornay, Lord of Plessis-Marly, a prominent Huguenot writer, though here used as a persona in the dialogue.</note>

And how will the Compiler <note>The author of the "Bavarian Chancery," a polemical work attacking the Protestants.</note> ever be able to prove the final conclusion of his book: namely, that the Calvinist spirit intended to shove most holy Christendom into the jaws of the sworn hereditary enemy of the Christian name / the Turk / for the purpose of servitude? Are the Reformed then no longer Christians? Or can one conclude / that they / against their conscience / and Christian faith / would have intentionally undertaken to introduce the Mohammedan faith into the Holy Roman Empire / and their own Fatherland? Were so few Reformed, and their power, sufficient to carry out such a great work? Is it according to common reason / or even believable that free Electors / Princes / Counts / and Estates would of their own accord subject themselves and their Fatherland to the Ottoman government (which respects neither the danger of the subjects / nor the Electors and Princes / nor Counts / Noblemen / etc. / but holds them all together / without any distinction / as the Sultan's slaves / and bondservants)? Who is he / who would / or could believe such absurd things? Therefore, the Compiler of the Bavarian Chancery (as he is hereby publicly challenged in this matter) has yet to demonstrate and prove all of this.

-> F A B I V S <-

The more vehemently you deny all of this / the more shameless you are, Plessius: Ask the Count of Thurn <note>Heinrich Matthias von Thurn, a leader of the Bohemian Revolt.</note>, who is well known to you / and the devastator of the German lands: He / that very man / I say / has led the Turks / and Bethlehem's <note>Refers to Gábor Bethlen, Prince of Transylvania, who allied with the Bohemians against the Habsburgs.</note> war-folk / here for the slaughter of Christians: since you yourselves hold / that such a thing is against the faith / and Christian conscience / then by your own judgment / you are no Christians. To which judgment I am not opposed / but rather believe such loathsome things / because you have committed them. You were indeed not minded to subject yourselves to the Turk / but rather through them / to subdue us / so that you might henceforth live more harmoniously with the Mohammedans / whose religion, or sect / almost agrees with your own. The Compiler does not need / at your command / to prove / that you sought friendship and help from the Turk: your godless deeds have sufficiently proven your godless desires.

<summary>This page features a heated dialogue between Plessius and Fabius regarding accusations that the Calvinists conspired with the Ottoman Empire to undermine the Holy Roman Empire. Plessius argues the absurdity of German princes seeking Turkish servitude, while Fabius cites the actions of the Count of Thurn and Gábor Bethlen as proof of treason.</summary>

<keywords>Calvinism, Ottoman Empire, Bohemian Revolt, Count of Thurn, Gábor Bethlen, Bavarian Chancery, religious polemic, Holy Roman Empire, Islam and Christianity</keywords>

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of the Protestant Union against accusations of treasonous diplomacy with the Ottoman Empire, specifically addressing claims made by Catholic officials.</meta>

shown: You have, in various ways and through various envoys, quite diligently petitioned the Turk <note>The "Turk" refers to the Ottoman Sultan, Ahmed I or Osman II, depending on the exact year of the correspondence.</note> for help; to that end, a Jew even allowed himself to be used in your service at the court of the Turkish Emperor.

### PLESSIS <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a prominent French Protestant strategist and writer.</note>

Now then, let the compiler <note>The author of the opposing polemic being refuted here.</note> look upon the documents intercepted at Prague once more with boldness, run through them, consider them well, and thereafter prepare himself for the struggle: time will bring to light that he will run aground just as shamefully as Cardinal Khlesl <note>Melchior Khlesl (1552–1630), a powerful Bishop of Vienna and Cardinal who led the Counter-Reformation in Austria.</note> did. For several years ago, it was reported by several credible men that the aforementioned Cardinal (at that time still a Bishop) first in common discourse, then in a written counsel, and likewise in that letter in which he recounted the reasons why Emperor Matthias had made peace with the Turk, accused the United <note>The "United" refers to the Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states.</note> of a suspicious and secret correspondence with the Sultan at Constantinople, and finally even ventured to point his finger at certain members of the Union. Consequently, a pious and upright man gave this faithful advice: namely, that one should not yield too much to Khlesl in the aforementioned slanders, but rather that he should be properly pressed to provide proof of such specifics. After the approval of such advice, a Prince of the Union sent a letter to Khlesl, the content of which, regarding its entire substance, was nearly as follows:

> Because it has been reported that Khlesl, without hesitation, has declared both orally and in writing that the United, or at least some of them, were keeping good and secret correspondence with the Turk, it is hereby requested of him—as is proper for a Secret Imperial Councilor to do—that he name the same individuals and then allow the evidence prepared against them to come to light. For he himself well knows and understands that if he cannot bring forth any proof for such accusations, which have no basis at all, but should continue in his undertaking, then the United Estates would initiate a legal action for injuries <note>original: "iniuriarum actionem" - a Roman law term for a lawsuit regarding defamation or insult to honor.</note> against him in the proper place, and would make use of such means as are fitting for the preservation of their good name, honor, and reputation.

But because the good Bishop feared that the words he had poured out might perhaps be shoved back down his own throat, he considered a retraction without harm to be better than a proof with harm, and answered the thought Prince, in effect, quite modestly in nearly this manner.

<summary>This page recounts a legal and diplomatic confrontation between the Protestant Union and Cardinal Khlesl, who had accused the Protestants of conspiring with the Ottoman Sultan. When threatened with a defamation lawsuit, the Cardinal reportedly backed down from his accusations.</summary>

<keywords>Cardinal Khlesl, Emperor Matthias, Protestant Union, Ottoman Empire, Constantinople, defamation, Prague, religious diplomacy, legal action</keywords>

--- Page 229 ---
<meta>This page continues the polemical dialogue between Fabius (representing the Catholic/Imperial perspective) and Plessius (representing the Protestant/Union perspective), specifically addressing the "Bavarian Chancery" documents.</meta>

“ The rumors spread by those expelled from the Union <note>The Protestant Union, a coalition of Protestant German states</note> regarding their good correspondence and understanding with the Turkish Sultan were nothing but mere outcry and flying rumors; therefore, the members of the Union should pay them no mind. I wished to recount this particular detail here to the kind-hearted reader, and thus conclude this information of ours on the most prominent chapters of the first part of the *Bavarian Chancery* <note>original: "Bayrischen Cantzley"; a famous 17th-century propaganda work exposing secret Protestant diplomatic correspondence</note> with this stately example.

### FABIUS.

Now you are defeated on the battlefield, Plessy. For even if the Prague Records <note>original: "Pragerische Acten"; documents seized after the Battle of White Mountain in 1620</note> are not a public indication of the help you requested from the Turk, they nevertheless show us your well-inclined spirits toward the Turk—spirits which could very easily desire and also obtain help. For what should one not give to those who love them? Now, if there were any doubt regarding this friendship and request for aid, such doubt is now refuted by your writings and deeds, which are known throughout the whole world. You are sons of the wise little hen <note>likely a derogatory metaphorical reference to a specific Protestant leader or faction</note> with the Turk, and for that reason, because you were of one mind, you have been protected. As for what you recount concerning Cardinal Khlesl <note>Melchior Khlesl (1552–1630), a powerful Bishop of Vienna and statesman</note>, the reader need not concern himself much with it, as your histories are well known to him. Even if what you recount is true—though you do not prove it—that you had no friendship with the Turk, and yet no one knew anything of it publicly? Many who are evil are not recognized; but the Calvinists are otherwise known everywhere.

### PLESSIUS.

As for what remains that might be worthy of information and an answer, the same shall, by the granting of Divine Grace, be examined in due course in the second part.

### FABIUS.

There are many things remaining, my Plessy, which, by the granting of Divine Grace, cannot be publicly examined, since even the heavenly air is not favorable to such a godless undertaking; and no one conducts an examination in which he has cause to be ashamed. You have selected very little that you were able to tap into, and even that quite unluckily. I have broken your teeth and placed a bridle upon your tongue. For what more do you wish to say now, Plessy? You have been caught red-handed once; if you speak further, you will be caught further, because your crimes are so publicly revealed that not even a word—which buries everything—can cover them. Therefore, do not trouble our ears in vain: we have seen it with our own eyes.

<summary>This page concludes the first part of a polemical debate regarding the "Bavarian Chancery," focusing on accusations that German Protestants (the Union) conspired with the Ottoman Empire against the Catholic Emperor. Fabius claims the Protestants have been "caught red-handed" by the evidence found in the Prague Records.</summary>

<keywords>Protestant Union, Ottoman Empire, Bavarian Chancery, Cardinal Khlesl, Calvinists, Thirty Years' War, polemic, Prague Records</keywords>

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page concludes the text, following the previous discussions on religious defense and polemics.</meta>

->Greetings and Peace.<-

->P L E S S I V S.<-
<note>Likely referring to Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a prominent French Protestant writer often cited in German religious debates.</note>

Whoever the reader may be, I beg of him that what was recently permitted to Camerarius <note>Joachim Camerarius the Younger (1534–1598), a famous physician and humanist scholar</note> at the beginning of the section might be granted to me here at the end: namely, to speak the truth. For in the other book, because I was otherwise occupied, I was unable to do so. I have not written all of this for my own sake, but rather have been moved by shameful causes to defend myself and my fellow believers <note>original: "Religions genossen"</note>, who are mocked by everyone on all sides. Since I could not do otherwise, I have sought to gloss over these matters with a bit of clever maneuvering <term>Practicken: tactical or strategic arguments used in legal or theological disputes</term>. I did not do this in the hope that I would completely persuade anyone—for I would strive in vain to achieve in others what I cannot even obtain for myself. For while I can indeed affirm my innocence with words, cunning, and maneuvers, I cannot truly feel it in my heart. Yet I desire so busily to make others perceive it; nevertheless, I achieve nothing anywhere, for as I am, so am I also regarded. Let the reader not wonder that I confess this myself: for even if I did not do so, this book of mine would confess it; which, when it is read, must feel ashamed of itself.

->T H E  E N D.<-

<summary>In this concluding postscript, the author (writing under the name Plessivs) offers a candid, almost self-deprecating confession regarding the rhetorical tactics used to defend his religious community against public mockery.</summary>
<keywords>Philippe de Mornay, Joachim Camerarius, religious defense, confession, rhetoric, truth, Protestantism</keywords>
